## XXXII An Unlooked-for Return / 第三十二章 意外的归来
===Original===

In spite of her troubles, as she sat by the fire, looking out through the window, fatigue overcame Mildred, and she nodded. But her brain being troubled, and her attitude uneasy, she awoke suddenly from a sinister dream, and as still unconscious where she was, her eyes opened upon the same melancholy foliage and moonlit sky and the dim enclosure of the yard, the scenery on which they had closed. She saw a pale face staring in upon her through the window. The fingers were tapping gently on the glass.
Old Mildred blinked and shook her head to get rid of what seemed to her a painful illusion.
It was Charles Fairfield who stood at the window, looking wild and miserably ill.
Mildred stood up, and he beckoned. She signed toward the door, which she went forthwith and opened.
“Come in, sir,” she said.
His saddle, by the stirrup-leather, and his bridle were in his hand. Thus he entered the kitchen, and dropped them on the tiled floor. She looked in his face, he looked in hers. There was a silence. It was not Mildred’s business to open the disagreeable subject.
“Would you please like anything?”
“No, no supper, thanks. Give me a drink of water, I’m thirsty. I’m tired, and﻿—we’re quite to ourselves?”
“Yes, sir; but wouldn’t ye better have beer?” answered she.
“No﻿—water﻿—thanks.”
And he drank a deep draught.
“Where’s the horse, sir?” she asked after a glance at the saddle which lay on its side on the floor.
“In the field, the poplar field, all right﻿—*well*?”
“Tom told you my message, sir?” she asked, averting her eyes a little.
“Yes﻿—where is she﻿—*asleep*?”
“The mistress is in her bed, asleep I do suppose.”
“Yes, yes, and quite well, Tom says. And where is the﻿—the﻿—you sent me word there was someone here. I know whom you mean. Where is she?”
“In the front bedroom﻿—the old room﻿—it will be over the hall-door, you know﻿—she’s in bed, and asleep, I’m thinkin’; but best not make any stir﻿—some folks sleep so light, ye know.”
“It’s late,” he said, taking out his watch, but forgetting to consult it, “and I dare say she *is*﻿—she came tonight, yes﻿—and she’s tired, or ought to be﻿—a long way.”
He walked to the window, and was looking, with the instinct which leads us always, in dark places, to look toward the light, above the dusky trees to the thin luminous cloud that streaked the sky.
“Pretty well tired myself, Mr.

===Chinese===

尽管心事重重，米尔德丽德（Mildred）坐在炉边望着窗外，倦意还是压倒了她，她打起盹来。可她脑子里搁着事，坐相又不安稳，忽地从一场凶梦中惊醒，一时还没意识到身在何处，眼睛睁开，看见的仍是方才合眼时那片愁惨的树叶、月光淋漓的天空和院里朦胧的围墙。她看见一张苍白的脸隔着窗子盯视她。手指正轻轻敲着玻璃。
老米尔德丽德眨眨眼，摇摇头，想甩掉这个在她看来痛苦难当的幻象。
站在窗外的正是查尔斯·费尔菲尔德（Charles Fairfield），神情狂乱，病得可怜。
米尔德丽德站起身，他招了招手。她朝门那边一示意，随即过去把门开了。
「进来吧，先生。」她说。
他手里拎着马鞍——攝着马镫皮带——还有笼头。他就这么进了厨房，把它们掠在砖地上。她看他的脸，他看她的脸。一阵沉默。那桩不痛快的事，轮不到米尔德丽德先开口。
「您要点什么吗？」
「不，不用晚饭，谢谢。给我口水喝，我渴。我累了，还有——就咱们自己人？」
「是啊，先生；不过您是不是喝啤酒更好？」她答道。
「不——水——谢谢。」
他仰头喝了一大口。
「马呢，先生？」她瞟了一眼横躺在地上的马鞍，问道。
「在地里，白杨地里，没事——*怎么样*？」
「汤姆（Tom）把我的话传给您了，先生？」她问，眼睛微微避开。
「传了——她在哪儿——*睡着*了？」
「太太在床上呢，我估摸着睡了。」
「好，好，人也好好的，汤姆说的。那么那个——那个——你捎信说这儿来了个人。我知道你指谁。她在哪儿？」
「在前头卧房——老那一间——就在正门上头，您知道的——她上床了，睡下了，我想；可顶好别弄出一点动静——有些人觉轻得很，您知道。」
「不早了，」他说着掣出表，却忘了看，「我料着她*是*累了——她今夜到的，是——走了那么远的路，也该累了。」
他走到窗前，凭着那种本能——在暗处，我们总会望向光亮——越过黑沉沉的树梢，望向天边那一抹亮云。
「我自己也乏得够呛，

===Original===

Charles; you may guess the night I’ve put in; I was a’most sleepin’ myself when ye came to the window. Tom said ye weren’t a-comin’; ’tis a mercy the yard door wasn’t locked; five minutes more and I’d have locked it.”
“It would not have mattered much, Mildred.”
“Ye’d a climbed, and pushed up the window, mayhap.”
“No; I’d have walked on; a feather would have turned me from the door as it was.”
He turned about and looked at her dreamily.
“On *where*?” she inquired.
“On, anywhere; on into the glen. If you are tired, Mildred, so am I.”
“You need a good sleep, Master Charles.”
“A long sleep, Mildred. I’m tired. I had a mind as it was to walk on and trouble you here no more.”
“Walk on﻿—hoot! nonsense, Mr. Charles; ’tisn’t come to that; giving up your house to a one like her.”
“I wish I was dead, Mildred. I don’t know whether it was a good or an evil angel that turned me in here. I’d have been easier by this time if I had gone on, and had my leap from the scaur to the bottom of the glen.”
“None o’ that nonsense, man!” said Mildred, sternly; “ye ha’ brought that poor young lady into a doubtful pass, and ye must stand by her, Charles. You’re come of no cowardly stock, and ye shan’t gi’e her up, and your babe that’s comin’, poor little thing, to shame and want for lack of a man’s heart under your ribs. I say, I know nout o’ the rights of it; but God will judge ye if ye leave her now.”
High was Mrs. Tarnley’s head, and very grim she looked as with her hand on his shoulder she shook up “Master Charles” from the drowse of death.
“I won’t, old Tarnley,” he said at last. “You’re right﻿—poor little Alice, the loving little thing!”
He turned suddenly again to the window and wept in silence strange tears of agony.
Old Tarnley looked at him sternly askance. I don’t think she had much pity for him, she was in nowise given to the melting mood, and hardly knew what that sort of whimpering meant.
“I say,” she broke out, “I don’t know the rights of it, how should I? but this I believe, if you thought you were truly married to that woman that’s come tonight, you’d never a found it in your heart to act such a villain’s part by the poor, young, foolish creature upstairs, and make a sham wife o’ her.”
“Never, never, by heaven. I’m no more that wretched woman’s husband than I’m married to you.”

===Chinese===

查尔斯（Charles）先生；您猜得着我这一夜是怎么熬的；您到窗前那会儿，我差点自己睡着了。汤姆还说您不来了呢；亏得院门没闩；再晚五分钟，我就闩上了。」
「那也不大要紧，米尔德丽德。」
「那您兴许就翻墙进来，推开窗子了。」
「不；我会接着往前走；那时节，一根羽毛也拦得下我，把我从门前挡回去。」
他转过身，恍惚地望着她。
「往前走到*哪儿*？」她问。
「往前，随便哪儿；往峡谷里去。你累了，米尔德丽德，我也累了。」
「您得好好睡一觉，查尔斯少爷。」
「长睡一场，米尔德丽德。我累了。方才我真有心一直走下去，再也不来搅扰你们。」
「走下去——哼！胡说，查尔斯先生；还没到那步田地；把自己的宅子白白让给她那样一个货。」
「我恨不得死了才好，米尔德丽德。我不知道引我进这道门的是善天使还是恶天使。要是接着走下去，从断崖一跃跳进谷底，这会儿我倒解脱了。」
「少说这种混话，男子汉！」米尔德丽德厉声说；「您把那位可怜的年轻太太带进了难料的境地，您就得守着她，查尔斯。您出身的热血可不是懦夫的种，不许您丢下她，丢下您那快出世的娃娃——可怜的小东西——叫他们因为您肋条底下缺一颗男人的心，去受辱受穷。我说，这事的曲直我一概不知；可您这会儿要是撏下她，上帝自会审判您。」
塔恩利（Tarnley）太太昂着头，神情严厉，一只手按在他肩上，把「查尔斯少爷」从死气沉沉的迷沉里摇醒了。
「我不会的，老塔恩利，」他终于说。「你说得对——可怜的小爱丽丝（Alice），那痴心的小东西！」
他英地又转向窗子，默默淌下煎熬的怪异泪水。
老塔恩利斜眼严峻地打量他。我看她对他没多少怜悯；她素来不是心软的性子，几乎不懂那种呜咽算什么意思。
「我说，」她朗声开口，「这事的曲直我不知道，我怎么会知道？可有这一层我是信的：您要是当真认为自己同今夜来的那个女人结过婚，您绝不会狠得下心，对楼上那个可怜、年轻、痴傻的人儿使出这样恶棍的手段，把她弄成一个挂名的假妻。」
「从来没有，从来没有，皇天在上。说我是那可怜女人的丈夫，还不如说我娶了你。」

===Original===

“Mildred knew better than marry anyone; there’s little I see but tears and wrinkles, and oftentimes rags and hunger comes of it; but ’twill be done, marryin’ and givin’ in marriage, says the Scriptures, ’tis so now, ’twas so when Noah went into the ark, and ’twill be so when the day of judgment breaks over us.”
“Yes,” said Charles Fairfield, abstractedly; “of course that miserable woman sticks at no assertion; her idea is simply to bully her way to her object. It doesn’t matter what she says, and it never surprised me. I always knew if she lived she’d give me trouble one day; but that’s all; just trouble, but no more; not the slightest chance of succeeding﻿—not the smallest; she knows it; I know it. The only thing that vexes me is that people who know all about it as well as I do, and people who, of all others, should feel for me, and feel with me, should talk as if they had doubts upon the subject now.”
“I didn’t say so, Master Charles,” said Mildred.
“I didn’t mean you, I meant others, quite a different person; I’m utterly miserable; at a more unlucky moment all this could not have happened by any possibility.”
“Well, I’m sure I never said it; I never thought but one thing of her; the foul-tongued wicked beast.”
“Don’t you talk that way of her,” said Charles, savagely. “Whatever she is she has suffered, she has been cruelly used, and I am to blame for all. I did not mean it, but it is all my fault.”
Mrs. Tarnley sneered, but said nothing, and a silence followed.
“I know,” he said, in a changed way, “you mean kindly to me.”
“Be kind to yourself. I hold it’s the best way in this bleak world, Mr. Charles. I never was thanked for kindness yet.”
“You have always been true to me, Mildred, in your own way﻿—in your own way, mind, but always true, and I’ll show you yet, if I’m spared, that I can be grateful. You know how I am now﻿—no power to serve anyone﻿—no power to show my regard.”
“I don’t complain o’ nothing,” said Mildred.
“Has my brother been here, Mildred?” he asked.
“Not he.”
“No letters for me?” asked he.
“Nothing, sir.”

===Chinese===

「米尔德丽德可比谁都明白，索性谁也不嫁；我眼里看不见别的，只有眼泪和皱纹，还常常落下破衣和饥饿；可这门事总要有人做，嫁了又嫁，娶了又娶，圣书上说的，如今如此，挪亚进方舟那会儿如此，审判日压到咱们头顶那天，还会如此。」
「是啊，」查尔斯·费尔菲尔德心不在焉地说；「当然，那个可鄙的女人什么话都敢咬定；她的主意无非是横着膀子逼到她的目的。她说什么都无所谓，也从没让我吃惊。我一向知道，只要她活着，早晚要给我惹麻烦；可也就止于此；只是麻烦，别无其他；成功的机会半点没有——一丝一毫都没有；她知道，我知道。唯一叫我恼的是，那些同我一样知根知底的人，那些在众人里头最该体谅我、同我一条心的人，如今说话倒像是心里存着疑。」
「我可没这么说，查尔斯少爷。」米尔德丽德说。
「我不是说你，我说的是旁人，完全是另一个人；我惨透了；这桩事偏赶在这个时辰，再没有比这更倒霉的了。」
「反正我从没说过；我对她从来只有一个看法；那长着毒舌头的东西，坏心眼的畜生。」
「不许你这样讲她，」查尔斯狠狠地说。「不管她如今是什么人，她受过苦，她被狠心地作践过，这全都怪我。我不是有意的，可一切都是我的错。」
塔恩利太太冷笑一声，没说什么，接着是一阵沉默。
「我知道，」他换了副口气说，「你对我是好意。」
「对您自己好些吧。我认定，在这凉薄世上这是最好的活法，查尔斯先生。我好心待人，还从没得过一声谢呢。」
「你对我一向是实的，米尔德丽德，按你自己的方式——记着，按你自己的方式，可一向是实的；只要我还活得成，日后我自会让你看见，我懂得感恩。你知道我眼下这光景——没力气替任何人效劳——没本事表一分心意。」
「我没什么好抱怨的。」米尔德丽德说。
「我兄弟来过吗，米尔德丽德？」他问。
「他才不来。」
「没有我的信？」他问。
「什么也没有，先生。」
「要人搭把手的时候从来没人——从来没有，」查尔斯苦涩地哼了一声；「从没有哪个汉子被逼得这样上墙——从没有哪个汉子离山穷水尽更近。真高兴，米尔德丽德，还有个人说话——一个老朋友。

===Original===

I don’t know what to do﻿—I can’t make up my mind to anything, and if I hadn’t you just now, I think I should go distracted. I have a great deal to ask you. That lady, you say, has been in her room some time﻿—did she talk loud﻿—was she angry﻿—was there any noise?”
“No, sir.”
“Who saw her?”
“No one but myself, and the man as drove her.”
“Thank God for that. Does she know about my﻿—did she hear that your mistress is in the house?”
“I said she was Master Harry’s wife, and told her, Lord forgive me, that he was here continually, and you hardly ever, and then only for a few hours at a time.”
“That’s very good﻿—she believed it?”
“Every word, so far as I could see. I a’ told a deal o’ lies.”

“And the beginning of sin is like the coming in of waters, and ’twill soon make an o’er wide gap for itself, and lay all under.”
“Yes﻿—and﻿—and﻿—you really think she believed all you said?”
“Ay, I do,” answered she.
“Thank God, again!” said he, with a deep sigh. “Oh, Mildred, I wish I could think what’s best to be done. There are ever so many things in my head.”
She felt a trembling she thought in the hand he laid upon her arm.
“Take a drink o’ beer, you’re tired, sir,” said she.
“No, no﻿—not much﻿—never mind, I’m better as I am. How has your mistress been?”
“Well, midlin’﻿—pretty well.”
“I wish she was quite well, Mildred﻿—it’s very unlucky. If the poor little thing were only quite well, it would make everything easy; but I daren’t frighten her﻿—I daren’t tell her﻿—it might be her death. Oh, Mildred, isn’t all this terrible?”
“Bad enough﻿—I can’t deny.”
“Would it be better to run that risk and tell her everything?” he said.
“Well, it *is* a risk, an’ a great one, and it might be the same as puttin’ a pistol to her head and killin’ her; ’tis a tryin’ time with her, poor child, and a dangerous bed, and mind ye this, if there’s any talk like that, and the crying and laughing fits mayhap that comes with it, don’t ye think but the old cat will hear it, and then in the wild talk a’s out in no time, and the fat in the fire; no, if she’s to hear it, it can’t be helped, and the will o’ God be done; but if I was her husband, I’d sooner die than tell her, being as she is.”
“No, of course, no﻿—she must not be told; I’m sure you’re right, Mildred. I wish Harry was here, he thinks of things sometimes that don’t strike me.

我不知道该怎么办——我什么都拿不定主意；方才要不是有你在，我怕是要疯了。我有好多话要问你。你说那位太太在她房里待了些时候了——她说话声响大吗——生气了没有——有什么动静？」
「没有，先生。」
「谁见过她？」
「就我，还有赶车送她来的那个男人。」
「为此感谢上帝。她知道我的——她听说你家太太在宅子里没有？」
「我说她是哈利（Harry）少爷的太太，还告诉她——主饶恕我——说他成天价在这儿，您呢难得来，来了也只待几个钟头。」
「这一着好极了——她信了？」
「字字都信，我看是。我撒了老大一堆谎。」
「好，好，还有呢？」
「罪的开端就像大水灌进来，不多时就给自己冲出一道收不住的口子，把一切都淹在底下。」
「是——还有——还有——你当真觉得你说她都信了？」
「嗯，我当真觉得。」她答。
「又是感谢上帝！」他长叹一声说。「哦，米尔德丽德，但愿我能想出上策。我脑子里搅着的事太多了。」
她觉出他搭上她胳膊的那只手，仿佛在抖。
「喝口啤酒吧，您累了，先生。」她说。
「不，不——不多喝——不用了，我这样就不错。你家太太近来怎么样？」
「还行，凑合——还算好。」
「巴不得她大好才好，米尔德丽德——太不巧了。只要那可怜的小东西身子大好，什么都好办；可我不敢吓她——我不敢告诉她——兴许要她的命。哦，米尔德丽德，这一切可怕不可怕？」
「够糟的——我不否认。」
「冒那个险、把一切全告诉她，是不是倒好些？」他说。
「呃，那*确是*个险，还是大险，就跟拿手枪顶着她头打死她差不多；她这阵子正熬着呢，可怜的孩子，又正是卧床危险的时候；您记着我这话：只要有那么一类话，兴许还带上哭一阵笑一阵的发作，您别以为那老猫听不见，她一听，胡话里一转眼的工夫就全抖搂出来，事情就砸进火里；不，她要是命里注定听见，那没法子，就依上帝的旨意；可我要是她的男人，照她眼下的光景，我宁死也不告诉她。」
「不，当然，不——决不能告诉她；你说得对，米尔德丽德。巴不得哈利在，他有时候能想到我想不到的地方。

I wish Harry would come, he might think of something﻿—he would, I dare say﻿—he would, I’m certain.”
“I wish that woman was back again where she came from,” said Mildred, from whose mind the puce *gros de Naples* was fading, for she had a profound distrust of her veracity, and the pelisse looked very like a puce-coloured lie.
“Don’t, Mildred﻿—don’t, like a good creature﻿—you won’t for my sake, speak harshly of that unhappy person,” he said gently this time, and laying his hand on her shoulder. “I’m glad you are here, Mildred﻿—I’m very glad; I remember you as long as I can remember anything﻿—you were always kind to me, Mildred﻿—always the same﻿—true as steel.”
He was speaking with the friendliness of distress. It is in pain that sympathy grows precious, and with the yearning for it, returns something of the gentleness and affection of childhood.
“She’s come for no good,” said Mildred, “she’s sly, and she’s savage, and if you don’t mind me saying so, I often thought she was a bit mad﻿—folk as has them fits, ye know, they does get sometimes queerish.”
“We can talk of her by-and-by,” said he; “what was in my mind was about a different thing. For a thousand reasons I should hate a fracas﻿—I mean a row with that person at present; you know yourself how it might affect the poor little thing upstairs. Oh, my darling, my darling, what have I brought you into?”
“Well, well, no help for spilled milk,” said Mildred. “What was you a-thinking of?”
“Oh, yes, thank you, Mildred﻿—I was thinking﻿—yes﻿—if your mistress was well enough for a journey, I’d take her away from this﻿—I’d take her away immediately﻿—I’d take her quite out of the reach of that﻿—that restless person. I ought to have done so at once, but I was so miserably poor, and this place here to receive us, and who could have fancied she’d have dreamed, in her state of health, and with her affliction﻿—her sight, you know﻿—of coming down here again; but I’m the unluckiest fellow on earth; I never, by any chance, leave a blot that isn’t hit. Don’t you think, Mildred, I had better not wake your mistress tonight to talk over plans?”
“Don’t you go near her; a sight of your face would tell her all wasn’t right.”
“I had better not see her, you think?”
“*Don’t* see her.

巴不得哈利来，他兴许能想出主意——他能，我敢说——他一定能。」
「我倒盼着那女人从哪儿来的回哪儿去，」米尔德丽德说；那件梅紫色的*斜纹绸*（gros de Naples）在她心里正渐渐褪色，因为她对那女人说真话的本事信得极浅，那件斗篷瞧着极像一袭梅紫色的谎言。
「别说了，米尔德丽德——别说了，做个好人——看在我的份上，别把那个不幸的人说得太重。」这回他的口气温和了，一只手搭上她的肩。「有你在，我真高兴，米尔德丽德——高兴得很；只要我还记得任何事，我就记得你——你一向待我好，米尔德丽德——一向一个样——铁一样实。」
他说这话，带着患难中的亲热。人正是在苦痛里，才觉出体恤的可贵；而渴望体恤的时候，儿时那份温存和依恋便多少回来了。
「她来就没安好心，」米尔德丽德说，「又阴，又凶；您别怪我多嘴，我一向就觉得她有点疯——害那种一阵阵毛病的人，您知道，有时候就是要出些怪样。」
「她的事回头再谈，」他说；「我心里搁着的是另一桩事。千条万条理由，我都怕一场厮闹——我是说眼下同那个人吵翻；你自己也明白，那会怎样波及楼上那个可怜的小东西。哦，我的宝贝，我的宝贝，我把你带进了什么境地？」
「罢了，罢了，泼出去的牛奶收不回，」米尔德丽德说。「您方才想的是什么呢？」
「哦，对了，谢谢你，米尔德丽德——我方才想——是啊——只要你家太太的身子经得起路途，我就带她离开这儿——马上就带她走——把她带得远远的，叫那个——那个不安分的家伙够不着。我本该一开始就这么办，可我穷得那么可怜，这儿又有现成地方安身；再说谁能想到，凭她那副身子、那桩残缺——她的眼睛，你知道——她还会起意再下到这儿来；可我是天底下最倒霉的人；我落下个墨点子，从来就没有不挨戳的。米尔德丽德，你说，我今晚是不是顶好别叫醒你家太太商量打算？」
「您千万别挨近她；一见到您的脸，她就知道出了事。」
「我顶好不见她，你是这意思？」
「*千万别*见她。

So soon as you know yourself what you’re going to do with her, and if you make up your mind tonight so much the better﻿—write you to tell her what she’s to do, and give me the letter and I’ll give it to her as if it came by a messenger; and take you my counsel﻿—don’t you stop here a minute longer than you can. Leave before daybreak, you’re no use here, and if she finds you ’twill but make bad worse. When will ye lie down﻿—you’ll not be good for nothin’ tomorrow if ye don’t sleep a bit﻿—lie down on the sofa in the parlour, and your cloak is hangin’ in the passage, and be you out o’ the house by daybreak, and I’ll have a bit o’ breakfast ready before ye go.”
“And there’s Lady Wyndale, I didn’t tell you, offered to take care of Alice, your mistress, and she need only go there for the present; but that might be too near, and I was thinking it might not do.”
“Best out o’ reach altogether when ye go about it,” said Mildred. “Sit here if you like it, or lie down, as I said, in the parlour, and if you settle your mind on any plan just knock at my door, and I’ll have my clothes about me and be ready at call, and Tom’s in his old crib under the stair, if you want him to get the saddle on the horse, and I won’t take down the fire, I’ll have it handy for your breakfast, and now I can’t stop talkin’ no longer, for Mildred’s wore off her feet﻿—will ye take a candle, or will ye stop here?”
“Yes, give me a candle, Mildred﻿—thanks﻿—and don’t mind the cloak, I’ll get it myself, I will lie down a little, and try to sleep﻿—I wish I could﻿—and if you waken shake me up in an hour or two, something must be settled before I leave this, something *shall* be settled, and that poor little creature out of reach of trouble and insult. Don’t forget. Good night, Mildred, and God bless you, Mildred, God forever bless you.”

等您自己拿定了主意拿她怎么办——今夜就能定下来更好——您写信告诉她该做什么，信交给我，我递给她，就当是捎信人送来的；再听我一句劝——能待多久也别多待一分钟。天亮前就走，您在这儿帮不上忙，她要是撞见您，只会把坏的弄得更坏。您几时躺下？——不眯一会儿，明天您就什么都顶不上了——客厅沙发上躺躺，您的斗篷就挂在过道里；天亮前务必出宅子，走之前我给您备下一口早饭。」
「还有温代尔夫人（Lady Wyndale），我没告诉过你，她提出来照看爱丽丝，你家太太，眼下只消到她那儿去住；可那儿兴许太近，我琢磨着怕不妥。」
「要办就索性办到够不着的地方，」米尔德丽德说。「愿意坐就坐这儿，愿意躺就照我说的，去客厅躺下；心里定下什么章程，就敲我的门，我衣服不下身，随叫随起；汤姆睡在楼梯底下他那老铺上，要他给马备鞍，喊他就是；炉火我不撤，留着给您温早饭；这会儿我可不能再聊了，米尔德丽德这两条腿都站散了——您是点支蜡烛呢，还是就在这儿待着？」
「好，给我一支蜡烛，米尔德丽德——谢谢——斗篷不用管，我自己去拿。我躺一会儿，想法子睡——但愿睡得着——你要是醒着，过一两个钟头把我摇醒；离开这儿之前，必须定下一件事，*一定要*定下一件事，让那可怜的小东西躲开祸事和羞辱。别忘了。晚安，米尔德丽德，上帝保佑你，米尔德丽德，上帝永远保佑你。」

## XXXIV Awake / 第三十四章 醒转
===Original===

In his dream, a pale frightened face approached him slowly, and recoiling uttered a cry. The scream was horribly prolonged as the figure receded. He thought he recognised someone﻿—dead or living he could not say﻿—in the strange, Grecian face, fixed as marble, that with enormous eyes, had looked into his.
With this sound ringing in his ears he awoke. As is the case with other overfatigued men, on whom, at length, slumber has seized, he was for a time in the attitude of wakefulness before his senses and his recollection were thoroughly aroused, and his dream quite dissipated. Another long shriek, and another, and another, he heard. Charles recognised, he fancied, his wife’s voice. Scared, and wide awake, he ran from the room﻿—to the foot of the stairs﻿—up the stairs. A tread of feet he heard in the room, and the door violently shaken, and another long, agonized scream.
Over this roof and around it is the serenest and happiest night. The brilliant moon, the dark azure and wide field of stars make it a night for holy thoughts, and lovers’ vigils, so tender and beautiful. There is no moaning night-wind, not even a rustle in the thick ivy. The window gives no sound, except when the gray moth floating in its shadow taps softly on the pane. You can hear the leaf that drops of itself from the treetop, and flits its way from bough to spray to the ground.
Even in that gentle night there move, however, symbols of guilt and danger. While the small birds, with head under wing, nestle in their leafy nooks, the white owl glides with noiseless wing, a murderous phantom, cutting the air. The demure cat creeps on and on softly as a gray shadow till its green eyes glare close on its prey. Nature, with her gentleness and cruelty, her sublimity and meanness, resembles that microcosm, the human heart, in which lodge so many contrarieties, and the shabby contends with the heroic, the diabolic with the angelic.
In this still night Alice’s heart was heavy. Who can account for those sudden, silent, but terrible changes in the spiritual vision which interpose as it were a thin coloured medium between ourselves and the realities that surround us﻿—how all objects, retaining their outlines, lose their rosy glow and golden lights, and on a sudden fade into dismallest gray and green?
“Dulcibella, do you think he’s coming? Oh! Dulcibella, do you think he’ll come tonight?”
“He may, dear.

===Chinese===

梦里，一张苍白惊恐的脸缓缓向他凑近，随即猛地缩回，发出一声喊。那身影退远时，尖叫声可怕地拖着长音。他觉得他认出了一个人——是死是活，他说不上——就藏在那张希腊式的怪脸里，那脸像大理石一样僵着，膛着一双巨大的眼睛，直望进他的眼睛。
耳朵里还嗡着这声音，他醒了。同所有终于被睡眠撞住的过度疲劳的人一样，有那么一阵子，他虽然摆出醒着的架势，知觉和记忆却还没完全回笼，梦也还没散尽。又一声长声尖叫，又一声，又一声。查尔斯觉得他听出是妻子的声音。他吓坏了，彻底醒了，从屋里冲出去——冲到楼梯脚——冲上楼梯。他听见屋里有脚步，门被狠狠搖晃，又是一声长长的、痛极的尖叫。
这片屋顶上空和四野，是顶静谧、顶幸福的夜。皎洁的月，墨蓝的天，漫漫一天星斗，这样的夜本该属于圣洁的思绪、情人的守候，那样温柔，那样美。没有呜咽的夜风，连厚厚的常春藤里也没有一丝窸窣。窗子悄然无声，只有灰蛾在暗影里飘荡，轻叩玻璃的时候才响一下。一片叶子自己从树梢脱落，从枝到杪，飘飘悠悠落到地上——这样你也听得见。
然而就在这样温柔的夜里，也有罪恶与凶险的影子在游动。小鸟们把头插在翅膀下，偎在枝叶深处安睡，白鸮却张开无声的翅膀滑翔，一个杀生的幽灵，划破夜气。端庄的猫像个灰影，悄没声地一寸寸蹭近，直到绿眼在猎物跟前幽幽放光。大自然，连同她的温柔与残忍、她的崇高与卑琐，恰似人心这个小小的天地——里面蛰居着多少互相抵触的东西，卑劣同英勇相争，魔性同神性相搏。
就在这静夜里，爱丽丝心头沉重。心灵的视象会起那种突兀的、无声的、却可怕的变化——仿佛隔上一层薄薄的滤光镜，横在我们同周围的实景之间——万物轮廓犹在，却褪尽了玫瑰色的暖光和金色的亮泽，霎时间黯淡成愁惨的灰与绿——这谁能说得清呢？
「杜西贝拉，你说他会来吗？哦！杜西贝拉，你说他今晚会来吗？」
「兴许会，亲爱的。

===Original===

Why shouldn’t he? Lie down, my child, and don’t be sitting up in your bed so. You’ll never go asleep while you’re listening and watching. Nothing but fidgets, and only the wider awake the longer you watch. *Well* I know it, and many a long hour I laid awake myself expectin’ and listenin’ for poor Crane a-comin’ home with the cart from market, long ago. He had his failin’s﻿—as who has not? poor Crane﻿—but an honest man, and good-natured, and would not hurt a fly, and never a wry word out of his mouth, exceptin’, maybe, one or two, which he never meant them, when he was in liquor, as who is there, Miss Ally, will not be sometimes? But he was a kind, handsome fellow, and sore was my heart when he was taken,” and Dulcibella wiped her eyes. “Seven-and-twenty years agone last Stephen’s Day I buried him in Wyvern Churchyard, and I tried to keep the little business agoin’, but I couldn’t make it pay nohow, and when it pleased God to take my little girl six years after, I gave all up and went to live at the vicarage. But as I was sayin’, miss, many a long hour I sat up a-watchin’ for my poor Crane on his way home. He would sometimes stop a bit on the way, wi’ a friend or two, at the Cat and Fiddle﻿—’twas the only thing I could ever say wasn’t quite as I could a’ liked in my poor Crane. And that’s how I came to serve your good mother, miss, and your poor father, the good vicar o’ Wyvern﻿—there’s not been none like him since, not one﻿—no, indeed.”
“You remember mamma very well?”
“Like yesterday, miss,” said old Dulcibella, who often answered that question. “Like yesterday, the pretty lady. She always looked so pleasant, too﻿—a smiling face, like the light of the sun coming into a room.”
“I wonder, Dulcibella, there was no picture.”
“No picture. No, miss. Well, ye see, Miss Ally, dear, them pictures, I’m told, costs a deal o’ money, and they were only beginnin’ you know, and many a little expense﻿—and Wyvern Vicarage is a small livelihood at best, and ye must be managin’ if ye’d keep it﻿—and good to the poor they was with all that, and gave what many a richer one wouldn’t, and never spared trouble for them; they counted nothin’ trouble for no one. They loved all, and lived to one another, not a wry word ever; what one liked t’other loved, and all in the light o’ God’s blessin’.

===Chinese===

「他凭什么不来？躺下吧，我的孩子，别这么在床上撑着坐。你这么又听又望的，永远睡不着。只有干着急，越守越醒。这个我懂，从前，多少个长长的钟头，我自己也睜着眼躺着，盼着听着可怜的克雷恩赶集的大车回家。他有他的毛病——谁没有呢？可怜的克雷恩——可是个老实人，好性子，一只苍蝇也不肯伤，嘴里从没吐出一句歪话，顶多一两句，那也是喝多了的时候，无心的话——艾莉小姐，谁能说自己没有偶尔喝多的时候呢？可他是个和善的、体面的男人，他被带走的那天，我的心都碎了。」杜西贝拉擦了擦眼睛。「去年圣司提反节，算来二十七年前了，我把他葬在怀弗恩教堂墓地。我想把那点小营生撑下去，可怎么也挣不出钱来；六年后上帝高兴，把我家小姑娘也接了去，我就掠下一切，搬到牧师住宅去了。不过我刚才是说，小姐，多少个长长的钟头，我坐着等可怜的克雷恩回家。他有时路上要停一停，同一两个朋友，在『猫与提琴』——可怜的克雷恩身上，我只说得出口这一样毛病。也就是打那时起，我来伺候您那位好母亲，还有您可怜的父亲，怀弗恩那位好牧师——后来再没有过他那样的人，一个也没有——真没有。」
「您很记得妈妈的样子？」
「跟昨天似的，小姐，」老杜西贝拉说，这个问题她回答过许多回。「跟昨天似的，那位漂亮的太太。她模样也总是那么可亲——一张笑脸，像太阳光照进屋子。」
「我一直纳闷，杜西贝拉，怎么没留下画像。」
「没有画像。没有，小姐。您瞧，亲爱的艾莉小姐，那种画像，听说要花好大一笔钱；您知道，他们那会儿才刚成家，处处要用钱——怀弗恩牧师俸禄本来就薄，要守得住就得精打细算——就这样，他们对穷人还是那么好，多少比他们阔的都不肯给的，他们给；为穷人从不怕麻烦；他们从不把为任何人费心当作麻烦。他们爱所有人，两口子相依为命，从没一句歪话；一个喜欢的，另一个就爱，一切都在上帝的恩光里。

===Original===

I never seen such a couple, never; they doted on one another, and loved all, and they two was like one angel.”
“Lady Wyndale has a picture of poor mamma﻿—very small﻿—what they call a miniature. I think it quite beautiful. It was taken when she was not more than seventeen. Lady Wyndale, you know, was ever so much older than mamma.”
“Ay, so she was, ten year and more, I dare say,” answered Dulcibella.
“She is very fond of it﻿—too fond to give it to me now; but she says, kind aunt, she has left it to me in her will. And oh! Dulcibella, I feel so lonely.”
“Lonely! why should you, darling, wi’ a fine handsome gentleman to your husband, that will be squire o’ Wyvern﻿—think o’ that﻿—squire o’ Wyvern, and that’s a greater man than many a lord in Parliament; and he’s good-natured, never a hard word or a skew look, always the same quiet way wi’ him. Hoot, miss! ye mustn’t be talkin’ that way. Think o’ the little baby that’s a-comin’. Ye won’t know yourself for joy when ye see his face, please God, and I’m a-longin’ to show him to ye.”
“You good old Dulcibella,” said the young lady, and her eyes filled with tears as she smiled. “But poor mamma died when I was born, and oh, Dulcibella, do you think I shall ever see the face of the poor little thing? Oh! wouldn’t it be sad! wouldn’t it be sad!”
“Ye’re not to be talkin’ that nonsense, darling; ’tis sinful, wi’ all that God has given you, a comfortable house over your head, and enough to eat, and good friends, and a fine, handsome husband that’s kind to you, and a blessed little child a-comin’ to make every minute pleasant to all that’s in the house. Why, ’tis a sin to be frettin’ like that, and as for this thing or that thing, or being afeard, why, everyone’s afeard, if they’d let themselves, and not one in a thousand comes by any harm; and ’tis sinful, I tell ye, for ye know well ye’re in the hands o’ the good God that’s took care o’ ye till now, and took ye out o’ the little nursery o’ Wyvern Vicarage, when ye weren’t the length o’ my arm, and not a friend near but poor, foolish, old Dulcibella, that did not know where to turn.

===Chinese===

「这样的两口子我再没见过，再没见过；他们你疼我我疼你，又爱着所有人，两个人合起来就像一位天使。」
「温代尔夫人那儿有一张可怜的妈妈的像——很小——就是他们说的微型画。我觉得美极了。画的时候她还不满十七岁。温代尔夫人您知道，比妈妈大好多呢。」
「是，大呢，我敢说十岁还不止。」杜西贝拉答道。
「她宝贝得不得了——宝贝得现在舍不得给我；可她说了，好姨妈，她在遗嘱里把它留给了我。哦！杜西贝拉，我觉得好孤单。」
「孤单！你孤什么单，亲爱的，你有位又俊俏又体面的绅士做丈夫，往后就是怀弗恩的乡绅——想想那个——怀弗恩的乡绅，比议会里多少勋爵还大的人物呢；他性子又好，从没有一句狠话、一个斜眼，永远那样安安静静。嘈，小姐！可不许这么说话。想想就要来的小宝宝。托上帝的福，等您见着他的小脸，喜还喜不过来呢，我正巴着把他抱给您瞧呢。」
「你这位好心的老杜西贝拉。」年轻小姐说，笑着，眼里却涌上泪水。「可可怜的妈妈生我的时候就死了；哦，杜西贝拉，你说我见得着那可怜的小东西的脸吗？哦！那不是太惨了吗！不是太惨了吗！」
「不许再说这种傻话，亲爱的；这是罪过。上帝给了你这么多——头顶上舒舒服服一所房子，吃穿不愁，好朋友，一位待你好的俊俏丈夫，还有一个蒙福的小宝宝要来，叫一屋子人时时刻刻都欢喜。唉，这样自苦是罪过；至于这个那个，至于害怕——谁不害怕呢，要是放开了怕；可一千个人里也未必有一个真遭殃的；我说，这是罪过，因为你清楚得很，你是在慈悲上帝的手心里，他一直看顾你到如今；当年把你从怀弗恩牧师宅的小育儿室里抱出来的，是我——那会儿你还没有我一条胳膊长，跟前一个亲人也没有，只有可怜的、傻乎乎的老杜西贝拉，转来转去不知怎么办。

===Original===

And your aunt, that only went out as poor as your darling mamma, brought home well again from t’other end o’ the world, and well to do, your own loving kith and kin, and good friends raised up on every side, and the old squire, Harry o’ Wyvern, although he be a bit angered for a while he’s another good friend, that will be sure to make it up, whatever it is came between him and Master Charles. Hot blood’s not the worst blood; better a blow in haste and a shake hands after than a smile at the lips and no goodwill wi’ it. I tell you, they’re not the worst, they hotheaded, hard-fisted, outspoken folk; and I’ll never forget that day to him, when he brought you home that had no home, and me that was thinkin’ o’ nout but the workhouse. So do or say what he will, God bless him for that day, say I, for ’twas an angel’s part he did,” said old Dulcibella.
“So I feel, God knows; so I feel,” said Alice, “and I hope it may all be made up; I’m sure it will; and, oh! Dulcibella, I have been the cause of so much sorrow and bitterness!”
She stopped suddenly, her eyes full of tears; but she restrained them.
“That’s the way ye’ll always be talking. I’d like to know where they’d be without you. Every man that marries will have care, more or less; ’tis the will o’ God; and if he hadn’t he’d never think o’ Him; and ’tis a short life at the longest, and a sore pilgrimage at the best. So what He pleases to lay on us we must even bear wi’ a patient heart, if we can’t wi’ a cheerful; for wi’ his blessin’ ’twill all end well.”
“Amen,” said Alice, with a cheerier smile but a load still at her heart; “I hope so, my good old Dulcibella. What should I do without you? *Wait!* hush! Is that a noise outside? No; I thought I heard a horse’s tread, but there’s nothing. It’s too late now; there’s no chance of him tonight. Do you think, Dulcibella, there is any chance?”
“Well, no, my dear; it’s gettin’ on too late﻿—a deal too late; no, no, we must even put that clean out of our heads. Ye’ll not get a wink o’ sleep if you be listening for him. Well I know them fidgets, and many a time I lay on my hot ear﻿—now this side, now that, listening, till I could count the veins o’ my head beating like a watch, and myself only wider and wider awake every hour, and more fool I; and well and hearty home wi’ him, time enough, and not a minute sooner for all my watching.

===Chinese===

「还有你姨妈，出门的时候同你亲爱的妈妈一样穷，从世界那一头回来，人好好的，家境也殷实了；你自己贴心的亲人，四面八方都有好朋友；还有老乡绅，怀弗恩的老哈利，虽然一时气头上，他也是一位好朋友，不管他和查尔斯先生之间隔着什么事，早晚讲得开。热血性子不是坏血性；宁可一时动粗、过后悔过握手，强过嘴上带笑、心里没有半点好意。我告诉你，那些性子急、拳头硬、有话直说的人，不是坏人；他带你们回家的那一天，我到死也忘不了——一个没有家的你，和一个只想着进济贫院的我。所以随他做什么说什么，那一天的福我给他祝，我说，因为他那天做的，是一位天使的事。」老杜西贝拉说。
「我也是这么想的，天知道；这么想的。」爱丽丝说，「我盼着一切都能讲开；一定会的；还有，哦！杜西贝拉，我惹出了多少伤心、多少苦楚啊！」
她突然打住，眼里满是泪；可她忍住了。
「你呀，说话老是这样。我倒要问问，没有你，他们会在哪儿。凡娶亲的男人，操心总是或多或少的；那是上帝的旨意；人要是无牵无挂，就永远想不起上帝来；再长寿，人生也是短短一遭；再顺遂，也是一场苦旅。所以他要给我们的担子，我们纵不能欢欢喜喜地受，也要耐心地受；有他的祝福，到头来一切都会好。」
「阿门。」爱丽丝说，笑得明朗了些，心头却仍压着石头；「但愿如此，我的好杜西贝拉。没有你我可怎么办？等等！嘘！外头是不是有动静？没有；我当是听见马蹄声了呢，可什么也没有。现在太晚了；他今夜是没指望到了。你说，杜西贝拉，还有指望吗？」
「唉，没了，亲爱的；天太晚了——晚透了；不，不，咱们还是把这事从脑子里干干净净掠出去。你要一直竖着耳朵等他，就一眨眼的觉也睡不成。那种干着急我是领教过的；多少回，我把滚烫的耳朵贴在枕上——一会儿这边，一会儿那边——听着听着，能数出自己脑袋里的血管突突跳，像块表；一个钟头一个钟头，人越来越清醒，越发傻；可他呢，到了时候，好端端、结结实实地到家，我守了半宿，也只叫他早回来一分钟。」

===Original===

And mind ye, what I often told ye when ye were a wee thing, and ye’ll find it true to the end o’ your days﻿—a watch-pot never boils.”
Alice laughed gently.
“I believe you are right, Dulcibella. No, he won’t come tonight. It was only a chance, and I might have known. But, perhaps, tomorrow? Don’t you think tomorrow?”
“Very like, like enough, tomorrow﻿—daylight, mayhap to breakfast﻿—why not?” she answered.
“Well, I do think he may; he said, perhaps tonight, and I know, I’m sure he’ll think how his poor wife is watching and longing to see him; and, as you advise, I’ll put that quite out of my head; he has so many things to look after, and he only said *perhaps*; and you think in the morning. Well, I won’t let myself think so, it would be too delightful; I won’t think it. But it can’t be many days, I’m sure﻿—and﻿—I won’t keep you up any longer, dear old Dulcibella. I’ve been very selfish. So, good night.”
And they kissed, as from little Allie’s infancy they had always done, before settling for the night.
“Good night, and God love it; it mustn’t be frettin’, and God bless you, my darling Miss Allie; and you must get to sleep, or you’ll be looking so pale and poor in the morning, he won’t know you when he comes.”
So, with another hug and a kiss they parted, and old Dulcibella leaving her young mistress’s candle burning on the table, as was her wont, being nervous when she was alone, and screened from her eyes by the curtain, with a final good night and another blessing she closed the door.
Is there ever an unreserved and complete confidence after marriage? Even to kind old Dulcibella she could not tell all. As she smiled a little farewell on the faithful old soul her heart was ready to burst. She was longing for a good cry all to herself, and now, poor little thing, she had it.
She cried herself, as children do, to sleep.
An hour later the old grange was silent as the neighbouring churchyard of Carwell. But there was not a household in the parish, or in the county, I suppose, many of whose tenants, at that late hour, were so oddly placed.
In his chair in the oak-panelled room, downstairs, sat Charles Fairfield, in that slumber of a tormented and exhausted brain, which in its first profound submersion, resembles the torpor of apoplexy.

===Chinese===

「还有你记着，你还是个小不点儿的时候我就常说，这话你到老都见它的真——盯着看的锅，水偏不开。」
爱丽丝轻轻地笑了。
「我想你说得对，杜西贝拉。不，他今夜不会来了。本来就只是个万一，我也该如何道。可是，也许明天？你说会不会是明天？」
「多半是，多半，明天——天亮，兴许赶得上早饭——怎么不会？」她答道。
「嗯，我也觉得也许会；他说的是『也许今晚』，我知道，他准会想到他可怜的妻子正守着、盼着见他；听你的劝，我就把这事掠开；他要操心的事那么多，他也只说了『也许』；你说的是明儿早上。好，我不许自己这么想，太美了，美得我不敢想；不想了。可要不了几天的，我敢说——还有——不留你了，亲爱的老杜西贝拉。我太自私了。那么，晚安。」
两人吻别——打小艾莉襁褓时起，安置就寝前她们一向如此。
「晚安，上帝疼你；不许再烦心了，上帝保佑你，我亲爱的艾莉小姐；你可得睡着了，不然明儿早上脸白得黄瘦，他回来都认不得你啦。」
又是掳了一下、亲了一下，两人分手。老杜西贝拉照老习惯，让年轻小姐的蜡烛在桌上点着——她一个人睡觉胆小——用帘子替她挡住光，最后道一声晚安、再祝一回福，关上了门。
婚后，可还有毫无保留、和盘托出的知心话吗？连对好心的老杜西贝拉，她也不能全说。她朝这忠厚的老好人微微一笑作别时，她的心几乎要胀裂。她正盼着痛痛快快独自哭一场——现在，可怜的小东西，她哭上了。
她哭着自己睡着了，像孩子们那样。
一个钟头后，老田庄静得像毗邻的卡威尔教堂墓地。可我想，本教区也好，本郡也好，再没有哪户人家，在那个深夜，一家子人落着这样古怪的位置。
楼下橡木护壁的屋里，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德坐在椅子上，沉入那种受尽折磨、耗竭殆尽的大脑的昏睡——起初的沉酣，简直像中风般的麻木。

===Original===

In his forsaken room lay on the pillow the pale face of his young wife, her eyelashes not yet dry, fallen asleep in the sad illusion of his absence﻿—better, perhaps, than his presence would have been, if she had known but all.
In her crib downstairs, at last asleep, lay the frightened Lilly Dogger, her head still under the coverlet, under which she had popped it in panic, as she thought on the possible return of the tall unknown, and the lobe of her ear still flaming from the discipline of her vice-like pinch.
Under his slanting roof, in the recess of the staircase, with only his coat off, stretched on the broad of his back, with one great horny hand half shut under his bullet head, and the other by his side, snored honest Tom, nothing the less soundly for his big mug of beer and his excursion to Cressley Common.
For a moment now we visit the bedside of good old Dulcibella. An easy conscience, a good digestion, and an easy place in this troublesome world, are favourable to sound slumbers, and very tranquilly she slept, with a large handkerchief pinned closely about her innocent bald head, and a nightcap of many borders outside it. Her thick, well-thumbed Bible, in which she read some half-dozen verses every night, lay, with her spectacles upon its cover, on the table by the brass candlestick.
Mildred Tarnley, a thin figure with many corners, lay her length in her clothes, her old brown stuff gown her cap and broad faded ribbons binding her busy head, and her darned black worsted stockings still on her weary feet, ready at call to jump up, pop her feet again into her misshapen shoes, and resume her duties.
In her own solitary chamber, at the deserted side of the house, the tall stranger, arrayed in a white woollen nightdress, lay her length, not stirring.
After Mildred Tarnley had got herself stiffly under her quilt, she was visited with certain qualms about this person, recollections of her abhorred activity and energy in old times, and fears that the “grim white woman” was not resting in her bed. This apprehension grew so intense that, tired as she was, she could not sleep. The suspicion that, barefooted, listening, that dreadful woman was possibly groping her way through the house made her heart beat faster and faster.

===Chinese===

他那被冷落的房间里，枕上躺着年轻妻子苍白的脸，睫毛还未干，在他不在的悲哀错觉中睡去——也许，倒好过他在身边：倘若她知道了一切。
楼下她的小床上，吓坏了的莉莉·多杰总算睡着了，头还缩在被子底下——想起那个高个子生人兴许会回来，她慌里慌张把头钻了进去——耳垂还火辣辣的，那是被人钳子似的一拧的管教。
斜屋顶下，楼梯的凹龛里，老实汤姆只脱了外衣，四仰八叉躺着，一只粗糙的大手半攞着垫在圆脑袋底下，另一只搁在身侧，鼾声大作——一大杯啤酒外加一趟克雷斯利公地之行，一点没碍着他睡得香。
现在让我们到好心的老杜西贝拉床边看一眼。无愧的良心、良好的消化，加上在这是非人世里一个安稳的位子，最养酣睡；她睡得极安详，一块大手帕严严实实裹着她那光溜溜的天真脑袋，外面又罩一顶镶了好几道边的睡帽。她那本厚厚的、翻毛了边的《圣经》——每夜她要读上五六节——连同眼镜，合在封皮上，搁在黄铜烛台旁的桌上。
米尔德里德·塔恩利，一个浑身棱角的瘦削身形，和衣直挺挺躺着，棕色的旧粗布裙还罩在身上，帽子连同褪色的宽缎带还束着那颗操劳的脑袋，织补过的黑色绒线袜还套在疲乏的脚上，只等一声呼唤，就能跳起来，把脚重新塞进走了形的鞋里，接着当差。
在她自己那间孤零零的屋子里——宅子背人的一侧——那高个子生人穿着白色羊毛睡袍，直挺挺躺着，一动不动。
米尔德里德·塔恩利硬邦邦钻进被窝后，心里对这个人的嘀咕却一阵阵涌上来——想起她当年那股叫人憎恶的精气神，又怕这「白衣煞神」并没有安分躺在床上。这疑心越涨越凶，累成那样，她竟睡不着。一想到那个可怕的女人也许正赤着脚、竖着耳，一路摸黑在这宅子里穿行，她的心就越跳越快。

===Original===

At last she could bear it no longer, and up she got, lighted her candle with a match, and in her stockings glided softly through the passage, and by the room where Charles Fairfield was at that time at his letters.
He recognised the step to which his ear was accustomed, and did not trouble himself to inquire what she was about.
So, softly, softly, softly﻿—Mildred Tarnley found herself at the door of the unwelcome guest and listened. You would not have supposed old Mildred capable of a nervous tremble, but she was profoundly afraid of this awful woman, before whose superior malignity and unearthly energy her own temper and activity quailed. She listened, but could hear no evidence of her presence. Was the woman there at all? Lightly, lightly, with her nail, she tapped at the door. No answer. Then very softly she tried the door. It was secured.
But was the old soldier in the room still, or wandering about the house with who could fathom what evil purpose in her head?
The figure in white woollen was there still; she had been lying on her side, with her pale features turned toward the door as Mildred approached. Her blind eyes were moving in their sockets﻿—there was a listening smile on her lips﻿—and she had turned her neck awry to get her ear in the direction of the door. She was just as wide awake as Mildred herself.
Mildred watched for a time at the door, irresolute. Excuse enough, she bethought her, in the feeble state in which she had left her, had she for making her a visit. Why should she not open the door boldly and enter? But Mildred, in something worse than solitude, was growing more and more nervous. What if that tall, insane miscreant were waiting at the door, in a fit of revenge for her suspected perfidy, ready to clutch her by the throat as she opened it, and to strangle her on the bed? And when there came from the interior of the room a weary bleating “heigh-ho!” she absolutely bounced backward, and for a moment froze with terror.
She took a precaution as she softly withdrew. The passage, which is terminated by the “old soldier’s” room, passes a dressing-room on the left, and then opens, on the other side, upon a lobby. This door is furnished with a key, and having secured it, Mrs.

===Chinese===

终于她受不了了，爬起身，划火柴点亮蜡烛，穿着长袜，悄悄滑过走廊，经过那间屋——查尔斯·费尔菲尔德那会儿正在里面写他的信。
他听出这双耳朵听惯了的脚步，也就懒得去问她在做什么。
轻轻地，轻轻地，轻轻地——米尔德里德·塔恩利到了那位不受欢迎的客人门前，侧耳听。你绝不会想到老米尔德里德也会神经质地发抖，可她对这可怕的女人怕得入骨——在那女人压倒一身的邪性同非人的精力面前，她自己的脾气和干练全折了。她听着，却听不出人在不在的凭证。那女人到底在不在里头？她用指甲，轻轻地、轻轻地敲了敲门。没有应声。她又轻轻去转门。门锁上了。
可那「老兵」是在屋里，还是正在宅子里游荡，脑袋里揣着谁也猜不透的什么歹毒主意？
穿白羊毛睡袍的身影还在屋里；米尔德里德走近时，她正侧身躺着，苍白的脸孔朝着门的方向。她那双瞎眼在眼窝里转动——唇上挂着一抹聆听的笑——脖子拧着，好把耳朵对准门口。她同米尔德里德一样，清醒得很。
米尔德里德在门口守了一阵，进退不定。她一想，自己先前留下她时她那样虚弱，正有现成的借口去看她一眼。她何不大大方方开门进去？可米尔德里德在那比独处更糟的境地里，越来越心慌。万一那个高个子的疯婆子就候在门后，正为着疑心她变卦而寻仇，只等她一开门就掐住她的喉咙，把她掐死在床上呢？这时屋里传来一声倦怠的、羊叫似的「嘿——嗬！」，她整个人猛地向后一弹，霎时僵在恐惧里。
她轻轻退开时，使了个防备。这条走廊的尽头是「老兵」的屋子，左边经过一间梳妆室，另一头通向一处门厅。那扇门配有钥匙；她锁上之后，塔恩利

===Original===

Tarnley, with that key in her pocket, felt that she had pretty well imprisoned that evil spirit, and returned to her own bed more serenely, and was soon lost in slumber.

===Chinese===

太太把那把钥匙揣进兜里，自觉已把那恶灵关了个严实，心境平顺了些，回到自己床上，很快便沉入梦乡。

## L Bertha Velderkaust / 第五十章 贝莎·费尔德考斯特
===Original===

Harry Fairfield, when, crossing Cressley Common, he reached the road that diverges eastward, took that turn, and rode towards Hatherton.
Surely enough he looked when he slackened his pace to a walk at the foot of the long low hill that interposes between the common and that town.
He had a short pipe in his pocket, with a big bowl, and a metal cover to it, into which he stuffed some pinches of tobacco﻿—a shilling went a good way in that sort of smoking, and Harry was economical﻿—and soon his pipe was in full play.
This narcotic helped his cogitative powers, and he had a good deal to think about. He was going to see his old friend Bertha Velderkaust, in her new situation, and he was considering how best to approach her.
From such ruminations﻿—too vague and irregular to be reduced to logical sequence and arrangement﻿—there arise, nevertheless, conclusions by no means unimportant, and quite distinct enough. By this time he had smoked his pipe out, and looked down from the summit of this rising ground upon the pretty town spreading among the trees, with its old tower and steeple, its courthouse, its parsonage, and that high-walled stronghold on the right, in which the object of his visit was at present secluded.
When, having complied with all formalities, he obtained an entrance, and obtained permission to visit that person, it was her pleasure to keep him waiting for some time for his audience. Harry grew cross and impatient, the more so as he heard that she had a friend with her, drinking tea, and reading the newspaper to her.
As Harry Fairfield was one of those persons who are averse to sacrificing themselves without a good consideration, the reader will conclude that his object was not altogether to serve the “old soldier.” If it had been only that, I think he would have left the town of Hatherton re infecta. As it was, he waited, and at last was admitted.
This lady, Bertha Velderkaust, chose to be known among her neighbours in misfortune as Madame Bertha Fairfield of Wyvern, which style and title she preferred to that by which she had been committed to the safe keeping of the gaoler.
When Harry Fairfield stepped into her small apartment he found her dressed and bedizened in a way that a little surprised him.
She had on a sky-blue satin dress, caught up at one side with a bunch of artificial flowers.

===Chinese===

哈利·费尔菲尔德（Harry Fairfield）穿过克雷斯利公地（Cressley Common），到了那条向东岔出的路上，便转上它，朝哈瑟顿（Hatherton）驰去。
行到公地与那座市镇之间横亘的长长矮山脚下，他放慢成信步时，那副模样确实够人瞧的。
他口袋里装着一支短烟斗，斗大，还带个金属盖；他往里捻了几撮烟丝——这么个抽法，一先令烟能抽老久，而哈利是节俭人——不多时，烟斗便抽得旺了。
这点麻醉剂帮了他的思虑，他要想的事也真不少。他要去探望老相识贝莎·费尔德考斯特（Bertha Velderkaust），在她这处新居里，正盘算着怎么开场最妥。
这类盘算——太散漫无章，归不成逻辑的先后条贯——却也能生出些分量绝不轻、条条清楚的结论来。这会儿烟斗抽空了，他从这高地的顶上俯瞰那座秀美的市镇，在树影间铺展开去：老公塔和尖顶，法院，牧师宅，还有右边那座高墙环卫的堡垒，他此行的目标眼下就深居其中。
等他办完一应手续，得了入门许可、得了探视许可，她却又摆起谱来，让他干等了好一阵才召见。哈利越来越没好气，尤其听说她正有朋友陪着，喝茶，听人给她念报纸。
哈利·费尔菲尔德属于那种没有可观的好处绝不肯白牺牲自己的人，读者由此可以断定，他的目的绝不单是为「老兵」效劳。倘若单单为那个，我看他早悻悻地离开哈瑟顿了。可他等了下去，终于获准进去。
这位太太，贝莎·费尔德考斯特，在她那些难友之间，喜欢人称她怀弗恩（Wyvern）的贝莎·费尔菲尔德夫人；这个名号，她中意胜过狱卒登记收押时用的那一个。
哈利·费尔菲尔德跨进她那间小屋，发现她打扮装点得让他微微吃了一惊。
她穿一条天蓝缎子长裙，一边用一束绢花别起。

===Original===

She had a lace scarf and a lace coiffure lying flat across her head, with a miniature coronet of Roman pearl in the centre, and lappets depending at each side. She had a double necklace of enormous Roman pearls about her throat, and a pair of pink velvet slippers, embroidered with beads and bugles, and this tawdry figure sat on the side of her truckle-bed to receive him, with the air of a princess in a pantomime. She accumulated her finery in this way, I think, for the purpose of impressing the people about the prison with a due sense of her position and importance. It may not have been quite without its effect.
“Hullo! madame, I came to tell you some news,” said he, as soon as the door was closed. “But, by the makins! you ’most took my breath away at first sight o’ ye.”
“Pity to have so nice a man breathless﻿—deplorable pity!”﻿—or *biddy*, as she pronounced it. “Suppose you go away. I did not ask you to come and get your breath again in the air of my place.”
“What place may that be﻿—not Hoxton Old Town, hey?”
“Not at all﻿—Wyvern, dear child?” she said, with a quiet sneer.
“Oh, thank ye﻿—yes﻿—well I will, I think, take a mouthful there as you are so good.”
As he concluded this speech Master Harry put out his tongue at the blind lady with a grimace that was outrageous.
“I’ll hide my name no longer,” she said, “I’m Mrs. Fairfield of Wyvern.”
“That’s as it may be,” he answered, serenely.
“I say, I’m Mrs. Fairfield of Wyvern,” repeated she.
“Boo!” answered Harry.
“Beast! By that noise what do you mean?”
“I’ll tell ye, by-and-by. Come, you mustn’t be cross, it wastes time.”
“More time than we know what to do with in this house,” she sneered.
“Well, that’s true for some, I’ll not deny; but there’s some as is pretty well worked I hear﻿—eh?﻿—and so long as we baint, we may endure the leisure, for as bad as that is, business here, I’m told, is a deal worse,” and Harry laughed.
“Pleasant was my Harry always,” again sneered the lady.
“And ye heard of poor Charlie, of course?” he asked.
“Yes, of course. Everyone is not like you. I did hear. I don’t thank you,” she answered, tartly, and turned her pale, malignant face toward him.
“But, dear girl, I could not. There was difficulties, eyes a-watchin’ on all hands, and ears cocked, and I knew you could not be long without knowing. So you heard; but mayhap you haven’t heard this﻿—there’s a child born o’ that marriage.”

===Chinese===

她披一条花边肩巾，头上平平贴着一顶花边头饰，正中一顶罗马珍珠小冠，两侧垂着飘带；颈间两串大颗的罗马珍珠项链，脚上一双粉红天鹅绒拖鞋，绣着珠子和亮管。这副俗艳的排场，她就坐在帆布矮床沿上接见他，派头活像哑剧里的公主。她这样攒排场，我看是要叫监狱里上下人等，对她老人家的身份体面起一份应有的敬意。这未必全无效果。
「喂！夫人，我来给你报个信儿，」门一关上他就说。「不过，乖乖！乍一看见你，差点把我一口气憋回去。」
「这么俊的汉子断了气，可惜——可惜得很哟！」——她把「可惜」念成「可费」。「要不你走吧。我没请你来，到我这块儿的地界里喘第二口气。」
「这地界能是哪儿——总不是霍克斯顿（Hoxton）老城吧，嗯？」
「当然不是——是怀弗恩呢，亲爱的孩子？」她安安静静地讥诮道。
「哦，多谢——好啊——既然夫人这么客气，我看我就上那儿吸一口吧。」
说完这句，哈利少爷朝那位瞎眼的夫人吐出舌头，配上一副放肆到家的鬼脸。
「我的名姓再不遮着掩着了，」她说，「我就是怀弗恩的费尔菲尔德（Fairfield）太太。」
「那要看怎么说了，」他若无其事地答。
「我说了，我是怀弗恩的费尔菲尔德太太。」她重复道。
「啐！」哈利答。
「畜生！你这一声是什么意思？」
「回头告诉你。来，可不许恼，恼了费工夫。」
「这宅子里的工夫，多得咱们不知怎么打发呢。」她讥诮道。
「呃，这话对某些人是不假，我不驳；可我听说，也有几位给使唤得不轻——嗯？——咱们既然没轮上，就凑合受用这份清闲吧；清闲虽糟，这里头的差事，听说还糟得多呢。」哈利笑了。
「我的哈利呀，一向嘴甜。」夫人又讥诮道。
「可怜的查理（Charlie）的事，你当然听说了？」他问。
「是，当然。不是人人都像你。我是听说了。我不谢你。」她尖刻地答，把那张苍白歹毒的脸转向他。
「可是，好姑娘，我实在来不了。难处多啊，四面八方都盯着眼睛，支着耳朵，我谅你也瞒不了几天。所以你听说了；可这个你兴许还没听说——那桩婚姻生了个孩子。」

===Original===

“Marriage!” and with an oath the big Dutchwoman burst into a discordant laugh.
For a moment Harry was alarmed, but the laugh was not hysterical﻿—purely emotional, and an escape for pent-up scorn and fury.
“Well, anyhow there’s a child﻿—a boy﻿—and a fine hale little chap, wi’ a big bald head and a bawlin’ mouth as ever a mother hugged﻿—the darlin’.”
“Well, let the brat lie on the dung heap, you’ll not lift him,” said the lady.
“I’ll not meddle or make. I’m not over-hot about Wyvern. I’d rather have a pocket full o’ money than a house full o’ debts any day; and anyhow there he is, and four bones that’s to walk off with my share o’t.”
“I should have got mourning,” said Bertha Velderkaust, speaking from some hidden train of thought.
“Bah! No one to see you here,” said Harry.
“If I had money or credit, I’d have got it,” she said.
“That’s very affectionate of you,” said Harry; “but why do you dress like that﻿—why do you dress like the lady wi’ the glass slipper, Cinderella, at the king’s ball, in the story book?”
“I should dress, you think, like Cinderella over the coal-scuttle?”
“Well, I wouldn’t set the folk a-laughing when I was in no laughing humour myself﻿—not that it makes much odds, and I do suppose it don’t matter﻿—not it.”
“It does matter something, perhaps, and perhaps nothing; but I know who I am, and I won’t let myself down,” said she. “I don’t want to lose myself among these people; I’ll keep myself distinct. I’m too high to put my foot in the mud.”
“Too high to put your foot in the mud﻿—too high to put your foot on the pavement,” said Harry, mischievously, with his eyes on this impulsive lady, and hitching his chair off a little to secure a fair start. “You’ll be too high, I’m thinkin’, to get your foot to ground at all, one o’ these days, if you don’t look sharp. It’s too high a flight, I’m told, to touch terra firma wi’ the top o’ your toe﻿—the gallows, I mean﻿—and that’s what you’re coming to quick, I’m afeard.”
As Harry concluded, he stood up, intending to get out, if possible, without the indignity of coming to handgrips with a woman.
The Herculean lady, in sky-blue satin and Roman pearls, leaned forward with sharpened features, but neither extended her arm nor attempted to rise. Then she sighed deeply, and leaned with her shoulders to the wall.
“Off in a coach for this bout,” thought Harry.

===Chinese===

「婚姻！」大个子荷兰女人咒骂一声，爆出一阵刺耳的笑。
哈利吓了一跳，好在那笑不是癔症——纯是动情，是憋了一肚子的轻蔑和狂怒寻了个出口。
「呃，不管怎么说，孩子是有一个——个男娃——结实漂亮的小家伙，光光的大脑袋，哇哇喊的大嘴，天底下做娘的见了都想搂——小宝贝。」
「呃，叫那小崽子躺在粪堆上吧，你不会去抱他的。」夫人说。
「我不搅和，也不插手。怀弗恩我也没那么眼热。哪一天都宁可兜里揣满钱，不愿宅里背满债；反正他在那儿了，凭那四条小腿，就要把我名下的一份走掉了。」
「我本该置一身孝。」贝莎·费尔德考斯特说，不知从哪条暗藏的思路上讲起。
「呸！这儿又没人看你。」哈利说。
「我要是有钱、有赊头，早置下了。」她说。
「你这份深情可真难得，」哈利说；「可你干吗穿成这样——干吗穿得像故事书里那位穿着玻璃鞋的灰姑娘，在国王的舞会上？」
「照你想，我该穿得像守着煤斗的灰姑娘？」
「呃，我自己笑不出的时候，就不爱招人发笑——虽说这也无大碍，我想不打紧——不打紧。」
「许要紧，也许不要紧；可我知道自己是谁，我不肯自跌身价，」她说。「我不愿在这路人里埋没自己；我要自家清清楚楚、另是一等人。我身份太高，不能把脚踩进泥里。」
「高得脚不能踩泥——高得脚不能踩人行道，」哈利促狭地说，眼睛盯着这位说风就是雨的夫人，一面把椅子往边上挪了挪，好占个脱身的先手。「照这么高下去，我看再过些日子，你那只脚就要落不了地了。太高了，我听说，脚尖都够不着坚实的大地——我是说绞架——只怕你正朝那儿快步赶去呢。」
哈利说完便站起来，打算尽可能不落到同女人扭作一团的难堪，全身而退。
那位大力士般的夫人，裹着天蓝缎子和罗马珍珠，五官一凛，朝前探过身来，却既不伸胳膊，也没起身。随后她深深叹了口气，把肩膀靠回墙上。
「这一局坐马车走。」哈利心里想。

===Original===

“Thank you, kind lad, always the same,” she sneered, quietly. “You wish it, no doubt, but, no, you don’t think it. I know better.”
“Why the devil should I wish you hanged, Bertha? Don’t be a fool; you’re not in my way, and never can be. There’s that boy, and, for reasons of my own, I’m glad he is﻿—I’m *glad* he’s where he is﻿—and Wyvern will be for him and not for me﻿—never!”
“Harry, dear, you know quite well,” she drawled, softly, with a titter, “you’ll poison that boy if you can.”
“You lie!” said Harry, turning scarlet, and then as suddenly pale. “You *lie*!﻿—and so that’s answered.”
Here followed a silence. The woman was not angry, but she tittered again and nodded her head.
“Wyvern’s out o’ my head. I never cared about it. I had my own reasons. I never did,” he swore, furiously, striking his hand on the table. “And I won’t see that boy ruined﻿—my flesh and blood﻿—my own nephew. No, no, Bertha, that would never do; the boy must have his own. I’ll see you made comfortable, but that lay won’t do﻿—you’ll find it won’t pay nohow.”
“Speak out, man﻿—what do you mean?” said Bertha.
“Come, come, come, Bertha, you’re no fool,” wheedled he, “there isn’t a sounder head from this to London; and though you be a bit hotheaded, you’re not as bad as you’d have us believe﻿—’taint the worst, always, that has an o’er-hasty hand. Why, bless ye, girl, I’d be sorry ye were hurt, and I’ll help to get ye out o’ this, without scathe or scorn, if you’ll let me.”
“Well, come; what’s in your mind, Harry Vairfield?” she asked.
“I tell ye what it is, it can do you no good, nohow, bein’ hard on that boy, and I know, and you know, you never were married to poor Charlie.”
“You lie!” cried the lady, bitterly. So they were quits on the point of honour.
“Now, Bertha, lass, come now﻿—reason, reason; don’t you be in a hurry, and just listen to reason, and I’ll make it better to you than fifty marriages.”
“Don’t you think I have no advice﻿—I’ve engaged Mr. Wynell, the best attorney in Hatherton; I know what I’m about.”

===Chinese===

「多谢了，好心的小子，倒是一贯如此，」她轻声讥诮道。「你是巴不得，不假；可你并不当真这么想。我心里有数。」
「我干吗要巴望你上绞架，贝莎？别犯傻；你碍不着我，永远碍不着。有那孩子在，出于我自己的缘故，我倒乐意——他在他那个位子上，我*高兴*——怀弗恩将来是他的，不是我的——永远不是！」
「哈利，亲爱的，你自己肚里明白，」她软软地拖长了调子，嗤嗤笑道，「有机会你就要毒死那孩子。」
「你放屁！」哈利说，脸涨得通红，转眼又刷地发白。「你*放屁*！——这句话算是对付过去了。」
接着是一阵沉默。那女人并不恼，只是又嗤嗤笑了两声，点了点头。
「怀弗恩在我心里早没影了。我从来没把它放在心上。我有我的道理。从来没有。」他狠狠起誓，一巴掌拍在桌上。「我也断不能眼看那孩子毁了——我的亲骨血——我的亲侄儿。不，不，贝莎，那可行不通；孩子必须有他自己的。我保你过得舒坦，可那条路子行不通——你会发觉它怎么算都不划算。」
「有话明说，汉子——你什么意思？」贝莎说。
「来，来，来，贝莎，你不是傻子，」他哄劝道，「从这儿到伦敦，再找不出一颗比你更扎实的脑袋；你虽说火爆些，可不像你装给咱们看的那样糟——手快的人未必最坏。嗳，天保佑你，姑娘，我不忍见你吃亏；你肯依我，我就设法把你从这儿捞出去，不伤皮肉，不落笑话。」
「好，说吧；你肚子里打的什么算盘，哈利·维尔菲尔德（Harry Vairfield）？」她问。
「我明说了吧，揪住那孩子不放，对你半点好处没有，半点；而且我知道、你也知道，你同可怜的查理从来没结过婚。」
「你放屁！」夫人凄厉地喊。在名誉这个关目上，两人算是扯平了。
「哎，贝莎姑娘，来吧——讲理，讲理；你别急，只管听理，我保管给你安排得比五十桩婚姻还强。」
「别以为我没人指路——我请了怀内尔（Wynell）先生，哈瑟顿最好的状师；我自有主张。」

===Original===

“The better you know it, the better I’m pleased; but the lawyerfolk likes always a bit of a row﻿—they seldom cries kiss and be friends until their hands be well greased, and their clients has a bellyful o’ law; therefore it’s better that friends should put their heads together and agree before it comes to that sort o’ milling, and I tell ye, ye shall be cared for; *I*’ll see to it, if you don’t be kickin’ up no rows about nothing.”
She laughed a quiet, scornful laugh.
“Oh ho! Master Harry, poor little fellow! he’s frightened, is he?”
“You’re damnably mistaken,” said he. “Frightened, indeed! I’ll see who’s frightened: I know there was no marriage﻿—I *know* it, and it won’t do tryin’ it on me, you’ll just get yourself into the wrong box; where’s the use of runnin’ your head into a cotton bag?”
“Cotton bag your own head. Who’s to do it?”
“They’ll be clumsy fingers that can’t tie that knot, lass. Come, you’re a clever girl, you’re not to be talking﻿—not like a fool. I know everything about it. If you try that on, it will turn out bad. ’Taint easy to green Harry Fairfield; I don’t think he was ever yet fooled by a lass but where he chose to be fooled, and it’s pretty well allowed there’s no use trying to bully him.”
“I ought to like you, if all that be so,” said she, “for you are very like my own self.”
“I’m not tryin’ to bully you, girl, nor to sell ye, neither; ye were always a bit rash, and too ready wi’ your hand; but them’s not the worst folk goin’. We Fairfields has a touch o’ it, and we shouldn’t be o’er hard on quick-tempered folk like that. There was no lass that ever I met, gentle or simple, that could match ye for good looks and pleasant talk, and ye dress so beautiful, and if ye had but your eyes this minute, you’d have who ye liked at your feet.”
And Harry Fairfield repeated this view of her charms with an oath.
“If ifs and ans were pots and pans,” repeated the lady with a sigh of gratification, and with that foreign accent and peculiar drawl which made the homely proverb sound particularly odd; “I forget the end﻿—there would be no use in tinkers, I think.”
“Well said, Bertha! but there’s none like ye, not one, this minute, so handsome,” exclaims he.
“Not that chit down at Carwell Grange, I dare say﻿—eh?”
“Alice! Not fit to stand behind your chair. If ye could but see her, and just look in the glass, ye’d answer that question yourself,” he replied.

===Chinese===

「你越有主张我越高兴；可状师这路人，向来喜欢闹上一场——手不抹足了油、主顾不吃法律吃到饱，他们轻易不肯喊亲嘴言和；所以趁还没到那种乱子，朋友之间顶好先碰碰头、讲讲妥；我告诉你，保你衣食有人照管；*我*亲自过问，只要你别为些没要紧的事闹腾。」
她轻轻笑了两声，满是不屑。
「哟嗬！哈利少爷，可怜的小家伙！他害了怕，是不是？」
「你错得离谱，」他说。「害怕，亏你说得出！我倒要看看谁怕谁：我知道没有那桩婚——我*知道*；在我身上使这一套不灵，你只会把自己装进错匣子里；好好的脑袋往棉花口袋里钻，图什么？」
「你自己的脑袋才往棉花口袋里钻。谁来装我？」
「打不成那个结的，那可真是笨手指了，姑娘。来，你是聪明人，别再说傻话了。这里头的事我桩桩清楚。你要硬来这一套，下场好不了。想哄哈利·费尔菲尔德可不容易；他这辈子还没被哪个丫头耍过，除非他自己乐意挨耍；而且大伙儿公认，想吓唬他，没用。」
「要真都这样，我倒该喜欢你，」她说，「因为你太像我自己了。」
「我不是要吓唬你，姑娘，也不是要卖你；你一向就是急了点，手快了点；可这样的人未必最坏。我们费尔菲尔德家也沾着几分这脾气，对这种火爆性子，不必苛责。我这辈子见过的姑娘，不分贵贱，论模样、论谈吐，没一个赶得上你；你穿戴又这样漂亮，这会儿你要是有眼睛，爱把谁踩在脚底下，就能把谁踩在脚底下。」
哈利·费尔菲尔德又赌了个咒，把这番对她魅力的高见重申了一遍。
「要是『如果』呀『但愿』呀能当锅碗瓢盆使，」夫人带着一声心满意足的叹息念叨，用的还是那口外国腔、那种怪拖长的调子，把这句家常谚语念得格外古怪；「后半句我忘了——大概是，补锅匠就全该没饭吃了。」
「说得好，贝莎！可这会儿天底下没一个比得上你，一个也没有，这么齐整。」他嚷道。
「总比卡韦尔（Carwell）格兰奇那小丫头标致吧，我料着——嗯？」
「爱丽丝（Alice）！给你提鞋都不配。你要是能看看她，再照照镜子，这问题你自己就答上了。」他答道。

===Original===

“There it is again﻿—*if* I could look in the glass﻿—it is fourteen years since I did that﻿—*if* I could see that fool of a girl﻿—if﻿—if﻿—*if*!” she said, with an irrepressible simper﻿—“the old proverb again﻿—ifs and ans were pots and pans﻿—’twas old Mistress Tarnley used to say that﻿—a d﻿—﻿—﻿d old witch she always was,” she broke out, parenthetically, “and should be broke alive on the wheel.”
“Bang away wi’ the devil’s broomstick, and break her to smash for me,” said Harry. “But I’d sooner talk o’ yourself. Hang me, if you ever looked better﻿—there’s no such figure; and, by the law, it’s looking up﻿—it *is*﻿—better and better every day. I like a tall lass, but ye beat them all, by the law, and ye shows off a dress so grandly.”
“Now don’t think, foolish thing, I like compliments﻿—in at one ear and out of the other,” she said, with the same smirk, shaking her great head.
“Hoot, lass! Compliments, indeed! Why should I? Only this, that knowing you so long I just blurts out everything that comes uppermost, and it’s a pity ye shouldn’t have money to dress as ye should.”
“I never had that,” said the lady.
“Never﻿—I know that well﻿—and if ye won’t be said by me, ye’ll have less,” said Harry.
“I don’t think you know much about it,” said Bertha, serenely.
“Now, Bertha, child, you mustn’t keep contradictin’ me. I do know a deal about it﻿—*everything*. There was no marriage, *never*.”
“As long as Charlie lived, ye never said that﻿—you always backed me.”
“I’m not going to tell lies for no one,” said he, sulkily.
“Not going! Why you have been lying all your life﻿—you’d lie for a shilling any day﻿—all lies, you mean, miserly liar.”
“Come, Bertha, draw it mild, won’t ye? Did you never hear say o’ the Fairfields that they were a quick-tempered folk? and it’s an old saying, don’t knock a mad horse over the head.”
“It’s true all I said,” she laughed; “and that’s why it stings.”
“And did ye never hear that true jests breed bad blood?” he laughed. “But no matter, I’m not a bit riled, and I won’t. I like ye better for speaking out; I hate that mealymouthed talk that fine-spoken folk goes on wi’. I likes a bit of a rub now and then; if ye were too civil I couldn’t speak my own mind neither, and that would never do.”
“Get along with ye. Have you any more to say?”
“Shall I say it out, plain and short, and will ye hear it through?” he asked.
“Ay.”

===Chinese===

「又是这一套——*要是*我能照照镜子——我上一回照镜子，是十四年前了——*要是*我能看看那个傻丫头——要是——要是——*要是*！」她说着，忍不住嗤嗤地笑——「又是那句老话——如果呀但愿呀能当锅碗瓢盆——从前老爱说这话的是塔恩利（Tarnley）家老娘娘——她一向就是个该死的老妖婆，」她插进来发作了一句，「就该把她绑上车轮活活轧死。」
「拿魔鬼的扫帚柄狠狠抽，替我把她抽个稀烂，」哈利说。「不过我更想谈谈你。叫我吊死好了，从没见你比这更好看的——这份身段，独一份；乖乖，越来越出挑——真是——一天好过一天。高挑姑娘我素来喜欢，可你把她们全比下去了，乖乖，你穿衣裳又这样提气。」
「可别当真以为，傻东西，我爱听奉承——这边耳朵进，那边耳朵出。」她说，还是那副嗤笑，摇着她那颗大脑袋。
「去你的，姑娘！奉承，瞧你说的！我图什么？不过是这样：认识你这么久，心里冒出什么嘴上就直说什么；可惜你没钱，穿不上你该穿的那样。」
「我从来没有过。」夫人说。
「从来没有——这我知道——可你要是不肯听我的，往后连现在的都要少。」哈利说。
「我看这事你知道得有限。」贝莎安详地说。
「行了，贝莎，孩子，别一个劲儿顶我。我知道的可不少——*什么都知道*。婚是没有结的，*从来没有*。」
「查理活着的时候，你从没这么说过——你一向是向着我的。」
「我不能为任何人撒谎。」他绷着脸说。
「不能！你撒了一辈子谎——哪天不为一个先令撒谎——满嘴谎话，你个吝啬的谎皮子。」
「得啦，贝莎，嘴下留情，行不行？费尔菲尔德家脾气急，这话你没听说过？老话说得好，疯马的脑袋，别去敲。」
「我说的句句是真，」她笑道；「所以才扎人。」
「真玩笑结冤家，这话你也没听说过？」他笑道。「不过不打紧，我一点儿不恼，也不打算恼。你直说，我反倒更喜欢你；我讨厌体面人那种黏黏糊糊的腔调。偶尔磕碰两句我不嫌；你太客气了，我也不好说真心话，那才不像话。」
「去你的吧。你还有话没有？」
「我明明白白、短短几句说出来，你肯一口气听完吗？」他问。
「肯。」

===Original===

“Well, here it is; if ye don’t sign that, I think ye’ll be hanged.”
“No, you don’t,” she said, more quietly.
“I do, by —﻿—﻿—,” he swore.
“No, you don’t,” she repeated, in the same tone, “who is to do it? Charlie’s gone, and vilely as he used me, he never would have done that; and Alice won’t, she told you so. I’m better informed, I believe, than you fancied. So don’t you suppose I am at all anxious.”
“I wanted to take you off in a coach, and you won’t let me,” said he.
“Thanks, simple Harry,” she sneered.
“And I’m coming this day week, and then it will be within ten days o’ the ’sizes.”
“And I’ll be discharged; and I’ll bring separate actions against every soul that had a hand in putting me here. Ask my attorney,” said the lady, with a pale angry simper.
“And Judge Risk is coming down, and you’d better ask your attorney, as you talk of him, whether he’s a hangin’ judge or no.”
“Cunning beast! all won’t do,” she said, sarcastically.
“Well, Bertha, this day week I’ll be here, and this day week will be your last chance, for things will begin that day, and no one can stop them.”
“Lord have mercy upon us!” she whined, with an ugly mockery and an upturning of her sightless eyes.
“You may be saying something like that in the press-room yet, if you won’t take the trouble to think in earnest before it’s too late. Now, listen, once for all, for it’s the last words I’ll say. That’s all true you say: Charlie’s gone, and if he was here, instead of in kingdom come, ’twould ’a been all one, for he wouldn’t never ’a moved a hand in the matter, nor ’a suffered it; and as for Alice, she won’t neither. But if you don’t sign that paper by this day week, and make no bones about it”﻿—here he swore a hard oath﻿—“blind as you be, I’ll open your eyes﻿—and I’ll prosecute the indictment myself. Goodbye, ma’am, and *think* between this and then.”
Harry Fairfield strode from the room, and was still full of the grim emotion which had animated the close of his interview, when he reached the little inn at which but a few weeks before his brother Charles had stabled his horse, when making his last visit to Hatherton.

===Chinese===

「好，就是这么一句：那张纸你不签，我看你就要上绞架。」
「不，你才不这么想呢。」她说，平静了些。
「我就是这么想，对——」他咒道。
「不，你才不这么想呢，」她还是那个声调，「谁来办？查理去了，他虽然那样恶待我，也绝不会做那种事；爱丽丝不肯，她亲口对你说了。我这里的消息，我看比你料的灵通。所以你休想我会着半点儿急。」
「我本想用马车把你接走，你偏不让。」他说。
「多谢了，傻哈利。」她讥诮道。
「下星期的今天我再来，那时离巡回法庭开审就不到十天了。」
「到时候我就开释了；还要一个个告过去，凡是插手把我弄到这儿来的，一个不饶。问我的状师去。」夫人说，惨白着一张脸，含着怒嗤笑。
「还有，下来主审的是里斯克（Risk）法官，你既然张口闭口你的状师，顶好问问他，这位是位爱送人上绞架的法官不是。」
「狡猾的畜生！说什么都没用。」她冷嘲道。
「好，贝莎，下星期的今天我到这儿来；那一天就是你最后的机会，因为事情当天就要开头，谁也拦不住了。」
「主啊，怜悯我们吧！」她哀声哼道，带着丑陋的嘲弄，那双瞎眼朝上一翻。
「要是你不趁早郑重其事想一想，往后在死囚房里，你兴许还真要这么念叨。听好了，就这一回，这是我最后的话。你说的都不假：查理去了；他就是在世不在天国，也是一样，这事他决不会动一根指头，也决不容人动；爱丽丝也不会。可下星期的今天你要还不签那张纸，别再磨蹭」——说到这儿他狠狠起了个誓——「你眼睛瞎，我来替你睁开——公诉由我亲自来办。再见了，夫人，这几天里，*好好想想*。」
哈利·费尔菲尔德大步走出房间；直到抵达那家小客栈时，他胸中仍激荡着会谈收尾时那股狠劲——不过几个星期前，他兄弟查尔斯（Charles）最后一次来哈瑟顿，正是在这家客栈寄的马。

## XXXIII Charles Fairfield Alone / 第三十三章 查尔斯·费尔菲尔德独自一人
===Original===

Charles Fairfield talked of sleeping. There was little chance of that. He placed the candle on one of the two old oak cupboards, as they were still called, which occupied corresponding niches in the wainscoted wall, opposite the fireplace, and he threw himself at his length on the sofa.
Tired enough for sleep he was; but who can stop the mill of anxious thought into which imagination pours continually its proper grist? In his tired head its wheels went turning, and its hammers beat with monotonous pulsation and whirl﻿—weariest and most wasting of fevers!
He turned his face, like the men of old, in his anguish, to the wall. Then he tried the other side, wide awake, and literally staring, from point to point, in the fear and fatigue of his vain ruminations. Then up he sat, and flung his cloak on the floor, and then to the window he went, and, opening the shutter, looked out on the moonlight, and the peaceful trees that seemed bowed in slumber, and stood, hardly seeing it﻿—hardly thinking in his confused misery.
One hand in his pocket, the other against the window-case, to which the stalworth good fellow, Harry, had leaned his shoulder in their unpleasant dialogue and altercation. Harry, his chief stay, his confidant and brother﻿—dare he trust him now? If he might, where could he find him? Better do his own work﻿—better do it indifferently than run a risk of treason. He did not quite know what to make of Harry.
So with desultory resolution he said to himself, “Now I’ll think in earnest, for I’ve got but two hours to decide in.” There was a pretty little German village, quite out of the ordinary route of tourists. He remembered its rocks and hills, its ruined castle and forest scenery, as if he had seen them but yesterday﻿—the very place for Alice, with her simple tastes and real enjoyment of nature. On that point, though under present circumstances by short journeys, they should effect their retreat.
In three hours’ time he would himself leave the Grange. In the meantime he must define his plans exactly. He must write to Harry﻿—he must write to Alice, for he was quite clear he would not see her; and, after all, he might have been making a great deal too much of this odious affair, which, rightly managed, might easily end in smoke.
Pen, ink, and paper he found, and now to clear his head and fix his attention. Luckily he had a hundred pounds in his pocketbook.

===Chinese===

查尔斯·费尔菲尔德嘴上说要睡。真睡着的指望却微乎其微。他把蜡烛搁在其中一只老橡木柜上——至今人们还这么叫它们——那两只柜子分踞护壁墙上相对的两个壁龛，正对着壁炉，随后他整个人直挺挺倒在沙发上。
他也确实累得够睡一觉了；可焦虑心思这座磨，想象不停地往里添料，谁又刹得住？在他倦极的脑袋里，磨盘转着，磨锤单调地搏动、回旋——最磨人、最耗人的高烧！
苦楚之中，他像古人那样，把脸转向墙壁。又翻到另一边，清醒得很，真真是睜着眼，从一点望到另一点，空自愁思，又怕又乏。他坐起来，把斗篷掙在地上；又走到窗前，推开窗板，望着月色，望着那些安眠般垂首的安静的树，就那样站着——视而不见——满心凄惶混沌，几乎不能思索。
一只手插在兜里，一只手抵着窗框——方才那番不快的对话争执里，那条好汉哈利就把肩膀靠在这窗框上。哈利，他的顶梁柱，他的知己，他的兄弟——如今他还敢信他吗？就算敢，又上哪儿寻他去？不如自己的事自己做——做得再糟，也好过冒一场遭人背叛的险。哈利这个人，他有点摸不透。
于是他胡乱打定主意，对自己说：「现在认真想想吧，满打满算只有两个钟头做决断。」有一座漂亮的德国小村，完全不在游客惯走的路线上。那些岩石山峦、废堡和森林景致，他记得清清楚楚，仿佛昨天才见过——正配爱丽丝，配她朴素的口味和对自然的真心喜爱。目标就定在那里，只是眼下的处境，他们得分成一小程一小程地撤退。
三个钟头后他就离开田庄。这段时间里，他必须把计划定得毫厘不爽。他得给哈利写信——也得给爱丽丝写信，他打定主意不见她了；再说，这件可恨的事，兴许被他瞧得太重，处置得当，很容易烟消云散。
笔墨纸张都寻齐了，现在得清一清脑子，定一定神。幸而他的皮夹里还有一百镑。

===Original===

Too hard that out of his miserable pittance, scarcely five hundred pounds a year, he should have to pay two hundred pounds to that woman, who never gave him an easy week, and who seemed bent on ruining him if she could. By the dull light of the mutton-fat with which Mildred had furnished him he wrote this note﻿—

> “My darling little Woman﻿—
> “You must make Dulcibella pack up your things. Tom will have a chaise here at eleven o’clock. Drive to Wykeford and change horses there, and go on to Lonsdale, where I will meet you *at last*. Then and there your own, poor, loving Ry will tell you all his plans and reasons for this sudden move. We must get away by easy stages, and baffle possible pursuit, and then a quiet and comparatively happy interval for my poor little fluttered bird. I live upon the hope of our meeting. Out of reach of all trouble we shall soon be, and your poor Ry happy, where only he can be happy, in your dear presence. I enclose ten pounds. Pay *nothing and nobody* at the Grange. Say I told you so. You will reach Lonsdale, if you leave Carwell not later than eleven, before five. Don’t delay to pack up any more than you actually want. Leave all in charge of old Mildred, and we can easily write in a day or two for anything we may want.
> “Ever, my own idolized little woman,
> “Your own poor adoring
> “Ry.”

So this was finished, and now for Harry:

===Chinese===

难就难在，他一年满打满算不到五百镑的可怜进项，还得掏二百镑给那个女人——她从没让他过过一个安生礼拜，还一门心思只要办得到就要毁掉他。就着米尔德里德给他备下的一支羊脂蜡烛昏沉的光，他写下这张字条——

> 「我亲爱的小妇人——
> 「你务必让杜西贝拉把你的东西收拾好。汤姆十一点会备一辆马车到这儿。先赶去怀克福德换马，再往前去朗斯代尔（Lonsdale），在那里，我终于能同你相会。到时候、当地方，你那可怜的、深爱你的瑞，会把他的全部计划和这番突然动身的原委讲给你听。咱们得一小程一小程地走，甩开可能的追赶，然后给我那颗惊惶扑腾的小鸟，换一段安静、还算快活的日子。我全靠着相会的指望活着。咱们很快就会逃出一切烦恼的掌心，你可怜的瑞也会快活起来——他只有在你亲爱的身边，才有快活可言。随信附上十镑。在田庄什么账也不必付，对谁也不必付。就说是我吩咐的。你只要不晚于十一点离开卡威尔，五点前就能到朗斯代尔。随身要带的东西，拣要紧的收拾，别多耽搁。其余一切都交给老米尔德里德照管，过一两天，咱们缺什么随便写封信就能取到。
> 「永远是你的，我崇拜的小妇人，
> 「你那可怜的、痴爱你的
> 「瑞」

这封写完，接着给哈利的：

===Original===

> “My dear Harry﻿—
> “How you must hate the sight of my hand. I never write but to trouble you. But, as you will perceive, I am myself in trouble more than enough to warrant my asking you again to aid me if it should lie in your way. You will best judge if you can, and how you can. The fact is that what you apprehended turns out to be too true. That person who, however I may have been at one time to blame, has certainly no right to charge me with want of generosity or consideration, seems to have made up her mind to give me all the annoyance in her power. She is at this moment *here* at Carwell Grange. I was absent when she arrived, and received timely notice, and perhaps ought to have turned about, but I could not do that without ascertaining first exactly how matters stood at Carwell. So I am here, without anyone’s being aware of it except old Mildred, who tells me that the person in question is under the impression that it is *you*﻿—and not *I*﻿—who are married, and that it is your wife who is residing in the house. As you have been no party to this deception, pray let her continue to think so. I shall leave this before daybreak, my visit not having exceeded four hours. I leave a note for poor little Alice, telling her to follow me tomorrow﻿—I should say *this* morning﻿—to Lonsdale, where I shall meet her; and thence we get on to London, and from London, my present idea is, to make our way to some quiet little place on the Continent, where I mean to stay quite concealed until circumstances alter for the better. What I want you, and *beg* of you to do for me at present, is just this﻿—to sell *everything* at Carwell that is saleable﻿—the horse, the mule, the two donkeys, the carts, plough, etc., etc., in fact everything out of doors; and let the farm to Mildred’s nephew, who wanted to take it last year. It is, including the garden, nineteen acres. I wish him to have it, provided he pays a fair rent, because I think he would be kind to his aunt, old Mildred. He must stipulate to give her her usual allowances of vegetables, milk, and all the rest from the farm; and she shall have her room, and the kitchen, and her £8 a year as usual. Do like a good old fellow see to this, and try to turn all you can into money for me. I shall have miserably little to begin with, and anything you can get together will be a lift to me. If you write under cover to J.

===Chinese===

> 「我亲爱的哈利——
> 「你一见我的笔迹，只怕就要厌恶。我一提笔就是给你添麻烦。可你也看得出，我眼下自己正陷在麻烦里，够不够格再求你搭一把手，你也明断。你能不能帮、怎么帮，你自行裁夺。实情是，你先前担心的事，不幸太成真了。那一位——不管我从前有什么对不住处——实在没资格怪我小气或薄情，如今却像是打定了主意，要怎样叫我不得安生便怎样来。她此刻就在卡威尔田庄。她到的时候我不在家，得了及时的信儿，本来也许该掉头就走，可我总得先摸清卡威尔这边的实情，才走得起。所以我来了，除了老米尔德里德，无人知觉。她告诉我，那位一口咬定，成亲的是你——不是我——住在这宅里的是你的妻子。这场误会既与你无干，就请让她这样想下去。天亮前我就走，此来前后不过四个钟头。我给可怜的小爱丽丝留了字条，叫她明天——该说今天早上——随后赶来朗斯代尔，我在那儿接她；从那儿咱们去伦敦，出了伦敦，我眼下的打算，是寻个大陆上安静的小地方，彻底隐姓埋名住下，直到时局好转。眼下我求你、恳你替我办的，就是这样一件事——把卡威尔凡是能卖的统统卖掉——马、骡、两头驴、大车、犁，等等等等，凡是院子外头的一概在内；农场租给米尔德里德的侄子，他去年就想接手。连园子算上，一共十九英亩。我愿意他接，只要他出公道的租钱，因为我想他总不忍待他姑妈、老米尔德里德。得讲定照老例从农场给她蔬菜、牛奶和其余一切；她的屋子、厨房照旧归她，一年八镑也照旧。像个老好人那样替我盯着这件事，能变卖的都替我变成钱。我起手的那点家底薄得可怜，你能凑出多少，都是拉我一把。你要是写信，封套转交威斯敏斯特老地方的J.

===Original===

Dylke at the old place in Westminster, it will be sure to reach me. I don’t know whether all this is intelligible. You may guess how distracted I am and miserable. But there is no use in describing. I ought to beg your pardon a thousand times for asking you to take all the trouble involved in this request. But, dear Harry, you will ask yourself who else on earth has the poor devil to look to in an emergency but his brother? I know my good Harry will remember how urgent the case is. Any advice you can spare me in my solitary trouble will be most welcome. I think I have said everything﻿—at least all I can think of in this miserable hurry﻿—I feel so helpless. But you are a clever fellow, and always were﻿—so much cleverer than I, and know how to manage things. God bless you, dear Harry, I know you won’t forget how pressed I am. You were always prompt in my behalf, and I never so needed a friend like you﻿—for delay here might lead to the worst annoyances.
> “Ever, dear Harry, your affectionate brother,
> “Charles Fairfield.
> “Carwell Grange.”

It was a relief to his mind when these letters were off it, and something like the rude outline of a plan formed.
Very tired was Charles Fairfield when he had folded and addressed his letters. No physical exertion exhausts like the monotonous pain of anxiety. For many nights he had had no sleep, but those wearying snatches of half-consciousness in which the same troublous current is still running through the brain, and the wasted nerves of endurance are still tasked. He sat now in his chair, the dim red light of the candle at his elbow, the window shutter open before him, and the cold serene light of the moon over the outer earth and sky.
Gazing on this, a weary sleep stole over his senses, and for a full hour the worn-out man slept profoundly.
Into this slumber slowly wound a dream, of which he could afterwards remember only that it was somehow horrible.
Dark and direful grew his slumber thus visited; and in a way that accorded well with its terrors, he was awakened.

===Chinese===

迪尔克（J. Dylke），信准能到我手里。这些话条理清楚不清楚，我不知道。你可想见我心绪多乱、多苦。多写无益。叫你为这封信里的事受累，我该赔一千个不是。可是，亲爱的哈利，你自问一句：这可怜虫急难之中，放眼世上，除了兄弟还有谁可指望？我知道我的好哈利记得这事情多么火急。你在我这孤身困顿里肯赏的任何主意，我都感激不尽。该说的我想都说到了——至少，这般凄惶匆忙里想得到的都说了——我觉得自己这样无依无靠。可你是个机灵人，从来都是——比我机灵得多，又懂得怎么张罗。上帝保佑你，亲爱的哈利，我知道你不会忘了我多么紧迫。你替我办事从来爽利，而我从来没有这样需要你这样的朋友——因为此地稍有耽搁，就可能招来最坏的纠缠。
> 「永远是你的，亲爱的哈利，你亲爱的兄弟，
> 「查尔斯·费尔菲尔德
> 「卡威尔田庄」

两封信落笔，心里卸下了些分量，一个计划也粗粗立起了轮廓。
信纸折好、写上名址时，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德已经疲惫不堪。任何体力的劳顿，都耗不过焦虑这种单调的苦刑。多少夜他没有睡过，只有那些半醒半昧的疲乏浅眠——同样的乱流依旧冲刷着脑子，磨耗的忍耐神经依旧绷着。此刻他坐在椅中，肘边是蜡烛昏红的火光，面前窗板敞开，外面天地间一片清冷的月光。
望着这月色，一阵困倦的睡意偷袭了他的知觉，这个心力交瘁的人沉沉睡去，足睡了一个钟头。
一个梦缓缓缠进这场酣睡，事后他只记得，那梦不知怎么十分可怕。
这般缠扰之下，他的睡眠越发的黑、越发的凶；而唤醒他的方式，也正配得上梦里的恐怖。

## XIX Coming In / 第十九章 来临
===Original===

When at last her husband entered the room where she awaited him that night﻿—
“Oh! Charlie, it is very late,” said Alice, a little reproachfully.
“Not very, is it, darling?” said he, glancing at his watch. “By Jove! it is. My poor little woman, I had not an idea.”
“I suppose I am very foolish, but I love you so much, Charlie, that I grow quite miserable when I am out of your sight.”
“I’m sorry, my darling, but I fancied he had a great deal more to tell me than he really had. I don’t think I’m likely, at least for a little time, to be pressed by my duns﻿—and﻿—I wanted to make out exactly what money he’s likely to get me for a horse he is going to sell, and I’m afraid, from what he says, it won’t be very much; really, twenty pounds, one way or other, seems ridiculous, but it does make a very serious difference just now, and if I hadn’t such a clever, careful little woman as you, I don’t really know what I should do.”
He added this little complimentary qualification with an instinctive commiseration for the pain he thought he saw in her pretty face.
“These troubles won’t last very long, Charlie, *perhaps*. Something, I’m sure, will turn up, and you’ll see how careful I will be. I’ll learn everything old Mildred can teach me, ever so much, and you’ll see what a manager I will be.”
“You are my own little treasure. You always talk as if you were in the way, somehow, I don’t know how. A wife like you is a greater help to me than one with two thousand a year and the reckless habits of a fine lady. Your wise little head and loving heart, my darling, are worth whole fortunes to me without them, and I do believe you are the first really good wife that ever a Fairfield married. You are the only creature I have on earth, that I’m quite sure of﻿—the only creature.”
And so saying he kissed her, folding her in his arms, and, with a big tear filling each eye, she looked up, smiling unutterable affection, in his face. As they stood together in that embrace his eyes also filled with tears and his smile met hers, and they seemed wrapt for a moment in one angelic glory, and she felt the strain of his arm draw her closer.
Such moments come suddenly and are gone; but, remaining in memory, they are the lights that illuminate a dark and troublous retrospect forever.
“We’ll make ourselves happy here, little Ally, and I﻿—in spite of everything, my darling!

「不算太晚吧，亲爱的？」他瞥了一眼表。「天哪！真是晚了。我可怜的小妇人，我一点儿没数。」
「我知道我很傻，可我太爱你了，查理，一不见你，我就难过得不行。」
「对不起，宝贝，我只当他还有好多话要告诉我，其实没有。至少眼前一阵子，债主们该不会逼上门了——还有——我想问准了，他替我卖那匹马到底能弄到几个钱；听他的口气，恐怕多不了；说真的，二十镑上下这么点钱，看着可笑，可眼下这一进一出，却是天大的出入；要不是有你这么个又机灵又细心的小妇人，我真不知道该怎么办。」
他添上这句小小的恭维，是出于本能的怜惜——他仿佛看见她那张俏脸上闪过的痛楚。
「这些难处不会拖很久的，查理，也许。我笃定，总会有转机；你瞧着吧，我会多么仔细。老米尔德里德能教我的，我样样都学，学好多好多，你瞧着吧，我会把家当得多好。」
「你是我一个人的小宝贝。你说话总像是自己碍了事似的，怎么会的，我也说不清。有你这样的妻子，比娶个一年两千镑、浑身阔太太挥霍习气的帮衬我大多了。你那颗聪明的小脑袋、那颗爱我的心，宝贝，就是没有陪嫁，对我也是整份整份的家私；而且我真信，你是费尔菲尔德家娶的头一个真正的好妻子。这世上你是我有的唯一的人，我拿得准的——唯一的一个。」
说着，他吻住她，把她拥进怀里；两大颗泪注满她的眼睛，她仰起脸，望着他的脸，笑出说不尽的深情。相拥而立时，他的眼睛也噙满泪，他的笑迎着她的笑，两人一时仿佛同裹在一片天使般的光辉里，她感到他手臂收紧，把她搂得更近。
这样的时刻说来就来，一晃即逝；可它们留在记忆里，便成了一盏盏灯，永远照亮回望中那片晦暗多艰的来路。
「咱们要在这儿过得快快活活，小艾莉，还有我——不管怎么样，宝贝！

===Original===

﻿—and I don’t know how it happened that I stayed away so long; but I walked with Harry further than I intended, and when he left me I loitered on Cressley Common for a time with my head full of business; and so, without knowing it, I was filling my poor little wife’s head with alarms and condemning her to solitude. Well, all I can do is to promise to be a good boy and to keep better hours for the future.”
“That’s so like you, you are so good to your poor, foolish little wife,” said Alice.
“I wish I could be, darling,” said he; “I wish I could prove one-half my love; but the time will come yet. I shan’t be so poor or powerless always.”
“But you’re not to speak so﻿—you’re not to think that. It is while we are poor that I can be of any use,” she said, eagerly; “very little, very miserable my poor attempts, but nothing makes me so happy as trying to deserve ever so little of all the kind things my Ry says of me; and I’m sure, Charlie, although there may be cares and troubles, we will make our time pass here very happily, and perhaps we shall always look back on our days at Carwell as the happiest of our lives.”
“Yes, darling, I am determined we shall be very happy,” said he.
“And Ry will tell me everything that troubles him?”
Her full eyes were gazing sadly up in his face. He averted his eyes, and said﻿—
“Of course I will, darling.”
“Oh! Ry, if you knew how happy that makes me!” she exclaimed. But there was that in the exclamation which seemed to say, “If only I could be sure that you meant it.”
“Of course I will﻿—that is, everything that could possibly interest you, for there are very small worries as well as great ones; and you know I really can’t undertake to remember everything.”
“Of course, darling,” she answered; “I only meant that if anything were really﻿—any great anxiety﻿—upon your mind, you would not be afraid to tell me. I’m not such a coward as I seem. You must not think me so foolish; and really, Ry, it pains me more to think that there is any anxiety weighing upon you, and concealed from me, than any disclosure could; and so I *know*﻿—won’t you?”
“Haven’t I told you, darling, I really will,” he said, a little pettishly. “What an odd way you women have of making a fellow say the same thing over and over again. I wonder it does not tire you, I know it does *us* awfully. Now, there, see, I really do believe you are going to cry.”
“Oh, no, indeed!”
