「可不许这么说——也不许这么想。正趁着咱们穷，我才派得上用场。」她急切地说，「我那点本事小得可怜、笨得可怜，可天下再没有比这更快活的了——试着配得上我的瑞说的那些好话，哪怕配上一星半点；而且我笃信，查理，操心也好，烦难也好，咱们在这儿的日子会过得很幸福；日后回想，说不定在卡威尔的这些天，就是咱们一辈子最快活的日子。」
「是，宝贝，我打定主意，咱们一定要幸福。」他说。
「还有，瑞有了烦心事，都会讲给我听？」
她盈盈的眼睛哀哀地仰望着他的脸。他避开了目光，说——
「当然会，宝贝。」
「哦！瑞，你要知道这话叫我多快活！」她喊道。可喊声里藏着一句话没说出口：「但愿我能信你是真心的。」
「当然会——就是说，凡是同你相干的，我都说；烦心事有大有小；你也知道，我可没法子把样样都记着交代。」
「当然，宝贝，」她答道，「我的意思只是，你心里若真有什么——什么大忧愁——你可别怕讲给我听。我不是看上去那么胆小。别把我当傻瓜；真的，瑞，一想到有什么心事压着你、又瞒着我，比什么吐露都叫我难受；所以我拿准了——你会的，是不是？」
「我不是说了嘛，宝贝，我真的会。」他说，有点不耐烦。「你们女人这套把戏真古怪，非逼着男人把一句话翻来覆去说。真奇怪你们不腻，我知道我们可腻透了。瞧，瞧，我看你真要哭了。」
「哦，不，才不呢！」

===Original===

she said, brightening up, and smiling with a sad, little effort.
“And now, kiss me, my poor, good little woman﻿—you’re not vexed with me?﻿—no, I’m sure you’re not,” said he.
She smiled a very affectionate assurance.
“And really, you poor little thing, it is awfully late, and you must be tired, and I’ve been﻿—no, *not* lecturing, I’ll never lecture, I hate it﻿—but boring or teasing; I’m an odious dog, and I hate myself.”
So this little dialogue ended happily, and for a time Charles Fairfield forgot his anxieties, and a hundred pleasanter cares filled his young wife’s head.
In such monastic solitudes as Carwell Grange the days pass slowly, but the retrospect of a month or a year is marvellously short. Twelve hours without an event is very slow to get over. But that very monotony, which is the soul of tediousness, robs the background of all the irregularities and objects which arrest the eye and measure distance in review, and thus it cheats the eye.
An active woman may be well content with an existence of monotony which would all but stifle even an indolent man. So long as there is a household﻿—ever so frugal﻿—to be managed, and the more frugal the more difficult and harassing﻿—the female energies are tasked, and healthily because usefully exercised. But in this indoor administration the man is incompetent and in the way. His ordained activities are out of doors; and if these are denied him, he mopes away his days and feels that he cumbers the ground.
With little resource but his fishing-rod, and sometimes, when a fit of unwonted energy inspired him, his walking-stick, and a lonely march over the breezy expanse of Cressley Common, days, weeks, and months, loitered their drowsy way into the past.
There were reasons why he did not care to court observation. Under other circumstances he would have ridden into the neighbouring towns and heard the news, and lunched with a friend here or there. But he did not want anyone to know that he was at the Grange; and if it should come out that he had been seen there, he would have had it thought that it was but a desultory visit.
A man less indolent, and perhaps not much more unscrupulous, would have depended upon a few offhand lies to account for his appearance, and would not have denied himself an occasional excursion into human society in those rustic haunts within his reach.

===Chinese===

她说着，振作起来，惨然地小小用了把力，笑出来。
「好了，亲亲我，我可怜的、好心的小妇人——你不恼我吧？——不，我拿准你不恼。」他说。
她盈盈一笑，深情地给了他保证。
「说真的，你这可怜的小东西，实在不早了，你准累了，而我一直在——不，不是训人，我从不训人，我讨厌训人——是磨人、逗人；我是条讨厌的狗，我恨我自己。」
这场小小的对白就这样欢喜收场，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德一时忘了愁烦，他年轻妻子的脑袋里，则装满了上百件愉快些的营生。
在卡威尔田庄这样修院般的僻壤，日子过得慢，可回头一望，一个月、一年却短得出奇。十二个钟头无事发生，熬起来长得很。可正是那份单调——沉闷的魂——把沿途一切凹凸起伏、一切留住目光的物事统统抹平了，回望时没了丈量远近的东西，眼睛就这样受了骗。
勤快的女人对这种单调的日子可以心满意足，而这样的日子连懒散男人都要憋闷死。只要有一个家——再节俭也有一个家——要操持，而且越节俭越难、越熬人——女人的精力就有用武之地，而且用得有益，所以用得康健。可屋里的这一摊，男人既插不上手，又碍手碍脚。他命定的营生在户外；户外若没了，他就闷闷地把日子耗过去，觉得自己是块多余的地面。
他手边无非一根钓竿，偶尔——一阵少见的劲头上来了——一根手杖，独个儿去克雷斯利公地空旷的风里走一遭；日子、礼拜、月份，就这样打着瞌睡、磨磨蹭蹭地进了往昔。
他有他的缘故，不愿招人耳目。换在别的境况，他会骑马去邻镇听听新闻，在哪儿同朋友吃顿午饭。可他不愿任何人知道他在田庄；万一被人撞见的消息传出去，他也只愿人家当作一次漫不经心的闲住。
换个懒性子轻些、脸皮未必厚些的人，会信口编几句谎话遮掩行踪，也断不肯亏待自己，偶尔还是要去够得着的乡下人堆里走动走动。

===Original===

But Charles Fairfield had not decision to try it, nor resource for a system of fibbing, and the easiest and dullest course he took.
In Paradise the man had his business﻿—“to dress and to keep” the garden, and, no doubt, the woman hers, suitable to her sex. It is a mistake to fancy that it is either a sign of love or conducive to its longevity that the happy pair should always pass the entire four-and-twenty hours in each other’s company, or get over them in anywise without variety or usefulness.
Charles Fairfield loved his pretty wife. She made his inactive solitude more endurable than any man could have imagined. Still it was a dull existence, and being also darkened with an ever-present anxiety, was a morbid one.
Small matters harassed him now. He brooded over trifles, and the one care, which was really serious, grew and grew in his perpetual contemplation until it became tremendous, and darkened his entire sky.
I can’t say that Charles grew morose. It was not his temper, but his spirits that failed﻿—careworn and gloomy﻿—his habitual melancholy depressed and even alarmed his poor little wife, who yet concealed her anxieties, and exerted her music and her invention﻿—sang songs﻿—told him old stories of the Wyvern folk, touched him with such tragedy and comedy as may be found in such miniature centres of rural life, and played backgammon with him, and sometimes écarté, and, in fact, nursed his sick spirits, as such angelic natures will.
Now and then came Harry Fairfield, but his visits were short and seldom, and what was worse, Charles always seemed more harassed or gloomy after one of his calls. There was something going on, and by no means prosperously, she was sure, from all knowledge of which, however it might ultimately concern her, and did immediately concern her husband, she was jealously excluded.
Sometimes she felt angry﻿—oftener pained﻿—always troubled with untold fears and surmises. Poor little Alice! It was in the midst of these secret misgivings that a new care and hope visited her﻿—a trembling, delightful hope, that hovers between life and death﻿—sometimes in sad and mortal fear﻿—sometimes in delightful anticipation of a new and already beloved life, coming so helplessly into this great world﻿—unknown, to be her little comrade, all dependent on that beautiful love with which her young heart was already overflowing.

===Chinese===

可查尔斯·费尔菲尔德既没有当机立断的魄力去试，也没有本钱经营一套谎话体系，便挑了最省事也最乏味的一条路。
伊甸园里，男人有男人的营生——「修理看守」园子，女人无疑也有适合她性情的营生。以为恩爱夫妻该一天二十四钟头形影不离，或以为这样不换花样、不干正事地把日子打发掉，就算得上爱情的明证、保得住爱情长久——那是想错了。
查尔斯·费尔菲尔德爱他美丽的妻子。她把他那段无所事事的幽居，调理得比任何男人能想象的都好受。话虽如此，那终究是乏味的日子，又时时罩着一层挥不去的忧惧，便成了病态的日子。
如今鸡毛蒜皮也来磨他。他为些琐事翻来覆去地想；而那件真正非同小可的心事，在他无休止的思量里越滚越大，大到骇人，遮黑了他整片天。
倒不能说查尔斯变得乖僻。垮掉的不是他的脾气，是他的兴头——满脸忧色，郁郁沉沉——他惯常的愁闷压得他可怜的小媳妇心里发沉，甚至发慌；可她把忧惧藏起来，拿出她的音乐和她的机变——唱歌——给他讲怀弗恩人家的老故事，把那种乡村小天地里才有的悲欢讲给他听，陪他下双陆棋，有时玩埃卡特牌，事实上，就是像天使心肠的人那样，看护着他害病的情绪。
哈利·费尔菲尔德偶尔来一趟，来得短，来得稀；更糟的是，他每来一回，查尔斯总要显得更烦恼或更阴沉。她断定，有一桩事正在进行，而且进行得绝不顺当；这桩事，不管日后会同她有怎样的干系，眼下就同她丈夫息息相关，而她却被小心地关在门外。
她有时恼——更多时候是疼——无时无刻不被说不出口的恐惧和猜疑搅扰着。可怜的小爱丽丝！正是在这些暗自的不安之中，一桩新的牵挂、一个新的指望，降临到她身上——一个战战兢兢、又甜滋滋的指望，悬在生死之间——有时陷进悲哀的、要命的恐惧——有时又欢喜地期待着一个崭新的、已经爱上的小生命，那样孤弱无助地要来到这个大世界上——无人识得，要做她的小伙伴，全全倚仗她年轻的心里正满满溢出的那份美丽的爱。

===Original===

So almost trembling﻿—hesitating﻿—she told her little story with smiles and tears, in a pleading, beseeching, almost apologetic way, that melted the better nature of Charles, who told her how welcome to him, and how beloved for her dear sake the coming treasure should be, and held her beating heart to his in a long, loving embrace, and more than all, the old love revived, and he felt how lonely he would be if his adoring little wife were gone, and how gladly he would have given his life for hers.
And now came all the little cares and preparations that so mercifully and delightfully beguile the period of suspense.
What is there so helpless as a newborn babe entering this great, rude, cruel world? Yet we see how the beautiful and tender instincts which are radiated from the sublime love of God, provide everything for the unconscious comer. Let us, then, take heart of grace when, the sad journey ended, we, children of dust, who have entered so, are about to make the dread exit, and remembering what we have seen, and knowing that we go in the keeping of the same “faithful Creator,” be sure that His love and tender forecast have provided with equal care for our entrance into another life.

===Chinese===

于是，几乎是哆嗦着——踌躇着——她又笑又泪地讲出了她的小小心事，那声气央告着、恳求着，几乎带着歉意，把查尔斯心肠里更好的那一面融化了。他告诉她，这个将临的珍宝对他多么受欢迎，看在她亲爱的份上，会多么被疼爱；他把她怦怦跳动的心，久久搂在自己怀里；更难得的是，旧日的爱又活了过来——他感觉到，他那痴心的小妻子若是没了，他会有多孤单；只要换她平安，他豁出自己的命也甘愿。
接下来，便是那一切小小的操心和预备——它们那样仁慈、那样可喜地把悬望的日子打发过去。
还有什么比初生的婴孩更无助呢——就这样闯进这个巨大、粗粝、冷酷的世界？可我们看见，从上帝崇高之爱里流溢出的那些美好温柔的天然心性，早已为这懵懂的来客备妥了一切。那么，待到悲苦的旅程走完，我们这些尘土的孩子——当年也是这样来的——将要踏上那可怕的归程时，也让我们这样鼓起勇气：想想我们见过的，知道我们同样是在那位「信实的造物主」看顾之下离去，便笃信他的爱与温柔的先见，必同样周到地为我们进入另一段生命做了安排。

## Conclusion / 结语
===Original===

At the George Inn, a little way out of Hatherton, the boy, to his inexpressible delight, at last found Tom Orange.
He told Tom at once of his adventure at the shop window, and the occurrence darkened Tom’s countenance. He peeped out and took a long look toward Hatherton.
“Put the horse to the fly and bring it round at once,” said Tom, who put his hand in his pocket and drew forth a rather showy handful of silver.
I don’t pretend to say, when Tom was out of regular employment, from what pursuits exactly he drew his revenue. They had rather improved than otherwise; but I dare say there were anxious compensations.
The boy had eaten his breakfast before he reached Hatherton. So much the better; for the apparition of the Sergeant-Major would have left him totally without appetite. As it was, he was in an agony to be gone, every moment expecting to see him approach the little inn to arrest him and Tom.
Tom Orange was uneasy, I am sure, and very fidgety till the fly came round.
“You know Squire Fairfield of Wyvern?” said the hostess, while they were waiting.
“Ay,” said Tom.
“Did you hear the news?”
“What is it?”
“Shot the night before last in a row with poachers. Gentlemen should leave that sort o’ work to their keepers; but they was always a fightin’ wild lot, them Fairfields; and he’s lyin’ now a dead man﻿—all the same﻿—gave over by Doctor Willett and another﻿—wi’ a whole charge o’ duck-shot lodged under his shoulder.”
“And that’s the news?” said Tom, raising his eyes and looking through the door. He had been looking down on the ground as Mrs. Gumford of the George told her story.
“There’s sharp fellows poachers round there, I’m told,” he said, “next time he’d a’ been out himself with the keepers to take ’em dead or alive. I suppose that wouldn’t answer *them*.”
“ ’Tis a wicked world,” said the lady.
“D﻿—﻿—﻿d wicked,” said Tom. “Here’s the fly.”
In they got and drove off.
Tom was gloomy, and very silent.
“Tom, where are we going to?” asked the boy at last.
“All right,” said Tom. “All right, my young master. You’ll find it’s to none but good friends. And, say now﻿—Haven’t I been a good friend to you, Master Harry, all your days, sir? Many a mile that you know nothing about has Tom Orange walked on your business, and down to the cottage and back again; and where would you or her have been if it wasn’t for poor Tom Orange?”
“Yes, indeed, Tom, and I love you, Tom.”

===Chinese===

出了哈瑟顿（Hatherton）不多远的乔治客栈（George Inn），男孩终于找到了汤姆·奥兰治——那份欢喜，难以言表。
他当即把橱窗前那番遭遇讲给汤姆听，这事让汤姆的脸色沉了下来。他探出头，朝哈瑟顿方向望了老半天。
「给单马轿车套上马，马上赶过来。」汤姆说着，手伸进口袋，掏出满满一把亮闪闪的银币。
汤姆没有正经营生的时候，进项究竟从哪些行当里来，我不装作知道。日子倒是一天好过一天；不过我敢说，来路总叫人捏着把汗。
男孩到哈瑟顿之前吃过早饭了。这样倒好；军士长的身影一出现，他准会一点胃口也没有。即便这样，他还是急得火烧火燎，只想赶紧走，时时刻刻提防着看见那人朝小客栈走来，把他和汤姆一把抓走。
汤姆·奥兰治心里也不踏实，我敢说，马车转过来之前，他坐立不安得很。
「你认得怀弗恩的费尔菲尔德乡绅吧？」等车的当儿，老板娘说。
「认得。」汤姆说。
「听说了那个消息没有？」
「什么消息？」
「前天夜里同偷猎的分伙打架，叫人开枪打了。老爷们那种事本该留给护林人去干；可费尔菲尔德家的人，从来就是一帮好斗的野家伙；他这会儿正躺着等死呢——没救了——威利特医生和另一位大夫都撒手了——一整枪的铁砂全窝在肩膀底下。」
「这就是那消息？」汤姆说着，抬起眼，朝门外望去。乔治客栈的冈姆福德太太（Mrs. Gumford）讲这段新闻时，他一直低头看着地面。
「听说那一带偷猎的有几个狠角色，」他说，「下回他就要亲自带着护林人出去，抓他们个死活。我琢磨着，这可不合那些人的心意。」
「这世道邪性。」老板娘说。
「邪性得该死。」汤姆说。「车来了。」
两人上车，车走了。
汤姆闷闷的，一路很少说话。
「汤姆，咱们这是上哪儿？」男孩终于问。
「放心，」汤姆说，「放心，我的小少爷。等着瞧吧，去的全是好朋友的地界。还有，我问你——哈利少爷，打你出生到今天，我难道不是你顶好的朋友吗，少爷？为你的事，汤姆·奥兰治跑过多少里路，你一点儿不知道——去那小屋，再回来；要没有可怜的汤姆·奥兰治，你，还有她，这会儿还不知在哪儿呢？」
「真是的，汤姆，我爱你，汤姆。」

===Original===

“And now, I’ve took you away from that fellow, and I’m told I’m likely to be hanged for it. Well, no matter.”
“Oh, Tom; poor Tom! Oh! no, no, no!” and he threw his arms round Tom’s neck in a paroxysm of agonised affection, and, in spite of the jolting, kissed Tom; sometimes on the cheek, on the eyebrow, on the chin, and in a great jolt violently on the rim of his hat, and it rolled over his shoulder under their feet.
“Well, that is gratifyin’,” said Tom, drying his eyes. “There is some reward for *prenciple* after all, and if you come to be a great man some o’ these days, you’ll not forget poor Tom Orange, that would have spent his last bob and spilt his heart’s blood, without fee or reward, in your service.”
Another explosion of friendship from the boy assured Tom of his eternal gratitude.
“Do you know this place, sir?” asked Tom, with a return of his old manner, as making a sudden turn the little carriage drove through an open gate, and up to a large old-fashioned house. A carriage was waiting at the door.
There could be no mistake. How delightful! and who was that? Mammy! at the hall door, and in an instant they were locked in one another’s arms, and “Oh! the darlin’,” and “Mammy, mammy, mammy!” were the only words audible, half stifled in sobs and kisses.
In a minute more there came into the hall﻿—smiling, weeping, and with hands extended toward him, the pretty lady dressed in black, and her weeping grew into a wild cry, as coming quickly she caught him to her heart. “My darling, my child, my blessed boy, you’re the image﻿—Oh! darling, I loved you the moment I saw you, and now I know it all.”
The boy was worn out. His march, including his divergence from his intended route, had not been much less than thirty miles, and all in chill and wet.
They got him to his bed and made him thoroughly comfortable, and with mammy at his bedside, and her hand, to make quite sure of her, fast in his, he fell into a deep sleep.
Alice had already heard enough to convince her of the boy’s identity, but an urgent message from Harry, who was dying, determined her to go at once to Wyvern to see him, as he desired. So, leaving the boy in charge of “mammy,” she was soon on her way to the old seat of the Fairfields.
If Harry had not known that he was dying, no power could ever have made him confess the story he had to tell.
There were two points on which he greatly insisted.

===Chinese===

「如今呢，我把你从那家伙手里带出来了，人家说我这事弄不好要吃绞刑。嗨，无所谓。」
「哦，汤姆；可怜的汤姆！哦！不，不，不！」他一阵心痛如绞的亲热发作，扑上去搂住汤姆的脖子，顾不得车颠，一路吻过去；一会儿腮上，一会儿眉毛上，一会儿下巴上，一个猛颠，狠狠吻在帽檐上，帽子翻过肩膀，滚到两人脚底下。
「嗬，这可受用。」汤姆擦着眼睛说。「看来『讲义气』到底还是有报偿的；往后哪天你成了大人物，可别忘了可怜的汤姆·奥兰治——他为了你，掏得出最后一个铜子儿，洒得出一腔心血，不讲酬劳，不图报答。」
男孩又发作了一阵掳心窝子的热乎，汤姆这才笃定了他这份情义永生不忘。
「这地方您认得吗，少爷？」汤姆问道，又拿出了他惯常的做派——小车一个急转弯，驶进敞开的大门，来到一座老式大宅前。门口正停着一辆马车。
错不了。多叫人欢喜！那是谁？妈妈！就在大厅门口。转眼间两人已经抱作一团，「哦！小宝贝！」「妈妈，妈妈，妈妈！」呜咽和亲吻压得半吞半吐，就听得清这两句。
又过了一会儿，走进大厅来的是那位一身黑衣的美丽太太——笑着，哭着，双手朝他伸过来；她快步迎上，把他搂进怀里，啜泣一下子放成了一声嘶喊。「我的心肝，我的孩子，我有福的宝贝，你活脱是——哦！心肝，我头一眼见你就疼你，如今我全明白了。」
男孩累垮了。这一路，算上偏离原定路线的岔道，走得几乎不下三十英里，还全是又湿又冷的天。
众人把他安顿上床，伺候得妥妥帖帖；妈妈守在床边，她的手紧紧攞在他手里——攞得实实的，好确定她在——他沉沉睡去。
爱丽丝听到的已足以叫她认下男孩的身份；可哈利捎来急信——他快不行了——她便依着他的心愿，当即动身去怀弗恩见他。于是男孩交给「妈妈」照看，她很快上了去费尔菲尔德家老宅的路。
哈利若不是自知将死，天大的力量也休想叫他说出他不得不讲的这段事。
有两层意思，他咬得很死。

===Original===

The first was, that believing that his brother was really married to Bertha Velderkaust, he was justified in holding that his nephew had no legal right to succeed.
The second was, that he had resolved, although he might have wavered lately a little, never to marry, and to educate the boy better than ever he was educated himself, and finally to make him heir to Wyvern, pretending him to be an illegitimate son of his own.
Whether the Sergeant-Major knew more than he was ordered or undertook to know, he never gave the smallest ground to conjecture. He stated exactly what had passed between him and Harry Fairfield. By him he was told that the child which was conveyed to Marjory Trevellian’s care was his own unacknowledged son.
On the very same evening, and when old Mildred Tarnley was in the house at Twyford, was a child taken, with the seeds of consumption already active in it, from a workhouse in another part of England and placed there as the son of Charles Fairfield and Alice. It was when, contrary to all assurances, this child appeared for a few days to rally, and the situation consequent on its growing up the reputed heir to Wyvern alarmed Harry, that he went over, in his panic, to the Grange, and there opened his case, that the child at Twyford was a changeling, and not his brother’s son.
When, however, the child began to sink, and its approaching death could no longer be doubtful, he became, as we have seen, once more quite clear that the baby was the same which he had taken away from Carwell Grange.
Dr. Willett’s seeing the child so often at Twyford, also prevented suspicion, though illogically enough, for had they reflected they might easily have remembered that the doctor had hardly seen the child twice after its birth while at the Grange, and that, like everyone else, he took its identity for granted when he saw it at Twyford.
Alice returned greatly agitated late that evening. No difficulty any longer remained, and the boy, with ample proof to sustain his claim, was accepted as the undoubted heir to Wyvern, and the representative of the ancient family of Fairfield.
The boy, Henry Fairfield, was as happy as mortal can be, henceforward.

===Chinese===

第一，他认定他哥哥同贝尔塔·费尔德考斯特的婚姻千真万确，因此他认定他侄子没有合法的承继权——这在他是有理的。
第二，他早已拿定主意——近来虽稍稍有过动摇——终身不娶，把这孩子调教得比自己当年受的教化还好，最后立他为怀弗恩的继承人，对外只说他是自己的一名私生子。
至于军士长，他到底知道多少——比上头吩咐他知道的、比他应承知道的——他从未露出半点可供揣度的口风。他原原本本讲了他和哈利·费尔菲尔德之间经过的一切：哈利告诉他，送到玛乔丽·特雷维里安手上抚养的那个孩子，是他自己一名没有名分的儿子。
就在同一个晚上——老米尔德里德·塔恩利正歇在特怀福德那户人家——另一个孩子被抱了来：那孩子身上痨病的根子已然发作，从英格兰另一头的济贫院里领出，安放在那里，充作查尔斯·费尔菲尔德和爱丽丝的儿子。没料到一切担保都落了空，这孩子竟有几天见好；他长起来便是众人认定的怀弗恩继承人而由此招来的局面，吓着了哈利，他才慌里慌张赶到田庄，摆出他的说法：特怀福德那孩子是个偷换来的假子，不是他哥哥的骨血。
可后来那孩子眼见着沉下去，死期已无可置疑，他心里便又落了定，如我们所见——认定这婴孩正是他从卡威尔田庄抱走的那个。
威利特医生常在特怀福德看视这孩子，也免去了众人的疑心——虽然这逻辑实在站不住：只要细想一下就不难记起，孩子在田庄时，医生产后统共没见过他两回；在特怀福德见着时，他同旁人一样，把「这就是那孩子」想当然了。
爱丽丝深夜归来，心潮翻涌。再无一丝疑窦了。男孩持有充分的凭证支撑他的名分，被认作怀弗恩毋庸置疑的继承人，古老的费尔菲尔德家的嗣脉。
男孩——亨利·费尔菲尔德——从此快活得凡人再无从超越。

===Original===

His little playmate, the pretty little girl whom Alice had adopted, who called her “mamma,” and yet was the daughter of a distant cousin only, has now grown up, and is as a girl even more beautiful than she was as a child. Henry will be of age in a few months, and they are then to be married. They now reside at Wyvern. The estate, which has long been at nurse, is now clear, and has funded money beside.
Everything promises a happy and a prosperous reign for the young Fairfield.
Mildred Tarnley, very old, is made comfortable at Carwell Grange.
Good old Dulcibella is still living, very happy, and very kind, but grown a little huffy, being perhaps a little over petted. In all other respects, the effect of years being allowed for, she is just what she always was.
Tom Orange, with a very handsome sum presented by those whom he had served, preferred Australia to the old country.
Harry Fairfield had asserted, in his vehement way, while lying in his last hours at Wyvern, that the fellow with the handkerchief over his face who shot him was, he could all but swear, his old friend Tom Orange.
Tom swore that had he lived he would have prosecuted him for slander. As it is, that eccentric genius has prospered as the proprietor of a monster tavern at Melbourne, where there is comic and sentimental singing, and some dramatic buffooneries, and excellent devilled kidneys and brandy.
Marjory Trevellian lives with the family at Wyvern, and I think if kind old Lady Wyndale were still living the consolations of Alice would be nearly full.

===Chinese===

他那位小伙伴——爱丽丝收养的漂亮小姑娘，唤她「妈妈」，其实不过是一位远房表亲的女儿——如今长大成人了，出落成姑娘，比做孩子时还要美。亨利再过几个月就成年，届时两人完婚。他们如今住在怀弗恩。那份产业，长久「寄养」在外，如今理清了，还多出几笔置下的公债。
一切都预示着，小费尔菲尔德治下的日子，又兴旺，又美满。
米尔德里德·塔恩利已十分年迈，在卡威尔田庄安享舒适晚年。
好心的老杜西贝拉还健在，很快活，也很慈爱，只是变得有点儿小脾气——兴许是叫人宠过头了。除开这一点，把年岁的因素刨去，她还是从前那个她。
汤姆·奥兰治得了一笔他效过力的人家奉上的丰厚馈赠，比起故土，他更中意澳大利亚。
哈利·费尔菲尔德在怀弗恩弥留的那些时辰里，以他那激烈的方式咬定：那个蒙着手帕朝他开枪的家伙，他几乎可以起誓，就是他的老朋友汤姆·奥兰治。
汤姆赌咒说，他若活着，非告他诽谤不可。如今的实情是：这位古怪天才在墨尔本开了一家特大的酒馆，生意兴隆——馆子里有滑稽小曲，有抒情小调，有几出闹剧把式，还有一手顶呱呱的辣味腰子和白兰地。
玛乔丽·特雷维里安跟着一家人住在怀弗恩。我想，倘若仁慈的温代尔老夫人还健在，爱丽丝的慰藉，就近乎圆满了。

## XLI Day﻿—Twilight﻿—Darkness / 第四十一章 白昼——黄昏——黑暗
===Original===

In their homely sitting-room, with old Dulcibella in friendly attendance, Mildred Tarnley found Alice. It is not always that a dreadful impression makes itself immediately manifest. Nature rallies all her forces at first to meet the danger. A certain excitement of resistance sustains the system through a crisis of horror, and often for a long time after; and it is not until this extraordinary muster of the vital forces begins to dissolve and subside that the shattered condition of the normal powers begins to declare itself.
The scene which had just occurred was a dreadful ordeal for Alice. To recount, and with effort and minuteness, to gather into order the terrific incidents of the night preceding, relate them bit by bit to the magistrate as he wrote them down, make oath to their truth as the basis of a public prosecution, and most dreadful﻿—the having to see and identify the spectre who had murderously assailed her on the night before.
Every step affrighted her, the shadow of a moving branch upon the wall chilled her with terror; the voices of people who spoke seemed to pierce the naked nerve of her ear, and to sing through her head; even for a moment faces, kind and familiar, seemed to flicker or darken with direful meanings alien from their natures.
In this nervous condition old Mildred found her.
“I come, ma’am, to know what you’d wish to be done,” said she, standing at the door with her usual grim little courtesy.
“I don’t quite understand﻿—done about what?” inquired she.
“I mean, ma’am, Tom said you asked him to be ready to drive you from here; but as master ha’n’t come back, and things is changed a bit here, I thought ye might wish to make a change, mayhap.”
“Oh, oh! thank you, Mrs. Tarnley; I forgot, I’ve been so frightened. Oh, Mrs. Tarnley, I wish I could cry﻿—I’d be so much better, I’m sure, if I could cry﻿—I feel my throat so odd and my head so confused﻿—it seems so many days. If I could think of anything to make me cry.”
Mildred looked at her from the corners of her eyes darkly, as if with a hard heart, but I think she pitied her.
“That blind woman’s gone, the beast﻿—I’m glad she’s away; and you’ll be the better o’ that, ma’am, I’m thinkin’. I was afeard o’ her a’most myself ever since last night; and Master Charles is gone, too, but he’ll be back soon.”
“He’ll come *today*?” she asked, in consternation.

===Chinese===

在那间家常的起居室里，老杜尔西贝拉（Dulcibella）亲切地陪着，米尔德里德·塔恩利（Mildred Tarnley）在这里找到了爱丽丝（Alice）。可怕的印象并不总是立刻显露出来。天性起先会聚齐全部力量去迎危险。一股抗拒的亢奋撑着人闯过恐怖的关口，还常能撑很久；直到这股非同寻常的生气大集结开始消解散去，正常机能那副支离的样子才显出来。
方才那一幕，对爱丽丝是一场可怕的磨难。要把前一夜那些骇人的事，费力地、一五一十地理出头绪，在治安官笔录时一段段讲给他听，宣誓属实，作为公诉的底子；而最可怕的是——还得亲眼看见、亲手指认那个昨夜行凶要她命的鬼影。
每一步都叫她心惊，墙上晃动的树枝影子能把她的血凉透；人说话的声音像扎进她耳中裸露的神经，在头颅里嗡嗡回响；有那么一刻，连亲切熟悉的面孔都仿佛闪烁、变暗，蒙上同它们本性全不相干的凶意。
老米尔德里德见到的她，正是这副神经兮兮的光景。
「我来，太太，问问您想怎么安排。」她说，站在门口，带着她惯常那点冷硬的小礼数。
「我不大明白——安排什么？」她问。
「我是说，太太，汤姆（Tom）说您叫他备好车，要离开这儿；可主人还没回来，这儿的事情也有了些变化，我寻思您兴许想改个主意。」
「哦，哦！谢谢您，塔恩利（Tarnley）太太；我忘了，我吓昏了头。哦，塔恩利太太，我但愿能哭出来——要是能哭，我准会好受得多——我喉咙发僵，脑袋发懵——恍恍惚惚像过了多少天。要是能想起件什么叫我落泪的事就好了。」
米尔德里德斜着眼、阴阴地打量她，仿佛心肠很硬，可我看她心里是怜悯的。
「那瞎眼女人走了，那畜生——她走了我高兴；您也能安生些，太太，我琢磨着。打昨晚起，连我差不多都怕她；查尔斯（Charles）少爷也走了，不过他快回来了。」
「他*今天*就回来？」她问，大惊失色。

===Original===

“Today, of course, ma’am﻿—in an hour or less, I do suppose; and it would not be well done, I’m thinkin’, ma’am, for you to leave the Grange till you see him again, for it’s like enough he’ll a’ changed his plans.”
“I was thinking so myself. I’d rather wait here to see him﻿—he had so much to distract him that he may easily think differently by this time. I’m glad, Mrs. Tarnley, you think so, for now I feel confident I may wait for his return﻿—I think I ought to wait﻿—and thank you, Mrs. Tarnley, for advising me in the midst of my distractions.”
“I just speak my mind, ma’am, and counsel’s no command, as they say; and I never liked meddlers; and don’t love to burn my fingers in other people’s brewes; so ye’ll please to mind, ma’am, ’tis for your own ear I speak, and your own wit will judge; and I wouldn’t have Master Charles looking askew, nor like to be shent by him for what’s kindly meant to you﻿—not that I owe much kindness nowhere, for since I could scour a platter I ever gave work for wage. So ye’ll please not tell Master Charles I counselled ye aught in the matter.”
“Certainly, Mrs. Tarnley, just as you wish.”
“Would you please wish anything to eat, ma’am?” inquired Mildred, relapsing into her dry, official manner.
“Nothing, Mildred﻿—no, thanks.”
“Ye’ll lose heart, miss, if ye don’t eat﻿—ye must eat.”
“Thanks, Mildred, by-and-by, perhaps.”
Mrs. Tarnley, like many worthy people, regarded eating as a simply mechanical process, and wondered why people affected a difficulty about it under any circumstances. Somewhat hard of heart, and with nerves of wire, she had no idea that a sufficient shock might rob one not only of appetite, but positively of the power of eating for days.
Alone, for one moment, Alice could not endure to be﻿—haunted unintermittingly by the vague but intense dread of a return of the woman who had so nearly succeeded in murdering her, and with nerves shattered in that indescribable degree which even a strong man experiences for a long time after a murder has been attempted upon him perfidiously and by a surprise. The worst panic comes after an interval of many hours.
As the day waned, more miserably nervous she became, and more defined her terror of the Dutchwoman’s return. That straggling old house, with no less than four doors of entrance, favoured the alarms of her imagination.

===Chinese===

「今天，那还用说，太太——我估摸着，一个钟头不到就到；依我看，您再见他之前就离开格兰奇（Grange），可不妥当，因为他的打算八成改了。」
「我也是这么想。我宁可在这儿等他回来——他操心的事那么多，这会儿主意早变了也未可知。您也这么看，我真高兴，塔恩利太太，这下我心安了，可以等他回来——我想我该等——谢谢您，塔恩利太太，在我六神无主的时候给我出主意。」
「我不过说实话，太太，俗话说，劝不是命令；我一向不喜欢多管闲事的人，也不爱在别人的汤里烫手指；所以请您记着，太太，这话是说给您一个人听的，怎么断随您自己的聪明；我不愿叫查尔斯少爷斜眼看我，也不愿为着对您的一片好意挨他数落——倒不是我受过谁多少恩惠，打我会刷盘子起，我做工从来是拿工钱换的。所以请您别告诉查尔斯少爷，这桩事上我给您出过什么主意。」
「一定，塔恩利太太，照您的意思。」
「您想吃点什么吗，太太？」米尔德里德问，又摆回那副干巴巴的公事腔。
「什么也不想吃，米尔德里德——不，谢谢。」
「不吃东西，人会垮的，小姐——您得吃。」
「谢谢，米尔德里德，回头再说吧，兴许。」
塔恩利太太同许多正经人一样，把吃饭看成一桩纯机械的事，不懂人怎么会在任何情形下拿它作难。她心肠偏硬，神经是铁丝打的，想都不敢想：一场够分量的惊吓，不但能夺人胃口，还真能叫人一连几天咽不下一口。
片刻的独处，爱丽丝也受不住——那女人差一点就得手害了她，如今那女人卷土重来的阴影像鬼一样缠着她，不松半刻；她的神经碎到了没法形容的地步——就是一个硬汉，遭人暗算、猝不及防地谋害过一回，此后长日子里也是这般光景。最凶的惊惶，是在好几个钟头之后才来的。
天色愈晚，她愈是惶惶不可终日，对那荷兰女人卷土重来的惧怕也愈来愈成形。那座散漫的老宅，进出的门竟有四扇之多，正好由着她的想象去生惊疑。

===Original===

Often she thought of her kind old kinswoman, Lady Wyndale, and her proffered asylum at her snug house at Oulton.
But that was a momentary picture﻿—no more. Miserable as she was at the Grange, until she had seen her husband, learned his plans, and knew what his wishes were, that loyal little wife could not dream of going to Oulton.
She remained there as the shades of evening darkened over the steep roof and solemn trees of Carwell Grange, and more and more grew the horror that deepened with darkness, and was aggravated and distracted by the continued absence of her husband.
In the sitting-room she stood, listening, with a beating heart. Every sound, which at another time would have been unheard, now thrilled her with hope or terror.
Old Dulcibella in the room was also frightened﻿—more a great deal than she could account for. And even Mildred Tarnley﻿—that hard and grim old lady﻿—was touched by the influence of that contagious fear, and barred and locked the doors with jealous care, and even looked to the fastenings of the windows, and caught some faint shadows of that supernatural fear with which Alice Fairfield had come to regard the wicked woman out of whose hands she had escaped.
Now and then, when appealed to, she said a short word or two of reassurance respecting Charles Fairfield’s unaccountably prolonged absence. But the panic of the young lady in like manner on this point began to invade her in uncomfortable misgivings.
So uneasy had she grown that at last she despatched Tom, when sunset had come without a sign of Charles Fairfield’s return, riding to Wykeford. Tom had now returned. A bootless errand it had proved. At Wykeford he learned that Charles Fairfield had been there﻿—had been at Squire Rodney’s house and about the town, and made inquiries. His pursuit had been misdirected. At Wykeford is a House of Correction and Reformatory, which institution acts as a prison of ease to the county jail. But that jail is in the town of Hatherton, as Charles would have easily recollected if his rage had allowed him a moment to think. Tom, however, made no attempt further to pursue him, on conjecture, and had returned to Carwell Grange, no wiser than he went.

===Chinese===

她时常想起她那位仁慈的老亲戚温代尔夫人（Lady Wyndale），想起她提出的庇护——奥尔顿（Oulton）那座舒适的小宅。
可那不过一瞬的图景——仅此而已。她在格兰奇再苦，不见到丈夫、不知他的打算、不晓他的心意，这位忠心的小妻子做梦也不会往奥尔顿去。
暮色一层层压上卡韦尔（Carwell）格兰奇陡峭的屋顶和肃穆的树木，她就这么留在这里；随着黑暗加深的恐怖一分分涨上来，又因丈夫迟迟不归而火上浇油，搅得人心焦。
她站在起居室里，心碎碎跳着谛听。每一个平日根本听不见的声响，此刻都叫她一阵希冀、一阵惊怖。
屋里的老杜尔西贝拉也怕了——怕得连自己都莫名其妙。就连米尔德里德·塔恩利——那位又硬又冷的老太太——也被这股会传染的惊惶沾了身，把门户一道道闩牢锁严，连窗子的插销都要查看，还沾上了几分爱丽丝·费尔菲尔德（Alice Fairfield）对那个恶女人的超自然恐惧——她正是从那女人手底下逃出来的。
有人问起时，她也不时为查尔斯·费尔菲尔德莫名其妙迟迟不归说上一两句宽心话。可在这件事上，年轻太太的那种慌乱，也同样开始侵入她心里，化成隐隐的不安。
她越想越不安，日落时分还不见查尔斯·费尔菲尔德回来的影子，终于打发汤姆骑马去威克福德（Wykeford）。汤姆这会儿回来了。这趟差事是白跑了。他在威克福德打听到，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德确实到过那儿——去过罗德尼（Rodney）乡绅宅上，在镇上四处打听过。他追错了方向。威克福德有一座感化院兼惩戒所，那机构算得郡监狱的宽松分号；可郡监狱本监在哈瑟顿（Hatherton）城里——查尔斯若不是怒火烧昏了头，本该一想就记起。汤姆却没有凭猜测再往下追，回了卡韦尔格兰奇，怎么去的，怎么回的，一无所获。

## XX Harry Appears at the Grange / 第二十章 哈利现身田庄
===Original===

It was about four o’clock one afternoon, while Charles was smoking a cigar﻿—for notwithstanding his self-denying resolutions, his case was always replenished still﻿—that his brother Harry rode into the yard, where he was puffing away contemplatively at an open stable-door.
“Delighted to see you, Harry. I was thinking of you this moment, by Jove, and I can’t tell you how glad I am,” said Charles, smiling as he advanced, yet with an anxious inquiry in his eyes.
Harry took his extended hand, having dismounted, but he was looking at his horse, and not at Charles, as he said﻿—
“The last mile or so I noticed something in the off forefoot; do you? Look now﻿—’taint brushing, nor he’s not gone lame, but tender like; do you notice?” and he led him round a little bit.
“No,” said Charles, “I don’t see anything, but I am an ignoramus, you know﻿—no﻿—I think, nothing.”
“ ’Taint a great deal, anyhow,” said Harry, leading him toward the open stable-door. “I got your note, you know, and how are you all, and how is Ally?”
“Very well, poor little thing, we are all very well. Did you come from Wyvern?” said Charles.
“Yes.”
“And the old man just as usual, I suppose?”
“Just the same, only not growing no younger, you’ll suppose.”
Charles nodded.
“And a d﻿—﻿—﻿d deal crosser, too. There’s times, I can tell you, he won’t stand no one nigh him﻿—not even old Drake, d﻿—﻿—﻿d vicious.”
Harry laughed.
“They say he liked Ally﻿—they do, upon my soul, and I wouldn’t wonder, ’tis an old rat won’t eat cheese﻿—only you took the bit out o’ his mouth, when you did, and that’s enough to rile a fellow, you know.”
“Who says so?” asked Charles, with a flush on his face.
“The servants﻿—yes﻿—and the townspeople﻿—it’s pretty well about, and I think if it came to the old boy’s ears there would be black eyes and bloody noses about it, I do.”
“Well, it’s a lie,” said Charles; “and don’t, like a good fellow, tell poor little Alice there’s any such nonsense talked about her at home, it would only vex her.”
“Well, I won’t, if I think of it. Where’s Tom? But ’twouldn’t vex her﻿—not a bit﻿—quite ’tother way﻿—there’s never a girl in England wouldn’t be pleased if old Parr himself wor in love wi’ her, so she hadn’t to marry him. But the governor, by Jove, I don’t know a girl twelve miles round Wyvern, as big an old brute as he is, would turn up her nose at him, wi’ all he has to grease her hand. But where’s Tom?

===Chinese===

一天下午四点来钟，查尔斯正抽着雪茄——尽管发过多少次克己的誓，他的雪茄匣却总是满着——他兄弟哈利骑马进了院子；他正对着敞开的马厩门，若有所思地吞云吐雾。
「真高兴见到你，哈利。天哪，我这会儿正想着你呢，多高兴我说不上来。」查尔斯笑着迎上前，眼睛里却带着焦灼的探问。
哈利下了马，握住他伸来的手，眼睛却望着马，不望查尔斯，说道——
「最后一里来地，我瞧出他右前蹄有点什么；你瞧呢？看——不是擦伤，也没瘪，就是发软似的；你看出来没有？」说着他把马牵得转了半圈。
「看不出，」查尔斯说，「我什么也瞧不出来，你知道我是个外行——不——我看，没事。」
「横竖不碍大事。」哈利说着，把马往敞开的厩门牵。「你的条子我收到了；家里都好吗，艾莉好吗？」
「很好，可怜的小东西，我们都很好。你从怀弗恩来？」查尔斯说。
「是。」
「老爷子还是老样子吧，我猜？」
「还是老样子，只是不见年轻，你料得到。」
查尔斯点点头。
「还坏脾气得要死。有的时候，我告诉你，他连一个人都不许近身——连老德雷克（Drake）都不行，凶得邪门。」
哈利笑了。
「人家说，他喜欢艾莉——真这么说，天知道；我也不奇怪——老耗子也有不吃奶酪的——只是你把到嘴的那一口抢走了，你抢的时候正赶在那一口上，换了谁不火，你知道。」
「谁说的？」查尔斯问，脸涨红了。
「仆人们——是啊——还有镇上人——传得差不离了；我看这话要是传到老家伙耳朵里，免不了打得鼻青眼肿，真的。」
「哼，那是放屁。」查尔斯说，「好兄弟，可别跟可怜的小爱丽丝说，家里有人嚼她这种混账舌头，只会惹她难过。」
「行，想着就不说。汤姆呢？不过这话才惹不着她呢——半点儿也惹不着——正相反——英格兰随便哪个姑娘，就算是老寿星帕尔本人看上了她，只要不用嫁他，没有不欢喜的。至于老爷子，天哪，凭他块头再大的老畜生，怀弗恩方圆十二英里内的姑娘，我还找不出一个会对他嗤鼻子——他有的是钱抹人家的手心。可汤姆呢？

===Original===

the nag must have a feed.”
So they bawled for Tom, and Tom appeared, and took charge of the horse, receiving a few directions about his treatment from Master Harry, and then Charles led his brother in.
“I’m always glad to see you, Harry, but always, at the same time, a little anxious when you come,” said Charles, in a low tone, as they traversed the passage toward the kitchen.
“ ’Taint much﻿—I have to tell you something, but first gi’ me a mouthful, for I’m as hungry as a hawk, and a mug o’ beer wouldn’t hurt me while I’m waitin’. It’s good hungry air this; you eat a lot, I dessay; the air alone stands you in fifty pounds a year, I reckon; that’s paying pretty smart for what we’re supposed to have for the takin’.”
And Harry laughed at his joke as they entered the dark old dining-room.
“Ally not here?” said Harry, looking round.
“She can’t be very far off, but I’ll manage something if she’s not to be found.”
So Charles left Harry smiling out of the window at the tops of the trees, and drumming a devil’s tattoo on the pane.
“Ho! Dulcibella. Is your mistress upstairs?”
“I think she is gone out to the garden, sir; she took her trowel and garden gloves, and the little basket wi’ her,” answered the old woman.
“Well, don’t disturb her, we’ll not mind, I’ll see old Mildred.”
So to old Mildred he betook himself.
“Here’s Master Harry come very hungry, so send him anything you can make out, and in the meantime some beer, for he’s thirsty, too, and, like a good old soul, make all the haste you can.”
And with this conciliatory exhortation he returned to the room where he had left his brother.
“Ally has gone out to visit her flowers, but Mildred is doing the best she can for you, and we can go out and join Alice by-and-by, but we are as well to ourselves for a little. I﻿—I want to talk to you.”
“Well, fire away, my boy, with your big oak stick, as the Irishman says, though I’d rather have a mouthful first. Oh, here’s the beer﻿—thank ye, Chick-a-biddy. Where the devil did you get that queer-looking fair one?” he asked, when the Hebe, Lilly Dogger, disappeared; “I’ll lay you fifty it was Ally chose that one.”
And he laughed obstreperously.
And he poured out a tumbler of beer and drank it, and then another and drank it, and poured out a third to keep at hand while he conversed.

===Chinese===

这马总得喂上一顿。」
于是两人高声唤汤姆，汤姆来了，把马接过去，哈利少爷又叮嘱了几句怎么照应它；随后查尔斯领兄弟进屋。
「见你我永远高兴，哈利；可你一来，我永远又有点心悬。」两人穿过往厨房去的过道时，查尔斯低声说。
「不是小事——我有话要告诉你；可先给我弄口吃的，我饿得像只鹰；等饭的工夫，来一大杯啤酒也不碍事。这地方的风，是开胃的好风；你饭量准不小；我算着，光这风一年就得搭进去五十镑——都说这儿的白食白拿，这代价可不小。」
两人走进黑黢黢的老饭厅，哈利为自己的笑话笑起来。
「艾莉不在？」哈利环顾道。
「走不远；真找不着人，我自会想法子。」
于是查尔斯丢下哈利——他正望着窗外树梢笑，手指在窗玻璃上敲着鬼点子。
「嗬！杜西贝拉。你家太太在楼上吗？」
「想是去园子了，先生；她带了小铲子、园艺手套，还有那只小篮子。」老妇人答道。
「好，别搅她，不妨事，我去找老米尔德里德。」
他便去找老米尔德里德。
「哈利少爷来了，饿得很；手边能弄出什么就给他送什么，先来点啤酒，他也渴了；好心的老人家，千万手脚麻利些。」
说完这番安抚的叮嘱，他回到丢下兄弟的那间屋。
「艾莉出去看她的花了；米尔德里德正尽力给你弄吃的。待会儿咱们再出去找爱丽丝；眼下咱们单独待一会儿正好。我——我有话要同你说。」
「那就开火吧，伙计，抡起你那根大橡木棍——爱尔兰人这么说——不过我宁可先垫一口。哦，啤酒来了——谢谢你，小乖乖。那个怪模怪样的金发丫头，你们打哪儿弄来的？」斟酒的——莉莉·多杰，那尊青春女神——退出去之后，他问：「我跟你赌五十块，准是艾莉挑的那个。」
他放肆地大笑。
他斟满一大杯啤酒，喝了；又斟一杯，又喝了；再斟上第三杯，搁在手边，留着说话时喝。

===Original===

“There used to be some old pewter goblets here in the kitchen﻿—I wonder what’s gone wi’ them﻿—they were grand things for drinking beer out of﻿—the pewter, while ye live﻿—there’s nothing like it for beer﻿—or porter, by Jove. Have you got any porter?”
“No, not any; but do, like a good old fellow, tell me anything you have picked up that concerns me﻿—there’s nothing pleasant, I know﻿—there can be nothing pleasant, but if there’s anything, I should rather have it now, than wait, be it ever so bad.”
“I wish you’d put some other fellow on this business, I know﻿—for you’ll come to hate the sight of me if I’m always bringing you bad news; but it is *not* good, that’s a fact; that beast is getting unmanageable. By the law, here comes something for a hungry fellow; thank ye, my lass, God bless ye, feeding the hungry. How can I pay ye back, my dear? I don’t know, unless by taking ye in﻿—ha, ha, ha!﻿—whenever ye want shelter, mind; but you’re too sharp, I warrant, to let any fellow take you in, with them roguish eyes you’ve got. See how she blushes, the brown little rogue!” he giggled after her with a leer, as Lilly Dogger, having placed his extemporized luncheon on the table, edged hurriedly out of the room. “Devilish fine eyes she’s got, and a nice little set of ivories, sir. By Jove, I didn’t half see her; pity she’s not a bit taller; and them square shoulders. But hair﻿—she has nice hair, and teeth and eyes goes a long way.”
He had stuck his fork in a rasher while making his pretty speech, and was champing away greedily by the time he had come to the end of his sentence.
“But what has turned up in that quarter? You were going to tell me something when this came in,” asked Charles.
“About the old soger? Well, if you don’t mind a fellow’s talkin’ with his mouth full, I’ll try when I can think of it; but the noise of eating clears a fellow’s head of everything, I think.”
“Do, like a dear fellow. I can hear you perfectly,” urged Charles.
“I’m afraid,” said Harry, with his mouth full, as he had promised, “she’ll make herself devilish troublesome.”
“Tell us all about it,” said Charles, uneasily.
“I told you I was running up to London﻿—we haven’t potatoes like these up at Wyvern﻿—and so I did go, and as I promised, I saw the old beast at Hoxton; and hang me, but I think someone has been putting her up to mischief.”
“How do you mean?﻿—what sort of mischief?” asked Charles.

===Chinese===

「厨房里原先有几只老白镴酒杯——不知哪儿去了——喝啤酒那可是好东西——白镴的，一辈子认它——喝啤酒再没有比它好的了——黑啤也是，天哪。你们有黑啤吗？」
「没有，一点没有。好兄弟，你打听到的同我相干的事，都告诉我吧——我知道不会有什么好的——不可能有什么好的；可要真有什么，我宁可现在就听，再糟也不等。」
「我倒盼着你把这差事派给别人，真的——净是我给你捎坏消息，往后你要一见我就烦了；不过事情确实不妙，这是实话；那头畜生快按不住了。嗬，给饿汉的东西来了；谢谢你，姑娘，上帝保佑你，给挨饿的饭吃。我怎么报答你呢，亲爱的？不知道啊，除非把你收留了——哈，哈，哈！——什么时候要个住处，记着；不过你精着呢，我担保，凭你那双促狭眼，哪个男人也哄不住你。瞧她脸红的，那个棕色的小促狭鬼！」他冲着她的背影挤眉弄眼地嘻笑。莉莉·多杰把他这顿临时午饭摆上桌，慌忙侧身退了出去。「一双眼睛邪门地俏，还有一口整整齐齐的小白牙，先生。天哪，我方才真没看仔细；可惜个头矮了点儿；肩膀也太方。不过头发——头发倒好，牙齿和眼睛顶要紧。」
他一边说漂亮话，一边早把叉子叉进一片熏肉，这句说完，已经大嚼起来。
「可那一头出了什么事？这饭菜进来之前，你不是有话要说？」查尔斯问。
「说那个老丘八？行，你不嫌我满嘴饭说话，等我想起来就说；不过依我看，吃东西的动静，能把人脑子里的东西全震干净。」
「说吧，好兄弟。你说的我全听得清。」查尔斯催道。
「我怕，」哈利满口食物，果然说到做到，「她要闹得邪门地凶。」
「原原本本讲给我听。」查尔斯不安地说。
「我说过要上伦敦跑一趟——怀弗恩那儿可没有这样的土豆——我果真去了，也照答应的，在霍克斯顿见了那头老畜生；该死，我看是有人在挑唆她使坏。」
「什么意思？——使什么坏？」查尔斯问。

===Original===

“I think she’s got uneasy about you. She was asking all sorts of questions.”
“Yes﻿—well?”
“And I wouldn’t wonder if someone was telling her﻿—I was going to say lies﻿—but I mean something like the truth﻿—ha, ha, ha! By the law, I’ve been telling such a hatful of lies about it myself, that I hardly know which is which, or one end from t’other.”
“Do you mean to say she was abusing me, or *what*?” urged Charles, very uncomfortably.
“I don’t suppose you care very much what the old soger says of you. It ain’t pretty, you may be sure, and it don’t much signify. But it ain’t all talk, you know. She’s always grumblin’, and I don’t mind *that*﻿—her ticdooleroo, and her nerves, and her nonsense. She wants carriage exercise, she says, and the court doctor﻿—I forget his name﻿—ha, ha, ha! and she says you allow her next to nothing, and keeps her always on the starving line, and she won’t stand it no longer, she swears; and you’ll have to come down with the dust, my boy.”
And florid, stalwart Harry laughed again as if the affair was a good joke.
“I can’t help it, Harry, she has always had more than her share. I’ve been too generous, I’ve been a d﻿—﻿—﻿d fool always.”
Charles spoke with extreme bitterness, but quietly, and there was a silence of two or three minutes, during which Harry’s eyes were on his plate, and the noise of his knife and fork and the crunching of his repast under his fine teeth, were the only sounds heard.
Seeing that Harry seemed disposed to confine his attention for the present to his luncheon, Charles Fairfield, who apprehended something worse, said﻿—
“If that’s all it is nothing very new. I’ve been hearing that sort of thing for fully ten years. She’s ungrateful, and artful, and violent. There’s no use in wishing or regretting now; but God knows, it was an evil day for me when first I saw that woman’s face.”
Charlie was looking down on the table as he spoke, and tapping on it feverishly with the tips of his fingers. Harry’s countenance showed that unpleasant expression which sometimes overcame its rustic freshness. The attempt to discharge an unsuitable smile or a dubious expression from the face﻿—the attempt, shall we bluntly say, of a rogue to look simple.
It is a loose way of talking and thinking which limits the vice of hypocrisy to the matter of religion.

===Chinese===

「我看她惦记上你了。各样的问话都问。」
「嗯——然后？」
「怕是有人在告诉她——我本想说谎话——可我是说，掺着几分实话的东西——哈，哈，哈！该死，我自己在这上头扯的谎，都装满一帽子了，搞得我自己也分不清哪是哪，头尾都颠了。」
「你是说她骂我，还是怎么？」查尔斯追问，浑身不自在。
「老丘八怎么讲你，你也不见得多在乎。话难听是肯定的，可也无关紧要。不过你知道，这不全是嘴上说说。她成天抱怨，那个我倒不在乎——她那通歇斯底里呀，神经呀，混话呀。她说她要坐马车兜风，还要御医——名字我忘了——哈，哈，哈！她说你给她的钱少得几乎没有，把她一直按在饿肚皮的边上；她赌咒再也受不了啦；你得掏腰包了，伙计。」
红光满面、膀大腰圆的哈利又笑起来，仿佛这桩事是句好笑话。
「我有什么法子，哈利，她拿的从来只多不少。我太大方了，我一向是个该死的傻瓜。」
查尔斯说得极苦，声音却平静。屋里静了两三分钟，只有哈利的眼睛盯着盘子，刀叉声和一口好牙咀焄饭菜的咯吱声，是仅有的响动。
眼见哈利眼下全副心思都在午饭上，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德预感还有更糟的，便说——
「要是就这些，倒不是什么新鲜事。这类话我足足听了十年。她忘恩、刁滑、暴戾。如今盼什么、悔什么都没用了；可天知道，头一回见那张脸，就是我的厄运临头。」
查理说着，低头望着桌面，指尖烦躁地敲着。哈利脸上浮起那副不受用的神情，有时它会盖过那张乡下脸的爽气——想把不相称的笑、可疑的神色从脸上撵走——说白了，就是一个滑头想装老实。
把伪善这门恶德只限于宗教一途——这是说话想事都欠严谨。

===Original===

It counterfeits all good, and dissimulates all evil, every day and hour; and among the men who frankly admit themselves to be publicans and sinners, whose ways are notoriously worldly, and who never affected religion, are some of the worst and meanest hypocrites on earth.
Harry Fairfield having ended his luncheon, had laid his knife and fork on his plate, and leaning back in his chair, was ogling them with an unmeaning stare, and mouth a little open, affecting a brown study; but no effort can quite hide the meaning and twinkle of cunning, and nothing is more repulsive than this semitransparent mask of simplicity.
Thus the two brothers sat, neither observing the other much, with an outward seeming of sympathy, but with very divergent thoughts.
Charles, as we know, was a lazy man, with little suspicion, and rather an admiration of his brother’s worldly wisdom and activity﻿—with a wavering belief in Harry’s devotion to his cause, sometimes a little disturbed when Harry seemed for a short time hard and selfish, or careless, but generally returning with a quiet self-assertion, like the tide on a summer day.
For my part I don’t exactly know how much or how little Harry cared for Charles. The Fairfields were not always what is termed a “united” family, and its individual members, in prosecuting their several objects, sometimes knocked together, and occasionally, in the family history, more violently and literally than was altogether seemly.

===Chinese===

伪善日日时时在假充一切善、遮掩一切恶；而那些自认酒色之徒、行径世故得出名、从不假装信教的人里头，偏有世上最坏、最卑劣的伪君子。
哈利·费尔菲尔德吃罢午饭，把刀叉搁在盘上，往椅背一靠，直勾勾地瞪着它们，嘴微微张着，装出一副痴想的神气；可任凭什么功夫也遮不全那点狡狯的意味和狡黠的闪光；再没有比这种半透明的老实面具更叫人厌恶的了。
兄弟俩就这样坐着，谁也不大看谁，表面上情意融融，心里的念头却南辕北辙。
我们知道，查尔斯是个懒散人，不多疑，倒挺佩服兄弟的世故干练——他对哈利的一片忠心，信得摇摇晃晃；哈利偶尔一小阵显得狠心、自私或漫不经心，这信念便受些惊扰，可大体总会安然回涨，像夏日的潮水。
至于哈利对查尔斯到底有几分真心，我说不准。费尔菲尔德家未必总当得起「和睦」二字；一家人各奔各的目标，有时难免撞在一起；家史上偶尔还撞得字面意义上的头破血流，实在有失体统。

## XV Harry Arrives / 第十五章 哈利到来
===Original===

Six o’clock came, and seven, and not until half-past seven, when they had nearly given him up, did Henry Fairfield arrive at the Grange.
“How does Madam Fairfield?” bawled Master Harry, as he strode across the floor, and kissed Alice’s pretty cheek. “Odds bobbins!﻿—as the man says in the playhouse﻿—I believe I bussed ye, did I? But don’t let him be angry; I wasn’t thinkin’, Charlie, no more than the fellow that put farmer Gleeson’s fippun-note in his pocket last Trutbury fair. And how’s all wi’ ye, Charlie, hey? I’m glad to see the old house is standing still with a roof on since last gale. And how do ye like it, Alice? Rayther slow I used to think it; but you two wise heads are so in love wi’ one another ye’d put up in the pound, or the cow-house, or the horsepond, for sake o’ each other’s company. ‘I loved her sweet company better than meat,’ as the song says; and that reminds me﻿—can the house afford a hungry man a cut o’ beef or mutton and a mug of ale? I asked myself to dinner, ye know, and that’s a bargain there’s two words to, sometimes.”
Master Harry was a wag, after a clumsy rustic fashion﻿—an habitual jester, and never joked more genially than when he was letting his companion in for what he called a “soft thing,” in the shape of an unsound horse or a foolish wager.
His jocularity was supposed to cover a great deal of shrewdness, and some dangerous qualities also.
While their homely dinner was being got upon the table, honest Harry quizzed the lord and lady of Carwell Grange in the same vein of delicate banter, upon all their domestic arrangements, and when he found that there was but one sitting-room in a condition to receive them, his merriment knew no bounds.
“Upon my soul, you beat the cobbler in the song that ‘lived in a stall, that served him for parlour, and kitchen, and hall,’ for there’s no mention of the cobbler’s wife, and he, being a single man, you know, you and your lady double the wonder, don’t ye, Alice, two faces under a hood, and a devilish pinched little hood, too, heh? ha, ha, ha!”
“When did you get to Wyvern?” asked Charles Fairfield, after a considerable pause.
“Last night,” answered his brother.
“You saw the old man?”
“Not till morning,” answered Henry, with a waggish leer, and a sly glance at Alice.

===Chinese===

六点过了，七点也过了，直到七点半，眼看就要把他死心不当回事了，亨利·费尔菲尔德（Henry Fairfield）才到格兰奇（Grange）。
「费尔菲尔德夫人安好啊？」哈利（Harry）少爷大着嗓门喊，一边大步穿过地板，亲了亲爱丽丝（Alice）漂亮的脸蛋。「乖乖——戏台上那人是这么说的——我恐怕方才亲了您一口，是不是？可别叫他恼了；我不是有心的，查理（Charlie），同特鲁茨伯里集市上把格里森（Gleeson）农夫的五镑票子揣进自己兜里的那位一样没心思。你一切都好，查理，嗯？真高兴这老宅子挺过了上回那场大风，屋顶还架在顶上。这地方你喜欢吗，爱丽丝？我从前觉得它闷得慌；可你们这两颗聪明的脑袋这样相亲相爱，为着做个伴，牲口圈、牛棚、饮马池也都住得下。『她甜甜的伴儿，我爱得胜过肉，』歌里唱的；这倒提醒我——这宅子能不能管一条饿汉一片牛羊肉、一大杯麦酒？我自己请自己来吃饭了，你知道，这桩买卖，有时候两头各有各的说法。」
哈利少爷是个谐谑子，带着乡下人那种笨拙的路数——惯会打趣，而最和气的玩笑，莫过于把同伴说进一桩他所谓「软乎便宜」里去：一匹有暗病的马，或一场糊涂的赌。
他那副打讲，据说包着一大团的精明，也包着几分危险的品性。
家常晚饭摆上桌的当口，老实的哈利用同样精致的风凉话，把卡韦尔（Carwell）格兰奇的爷和奶奶的家什安排一路取笑过去；待发现能坐人的起居室统共只有一间，他的乐子更是没了边。
「我的老天，你们赛过歌里那个皮匠了，『住在一个摊棚里，客厅、厨房、门厅一担当』；歌里可没提皮匠的老婆，他是个光棍儿，你知道；你们爷俩可就把奇迹翻了一倍，是不是，爱丽丝——一个风帽底下两张脸，还是顶顶挤的一个小风帽，嗯？哈，哈，哈！」
「你几时到的怀弗恩（Wyvern）？」隔了好一阵，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德（Charles Fairfield）问。
「昨儿夜里。」他兄弟答。
「见着老头儿了？」
「今天早上才见着。」亨利答，带着滑稽的斜睨，又朝爱丽丝狡黠地溜了一眼。

It was lost, however, for the young lady was looking dreamily and sadly away, thinking, perhaps, of the old Squire, not without self-upbraidings, and hearing nothing, I am sure, of all they said.

哈利又笑了。
「听过头痛的熊没有？」哈利说。「呃，他眼下就是这么一头；我把话放明白在这儿，我看他能不留给你的，一样都不会留。」
「由他去；只要留给你，哈利，我不眼红。」长子说。
「这话漂亮，说的人有福。能给杯白兰地吗？这种红葡萄酒我素来消受不了，」哈利又笑了；「再说喝它要把你喝穷。」
「我统共两瓶，在这儿搁了三年了。行，白兰地给你喝，就在这儿。」

「我来，亲爱的，在那边，我该记得，是我亲手放的。」查尔斯说着起身，从她手里接过钥匙，开了另一只柜。

and they all went, I warrant, the same road; the gilding rubs away, and then off with his head, and eat him up slops! That’s not bad cognac﻿—where do you get it?﻿—don’t know, of course; well, it *is* good.”

「这种事甭担心，哈利。」查尔斯说。
「担心！我才不怕他，从来就没怕过。」
「那你就是瞎操心。」查尔斯说。
「只是，」哈利接着说，「我不比你——我没有房子没有一块地好退；明白吗？我惹恼了他，他就把我逼到围绳上。他会当我的面把怀弗恩的门掉上，断了我的贴用，卖掉我的马，把我的名分丢给法庭和律师去撕扯；到那时候，我总不能上你这儿吃白食，你知道。」
「你永远受欢迎，哈利。」查尔斯说。
「永远。」他的妻子应和；凡属查理的人，在她这儿都有一份受欢迎的权利。
可哈利的话头直往前赶，半点不拐弯来道谢。
「你们在这儿尽够舒坦的了，瞧着吧，我可就去吹口哨吧，天老爷，到头来除出去当兵、驯烈马、砸石头，怕是没第二条道；你们这桩事里，我冒的险也够本了——必要时再冒我也愿意；可把自己也搭进牢里，那就没意思了，你知道。」

## XLVI Harry Drinks a Glass and Spills a Glass / 第四十六章 哈利饮一杯，洒一杯
===Original===

About an hour after, old Dulcibella came to the door and knocked. Charles Fairfield had slept a little, and was again awake. Into that still darkened room she came to whisper her message.
“Mr. Harry’s come, and he’s downstairs, and he’d like to see you, and he wanted to know whether he could see the master.”
“I’ll go down and see him; say I’ll see him with pleasure,” said Alice. “Harry is here, darling,” she said gently, drawing near to the patient, “but you can’t see him, of course.”
“I must,” whispered the invalid peremptorily.
“Darling, are you well enough? I’m sure you ought not. If the doctor were here he would not allow it. Don’t think of it, darling Ry, and he’ll come again in a few days, when you are stronger.”
“It will do me good,” whispered Charles. “Bring him﻿—you tire me; *wait, she* can tell him. I’ll see him alone; go, go, Ally, go.”
She would have remonstrated, but she saw that in his flushed and irritated looks, which warned her against opposing him further.
“You are to go down, Dulcibella, and bring Mr. Harry to the room to see your master; and, Dulcibella, like a dear good creature, won’t you tell him how weak Master Charles is?” she urged, following her to the lobby, “and beg of him not to stay long.”
In a minute or two more the clank of Harry Fairfield’s boot was heard on the stair. He pushed open the door, and stepped in.
“Hullo! Charlie﻿—dark enough to blind a horse here﻿—all right, now. I hear you’ll be on your legs again﻿—I can’t see you, upon my soul, not a stim a’most﻿—before you see three Sundays﻿—you mustn’t be tiring yourself. I’m not talking too loud, eh? Would you mind an inch or two more of the shutter open?”
“No,” said Charles, faintly. “A little.”
“There, that isn’t much. I’m beginning to see a bit now. You’ve had a stiff bout this time, Charlie, ’twasn’t typhus, nothing infectious, chiefly the upper story; but you had a squeak for it, my lad. I’d ’a came over to look after you but my hands was too full.”
“No good, Harry; could not have spoken, or seen you. Better now.”
“A bit shaky still,” said Harry, lowering his voice. “You’ll get o’er that, though, fast enough. Keeping your spirits up, I see,” and Harry winked at the decanters. “Summat better than that rotgut claret, too. This is the stuff to put life in you. Port, yes.” He filled his brother’s glass, smelled to it, and drank it off. “So it is, and right good port.

「哈利先生来了，在楼下，想见您，还问能不能见老爷。」
「我这就下去见他；说我乐意见他。」爱丽丝说。「哈利来了，亲爱的。」她轻轻走到病人跟前，「可你当然不能见他。」
「我必须见。」病人低声说，斩钉截铁。
「亲爱的，你撑得住吗？我看不应当。医生在这儿也不会答应的。想都别想，亲爱的瑞，过几天你硬朗些，他还会再来。」
「见他对我是好事。」查尔斯低声说。「带他来——你累着我了；等等，她可以去说。我要单独见他；去吧，去吧，艾莉，去。」
她还想劝，可看见他那张涨红、恼怒的脸，那神色警告她别再拗着他。
「杜西贝拉，你下去把哈利先生带到屋里来见老爷；还有，杜西贝拉，好心的老人家，你跟他说说查尔斯少爷有多虚弱，好不好？」她跟着她到门厅，一路叮嘱，「求他别待久。」
又过了一两分钟，楼梯上响起哈利·费尔菲尔德靴子的咔咔声。他推开房门，跨了进来。
「哟！查理——这儿黑得能把马晃瞎——好了，这就好。听说你就要下地了——天知道，我几乎瞧不见你——不等三个礼拜天过完——你可别累着自己。我嗓门没太大吧，嗯？窗板再开一两寸，介意不？」
「不，」查尔斯虚弱地说，「开一点。」

I’ll drink your health, Charlie,” he added, playfully filling his glass again.

“Very bad, Harry. Oh! God help me,” moaned Charles.

「有你说话真好。」查尔斯又喃喃道，又长叹一声。「这事在我脑子里赶不走。你会帮我吧，哈利？」

「我现在说话不中用了，哈利。下回你来，我会好些；只是，哈利，记住，想着，我在世上的日子兴许不长了；还有——我拿名誉向你担保——我当着上帝的面起誓，他会审判我的——我从没同贝尔塔结过婚。那是谎话。我早知道她早晚要给我惹祸；可那是谎话。爱丽丝是我的妻子。除了爱丽丝，我从没有过妻子，全能的上帝作证！那另一个是谎话。

Don’t you know it’s a lie, Harry?”
“Don’t be botherin’ yourself about that now,” said Harry, coldly, with rather a sullen countenance, looking askance through the open space in the window shutter to the distant horizon. “Long heads, my lad, and lawyers lear for the quips and cranks o’ law. What should I know?”
“Harry, I know you love me; you won’t let wrong be believed,” said Charles Fairfield, in a voice suddenly stronger than he had spoken in before.
“I won’t let wrong be believed,” he answered coolly, perhaps sulkily; and he looked at him steadily for a little with his mouth sullenly open.
“You know, Harry,” he pleaded, “there’s a little child coming: it would not do to wrong it. Oh! Harry, don’t you love your poor, only brother?”
Harry looked as if he was going to say something saucy, but instead of that, he broke into a short laugh.
“Upon my soul, Charlie, a fellow’d think you took me for an affidavit-man. When did I ever tell now’t but the truth? Sich rot! Of course I don’t say I love anyone, all out, as well as Harry Fairfield. You’re my brother, and I stand by you according; but as I said before, I love my shirt very well, but I like my skin better. Hey! And that’s all fair.”
“All fair, Harry﻿—I’ll﻿—I’ll talk no more now, Harry. I’ll lie down for a little, and we’ll meet again.”
Harry was again looking through the space of the open shutter, and he yawned. He was thinking of taking his leave.
In this “brown study” he was interrupted by a sound. It was like the beginning of a little laugh. He looked at Charlie, who had uttered it; his thin hand was extended toward the little table at the bedside, and his long arm in its shirtsleeve. His eyes were open, but his face was changed. Harry had seen death often enough to recognise it. With a dreadful start, he was on his feet, and had seized his brother by the shoulder.
“Charlie, man﻿—Charlie! look at me﻿—my God!” and he seized the brandy bottle and poured ever so much into the open lips. It flowed over from the corners of the mouth, over cheek and chin; the throat swallowed not; the eyes stared their earnest stare, unchanging into immeasurable distance. Charles Fairfield was among the Fairfields of other times; hope and fear, the troubles and the dream, ended.

===Chinese===

你不知道那是谎话吗，哈利？」
「现在别拿这个折磨自己了。」哈利冷冷地说，脸沉沉的，斜着眼，透过窗板的缝隙望向远处的天边。「脑瓜得放长，小子，还得请律师来对付法律的弯弯绕。我知道什么？」
「哈利，我知道你爱我；你不会任由别人信那些错话。」查尔斯·费尔菲尔德说，嗓音忽然比先前亮了。
「我不会任由别人信错话。」他答道，冷冷的，也许带气；嘴闷闷地张着，直勾勾望了他一小会儿。
「你知道，哈利，」他央求道，「就要有个小孩子了：委屈了他可不行。哦！哈利，你不疼你这可怜的、唯一的兄弟吗？」
哈利看那样子，正要说句噎人的话，却没说，短促地笑了一声。
「天知道，查理，人家还当你把我当成专管宣誓的证人了。我几时说过半句瞎话？废话！我这种人，一辈子挨的埋怨就是嘴太直、话太冲；至于疼不疼——我倒要问问，不是这份情分，我跑这儿来干什么。当然，我不会说，我爱谁爱到——爱到彻头彻尾——像哈利·费尔菲尔德该有的那样。你是我兄弟，我照兄弟的份上向着你；可像我方才说的，衬衫我爱得很，皮肉我更爱。嘿！这总不算不公道。」
「都公道，哈利——我——我现在不多说了，哈利。我躺一小会儿，咱们回头再谈。」
哈利又望着窗板的缝隙出神，打了个哈欠。他正想着告辞。
这阵「出神」叫一个声响打断。像是冷笑起了个头。他望向出声的查理；那只枯瘦的手正伸向床边的小桌，衬衫袖子里那条长长的手臂。眼睛睜着，脸却变了。哈利见过死的多了，一眼认了出来。他惊得魂飞，一跃而起，一把抓住兄弟的肩膀。
「查理，老弟——查理！看着我——我的上帝！」他抓起白兰地瓶，往那张开的嘴唇里灌了好多。酒从嘴角漫出来，淌过腮帮和下巴；喉咙咽也不咽；那双眼睛直直地膛着，一动不动，望进无边的远处。查尔斯·费尔菲尔德去了往昔费尔菲尔德们中间；希望与恐惧、烦难与一梦，都完了。

## LIII Harry Fairfield Grows Uneasy / 第五十三章 哈利·费尔菲尔德渐起不安
===Original===

A few days later Harry Fairfield rode from Wyvern into the picturesque little town of Wykeford, and passing the steep, narrow bridge, pulled up near the church, at the door of Dr. Willett. Harry had something to say to the doctor, but, like a good diplomatist, that shrewd dealer in horses preferred letting the doctor talk a bit on his own account first.
He found him in slippers and dressing-gown, clipping the evergreens that grew in front of his house, the hour of his forenoon excursion not having yet arrived.
“Woodman, spare that tree,” said Harry, quoting a popular song, facetiously.
The doctor looked up.
“And how is Doctor Willett this morning?” said Harry.
“Oh! oh! Is that you?” said the doctor, straightening his back with a little effort, for he had been stooping to his task, and old backs don’t unbend in a moment.
“Quite well, thank you﻿—so are you, I see.”
“Can’t complain.”
“And how’s the old Squire?” said the doctor.
“How’s the old house?” answered Harry; “staunch and straight, and like to stand forever. I see no change in him. And all well over at Carwell?”
“Far from it,” said the doctor.
“And who’s sick?”
“The poor young mother﻿—very ill indeed,” said he﻿—“nervous, low, and feverish, she has been, and yesterday, when I saw her, it was plainly fever﻿—quite declared.”
“What sort of fever?” asked Harry.
“Well, the nerves are very much engaged,” began the doctor﻿—
“Take care it ain’t typhus,” said Harry. “The baby ha’n’t got it, I hope?”
“No, the child’s all safe.”
“There’s typhus down at Gryce’s mill, and a child in scarlatina in the glen, I hear.”
“Is there? ha! It has been going a good deal at that side, I’m told,” said Dr. Willett. “There’s Lady Wyndale at Oulton﻿—very good-natured she seems to be﻿—wouldn’t she take the child and nurse it for a while? It’s a nice place, well enclosed, and lies high﻿—not likely to get in there. I attended a patient there in dropsy, once, when it was let, and the Wyndales away in India.”
“Ay, she’s good-natured; she’d have the mother and child together, with a welcome, but she says she won’t take no one’s babby to nurse away from its people, and she’s right, I think, so the young chap must stand his ground, and bide the fortune o’ war, you know. What time shall you be there today?” he inquired.
“Three o’clock.”

===Chinese===

过了几天，哈利·费尔菲尔德（Harry Fairfield）骑马从怀弗恩（Wyvern）出发，进了威克福德（Wykeford）那座风景如画的小镇，越过陡而窄的桥，在教堂近旁、威利特（Willett）大夫门前勒住了马。哈利有话要同大夫讲，可这位精明的马贩子深谙外交家的门道，宁可先让大夫自己絮叨一阵。
他撞见他穿着拖鞋晨袍，正在修剪房前的常青树——上午出诊的钟点还没到。
「樵夫啊，饶了那棵树。」哈利引着流行歌里的词，打趣道。
大夫抬起头。
「威利特大夫今早安好？」哈利说。
「哦！哦！是你？」大夫说，略略费了点劲才直起腰——他一直弓着干活呢，老腰板可不能说直就直。
「挺好，谢谢——您也不错，我看得出。」
「还过得去。」
「老乡绅（Squire）可好？」大夫说。
「老宅子可结实？」哈利答道；「严丝合缝，四梁八柱，像是要站到地老天荒。我看他一点没变。卡韦尔（Carwell）那边都好？」
「差得远。」大夫说。
「谁病了？」
「那位可怜的年轻母亲——病得不轻，」他说——「神经衰弱，情绪低落，还发着烧；昨天我见她，明明白白是热病——全发起来了。」
「哪种热病？」哈利问。
「呃，神经紧张得很——」大夫刚起头——
「当心别是斑疹伤寒，」哈利说。「娃娃没染上吧，但愿？」
「没有，孩子平安。」
「我听说格赖斯（Gryce）磨坊那边有斑疹伤寒，峡谷里还有一个孩子出猩红热。」
「是吗？哈！听说那一边闹得凶。」威利特大夫说。「奥尔顿（Oulton）的温代尔夫人（Lady Wyndale）——她看上去脾气好得很——把孩子接去照看一阵不成吗？那地方干净齐整，围得严实，又居高处——病气不容易进去。有一回宅子出租、温代尔家的人都在印度时，我在那儿看过一个害水肿的病人。」
「是啊，她脾气好；母子一起接去，她欢迎；可她说什么也不肯把谁家的娃娃抱离开家里人养活，我想她这话也对；所以这小家伙只好守住阵地，听天由命碰运气了，你知道。您今天几点到那边？」他问。
「三点。」

===Original===

“Very well, then, I’ll be passin’ at the mill end o’ the glen about that time, and I’ll ride up, and look in, just to hear what you have to say, and I’ll get home by Cressley Common. It will do me as well as t’other way. I turned aside a bit to reach you, and hear the news, and I must be joggin’ again. Goodbye, doctor. Is your church clock right?” said Harry, looking up at the old tower and pulling out his watch to compare.
“ ‘The clock goes as it pleaseth the clerk,’ the old saw tells us, but we all go by the clock here, and it does keep right good time,” said old Dr. Willett, with his hand over his eyes, reading its golden hands and figures, as Harry was.
“Well, then, doctor, goodbye, and God bless ye,” said Harry, and away he rode, without hearing the doctor’s farewell.
At Carwell Grange, at three o’clock, there was the gloom and silence of a sick house.
The tiptoe tread of old Dulcibella, and her whisperings at the door, were scarcely audible, and now and then a weary moan was heard in the darkened room, and the wail and squall of a little child from another room not far off.
Old Mildred Tarnley had undertaken the charge of the child, while Dulcibella, with the aid of a neighbour brought in for the occasion, took charge of the sick lady.
Before three o’clock came, to the surprise of this sad household, Harry Fairfield arrived. He did not come riding; he arrived in a tax-cart. He had got through more real work that day than many men who were earning their bread by their labour.
“Give this one a feed, Tom; and how’s all here?” said he, throwing the apron off and jumping down.
“Bad enough, I’m afraid, sir.”
“*Worse?*”
“I don’t know, sir, till the doctor comes; but can’t be no better, for I heard Mrs. Crane say she didn’t close an eye all night.”
“I hope they’re not forgetting the child in the hurry?” said Harry.
“Mrs. Tarnley and Lilly Dogger looks after it, turn about.”
“That wouldn’t do nohow, you know,” said Harry﻿—“and give her a good feed, Tom, good dog, good bone. She came at a good lick, I can tell you, up the glen. The doctor will be here soon.”
“Ay, sir.”
“Well, I’ll stay till I hear what he says; and there’s sickness in Carwell Glen here, I’m told.”
“I dessay, sir, there’s a good deal going, I hear.”
“Ye needn’t take her out of the shafts, Tom.

「好极了，那我那个钟点正打峡谷磨坊那头经过，我就骑上去望一眼，听听您的高见，再取道克雷斯利公地（Cressley Common）回家。那条道同这条一样顺。我绕了点路来寻您、听听消息，这会儿又得上路了。再见，大夫。你们教堂的钟准吗？」哈利说，抬头望了望老公塔，掣出表来对一对。
「老话说，『钟走不走，全看司事的兴头』；可我们这儿人人照它行事，它走得也确实准。」老威利特大夫说，一手搭在眼上遮光，同哈利一道辨认那金色的指针和字码。
「那么，大夫，再会，上帝保佑您。」哈利说罢拍马而去，连大夫的道别也没听见。
三点钟的卡韦尔格兰奇（Grange），满宅是病人家的阴沉和肃静。
老杜尔西贝拉（Dulcibella）踮着的脚步、她在门口的低语，几乎听不见；只偶尔从遮暗的屋里传出一声疲惫的呻吟，从不远另一间屋里传出小娃娃的啼哭和尖叫。
老米尔德里德·塔恩利（Mildred Tarnley）担起了看孩子的差事，杜尔西贝拉则请来一位邻居帮手，照看病人。
还没到三点，哈利·费尔菲尔德就到了，叫这户愁惨人家好生吃惊。他不是骑马来的；是坐一辆轻便双轮马车来的。这一天他办下的实在差事，比许多挣工钱吃饭的人还多。
「喂它一顿料，汤姆（Tom）；家里都怎么样？」他说着甩掉围毯，跳下车来。
「怕是够糟的，先生。」
「*更坏了*？」
「大夫没来，我说不准，先生；可好不了，我听克兰太太说，她一整夜没合过眼。」
「没忙中把孩子摸下吧？」哈利说。
「塔恩利太太和莉莉·多格（Lilly Dogger）轮着照看。」
「那样总归不行，你知道，」哈利说——「给它好好喂一顿，汤姆，好马，好料。它一路打峡谷上来，跑得可真欢，我告诉你。大夫就快到了。」
「是，先生。」
「好，我待到听他说完；再说，听说卡韦尔峡谷这边闹病。」
「我猜是，先生，听说闹得不少人。」
「你不用把它从辕杆里卸出来，汤姆。

===Original===

Fix her head in a halter by the gate﻿—in the ring there, if ye have a nosebag at hand﻿—and come in here. She’s as quiet as a lamb; I want to talk to you a bit. I’m goin’ to buy two or three fillies, and think of any you may have seen down about here. Old Tarnley’s in the kitchen now, is she?”
“I think she is, sir.”
“Well, think of them fillies if you can; there’s business to be done if I can get ’em to suit.”
So in marched Harry, and tapped at the kitchen window, and nodded and smiled to Mrs. Tarnley.
“So you’re all sick down here, I’m told; but sickness is better than sadness. That’s all I can say, lass,” said Harry, pacing, much in his usual way, into the kitchen, and clapping his big hand down on Mildred’s shoulder.
“Sick, sore, and *sorry* we be, sir. Your brother’s not that long buried that there should be no sadness in the Grange, his own house that was, and his widow’s that is﻿—sickness may well be better than sadness, but ’taint turn about wi’ them here, but one and t’other, both together. And that slut upstairs, Miss Dogger, if you please, out of the scullery into the bedchamber, she’s no more use to me than the cock at the top o’ Carwell steeple. I never knew such times in Carwell Grange; I’m wore off my old feet﻿—I can’t stan’ it long, and I wish twenty times in a day I was quiet at last in my grave.”
“A gruntin’ horse and a grumblin’ wife, they say, lasts long. Never you fear, you won’t die this time, old girl, and I wouldn’t know the Grange if *you* wasn’t here. ’Twill all be right again soon, I warrant﻿—no wind blows long at the highest, ye know, and we’ll hear what the doctor says just now.”
“Hoot! what can the doctor say but just the old thing? The leech to the physic and God to the cure, and death will do as God allows, and sickness shows us what we are, and all fears the grave as the child does the dark. I don’t know much good he’s doin’, or much he did for Master Charles﻿—not but he’s as good as another, and better than many a one, maybe﻿—but he costs a deal o’ money, and only Lady Wyndale came over here yesterday﻿—poorly though she is, and not able to get out o’ her coach﻿—and saw Mrs. Crane, and lent a fifty-pun note to keep all straight till the young lady, please God, may be able to look about her, and see after ’em herself, we’d a bin at a sore pinch before the week was out. Pity’s good, but help’s better.

===Chinese===

拿笼头把它的头拴在门边——就拴那个环上，手边有料袋就挂上——你进来一趟。它温顺得像只羊羔；我有话同你说。我打算买两三匹小母马，这一带你见过的马，替我留意着。老塔恩利这会儿在厨房吧？」
「我想是在，先生。」
「好，那几匹小母马能留意就留意；找得着合意的，就有买卖做。」
于是哈利迈步进去，敲了敲厨房的窗，朝塔恩利太太点头微笑。
「听说你们这儿全病倒了；可病总比愁强。我就会说这么一句，老太太。」哈利还是往常那副大大咧咧的做派，踱进厨房，一只大手拍在米尔德里德的肩上。
「病了，痛了，还*愁*着呢，先生。您兄弟入土才几天，格兰奇哪能没有愁——那是他自个儿的宅子，如今是他遗孀的——病兴许比愁强，可我们这儿不兴轮流，是两样一齐来。还有楼上那个懒丫头，多格小姐，您听听，从洗碗间一步登天进了卧房，她于我的用处，还顶不上卡韦尔尖塔顶上那只风信鸡。卡韦尔格兰奇这样的日子我从没经过；我这双老脚都站散了——撑不久了，一天里我有二十回巴望自己早些安安静静躺进坟里。」
「俗话说，打响鼻的马、爱抱怨的老婆，命都长。你别怕，这一回死不了，老太太；*你*要是不在，我都要不认得格兰奇了。我担保，转眼就又是一切如常——再高的风也刮不长，你知道；咱们这就听听大夫怎么说。」
「哼！大夫还能说出什么新花样？开药的是郎中，治病的是上帝；死亡照上帝允许的办；病来照出我们的本相；人人都怕坟，就像孩子怕黑。我不见他眼下顶什么用，也不见他当年替查尔斯少爷顶了什么用——倒不是说他就比别人差，兴许还强过许多——可他花钱海了去；若不是温代尔夫人昨天过来——她自己也不爽利，下车都下不了——见了克兰太太，借出一张五十镑的票子，撑着一切不塌，等少奶奶蒙上帝保佑，能自己起身张罗、亲自照看这些事，不出一个礼拜，我们就得捉襟见肘。怜悯是好，帮衬更好。

===Original===

’Tis well in this miserly world there’s a kind one left here and there, that wouldn’t let kindred want in the midst of plenty. There’s Squire Harry o’ Wyvern and his own little grandson lyin’ up in the cradle there, and look at you, Master Harry. I wonder you hadn’t the thought.”
Harry laughed, perhaps, the least degree awkwardly.
“Why, chick-a-biddy﻿—” began Harry.
“I’m none o’ yer chick-a-biddies. I’m old Mildred Tarnley, o’ the Grange o’ Carwell, that’s in the service o’ the family﻿—her and hers﻿—many a long year, and I speaks my mind, and I shouldn’t like the family to be talked of as it will for meanness. If there’s a want o’ money here in times of sickness, ’tis a shame!”
“Well, ye know there’s no want, but the Governor’s riled just now, and he’ll come round again; and as for me, I’m as poor a dog as is in the parish. Take me and turn me round and round, and what more am I than just a poor devil that lives by horses, and not always the price of a pot o’ stout in my pocket﻿—

> ‘Four farthings and a thimble

Makes the tailor’s pocket jingle.’

Your tongue’s a bit too hard, Mildred; but ye mean well, and there’s kindness at the bottom o’ the mug, though the brew be bitter.”
“I think I hear the doctor,” said Mildred, placing her palm behind her ear and listening.
“Ay,” said Harry; “I hear him talkin’.”
And forth he strode to meet him.
Before he went up, Harry and the doctor talked together for a little in the panelled sitting-room, with which we are familiar.
“I’m sure to see you here, eh?”
“Before I go? Yes. I shall look in here.”
“All right,” said Harry, and the doctor walked up the stairs on his exploration.

===Chinese===

这吝啬世上，还好东一个西一个留着几个厚道人，不肯眼看骨亲在丰足里受穷。怀弗恩有的是哈利乡绅，摇篮里躺着的又是他嫡亲的小孙儿，再瞧瞧您，哈利少爷。亏您连这一点念头都没动过。」
哈利笑了，笑得或许有一丝讪讪的。
「嗳，小乖乖——」哈利起了个头。
「我才不是谁的小乖乖。我是卡韦尔格兰奇的老米尔德里德·塔恩利，在这家人手底下当差——当了她、也当了她的家里人——当了不知多少年；我心里有话就直说，我不愿这家人家为着悭吝，落人闲话。病中缺钱，这是耻辱！」
「呃，你知道缺倒不缺，只是老爷子这会儿正憨着火，气头过了就回心转意；至于我，我是教区里数得着的穷小子。把我拎起来转上一百圈，我也不过是个靠马吃饭的可怜虫，兜里连一壶黑啤酒的钱都不总有——

> 「『四个铜子加一枚顶针，
> 裁缝的口袋就叮当作响。』

你这张嘴厉害了点，米尔德里德；可你是好意；这一缸酒酿得虽苦，缸底藏着的是好心。」
「我像是听见大夫来了。」米尔德里德说，手掌拢在耳后听着。
「嗯，」哈利说；「我听见他说话了。」
他大步迎了出去。
上楼之前，哈利同大夫在那间镶板起居室里谈了一小会儿——那间屋子我们已经熟了。
「待会儿准能在这儿见着您，嗯？」
「走之前？是的。我会进来一趟。」
「好。」哈利说；大夫便上楼看他的病人去了。

## XXI Harry’s Beer and Conversation / 第二十一章 哈利的啤酒与谈话
===Original===

At last Harry, looking out of the window as he leaned back in his chair, said, in a careless sort of way, but in a low tone﻿—
“Did you ever tell Alice anything about it before you came here?”
“Alice?” said Charles, wincing and looking very pale. “Well, you know, why should I?”
“You know best of course, but I thought you might, maybe,” answered Harry, stretching himself with an imperfect yawn.
“No,” said Charles, looking down with a flush.
“She never heard anything about it at any time, then?﻿—and mind, my dear fellow, I’m only asking. You know much better than me what’s best to be done; but the old brute will give you trouble, I’m afeard. She’ll be writing letters, and maybe printing things; but you don’t take in the papers here, so it won’t come so much by surprise like.”
“Alice knows nothing of it. She never heard of her,” said Charles.
“I wish she may have heard as little of Alice,” said Harry.
“Why, you don’t mean to say”﻿—began Charles, and stopped.
“I think the woman has got some sort of a maggot in her head. I think she has, more than common, and you’ll find I’m right.”
Charles got up and stood at the window for a little.
“I can’t guess what you mean, Harry. I don’t know what you think. Do tell me, if you have any clear idea, what is she thinking of?”
“I don’t know what to think, and upon my soul that one’s so deep,” said Harry. “But I’d bet something she’s heard more than we’d just like about this, and if so, there’ll be wigs on the green.”
“There has been nothing﻿—I mean no letter; I have not heard from her for months﻿—not since you saw her before. I think if there had been anything unusual in her mind she would have written. Don’t you? I dare say what you saw was only one of those ungoverned outbreaks of temper that mean nothing.”
“I hope so,” said Harry.
“I blame myself, I’m no villain, I didn’t mean badly, but I’m a cursed fool. It’s all quite straight though, and it doesn’t matter a farthing what she does﻿—not a farthing,” broke out Charles Fairfield. “But I would not have poor little Alice frightened and made miserable, and what had I best do, and where do you think we had best go?” He lowered his voice, and glanced toward the door as he said this, suddenly remembering that Alice might come in the midst of their consultation.
“Go? For the present arn’t you well enough where you are? Wait a bit anyhow.

末了，哈利仰在椅子里，望着窗外，一副漫不经心的样子，声音却压得很低——

「爱丽丝？」查尔斯一缩，脸色煞白。「这个——你知道，我为什么要提？」
「当然你最清楚；不过我想，兴许你提过。」哈利答道，伸了个懒腰，打了个不完整的哈欠。
「没有。」查尔斯低头说，涨红了脸。
「那么她从来——什么时候都没听说过？——记着，亲爱的伙计，我只是问问。该怎么办你最清楚，比我清楚得多；可那头老畜生要给你惹麻烦了，我怕是。她要写信了，兴许还要登报；好在你们这儿不订报，事情砸过来，也不至于太突然。」
「爱丽丝一点不知道。她从没听说过她。」查尔斯说。
「我倒盼着她听爱丽丝，也听得这么少。」哈利说。
「怎么，你该不是说」——查尔斯起了个头，又停住。
「我看那女人脑子里钻进了一条蛆。我看是钻进去了，比寻常的还邪乎，往后你自会知道我说得对。」
查尔斯站起身，在窗前立了片刻。
「我猜不透你的意思，哈利。我不知道你怎么想。你要是有点儿准主意，务必告诉我，她打的什么算盘？」

「一直没动静——我是说没有信；我好几个月没她的信了——打你上回见她之后就没有。她心里要真有什么反常，我想她会来信的。你说呢？我敢说你瞧见的，不过是她那类不由分的坏脾气发作一场，当不得真。」
「但愿如此。」哈利说。
「怪只怪我自己；我不是恶棍，我没存过坏心，可我是个该死的傻瓜。不过事情本身干干净净；她怎么闹，一个铜板也碍不着——一个铜板也碍不着。」查尔斯·费尔菲尔德脱口而出。「可我不愿叫可怜的小爱丽丝受惊、受罪。你看我最好怎么办？你看咱们最好上哪儿去？」说到这里他放低声音，朝门边瞟了一眼——猛然想起爱丽丝也许正撞进他们这场密谈中间来。
「走？眼下你待的这地方不是挺好？横竖再等等。

But I wonder you didn’t tell Alice; she ought to ’a known something about it﻿—oughtn’t she, before you married her, or whatever you call it.”
“Before I married her? of course,” said Charles sternly; “married her!﻿—you don’t mean, I fancy, to question my marriage?”
Charles was looking at him with a very grim steady gaze.
“Why, what the devil should I know, or care about lawyers’ nonsense and pleadings, my dear fellow; I never could make head or tail of them, only as we are talking here so confidential, you and me, whatever came uppermost﻿—I forget what﻿—I just rapped out﻿—has that Hoxton lady any family?”
“Don’t you know she has not?” replied Charles.
“I know it now, but she might have a sieve full for anything I knew,” answered Harry.
“I think, Harry, if you really thought she and I were married, that was too important a question for you, wasn’t it, to be forgotten so easily?” said Charles.
“Important, how so?” asked Harry.
“How so, my dear Harry? Why, you can’t be serious﻿—you haven’t forgot that the succession to Wyvern depends on it,” exclaimed Charles Fairfield.
“Bah! Wyvern indeed! why, man, the thought never came near me﻿—me Wyvern! Sich pure rot! We Fairfields lives good long lives mostly, and marries late sometimes; there’s forty good years before ye. Gad, Charlie, you must think o’ summat more likely if you want folk to believe ye. Ye’ll not hang me on that count, no, no.”
And he laughed.
“Well, I think so; I’m glad of it, for you know I wrote to tell you about what is, I hope, likely to be, it has made poor little Alice so happy, and if there should come an heir, you know he’d be another squire of Wyvern in a long line of Fairfields, and it wouldn’t do, Harry, to have a doubt thrown on him, and I’m glad to hear you say the pretence of that d﻿—﻿—﻿d woman’s marriage is a lie.”
“Well, you know best,” said Harry. “I’m very sorry for Alice, poor little thing, if there’s ever any trouble at all about it.”
And he looked through the windows along the tops of the tufted trees that caught the sunlight softly, with his last expression of condolence.
“You *have* said more than once, I don’t say today, that you were sure﻿—that you knew as well as I did there was nothing in that woman’s story.”
“Isn’t that someone coming?” said Harry, turning his head toward the door.
“No, no one,” said Charles after a moment’s silence.

===Chinese===

「可你竟没告诉爱丽丝，我奇怪；她本该知道一点儿——是不是？——在你娶她之前，不管你们管那叫什么。」
「在我娶她之前？当然。」查尔斯疾言道，「娶她！——你该不是要质疑我的婚姻吧？」
查尔斯直勾勾地盯着他，目光冷峻。
「嘈，律师那套屁话状纸，我知道什么、操哪门子心，亲爱的伙计；我从来理不出个头尾。只不过你我在这儿掳心窝子说话，想起什么——忘了是什么了——我就那么随口一问——那位霍克斯顿太太有亲戚没有？」
「你不是知道她没有吗？」查尔斯答。
「我如今知道了；可就我所知，她满筛子的亲戚也可能有。」哈利答道。
「哈利，你要是真以为她同我结了婚，那这个问题对你非同小可，是不是？哪能这么容易就忘了？」查尔斯说。
「非同小可，怎么说？」哈利问。
「怎么说，亲爱的哈利？嘈，你不会是当真的吧——你没忘吧，怀弗恩的承继，就系在这上头。」查尔斯·费尔菲尔德嚷道。
「哱！怀弗恩！嘈，老弟，这念头压根儿没沾过我——我图怀弗恩！纯粹的浑话！咱们费尔菲尔德家的人多半长寿，有时成家又晚；你前头还有整四十年好活呢。天哪，查理，想叫人信你，你可得编个像点儿的。这一条你栽不到我头上，不能，不能。」
他笑了。
「这就好，我也这么想；听你这么说我高兴。你知道，我写信告诉过你那件——但愿成真的事，可怜的小爱丽丝高兴得很；倘若真添了嗣子，你知道，他就是费尔菲尔德一脉又一脉的怀弗恩乡绅；要是往他身上泼疑水，那可不行，哈利。听你说那该死女人自吹的婚事是谎话，我高兴。」
「喏，你最清楚。」哈利说。「真要为这事闹出什么乱子，我只为爱丽丝难过，可怜的小东西。」
他望着窗外，目光顺着团团树冠滑过去，树冠承着软软的日光——脸上挂着他最后那副哀悼的神气。
「你说过不止一回——我不说今天——说你拿得稳——说你同我一样清楚，那女人的说法没半点儿影。」
「是不是有人来了？」哈利说，把头朝门口一转。
「没有，没人。」查尔斯静了片刻说。

“I mean marriage or no marriage, I never meant to say as you suppose﻿—I know nothing about it, whatever I may think,” said Harry, sturdily.
“You know everything that I know, I’ve told you everything,” answered Charles Fairfield.
“And what o’ that? How can you or me tell whether it makes a marriage or not, and I won’t be quoted by you or anyone else, as having made such a mouth of myself as to lay down the law in a case that might puzzle a judge,” said Harry, darkening.
“You believe the facts I’ve told you, I fancy,” said Charles sternly.
“You meant truth, I’m sure o’ that, and beyond that I believe nothing but what I have said myself, and more I won’t say for the king,” said Harry, putting his hands in his pockets, and looking sulkily at Charles, with his mouth a little open.
Charles looked awfully angry.
“You know very well, Harry, you have fifty times told me there was nothing in it, and you have even said that the person herself thinks so too,” he said at last, restraining himself.
“That I never said, by —﻿—﻿—,” said Harry, coolly, who was now standing with his back against the window-shutters, and his hands in his pockets. As he so spoke he crossed one sinewy leg over the other, and continued to direct from the corner of his eye a sullen gaze upon his brother.
With the same oath that brother told him he lied.
Here followed a pause, as when a train is fired and men are doubtful whether the mine will spring. The leaves rustled and the flies hummed happily outside as if those seconds were charged with nothing, and the big feeble bee, who had spent the morning in walking up a pane of glass and slipping down again, continued his stumbling exercise as if there was nothing else worth attending to for a mile round Carwell Grange.
Harry had set both heels on the ground at this talismanic word; one hand clenched had come from his pocket to his thigh, and from his eyes “leaped” the old Fairfield fury.
It was merely, as Harry would have said, the turn of a shilling, whether a Fairfield battle, short, sharp, and decisive, had not tried the issue at that instant.

「我知道的你全知道；我什么都告诉过你。」查尔斯·费尔菲尔德答道。

「我相信，我讲给你的那些实情，你是信的。」查尔斯疾言道。

查尔斯看上去怒极了。

他兄弟就用他赌的那同一个咒，骂他撒谎。

这句咒语一出口，哈利双脚跟砰地落了地；一只攞紧的手从兜里抽出来，按在大腿上；眼睛里「蹦」出费尔菲尔德家祖传的怒火。
照哈利自己的说法，就差一个铜板翻面——一场费尔菲尔德式的战斗，短促、狠辣、立见分晓——当时就差一点当场开了张。

I’m about the last friend ye’ve left just now, and I don’t see why ye should seek to put a quarrel on me. It’s little to me, you know﻿—no thanks, loss o’ time, and like to be more kicks than ha’pence.”
Harry spoke these words after a considerable pause.
“I was wrong, Harry, I mean, to use such a word, and I beg your pardon,” said Charles, extending his hand to his brother, who took his fingers and dropped them with a rather short and cold shake.
“Ye shouldn’t talk that way to a fellow that’s taken some trouble about ye, and ye know I’m short tempered﻿—we all are, and ’tisn’t the way to handle me,” said Harry.
“I was wrong, I know I was, and I’m sorry﻿—I can’t say more,” answered Charles. “But there it is! If there’s trouble about this little child that’s coming, what am I to do? Wouldn’t it be better for me to be in Wyvern churchyard?”
Harry lowered his eyes with his mouth still open, to the threadbare carpet. His hands were again both reposing quietly in his pockets.
After a silence he said﻿—
“If you had told me anything about what was in your head concerning Alice Maybell, I’d a told you my mind quite straight; and if you ask it now, I can only tell you one thing, and that is, I think you’re married to t’other woman﻿—I hate her like poison, but that’s nothing to do wi’ it, and I’d a been for making a clear breast of it, and telling Ally everything, and let her judge for herself. But you wouldn’t look before you, and you’re got into a nice pound, I’m afraid.”
“I’m not a bit afraid about it,” said Charles, very pale. “Only for the world, I would not have her frightened and vexed just now﻿—and, Harry, there’s nothing like speaking out, as you say, and I can’t help thinking that your opinion [and at another time, perhaps, he would have added, your memory] is biased by the estate.”
Charles spoke bitterly or petulantly, which you will. But Harry seemed to have made up his mind to take this matter coolly, and so he did.
“Upon my soul I wouldn’t wonder,” he said, with a kind of laugh. “Though if it does I give you my oath I am not aware of it. But take it so if you like; it’s only saying a fellow loves his shirt very well, but his skin better, and I suppose so we do, you and me, both of us; only this I’ll say, ’twill be all straight and above board ’twixt you and me, and I’ll do the best I can for ye﻿—you don’t doubt that?”
“No, Harry, you’ll not deceive me.”
