===Original===

I detest a chill,” said this selfish person.
At that hour no doubt Mrs. Tarnley resented this tax on her rheumatics; but though she was not a woman to curb her resentments, she made shift on this occasion; that did not prevent her, however, from giving the stranger a furious look, while she muttered inaudibly a few words.
“I’ll go with pleasure, ma’am; but I’m sure it’s all right,” she said aloud, very civilly, and paused, thinking perhaps that the lady would let her off the long walk upstairs to the front of the house.
“Very good; I’ll wait here,” said the guest, unfeelingly.
“As you please ’m,” said Mildred, and, with a parting look round the kitchen, she took the candle, and left the lady to the light of the fire.
The lady was almost reclining in her chair, as if she were dozing; but in a few moments up she stood, and placing her hand by her ear, listened; then, with her hands advanced, she crept slowly, and as noiselessly as a cat, across the floor. She jostled a little against the table at Lilly Dogger’s door; then she stopped perfectly still, withdrew the table without a sound; the door swung a little open, and the gaunt figure in grey stood at it, listening. A very faint flicker from the fire lighted this dim woman, who seemed for the moment to have no more life in her than the tall, gray stone of the Druid’s hoe on Cressley Common.
Lilly Dogger was fast asleep; but broken were her slumbers destined to be that night. She felt a hand on her neck, and, looking up, could not for a while see anything, so dark was the room.
She jumped up in a sitting posture, with a short cry of fear, thinking that she was in the hands of a robber.
“*Be* quiet, fool,” said the tall woman, slipping her hand over the girl’s mouth. “I’m a lady, a friend of Mrs. Mildred Tarnley, and I’m come to stay in the house. Who is the lady that sleeps upstairs in the room that used to be Mr. Harry’s? You must answer true, or I’ll pull your ear very hard.”
“It is the mistress, please ’m,” answered the frightened girl.
“Married lady?”
“Yes ’m.”
“Who is her husband?”
With this question the big fingers of her visitor closed upon Lilly Dogger’s ear with a monitory pinch.
“The master, ma’am.”
“And what’s the master’s name, you dirdy liddle brevarigator?”
And with these words her ear was wrung sharply.

「乐意效劳，太太；不过管保没错儿的。」她高声说，客客气气，顿了顿——兴许巴望着夫人免了她这趟上楼走到宅子前头的远路。
「很好；我在这儿等。」客人说，全无体恤。
「随您，太太。」米尔德里德说；又把厨房扫了一眼，这才端起蜡烛，把夫人掠给炉火的光。
夫人半躺在椅中，像已入眠；可不多时她便站起，一只手拢在耳侧，听了听；随后双手前探，慢慢爬过地板，无声无息像只猫。她在莉莉·多格门口的桌角轻轻一碰；随即纹丝不动地停住，无声无息把桌子挪开；门荡开一条缝；那个瘦长的灰影立在门口，听着。炉火极淡的一闪，照着这个影影绰绰的女人——那一刻，她仿佛同克雷斯利公地上那块德鲁伊灰石一样，再没有多一丝生气。
莉莉·多格睡得正沉；可这一夜，她的觉注定要碎。她觉出脖子上落了一只手，抬头望去，屋里漆黑，一时什么也看不见。
她吓得短叫一声，弹坐起来，只当落进了贼手里。
「*放*安静，蠢货。」高个子女人说，一只手滑过来捂住姑娘的嘴。「我是位夫人，米尔德里德·塔恩利太太的朋友，来这宅里住。楼上哈利先生从前那间屋里睡的是哪位太太？必须照实答，不然我扯你耳朵，狠狠地扯。」
「是主母，太太。」吓坏了的姑娘答。
「嫁了人的太太？」
「是，太太。」
「她丈夫是谁？」
问这一句时，那生客的粗手指已掐住莉莉·多格的耳朵，警告似的一拧。
「是老爷，太太。」
「老爷叫什么名字，你这个脏兮兮的小滑嘴？」
话音未落，耳朵已给狠狠拧了一把。

===Original===

She would have cried, very likely, if she had been less frightened, but she only winced, with her shoulders up to her ears, and answered in tremulous haste﻿—
“Mr. Fairfield, sure.”
“There’s three Mr. Vairvields: there’s old Mr. Vairvield, there’s Mr. Charles Vairvield, and there’s Mr. Harry Vairvield﻿—you *shall* speak plain.”
And at each name in her catalogue she twisted the child’s ear with a sharp separate wring.
“Oh, law, ma’am. Please ’m, I mean Mr. Charles Fairfield. I didn’t mean to tell you no story, indeed, my lady.”
“Ho, ho﻿—yes﻿—Charles, Charles﻿—very goot. Now, you tell me how you know Mr. Harry from Mr. Charles?”
“Oh, law, ma’am! oh, law! oh, ma’am, dear! sure, you won’t pull it no more, good lady, please﻿—my ear’s most broke,” gasped the girl, who felt the torture beginning again.
“You tell truth. How do you know Mr. Charles from Mr. Harry?”
“Mr. Charles has bigger eyes, ma’am, and Mr. Harry has lighter hair, and a red face, please ’m, and Mr. Charles’s face is brown, and he talks very quiet-like, and Mr. Harry talks very loud, and he’s always travellin’ about a-horseback, and Mr. Charles is the eldest son, and the little child they’re lookin’ for is to be the Squire o’ Wyvern.”
The interrogator here gave her a hard pinch by the ear, perhaps without thinking of it, for she said nothing for a minute nearly, and the girl remained with her head buried between her shoulders, and her eyes wide open, staring straight up where she conjectured her examiner’s face might be.
“Is the man that talks loud﻿—Mr. Harry﻿—here often?” asked the voice at her bedside.
“But seldom, ma’am﻿—too busy at fairs and races, I hear them say.”
“And Mr. Charles﻿—is he often here?”
“Yes ’m; master be always here, exceptin’ this time only; he’s gone about a week.”
“About a week, Mr. Charles?”
“Oh, la, ma’am﻿—yes, indeed, ma’am, dear, it’s just a week today since master went.”
Here was a silence.
“That will do. If I find you’ve been telling me lies I’ll take ye by the back of the neck and squeeze your face against the kitchen bars till it’s burnt through and through﻿—do you see; and I give you this one chance, if you have been telling lies to say so, and I’ll forgive you.”
“Nothing but truth, indeed and indeed, ma’am.”
“Old Tarnley will beat you if she hears you have told me anything. So keep your own secret, and I’ll not tell of you.”

若不是吓得厉害，她多半要哭出来；她只把肩膀缩到耳朵边，哆哆嗦嗦赶忙答——
「费尔菲尔德先生，真的。」
「维尔菲尔德先生有三位：老维尔菲尔德先生一位，查尔斯·维尔菲尔德先生一位，哈利·维尔菲尔德先生一位——你*给我*说清楚。」
她每点一个名字，就把孩子的耳朵又快又狠地拧一把。
「哎哟，老天，太太。求您，我说的是查尔斯·费尔菲尔德先生。我没想哄您，真的，夫人。」
「嚯，嚯——对——查尔斯，查尔斯——很好。那你说说，哈利先生同查尔斯先生，你怎么分？」
「哎哟，老天，太太！哎哟，老天！哎哟，好太太！您行行好，别再拧了，好夫人，求您——我耳朵快掉了。」姑娘喘着气说，只觉那刑罚又要开头。
「说实话。查尔斯先生同哈利先生，你怎么分？」
「查尔斯先生眼睛大些，太太；哈利先生头发色浅，脸红，太太；查尔斯先生脸是棕色的，说话轻轻的；哈利先生嗓门大，成天骑马在外头跑；查尔斯先生是长子；他们盼的那个小孩子，将来是怀弗恩的乡绅。」
审问的人在这儿又狠狠掐了她耳朵一把——兴许是无心的；因为她将近一分钟没做声；姑娘缩着脖子，头埋在肩膀间，眼睛睁得老大，直望着她猜想那位审官的脸所在的黑暗。
「那个大嗓门的——哈利先生——常来么？」床边的声音问。
「稀罕来，太太——赶集赛马忙不过来，我听他们说。」
「那查尔斯先生——他常在？」
「是，太太；老爷一向都在，就这一回不在；走了约莫一礼拜。」
「约莫一礼拜，查尔斯先生？」
「哎哟，太太——是，真的，好太太，今儿个老爷走整一礼拜。」
这里是一阵沉默。
「就这样吧。我若查出你说了谎，就拎住你后脖颈，把你的脸摁在厨房炉条上，燎个透——明白么；现在给你这一回机会，撒了谎就招，我饶你。」
「句句实话，真的真的，太太。」
「老塔恩利若听说你告诉了我什么，要打你。所以管好你自己的嘴，我不告发你。」

She saw the brawny outline of the woman faintly like a black shadow as she made her way through the door into the kitchen, and she heard the door close, and the table shoved cautiously back into its place, and then, with a beating heart, she lay still and awfully wide awake in the dark.

===Chinese===

她望见那女人魁伟的身形淡淡一团，像道黑影，摸出门进了厨房；听见门合上，桌子被小心翼翼推回原位；随后，她心口突突地跳着，一动不动躺在黑暗里，清醒得吓人。

## LXIV The March by Night / 第六十四章 夜行军
===Original===

The next day the Sergeant was away in his gig to Wyvern, a long journey, to report to the Squire, and obtain leave of absence from his duties for a day or two. He was to spend that night at Hatherton, there to make arrangements about the funeral.
It was a relief to all at Noulton Farm, I need hardly say, when the master of the house was away.
A very sad day it was for the boy; a day whose gloom was every now and then crossed by the thrill and fear of a great excitement.
As evening darkened he went out again to the garden in the hope of seeing Tom Orange. He would have liked that cheer at the eve of his great venture. But Tom was not there. Neither counsel nor encouragement to be heard; nothing but the song of the small birds among the leaves, and the late flowers, soon to close, peeping from among the garden plants, and the long quiet shadows of the poplars that stood so tall and still against the western sky.
The boy came in and had his lonely cup of tea in the “parlour,” and a little talk with the somewhat sour and sad old servant. He was longing for the night. Yearning to see Tom’s friendly face and to end his suspense.
At last the twilight was gone. The night had indeed come, and the moon shone serenely over the old gray roof and the solemn trees; over the dead and the living.
The boy lay down in his bed at the accustomed early hour. The old woman had taken away his candle and shut the door. He lay with his eyes wide open listening with a palpitating heart for every sound.
The inflexible regularity which the absent master had established in his household was in the boy’s favour. He heard the servant shut and bar the outer door at the wonted hour. He saw the boy’s candle in his window for a while and then put out. Tony was in his bed, and for tired Tony to lie down was generally to be asleep.
Peeping stealthily from his lattice he saw the old servant’s candle glimmering redly through the window on the juniper that stood near the wall in the shadow; and soon that light also disappeared, and he knew that the old woman had gone into her room. It was half-past ten. She would be asleep in a quarter of an hour, and in another fifteen minutes his critical adventure would have commenced.
Stealthily, breathlessly, he dressed. His window looked toward the ozier trees, where Tom was to await him. It opened, lattice fashion, with a hinge.

===Chinese===

第二天，军士坐上双轮轻马车往怀弗恩去了，一段长路——去向乡绅票报，顺带告个一两天假。当夜他宿在哈瑟顿，在那儿料理丧事的一应安排。
不必说，宅主一走，诺尔顿农场上下人人松了口气。
对男孩，那是愁惨的一天；这一天的阴郁里，又不时横过一阵大冒险的悸动和害怕。
暮色四合时，他又去了园子，指望见着汤姆·奥林奇。大举前夜，他很想得那一声喝彩。可汤姆不在。忠告和打气一句也无；只有叶间小鸟的歌，园圃草木间探出、不久就要合上的迟开花朵，还有白杨投在西天下又长又静的影子——那些树立得那样高、那样纹丝不动。
男孩进屋，独自在「客厅」用了茶，同那位有点酸苦哀愁的老仆人说了几句话。他巴望着夜。巴望见汤姆那友好的脸，好结果这场悬心。
暮色终于尽了。夜当真来了，月光安详地照着灰色的老屋顶和肃穆的树；照着死者，也照着生者。
男孩照惯常的早钟点上了床。老妇人端走蜡烛，带上了门。他睁着眼躺着，心突突跳着，谛听每一个声响。
不在家的主人给这宅子立下的那套铁打规律，倒帮了男孩的忙。他听见仆人照老时辰闩死了外门。他看见托尼（Tony）窗口的烛光亮了一阵，随即熄灭。托尼已上了床；而累了一天的托尼，一躺下多半就睡死。
他从花格窗里悄悄张望，看见老仆人的烛光红红地透窗映在墙边阴影里那棵杜松上；不多时那点光也灭了，他知道老妇人已进了自己房。十点半。一刻钟后她就会睡熟；再过十五分钟，他这场性命交关的冒险就要开场。
他屏着气，蹑手蹑脚穿好衣裳。他的窗朝着柳丛——汤姆约定在那儿等他。窗是花格式，装着铰链。

===Original===

Happily the night was still, and the process of preparing to descend perfectly noiseless. The piece of old rope that lay in the corner he had early fixed on as his instrument of escape. He made it fast to the bedpost, and began to let himself down the wall. The rope was too short, and he dangled in air from the end of it for a second or two, and then dropped to the ground. The distance of the fall, though not much, was enough to throw him from his feet, and the dog in the lockup yard at the other side of the house began to bark angrily. For a minute the boy gave himself up.
He lay, however, perfectly still, and the barking subsided. There was no other alarm, and he stole very softly away under cover of the trees, and then faster down the slope toward the appointed oziers.
There indeed was Tom Orange in that faint light, more solemn than he ever remembered to have seen him before. Tom was thinking that the stealing away this boy might possibly turn out the most serious enterprise he had yet engaged in.
He had no notion, however, of receding, and merely telling the boy to follow him, he got into a swinging trot that tried the little fellow’s endurance rather severely. I think they ran full three miles before Tom came to a halt.
Then, more like himself, he inquired how he was, and whether he thought he could go on fifteen miles more that night.
“Oh, yes, he could do anything that night. Quite well.”
“Well, walk a bit that you may get breath, and then we’ll run again,” said Tom, and so they set forward once more.
They had now accomplished about four miles more. The little fellow was not so fresh as at starting. A drizzling rain, too, had commenced, with a cold change of wind, and altogether the mere adventure of running away was not quite so pleasant, nor even Tom’s society quite so agreeable on the occasion, as he had fancied.
“You have done four out of the fifteen; you have only eleven of the fifteen before you now. You have got over seven altogether up to this. Not so bad. You’re not tired, youngster?”
“Not the least.”
“That’s right. You’re a good soldier. Now come, we’ll stand close under this hedge and eat a bit.”
They supped very heartily on great slices of bread and corned beef, which bore ample traces of the greens in which it had been served when hot.

===Chinese===

幸而夜里没风，下墙的准备做得悄然无声。墙角那截旧绳，他早就相中了，当逃跑的家什。他把绳拴牢床柱，顺墙往下溜。绳太短，他在绳头上悬空荡了一两秒，随后落了地。下坠不高，却也足以把他撑个趔趄；宅子那头拘留院里的狗狂吠起来。有一分钟，男孩自认完了。
可他躺得纹丝不动，吠声平息了。再没有别的动静；他借着树荫轻轻溜开，随后加快脚步，顺坡而下，奔向约定的柳丛。
微光里，汤姆·奥林奇果然在；比他记忆里任何时候都庄重。汤姆心里正想：拐走这个孩子，兴许是他干过的营生里最要紧的一桩。
可他毫无退意，只吩咐男孩跟上，便撒开大步小跑起来，把小家伙的腿脚磨得够呛。我算他们足足跑了三英里，汤姆才收住脚。
这才恢复了他平日的做派，问他怎么样，今晚还能不能再赶十五英里。
「哦，能，今晚他什么都干得动。好得很。」
「好，走一段，喘匀了，咱们再跑。」汤姆说；于是两人又上了路。
这会儿他们又赶出约莫四英里。小家伙不如起步时精神了。天上又下起毛毛雨，风头转冷；总而言之，离家出走这场冒险，同他想象的不大一样痛快，连汤姆这回做伴，也不如想象中可亲。
「十五英里你跑完四英里了；眼面前只剩十五里的十一里。到这儿你统共赶完七里。不算赖。不累吧，小伙子？」
「一点不累。」
「这就对了。是个好兵。来，紧贴这树篱站站，吃一口。」
两人大嚼了一顿：大片面包夹腕牛肉；牛肉上菜叶的印子还历历可见——热着端上来时，原是垫着菜叶的。

===Original===

“And now, boy, you must get on to Hatherton by yourself, for I’m known about here, and there’s a fair there in the morning, and there will be people on the way before light. You must go a mile beyond the town, to the George public. Mrs. Gumford keeps it, and there I’ll meet you.” Then he detailed the route and the landmarks for the boy’s guidance. “Take a drink of this,” said he, pulling a soda-water bottle full of milk out of his coat pocket.
And when he had done﻿—
“Take a mouthful of this, my hero, it will keep you warm.”
And he placed a flask of brandy to the boy’s lips, and made him swallow a little.
“And here’s a bit more bread, if you should be hungry. Good night, and remember.”
After about an hour’s solitary walking, the boy began to grow alarmed. Tom’s landmarks failed him, and he began to fear that he had lost his way. In half an hour more he was sure that he was quite out of his reckoning, and as his spirits sank he began to feel the cold wind and drenching rain more and more.
And now he found himself entering a town not at all answering Tom’s description of Hatherton.
The little town was silent, its doors and windows shut, and all except a few old-fashioned oil-lamps dark.
After walking listlessly about﻿—afraid to knock and ask anywhere for shelter﻿—worn out, he sat down on a doorstep. He leaned back and soon fell fast asleep.
A shake by the shoulder roused him. A policeman was stooping over him.
“I say, get up out o’ that,” said the imperious voice of the policeman.
The boy was not half awake; he stared at him, his big face and leather-bound chimney-pot looked like a dream.
“I say,” he continued, shaking him, but not violently, “you must get up out o’ that. You’re not to be making yourself comfortable there all night. Come, be lively.”
Comfortable! Lively!﻿—all comparative﻿—all a question of degrees.
The boy got up as quickly as the cold and stiffness of his joints would let him.
Very dutifully he got up, and stood drenched, pale, and shivering in the moonlight.
The policeman looked down not unkindly now, at the little wayfarer. There was something piteous, I dare say. He looked a grave, thoughtful man, of more than fifty, and he put his hand on the child’s shoulder.
“Ye see, boy, that was no place to sleep in.”
“No, sir, I’ll never do it again, sir, please.”
“You’re cold; you’d get pains in your bones.”
“I’ll not any more, sir, please.”

===Chinese===

「好了，孩子，到哈瑟顿这段你得自己走了；这带地方我脸熟，明早镇上又有集市，天不亮路上就有人。出了镇再走一英里，到『乔治』酒馆。掌柜的是冈姆福德（Gumford）太太，我在那儿同你碰头。」随后他把路线和地标一一交代清楚，好给孩子引路。「喝一口这个。」他说着，从外套兜里掏出一汽水瓶牛奶。
等他喝完——
「再抿一口这个，我的英雄，暖身子。」
他把一小瓶白兰地递到男孩唇边，逼他咽下一小口。
「还有一块面包，饿了吃。晚安；记住我的话。」
独自走了约莫一个钟头，男孩发慌了。汤姆交代的地标对不上号，他开始怕迷了路。再过半个钟头，他拿准自己完全走岔了；心气一沉，寒风冷雨也越觉砭骨。
这时他发现自己走进一座镇子，同汤姆描述的哈瑟顿全不像。
小镇静悄悄，门户尽闭，除了几盏老式油灯，一片漆黑。
他蔫头耷脑地转了一阵——不敢敲门，哪里也不敢开口借宿——筋疲力尽，在人家门口的台阶上坐下来，往后一靠，很快就睡熟了。
肩头一摇，把他摇醒了。一名警察正俯身望着他。
「喂，起来，别赖那儿。」警察发号施令的嗓音说。
男孩还没醒透；直愣愣望着他，那张大脸和那顶皮箍大礼帽，像一场梦。
「喂，」他接着摇他，摇得不重，「你得起来了。不能由你在这儿舒舒服服过一夜。来，麻利点。」
舒服！麻利！——全是比出来的——全是程度问题。
男孩凭着他那冻僵的关节所许可的速度，尽快爬了起来。
他乖乖地起来，湿淋淋站在月光里，苍白，发抖。
这会儿警察低头看这小小赶路人，眼色并不凶。想必有几分可怜见的。他像个持重多思的人，五十开外，把手搭在孩子肩上。
「你瞧，孩子，那不是睡觉的地儿。」
「是，先生，我再不敢了，先生，求您。」
「你冻着了；骨头要作痛的。」
「不了，再也不了，先生，求您。」

===Original===

“Come with me, my boy, it’s only a step.”
He brought the boy into his house down the lane close by.
“There’s a fire. You warm yourself. There’s my little one in fever, so you can’t stop long. Sit down, child, and warm yourself.”
He gave him a drink of hot milk and a piece of bread.
“You don’t get up, you know; there’s no need,” he added.
I think he was afraid of his pewter spoons. He kept the little fellow nearly half an hour, and he lent him an old bottomless sack to wrap about his shoulders, and charged him to bring it back in the morning. I think the man thought he might be a thief. He was a kind man﻿—there was a balancing of great pity and suspicion.
The boy returned the sack with many thanks, in the first faint twilight of morning, and set forth again for Hatherton. It was, the fellow who directed him said, still five miles on.
At about a mile from Hatherton, cold and wet, and fearing to be too early at the George Inn, the rendezvous agreed on, the tired little fellow crept in, cold and wet, to a roadside pothouse.
At the fire of the alehouse three fellows were drinking beer. Says one who had now and then had his eye on the boy﻿—
“That boy there has run away from school.”
I cannot describe the terror with which the little fellow heard those words. The other two looked at him. One was a fat fellow in breeches and top boots, and a red cloth waistcoat, and a ruddy good-humoured face; and after a look they returned to their talk; and in a little while the lean man, who seemed to find it hard to take his eyes off him, said, “That’s a runaway, that chap; we ought to tell the police and send him back to school.”
“Well, that’s no business of ours; can’t you let him be?” said the red waistcoat.
“Come here,” said the lean man, beckoning him over with his hard eye on him.
He rose and slowly approached under that dreadful command.
I can’t say that there was anything malevolent in that man’s face. Somewhat sharp and stern, with a lean inflexibility of duty. To the boy at this moment no face could have been imagined more terrific; his only hope was in his fat companion. He turned, I am sure, an imploring look upon him.
“Come, Irons, let the boy alone, unless ye mean to quarrel wi’ me; d﻿—﻿— me ye *shall* let him alone! And get him his breakfast of something hot, and be lively,” he called to the people; “and score it up to me.”

===Chinese===

「跟我来，孩子，就几步路。」
他把男孩领进巷子尽头近旁自己的家。
「有炉火。你烤烤。我小的那个在发烧，你不能久待。坐下，孩子，烤烤火。」
他给他一杯热牛奶、一块面包。
「不用起来，知道么；犯不着。」他添一句。
我看他是惦记他那些白铁勺子。他把小家伙留了将近半个钟头，又借他一条漏底的旧麻袋裹肩，叮嘱他明早送还。我看这人是防着他做贼。他是个好心人——大大的怜悯和大大的疑心，两头掂着。
清晨最淡的一线曙色里，男孩千恩万谢还了麻袋，重又上路去哈瑟顿。给他指路的人说，还有五英里。
离哈瑟顿约一英里，又冷又湿，又怕到「乔治」客栈——约定的碰头处——到得太早，累坏了的小家伙溜进路边一家小酒铺。
酒铺炉边有三条汉子喝啤酒。其中一个不时拿眼瞟男孩的开了腔——
「那孩子是从学校跑出来的。」
这话钻进小家伙耳朵里的那份恐怖，我没法描摹。另外两人朝他看。一个是胖子，马裤长靴，红布背心，红彤彤一张和气脸；看了一眼，他们又接着谈自己的；不多时，那瘦子——他的眼睛仿佛离不开男孩——说：「那小子是个逃学的；该报警察，送他回学校去。」
「嗐，那不干咱们的事；由他去不成么？」红背心说。
「过来。」瘦子说，狠眼盯着他，朝他勾手指。
他站起身，在那道可怕的命令下，慢慢挨过去。
那人的脸上说不上有什么歹意。只是又尖又峻，一副瘦硬的公事公办。可在此刻的男孩眼里，再没有一张脸比它更可怕；他唯一的指望在那个胖同伴身上。我敢说，他朝那边转过一脸哀求去。
「行了，艾恩斯（Irons），放那孩子去吧，除非你要同我吵——见鬼，你*给我*放开他！给他弄点热乎的当早饭，麻利点，」他朝店里的人喊；「账记我头上。」

===Original===

So, thanks to the good Samaritan in top boots and red waistcoat, the dejected little man pursued his way comforted.
As he walked through Hatherton he was looking into a shop window listlessly, when he distinctly saw, reflected in the plate glass, that which appalled him so that he thought he should have fainted.
It was the marble, blue-chinned face of the Sergeant-Major looking over his shoulder, with his icy gray eyes, into the same window.
He was utterly powerless to move. His great eyes were fixed on that dreadful shadow. He was actually touching his shoulder as he leaned over. Happily the Sergeant did not examine the reflection, which he would have been sure to recognise. The bird fascinated by the cold eye of a snake, and expecting momentarily, with palpitating heart, the spring of the reptile, may feel, when, withdrawing the spell, it glides harmlessly away, as the boy did when he saw that dreaded man turn away and walk with measured tread up the street. For a moment his terror was renewed, for Bion, that yellow namesake of the philosopher, recognising him, stood against the boy’s leg, and scratched repeatedly, and gave him a shove with his nose, and whimpered. The boy turned quickly, and walking away the dog left him, and ran after his master, and took his place at his side.

===Chinese===

就这样，多亏那位长靴红背心的好撒玛利亚人，丧气的小人儿得了抚慰，重新上路。
他走进哈瑟顿镇，正没精打采望着一家铺子的橱窗，忽然清清楚楚看见，橱窗玻璃里映出一样东西，吓得他险些晕过去。
那是军士长那张大理石般、下巴泛青的脸，正越过他的肩头，用那双冰灰的眼睛，望着同一扇橱窗。
他整个人钉住了，动弹不得。大眼睛锁在那可怕的影子上。那人俯身时，真的贴着他的肩。万幸军士没有细看倒影——看了一定认得。蛇的冷眼摄住的小鸟，心突突跳着，时时刻刻等着那爬虫扑来；待咒术一撤，蛇无害地滑走了——男孩看见那个可怕的人转身离开，迈着规整的步子沿街而上时，便是这般滋味。他的恐怖又惊起一瞬：比昂（Bion）——那位同古代哲人同名的大黄狗——认出了他，抵住男孩的腿，一次次蹭，用鼻子拱他，呜呜地哼。男孩急忙转身走开；狗离开他，追上主人，在他身边归了位。

## LXII The March to Noulton Farm / 第六十二章 开赴诺尔顿农场
===Original===

“I think, ma’am, the boy’s in the house. You’d best give him up, for I’ll not go without him. How many rooms have you?”
“Three and a loft, sir.”
The Sergeant stood up.
“I’ll search the house first, ma’am, and if he’s not here I’ll inform the police and have him in the Hue-and-Cry; and if you have had anything to do with the boy’s deserting, or had a hand in making away with him anyhow, I’ll have you in gaol and punished. I must secure the door, and you can leave the house first, if you like best.”
“Very well, sir,” answered she.
But at this moment came a knocking and crying from within the press.
“Oh! no﻿—’twasn’t mammy; ’twas I that did it. Don’t take mammy.”
“You see, ma’am, you give useless trouble. Please open that door﻿—I shall have to force it, otherwise,” he added, as very pale and trembling she hesitated.
Standing as he might before his commanding officer, stiff, with his heels together, with his inflexibly serene face, full before her, he extended his hand, and said simply, “The key, ma’am.”
In all human natures﻿—the wildest and most stubborn﻿—there is a point at which submission follows command, and there was that in the serenity of the ex-Sergeant-Major which went direct to the instinct of obedience.
It was quite idle any longer trying to conceal the boy. With a dreadful ache at her heart she put her hand in her pocket and handed him the key.
As the door opened the little boy shrank to the very back of the recess, from whence he saw the stout form of the Sergeant stooped low, as his blue, smooth fixed countenance peered narrowly into the dark. After a few seconds he seemed to discern the figure of the boy.
“Come, you sir, get out,” said the commanding voice of the visitor, as the cane which he carried in his hand, paid round with wax-end for some three inches at the extremity, began switching his little legs smartly.
“Oh, sir, for the love of God!” cried Marjory, clinging to his hand. “Oh, sir, he’s the gentlest little creature, and he’ll do whatever he’s bid, and the lovingest child in the world.”
The boy had got out by this time, and looking wonderingly in the man’s face, was unconsciously, with the wincing of pain, lifting his leg slightly, for the sting of the cane was quite new to him.
“If I catch you at that work again I’ll give you five dozen,” said his new acquaintance.
“Is this his?” said he, touching the carpetbag with his cane.

===Chinese===

「我想，太太，孩子就在屋里。您顶好交出来，没有他我不走。您有几间屋？」
「三间，带一间阁楼，先生。」
军士站起身。
「我先搜屋，太太；人若不在这儿，我就报警察，把他登进缉捕通告；您若同这孩子的逃走有半点干系，或出过一手把他藏匿，我就叫您坐牢受罚。我须先守住门；您愿意的话，可以先出屋去。」
「很好，先生。」她答。
可就在这时，衣橱里响起敲打和哭喊。
「哦！不——不是妈妈；是我干的。别带走妈妈。」
「您瞧，太太，这是白添麻烦。请开那扇门——不然我只好破开了。」见她脸色煞白、浑身发抖地迟疑，他又补一句。
他立得像在上官面前，笔挺，脚跟并拢，那张纹丝不变的安详脸正对着她，伸出手，简简单单说：「钥匙，太太。」
天下人心——再野再倔的也算在内——总有一个点上，命令一到，屈服随至；这位前军士长的那份安详里，正有一种东西，直通人服从的本能。
再想藏孩子已是徒劳。她心头一阵剜痛，把手伸进兜里，把钥匙递给了他。
橱门开处，小男孩缩到最里面的角落；从那里，他看见军士敦实的身影深深俯下，那张青色、光洁、刻板的脸，眯着眼细觑暗处。几秒钟后，他像是认出了男孩的身形。
「出来，小先生，出来。」来客发令的嗓音说；他手里那根手杖，梢头用蜡线缠了约莫三英寸，开始利利落落抽他那两条小腿。
「哦，先生，看在上帝份上！」玛乔丽喊，死死攝住他的手。「哦，先生，他是最温柔的小东西，叫他做什么他做什么，是天底下最亲人的孩子。」
男孩这时已爬了出来，纳罕地望着那人的脸，痛得一缩一缩，不自觉地微微抬腿——杖头的火辣，他生平头一遭尝。
「再叫我撞见你干这种事，就赏你五打。」他的新相识说。
「这是他的？」他说，手杖点了点那只提包。

===Original===

“Yes, sir, please.”
He took it in his hand, and glanced at the boy﻿—I think it was in his mind to make him carry it. But the child was slender, and the bag, conscientiously packed with everything that had ever belonged to him, was a trifle too heavy.
“Anything else?” demanded the Sergeant-Major.
“This﻿—this, God bless him.”
It was the little box with his ships.
“And this;” and she thrust the griddle cake, broken across and rolled up in brown paper, into the boy’s pocket.
“And these;” and three apples she had ready, she thrust after them.
“And ho! my blessed darlin’, my darlin’, darlin’, darlin’.”
He was lifted up against her heart, folded fast, and hugging her round the neck, they kissed and cried and cried and kissed, and at last she let him down; and the Sergeant-Major, with the cane under his arm, the carpetbag in one hand, and the boy’s wrist firmly held in the other, marched out of the door.
“That’s enough﻿—don’t follow, woman,” said he, after they had gone about twenty yards on the path; “and I’ll report you,” he added with a nod which, with these pleasant words, she might take as a farewell or not as she pleased.
She stood on the little rising ground by the hawthorn-tree, kissing her hands wildly after him, with streaming eyes.
“I’ll be sure to see you soon. I’d walk round the world barefoot to see my pretty man again,” she kept crying after him; “and I’ll bring the ninepins, I’ll be sure. Mammy’s comin’, my darlin’.”
And the receding figure of the little boy was turned toward her all it could. He was gazing over his shoulder, with cheeks streaming with tears, and his little hand waving yearningly back to her until he was out of sight. And after a while she turned back, and there was their ninepins’ ground, and the tarn, and her sobs quickened almost to a scream; and she sat down on the stone bench under the window﻿—for she could not bear to enter the dark cottage﻿—and there, in Irish phrase, she cried her fill.
In the meantime Archdale and his companion, or prisoner﻿—which you will﻿—pursued their march. He still held the boy’s wrist, and the boy cried and sobbed gently to himself all the way.
When they came down to the little hamlet called Maple Wickets he hired a boy to carry the carpetbag to Wunning, four miles further on, where the Warhampton bus passes, as everybody knows, at half-past twelve o’clock daily.
They resumed their march.

===Chinese===

「是，先生，劳驾。」
他把它提到手里，瞥了男孩一眼——我疑心他本想叫孩子自己背。可孩子瘦弱；那提包收拾得极有良心，他所有的一切家当都在里头，分量嫌重了些。
「还有别的没有？」军士长问。
「这个——这个，上帝保佑他。」
是那只装着他的船的小盒。
「还有这个；」她把掰开、卷在棕纸里的烙饼，塞进男孩口袋。
「还有这些；」她早备好的三只苹果，也一并塞了进去。
「还有，喋！我有福的宝贝，我的宝贝，宝贝，宝贝。」
他被举起来贴上她的心口，紧紧搂定；他也搂着她的脖子；两人亲了哭，哭了亲；最后她放他下来。军士长手杖夹在腋下，一手提包，一手牢牢攝着男孩的手腕，开步走出了门。
「够了——别跟着，婆娘。」上了小路约莫二十码，他说；「我还要上报你。」他一点头补充道；配上这句好听的话，这一点头，她愿意当道别也好，不当也好，随她的便。
她立在山楂树旁那块小小高地上，泪流满面，朝他的背影没命地飞吻。
「我一定很快就来看你。光着脚绕地球一圈，我也要再见我的漂亮人儿。」她一路追着喊；「九柱我也带来，一定。妈妈会来的，我的宝贝。」
小男孩越走越远的身影，也尽力朝着她。他回头张望，两颊泪水横流，小手恋恋地朝后挥，直到望不见。过了好一阵她转回身——那边是他们玩九柱的场子，还有那方小湖；她的呜咽骤然急起来，几乎成了尖叫；她在窗下的石凳上坐下——那间黑下去的村舍，她不忍进——就在那里，照爱尔兰人的说法，哭了个足哭。
与此同时，阿奇代尔同他的同伴——或者说囚徒，随你怎么叫——继续赶路。他仍攝着男孩的手腕；男孩一路低低地哭着、抽噎着。
到了那个叫梅普尔威克茨（Maple Wickets）的小村子，他雇了个男孩，替他把提包背到四英里外的温宁（Wunning）——沃勒姆普顿的班车每天十二点半打那儿过，这是人人皆知的。
他们重又开步。

===Original===

The Sergeant was a serenely taciturn man. He no more thought of addressing the boy than he did of apostrophising the cane or the carpetbag. He let him sob on, and neither snubbed nor consoled him, but carried his head serene and high, looking straight before him.
At length the novelty of the scene began to act upon the volatility of childhood.
As he walked by the Sergeant he began to prattle, at first timidly, and then more volubly.
The first instinct of the child is trust. It was a kind of consolation to the boy to talk a great deal of his home, and Tom Orange was of course mentioned with the usual inquiry, “Do you know Tom Orange?”
“Why so?”
Then followed the list of that facetious and brilliant person’s accomplishments.
“And are we to go near a place called Wyvern or Carwell Grange?” asked the boy, whose memory, where his fancy was interested, was retentive.
“Why so?” again demanded the Sergeant, looking straight before him.
“Because Tom Orange told me there’s the biggest mushroom in the world grown up there, and that the owner of the house can’t get in, for it fills up the door.”
“Tom Orange told you that?” demanded the Sergeant in the same way.
And the boy, supposing it incredulity on his part, assured him that Tom, who was truth itself, *had* told him so only yesterday.
The Sergeant said no more, and you could not have told in the least by his face that he had made a note of it and was going to “report” Tom Orange in the proper quarter. And in passing, I may mention that about three weeks later Tom Orange was peremptorily dismissed from his desultory employments under Mr. Archdale, and was sued for stealing apples from Warhampton orchard, and some minor peccadillos, and brought before the magistrates, among whom sat, as it so happened, on that occasion, Squire Fairfield of Wyvern, who was “precious hard on him,” and got him in for more than a month with hard labour. The urchin hireling with the carpetbag trudged on in front as the Sergeant-Major had commanded.
Our little friend, with many a sobbing sigh, and a great load at his heart, yet was looking about him.
They were crossing a moor with beautiful purple heather, such as he had never seen before. The Sergeant had let go his wrist. He felt more at his ease every way.

===Chinese===

军士是个安详的闷葫芦。他想不到要同男孩攀谈，正如想不到对手杖或提包致辞。他任他抽噎，不喝止，也不宽慰，只把头端得又静又高，目视前方。
终于，一路的新鲜景物，对孩童的心浮发生了作用。
走在军士身边，他叽喳起来了，起先怯怯的，后来越说越溜。
孩子的第一本能是信人。絮絮讲起家，于男孩竟是一种安慰；汤姆·奥林奇自然也被讲到了，照例附一句问话：「你认得汤姆·奥林奇么？」
「问这做什么？」
接着便数起了那位谐趣而辉煌的人物的诸般绝技。
「咱们要打一处叫怀弗恩或卡韦尔格兰奇的地方附近过么？」男孩问；凡他的想象着了迷的事，他的记性便牢。
「问这做什么？」军士又问，目视前方。
「因为汤姆·奥林奇告诉我，那儿长着全世界最大的蘑菇，把门都堵满了，宅子的主人进不了家。」
「汤姆·奥林奇告诉你的？」军士照那个腔调问。
男孩只当他不信，赌咒说汤姆本人就是真理化身，*就是*昨天亲口告诉他的。
军士不再言语；他已把这事记下一笔，要向该管的地方「上报」汤姆·奥林奇——这一点，你从他脸上丝毫看不出来。顺带提一句：约莫三个星期后，汤姆·奥林奇被阿奇代尔先生手下那些零碎差事一脚辞了，又因偷沃勒姆普顿园子的苹果等几桩小过被控，解到了治安官面前；那天坐在治安官席上的，恰好有怀弗恩的费尔菲尔德乡绅，对他「狠透了」，判他一个多月苦工。那个受雇背包的小顽童，遵军士长之命，在头里蹬蹬地走。
我们的小朋友，抽噎叹气不断，心头坠着铅，眼睛却仍在四下打量。
他们正穿过一片荒野，满野美丽的紫石南，他从未见过。军士已松开他的手腕。他觉得浑身松快了些。

===Original===

There were little pools of water here and there which attracted the boy’s attention, and made him open his box of cork boats and peep at them. He wondered how they would sail in these dark little nooks, and at last, one lying very conveniently, he paused at its margin, and took out a ship and floated it, and another, and another. How quickly seconds fly and minutes.
He was roused by the distant voice of the Sergeant-Major shouting, “Hollo, you sir, come here.”
He looked up. The Sergeant was consulting his big silver watch as he stood upon a little eminence of peat.
By the time he reached him the Sergeant had replaced it, and the two or three seals and watchkey he sported were dangling at the end of his chain upon his paunch. The Sergeant was standing with his heels together and the point of his cane close to the side of his boot.
“Come to the front,” said the Sergeant.
“Give up that box,” said he.
The boy placed it in his hand. He uncovered it, turned over the little navy with his fingers, and then jerked the box and its contents over the heath at his side.
“Don’t pick one of ’em up,” said he.
“Move half a pace to the right,” was his next order.
His next command was﻿—
“Hold out your hand.”
The boy looked in his face, surprised.
The Sergeant’s face looked not a bit angrier or a bit kinder than usual. Perfectly serene.
“Hold out your hand, sir.”
He held it out, and the cane descended with a whistling cut across his fingers. Another. The boy’s face flushed with pain, and his deadened hand sunk downward. An upward blow of the cane across his knuckles accompanied the command, “Hold it up, sir,” and a third cut came down.
The Sergeant was strong, and could use his wrist dexterously.
“Hold out the other;” and the same discipline was repeated.
Mingled with and above the pain which called up the three great black weals across the slender fingers of each hand, was the sense of outrage and cruelty.
The tears sprang to his eyes, and for the first time in his life he cried passionately under that double anguish.
“Walk in front,” said the Sergeant, serenely.
And squeezing and wringing his trembling hands together, the still writhing little fellow marched along the path, with a bitterer sense of desolation than ever.

===Chinese===

这里一汪、那里一汪的小水塘，勾住了男孩的眼睛，叫他打开软木船的小盒来看。他盘算这些黑黢黢的小旮旯里船怎么航行；终于有一塘水伏得正好，他在塘边停下，放下一只船，又一只，又一只。秒针飞得快，分针更快。
军士长的喊声远远把他惊醒：「喂，小先生，过来。」
他抬头。军士立在一小块泥炭高地上，正对着他那块大银表。
等他走到跟前，军士已把表收回，他招摇的那两三枚印章和表钥匙，在链头垂着，荡在他肚皮上。军士脚跟并拢立着，杖尖抵着靴边。
「到前头去。」军士说。
「把盒子交出来。」他说。
男孩把盒子放到他手里。他揭开盖，手指拨了拨那支小小舰队，随即把盒子连船，朝身侧的石南丛里一甩。
「一只也不许捡。」他说。
「向右挪半步。」下一道令。
再下一道令——
「伸手。」
男孩惊奇地望着他的脸。
军士的脸，不比平时凶一分，也不比平时和一分。安详之至。
「伸手，先生。」
他伸出手；手杖呼哨一声，横着斩上手指。又是一下。男孩痛得满脸涨红，打麻了的手往下沉。手杖自下而上磕上他的指节，伴着口令：「抬住，先生」；第三下又落下来。
军士有力气，腕子使得又巧。
「换一只伸出来」；同一套操练重来一遍。
两只瘦手的手指上，各自横起三道乌黑的杖痕；痛楚之上、之中，更压着一层受辱和残忍的感觉。
泪水涌上眼睛；这双重的苦楚之下，他生平头一回放声痛哭。
「到前头走。」军士安详地说。
那小小的身影还在瑟缩，把两只发抖的手攝了又攝、拧了又拧，顺着小路朝前走，心里的荒凉比先前更苦。

===Original===

The bus was late at Wunning; and a lady in it, struck by the beauty and sadness of the little boy’s face, said some kind words, and seemed to take to him, he thought, with a tenderness that made his heart fuller; and it was a labour almost too great for him to keep down the rising sobs and the tears that were every moment on the point of flowing over. This good Samaritan bought a bag of what were called “Gingerbread nuts”﻿—quite a little store; which Archdale declined leaving at the boy’s discretion. But I am bound to say that they were served out to him, from day to day, with conscientious punctuality by the Sergeant-Major, who was strictly to be depended on in all matters of property; and would not have nibbled at one of those nuts though his thin lips had watered and not a soul had been near. He must have possessed a good many valuable military virtues, or he could not, I presume, have been where he was.
Noulton Farm is a melancholy but not an ugly place. There are a great many trees about it. They stand too near the windows. The house is small and old, and there is a small garden with a thick high hedge round it.
The members of the family were few. Miss Mary Archdale was ill when they arrived. She was the only child of the ex-Sergeant, who was a widower; and the new inmate of the house heard of her with a terror founded on his awe of her silent father.
They entered a small parlour, and the boy sat down in the chair indicated by the Sergeant. That person hung his hat on a peg in the hall, and placed his cane along the chimneypiece. Then he rang the bell.
The elderly woman who was the female staff of the kitchen entered. She looked frightened, as all that household did, in their master’s presence, and watched him with an alarmed eye.
“Where’s Miss Mary?”
“A-spitting blood, sir, please.”
“Bring in supper,” said the Sergeant.
The boy sat in fear at the very corner of the table. His grief would not let him eat, and he sipped a cup of tea that was too hot, and had neither milk nor sugar enough. The Sergeant snuffed his candle, and put on a pair of plated spectacles, and looked through his weekly paper.
While he was so employed there glided into the room a very slight girl, with large eyes and a very pale face. Her hair was brown and rich.

===Chinese===

班车在温宁晚点了；车里一位夫人，见小男孩的脸又俊又愁，说了几句好话，看样子——他觉得——还对他生出一份柔情，叫他的心更满了；把那阵阵上涌的抽噎、那随时要夺眶的泪压下去，于他几乎是一桩太重的活。这位好撒玛利亚人买了一包所谓「姜饼坚果」——不小的一笔存货；阿奇代尔不肯把它们交由男孩自行支配。但我必须说一句：这些坚果，军士长日复一日、凭良心按点发给他——财物一途，这位是严格靠得住的；哪怕他薄嘴唇流出口水、四下无一个人影，他也不会偷咬其中一粒。他身上想必不乏种种可贵的军人美德；不然，我料想，他也到不了他如今的位子。
诺尔顿农场是个愁惨却不丑的地方。四围树很多，都立得离窗太近。宅子又小又旧，附一座小园子，围着一道又密又高的树篱。
家里人口寥寥。他们到时，玛丽·阿奇代尔小姐正病着。她是这位前军士的独女——军士是个鳏夫；宅子里的新住客听人说起她，心里那份惶恐，正是从他对她那沉默父亲的敬畏里生出来的。
他们走进一间小客厅，男孩在军士指的椅子上坐下。那位把帽子挂上厅里的木钉，把手杖平搁在壁炉台上，然后拉铃。
进来的是那位上了年纪的女厨，厨房阴性的全部班底。她一脸惊惶——这家人在主人面前个个如此——提心吊胆地觑着他。
「玛丽小姐呢？」
「咳血呢，先生，回您。」
「端晚饭进来。」军士说。
男孩缩在桌子顶角上，战战兢兢。悲伤压得他吃不下，只啜着一杯太烫的茶，奶不够，糖也不够。军士剪了烛芯，戴上一副镀金眼镜，看他的周报。
他正看着，一个极瘦弱的姑娘轻轻滑进屋来，大大的眼睛，极白的脸。头发是棕色，丰厚。

===Original===

The hand with which she held her shawl across was very thin; and in her pale face and large eyes was a timid and imploring look that struck the little boy. She looked at him and he at her silently; her sad eyes lingered on his face for a moment, and he felt that he liked her.
She took a chair very softly and sat down without saying a word.
In a little while the Sergeant laid down his paper and looked at her. Her large eyes were raised toward him with timid expectation, but she did not speak.
“Not well just now?”
“No, sir.”
“You take the bottle regularly?”
“Yes, sir.”
“You’ll be better in the morning belike.”
“I’m sure I shall, sir.”
He lighted a candle that stood on a side-table, and his dog Bion got up to attend him. It was a large pug-dog, gamboge-coloured, with a black nose. The boy often afterwards wished to play with Bion, and make his acquaintance. But he did not know how the attempt would be taken either by the dog or his master, and so he did not venture.
No caresses passed between the dog and the Sergeant. Each did his duty by the other, and they understood one another, I suppose, but no further signs of love appeared.
The Sergeant went out and shut the door, and the girl smiled very sweetly on the little guest, and put out her hand to welcome him.
“I’m very glad you are come here. I was very lonely. My father is gone to the workroom; he’s making an organ there, and he won’t come back till a quarter to nine. That’s an hour and three-quarters. Do you hear﻿—listen.”
She raised her finger and looked toward the partition as she spoke, and he heard a booming of an organ through the wall.
“Tony blows the organ for him.”
Tony was a little boy from the workhouse, who cleaned knives, forks, shoes, and made himself generally useful, being the second servant, the only male one in their modest establishment.
“I wish I was better, I’m so out of breath talking. We’ll be very happy now. That’s tuning the pipes﻿—that one’s wolving. I used to blow the bellows for him, but the doctor says I must not, and indeed I couldn’t now. You must eat something and drink more tea, and we’ll be great friends, shan’t we?”
So they talked a great deal, she being obliged to stop often for breath, and he could see that she was very weak, and also that she stood in indescribable awe of her father.

===Chinese===

她拢着披肩的那只手瘦极了；苍白的脸和大眼睛里，有一种怯怯恳恳的神情，击中了小男孩。她望他，他望她，都不作声；她那双忧愁的眼睛在他脸上留了一瞬，他觉得，他喜欢她。
她轻轻挪过一把椅子，一句话不说地坐下了。
过了一小会儿，军士放下报纸，朝她看。她那双大眼睛怯怯期待地迎着他，却不说话。
「这会儿又不舒服？」
「嗯，先生。」
「药水按时吃？」
「按时，先生。」
「明早兴许就好些。」
「我保准会，先生。」
他点亮小几上的一支蜡烛；他的狗比昂起身跟上去伺候。那是一只大哈巴狗，藤黄色，黑鼻子。男孩后来常常想同比昂玩玩，结识结识。可这一试探，狗怎么领情、主人怎么领情，他都不知道，于是没敢。
狗同军士之间，没有任何亲昵。彼此各尽本分，也算是彼此了解；但再深一层的爱的表示，一样也没有。
军士出去，带上门；姑娘朝小客人甜甜一笑，伸出手来迎他。
「你来了我真高兴。我先前好孤单。爸爸去工房了；他在那儿造一架风琴，九点差一刻才回来。那是一个钟头又三刻钟。你听——听。」
她说着，竖起一根手指，望向隔板；他听见墙那头传来风琴的嗡鸣。
「托尼替他鼓风。」
托尼是济贫院来的小男孩，擦刀叉、刷鞋，样样杂活都干；在这户简朴人家，他是二号仆人，也是唯一的男仆。
「我要是好些就好了，说说话就喘。往后咱们会很快乐的。那是在调管子——那一根在呜呜叫。从前是我替他鼓风的，可大夫不许了；说真的，如今也鼓不动了。你得吃点东西，多喝点茶；咱们要做好朋友，好不好？」
两人就这样谈了许多；她常常不得不停下来喘气。他看得出她十分虚弱，也看得出她对父亲怀着一种说不出的敬畏。

===Original===

But she said, “He’s a very good man, and he works very hard to earn his money, but he does not talk, and that makes people afraid of him. He won’t be back here until he comes here to read the Bible and prayers at a quarter to nine.”
So she talked on, but all the time in an undertone, and listening every now and then for the boom of the pipes, and the little boy opened his heart to her and wept bitterly, and she cried too, silently, as he went on, and they became very near friends. She looked as if she understood his griefs. Perhaps her own resembled them.
The old woman came in and took away the tea things, and shortly after the Sergeant entered and read the chapter and the prayers.

===Chinese===

可她说：「他是个很好的人，挣钱挣得极辛苦；只是不说话，这就叫人怕他。九点差一刻，他来读《圣经》和祷文，那之前不会回这屋。」
她就这么絮絮地说下去，全程压着嗓，不时侧耳听管子的嗡鸣；小男孩向她敞开了心，哭得好苦；他一路讲，她也无声地陪着落泪；两人成了极亲的朋友。她那样子像是懂得他的苦。她自己的，兴许也同这一般。
老妇人进来收走茶具；不久，军士进来，读了那一章经文和祷文。

## XXX The Old Soldier Grows More Friendly, and Frightens Mrs. Tarnley / 第三十章 「老兵」热络起来，吓着了塔恩利太太
===Original===

The “Dutchwoman” resumed in a minute, and observed﻿—
“Well, old Tarnley, there’s no good in talking where you can’t right yourself, and where you can revenge, there’s no good in talk either; but gone it is, and the doctors say no cutting, nothing safe in my case; no cure, so let it be. I liked dress once; I dressed pretty well.”
“Beautiful!” exclaimed old Mildred, kindling for a moment into her earlier admiration of the French and London finery, with which once this tall and faded beauty had amazed the solitudes of Carwell.
The bleached, big woman smiled﻿—almost laughed with gratified vanity.
“Yes, I was well dressed﻿—something better than the young dowdies and old fromps, in this part of the world. How I used to laugh at them! I went to church, and to the races, to see them. Well, we’ll have better times yet at Wyvern; the old man there can’t live forever; he’s not the Wandering Jew, and he can’t be far from a hundred; and so sure as Charles is my husband, I’ll have you there, if you like it, or give you a snug house, and a bit of ground, and a garden, and a snug allowance monthly, if you like this place best. I love my own, and you’ve been true to me, and I never failed a friend.”
“I’m growing old and silly, ma’am﻿—never so strong as I was took for. The will was ever stronger with Mildred than the body, bless ye﻿—no, no; two or three quiet years to live as I should a lived always, wi’ an eye on my Bible and an eye on my ways﻿—not that I ever did aught I need be one bit ashamed on﻿—no, not I; honest and sober, and most respectable, thank God, as the family will testify, and the neighbours; but I’ll not deny, ’twould be something not that bad, if my old bones could rest a bit,” said old Mildred.
“Ha, girl, they *shall*; your old bones shall rest, my child,” said the lady.
“They’ll rest some day in the old churchyard o’ Carwell, but not much sooner, I’m thinking,” said Mrs. Tarnley.
“Folly, folly! ole girl! you’ve many a year to go before that journey; you’ll live to see me, Mrs. Vairvield of Wyvern, and it won’t be a bad day for you, old Mildred.”
The “Dutchwoman,” or the old soldier, as they used to call her long ago in this sequestered nook, drawled this languidly, and yawned a long, listless yawn.

===Chinese===

「荷兰女人」过了一会儿又接着说——
「唉，老塔恩利，伸不了冤的地方，说也无益；报得了仇的地方，说也多余；可病是成了的，大夫说开不得刀，我这身子什么稳妥法子也没有；治不好，那就由它去。我从前爱穿戴；穿戴得也真不坏。」
「漂亮极了！」老米尔德里德失声喊，一时又燃起早年那份倾倒——当年这位高挑的美人虽已凋残，一身法兰西和伦敦的华服，曾叫卡韦尔这方僻壤惊为天人。
这位褪了色的大个子女人笑了——虚荣心一饱，几乎笑出声。
「是啊，我穿得讲究——比这一带年轻的邋遢货、老来的糟婆娘，总高出一头。我从前多会笑话她们！上教堂、赛马场，我专为看她们去。呃，咱们在怀弗恩还有好日子；那老头儿不能长生不死；他不是流浪的犹太人，离一百岁也差不多了；查尔斯既千真万确是我丈夫，你若愿意，我带你上那边去；你若顶爱这地方，我给你一所舒坦宅子，一小块地，一座园子，每月一笔安逸的贴用。我疼自家人，你对我实心，我从没亏待过朋友。」
「我一年老似一年，人也糊涂了，太太——从不曾有人想的那么硬朗。米尔德里德向来是心比身子强，老天保佑——不，不；安安静静再活两三年，照我一贯的活法过——一眼看圣经，一眼看自己的行止——倒不是我做过什么见不得人的一星半点——不，我没有；诚实，本分，顶顶正派，感谢上帝，这家人家可以作证，乡邻也可以；可我也不否认，若我这把老骨头能歇一歇，倒也不算坏事。」老米尔德里德说。
「哈，婆娘，*管保*歇得成；你这把老骨头一定歇得着，我的孩子。」夫人说。
「歇是要歇的，早晚在卡韦尔老教堂墓地里，只是怕早不了。」塔恩利太太说。
「傻话，傻话！老太太！上那条路还早着呢；你准能活着看到我当上怀弗恩的维尔菲尔德太太；那一天，于你也不坏，老米尔德里德。」
「荷兰女人」——或者说「老兵」，多年前这处僻壤上人们就这样叫她——懒洋洋拖着腔说完，打了个长长的、百无聊赖的哈欠。

===Original===

“Well, ma’am, if you’re tired, so am I,” said Mildred, a little tartly; “and as for dreamin’ o’ quiet in this world, I ha’ cleared my head o’ that nonsense many a year ago. There’s little good can happen old Mildred now, and less I look for, and none I’ll seek, ma’am; and as for a roof over my head for nothing, and that bit o’ ground ye spoke of, and wages to live on without no work, I don’t believe there’s no such luck going for no one.”
“Listen to me, Mildred,” said the stranger, more sternly than before; “is it because I don’t swear you won’t believe? Hear, now, once for all, and understand: I’ll make *that* a good day for you that makes me the lady of Wyvern. Sharp and hard I’ve been with those I owed a knock to, but I never yet forgot a friend; you may do me a service tomorrow or next day, *mind*, and if you stand by me, I’ll stand by you; you need but ask and have, ask what you *will*.”
“Well, now, ma’am﻿—bah! what talk it is! Lawk, ma’am; don’t I know the world, ma’am, and what sort o’ place it is? I a’ bin promised many a fine thing in my day, and here I am still﻿—old and weary﻿—among the pots and pans every night and mornin’, and up to my elbows in suds every Saturday; that’s all that ever came o’ fine promises to Mildred Tarnley.”
“Well, you used to say, it’s a long lane that has no turn. You’ll have a glass of this?” and she popped the brandy-bottle on the table beside her, with her hand fast on its neck.
“No brandy﻿—no nothing, ma’am, I thank ye.”
“What! no brandy? Pish, girl, nonsense.”
“No, ma’am, I thank ye, I never drinks nothing o’ the sort﻿—a mug o’ beer after washing or the like﻿—but my headache never would abear brandy.”
“Once and away﻿—come,” solicited the old soldier.
“No, I thank ye, ma’am; I’ll swallow nothing o’ the kind, please.”
“What a mule! You won’t have a nip with an old friend, after so long an absence﻿—come, Mildred, come; where’s the glass?”
“Here’s the glass, ’m, but not a drop for me, ma’am; I won’t drink nothing o’ the sort, please.”
“Not from me, I suppose; but if you mean to say you never do, I don’t believe you,” said the Dutchwoman, more nettled, it seemed, than such a failure of good fellowship in Mrs. Tarnley would naturally have warranted. Perhaps she had particularly strong reasons for making old Mildred frank, genial, and intimate that night.
“I don’t tell lies,” said Mildred.
“Don’t you?”

===Chinese===

「喏，太太，您乏了，我也乏了。」米尔德里德说，话里带刺；「至于在这世上做梦享清福，那种糊涂念头，我多年前就清出脑子了。如今老米尔德里德再碰不上什么好事，盼也少盼，求更不求，太太；至于白得一片瓦遮头，外带您说的那块地、不干活白拿的工钱——我不信天下谁有这种运气。」
「听我说，米尔德里德。」生客的口吻比先前更硬；「是不是我不起誓，你就不信？听好，就这一回，听明白：哪一天我做了怀弗恩的女主人，那一天*就*是你的好日子。该敲打的人，我下手又快又狠；可朋友，我从来没有忘过；明儿后天你兴许用得着替我出一把力，*记着*；你撑我，我就撑你；你要什么只管开口，开口就有，*什么都*成。」
「嗐，太太——呸！这叫什么话！老天爷，太太；这世道什么路数，我会不知道么？我这辈子听过的漂亮许诺多了，到如今人还在老地方——又老又乏——早晚泡在锅碗瓢盆里，每个礼拜六胳膊肘齐着皂水；米尔德里德·塔恩利从漂亮许诺里得来的，拢共就这些。」
「呃，你不是常说么，再长的巷子也有拐弯。来一杯这个？」她把白兰地酒瓶砰地墩在桌上手边，一只手死死扣住瓶颈。
「白兰地不要——什么都不要，太太，谢谢您。」
「怎么！不喝白兰地？呸，婆娘，胡说。」
「不，太太，谢谢您，那种东西我从不沾——洗完衣裳什么的喝一大杯啤酒——我这脑袋，从来受不住白兰地。」
「就这一回，破个例——来嘛。」「老兵」劝道。
「不，谢谢您，太太；那种东西我一口也不咽，您包涵。」
「好一头骡子！多年不见的老朋友，抿一口都不赏脸——来，米尔德里德，来；杯子呢？」
「杯子在这儿，太太；可我一滴也不喝，太太；那种东西我不喝，您包涵。」
「是不肯赏*我*的脸，我猜；可你若打算说从来不沾，我不信。」荷兰女人说，恼得似乎过了头——塔恩利太太这一点点不赏脸，照理值不得这么大的气。那一夜她偏要老米尔德里德放得开、热络、亲近，兴许有顶要紧的缘故。
「我不撒谎。」米尔德里德说。
「是么？」

===Original===

said the “old soldier,” and elevated the brows of her sightless eyes, and screwed her lips with ugly ridicule.
Mrs. Tarnley looked with a dark shrewdness upon this meaning mask, trying to discover the exact force of its significance. She felt very uncomfortable.
The blind woman’s face expanded into a broad smile. She shrugged, shook her head, and laughed. How odiously wide her face looked as she laughed! Mildred did not know exactly what to make of her.
“But if you did tell lies,” drawled the lady, “even to me, what does it matter, if you promised to tell no more? So let us shake hands﻿—where’s your hand?”
And she kept shuffling her big hand upon the table, palm upward, with its fingers groping in the air like the claws of a crab upon its back.
“Give me﻿—give me﻿—give me your hand, I say,” said she.
“ ’Tain’t for the like o’ me,” replied Mildred, with grim formality.
“You’d better be friendly. Come, give me your hand.”
“Well, ma’am, ’tain’t for me to dispute your pleasure,” answered the old servant, and she slipped her hard fingers upon the upturned palm of the Dutchwoman, who clutched them with a strenuous friendship, and held them fast.
“I like you, Tarnley; we’ve had rough words, sometimes, but no ill blood, and I’ll do what I said. I never failed a friend, as you will see, if only you *be* my friend; and why or for whom should you *not*? Tut, we’re not fools!”
“The time is past for me to quarrel, being to the wrong side o’ sixty more than you’d suppose, and quiet all I wants﻿—quiet, ma’am.”
“Yes, quiet and comfort, too, and both you shall have, Mildred Tarnley, if you don’t choose to quarrel with those who *would* be kind to you, if you’d let them. Yes, indeed, who *would* be kind, and *very* kind, if you’d only let them. No, leave your hand where it is, I can’t see you, and it’s sometimes dull work talking only to a voice. If I can’t see you I’ll feel you, and hold you, old girl﻿—hold you fast till I know what terms we’re on.”
All this time she had Mildred Tarnley’s hand between hers, and was fondling and kneading it as a rustic lover in the agonies of the momentous question might have done fifty years ago.
“I don’t know what you want me to say, ma’am, no more than the plate there. Little good left in Mildred Tarnley now, and small power to help or hurt anyone, great or small, at these years.”

===Chinese===

「老兵」说，瞎眼上的眉毛高高挑起，嘴唇一撇，撇出难看的讥诮。
塔恩利太太阴阴地、精明地端详这张有话的脸，想掂出这话的斤两。她浑身不自在。
瞎眼女人的脸豁然铺开一个大笑。她耸耸肩，摇摇头，笑出声来。她一笑，那张脸摊得多么可憎地宽！米尔德里德简直不知拿她怎么办。
「可你就算撒过谎，」夫人拖长了音，「哪怕是对我，那又何妨——只要你应承往后不再撒？那咱们握握手——你的手呢？」
她那只大手在桌面上蹭来蹭去，掌心朝天，手指在空中乱抓，像仰翻的蟹钳。
「给我——给我——我说，把手给我。」她说。
「我这样的可当不起。」米尔德里德答，板着正经脸。
「你还是亲善些好。来，把手给我。」
「好吧，太太，您的兴头我不便驳。」老仆人答着，把那几根硬手指搭上荷兰女人摊开的掌心；对方一把攝住，攝得热络，攝得死紧。
「我喜欢你，塔恩利；咱们说过硬话，有时还难听，可没有仇；我说到做到。我从没亏待过朋友，你自会看见——只要你*做*我的朋友；凭什么不做？为谁不做？呸，咱们都不是傻子！」
「吵架的年纪我是过掉了，过了六十还往错的那边去了些，比您猜的多；我要的就是清净——清净，太太。」
「对，清净，外加安逸，两样你都会有，米尔德里德·塔恩利——只要你别偏去同那些一心想待你好的人过不去，只要你容得下他们。是啊，一心想待你好，待你*非常*好的人，你只消容得下。别抽手，就搁在那儿；我看不见你，光对着一个声音说话，有时怪乏的。看不见，我就摸得着你，攝住你，老太太——攝得紧紧的，直到弄清咱们是什么交情。」
这一席话间，她一直把米尔德里德·塔恩利的手夹在自己两手当中，又抚又搓，恰似五十年前，乡下情郎熬到那句终身大事关头时的那副揉搓。
「您要我说什么，太太，我不比那只盘子明白。米尔德里德·塔恩利如今剩不下什么用处了；这把年纪，帮人害人，大的小的，都没多大力量。」

===Original===

“I want you to be friendly with me, that’s all; I ask no more, and it ain’t a great deal, all things considered. Friendly talk, of course, ain’t all I mean, that’s civility, and civility’s very well, very pleasant, like a lady’s fan, or her lapdog, but nothing at a real pinch, nothing to fight a wolf with. Come, old Mildred, Mildred Tarnley, good Mildred, can I be sure of you, *quite* sure?”
“Sure and certain, ma’am, in all honest service.”
“Honest service! Yes, of course; what else could we think of? You used to like, I remember, Mildred, a nice ribbon in your bonnet. I have two pieces quite new. I brought them from London. Satin ribbon﻿—purple one is﻿—I know you’ll like it, and you’ll drink a glass of this to please me.”
“Thanks for the ribbons, ma’am, I’ll not refuse ’em; but I won’t drink nothing, ma’am, I thank you.”
“Well, please yourself in that. Pour out a little for me, there’s a glass, ain’t there?”
“Yes, ’m. How much will you have, ma’am?”
“Half a glass. There’s a dear. Stingy half glass,” she continued, putting her finger in to gauge the quantity. “Go on, go on, remember my long journey today. Do you smoke, Mildred?”
“Smoke, ’m? No, ’m! Dear me, there’s no smell o’ tobacco, is there?” said Mildred, who was always suspecting Tom of smoking slyly in his crib under the stairs.
“Smell, no; but I smoke a pinch of tobacco now and again myself, the doctor says I must, and a breath just of opium when I want it. You can have a pipe of tobacco if you like, child, and you needn’t be shy. Well?”
“Ho, Fau! No, ma’am, I thank ye.”
“Fau!” echoed the Dutchwoman, with a derisive, chilling laugh, which apprised old Mildred of her solecism. But the lady did not mean to quarrel.
“What sort of dress have you for Sundays, going to church, and all that?”
“An old dress it is now. I had the material, ye’ll mind, when ye was here, long ago; but it wasn’t made up till long after. It’s very genteel, the folk all says. Chocolate colour﻿—British cashmere﻿—’twas old Mrs. Hartlepool, the parson’s widow, made me a compliment o’t when she was goin’, and I kept it all the time, wi’ whole pepper and camphor, in my box, by my bed, and it looked as fresh when I took it out to give it to Miss Maddox to make up as if ’twas just put new on the counter. She did open her eyes, that’s nigh seven years gone, when I told her how old it was.”

===Chinese===

「我要你同我亲善，就这一样；再多的我不求，通盘算来，这也不算多。嘴上的亲热话，当然不全是我要的；那叫客气；客气也不错，很受用，像夫人的扇子、膝上的哈巴狗，可真到了急处，顶不了事，斗不了狼。来，老米尔德里德，米尔德里德·塔恩利，好米尔德里德，我能指望得上你么？*十足*指望得上？」
「十足十，太太，凡属正派的差事。」
「正派的差事！是啊，当然；咱们还能想哪一路？我记得，米尔德里德，你从前喜欢帽子上缀一条好缎带。我有两根，崭新。伦敦带来的。缎带——一根是紫的——我知道你会喜欢；这一杯呢，你也喝了，讨我个高兴。」
「缎带多谢，太太，我不推辞；可酒我一口不喝，太太，谢谢您。」
「呃，这个随你。给我斟一点，杯子不是有一只么？」
「有，太太。您要多少，太太？」
「半杯。真乖。小气半杯。」她接着说，伸一根手指进去量深浅。「倒啊，倒啊，别忘了今天我赶了多远的路。你抽烟么，米尔德里德？」
「抽烟，太太？不抽，太太！老天爷，屋里没烟味吧？」米尔德里德说——她一天到晚疑心汤姆躲在楼梯底下铺上偷着抽。
「烟味，没有；我自己倒时不时抽一撮烟，大夫说非抽不可；想要的时候，还吸一口鸦片。你高兴也可以来一斗烟，孩子，别害臊。怎么样？」
「嗬，呸！不，太太，谢谢您。」
「呸！」荷兰女人冷冷一笑，笑里带嘲；老米尔德里德这才醒悟自己失了口。好在夫人无意争吵。
「你礼拜天穿什么衣裳——上教堂那些个？」
「如今是件旧衣裳了。料子您兴许记得，您那回在这儿时我就有了；做成却是好久以后。顶体面，人人都这么说。巧克力色——英国开司米——是老哈特尔普尔太太，牧师的遗孀，临走时赏我的体面；我一直收着，整胡椒、樟脑，搁在我床边的箱子里；等我取出来交麦多克斯小姐去做的时候，那新鲜劲儿，就跟才摆上柜台一样。我告诉她这料子多少年份——那都是快七年前了——她眼珠子都瞪圆了。」
「哎哟！咦！」

===Original===

I think I do remember that old chocolate thing. Why, it can’t be that, that’s twenty years old. Well, look in my box, here’s the key. You’ll see two books with green leather backs and gold. Can ye read? I’m going to make you a present.”
“I *can* read, ma’am; but I scarce have time to read my Bible.”
“The Bible’s a good book, but that’s a better,” said the lady, with one of her titters. “But it ain’t a book I’m going to give you. Look it out, green and gold, there are only two in the box. It is the one that has an I and a V on the back, *four*, the fourth volume. I have little else to amuse me. I have the news of the neighbours, but I don’t like ’em, who could? A bad lot, they hate one another; ’twouldn’t be a worse world if they were all hanged. They hate me because I’m a lady, so I don’t cry when baby takes the croup, nor break my heart when papa gets into the ‘Gazette.’ Have you found it? Why, it’s under your hand there. They would not cry their eyes out for me, so I can see the funny side of their adventures, bless them!”
“Is this it, ma’am?”
“There are but two books in the box. Has it an I and a V on the back?”
“V, O, L, I, V,” spelled out old Mildred, who was listening in a fever for the sounds of Charles Fairfield’s arrival.
“That’s it. That’s the book you should read. I take it in, and I hire all the others, and a French one, from the Hoxton library. I make Molly Jinks, the little, dirty, starving maid, read to me two hours a day. She’s got rather to like it. How are your eyes?”
“I can make out twelve or fourteen verses wi’ the glasses, but not more, at one bout.”
“Well, get on your glasses. This is the *Magazine of the Beau-Monde, and Court and Vashionable Gazette*, and full of pictures. Turn over.”
“La, ma’am, ’tis beautiful, but what have I to do with the like?”
“Well, look out for the puce *gros de Naples* walking dress, about page twenty-nine, and I’ll show you the picture afterwards. Do be quick. I have had it four years, it’s quite good though, only I’m grown a little fuller since, and it don’t fit now. So read it, and you’ll see how I’ll dress you.”
And bending her head forward and knitting her brows, she listened absorbed, while old Mildred helped, or corrected, at every second word, by her blind patroness, babbled and stuttered on with her in duet recitation.

===Chinese===

「那件巧克力色的老东西，我还真记得。嗐，总不见得是它，那都二十年了。这样，开开我的箱子，钥匙给你。你会看见两本书，绿皮烫金的书脊。你识字么？我要送你一件礼。」
「识是识，太太；只是连圣经都难得抽空读。」
「圣经是好书，那本更好。」夫人说着，嗤嗤一笑。「可我要送你的不是书。找出来，绿皮烫金，箱里就那两本。要书脊上有 I 和 V 的那本，*四*，第四卷。我这儿旁的消遣少。左邻右舍的新闻有是有，我不喜欢他们——谁喜欢得了？一窝子坏种，彼此咬来咬去；全绞死了这世界也不见得更坏。他们恨我，因为我是位夫人；于是娃娃得了哮吼我不哭，老子上了《公报》破产栏我也不心碎。找着了么？喏，就在你手底下。他们又不会为我哭瞎眼，所以他们家的热闹，我尽可以看看可笑的一面，上帝保佑他们！」
「是这本么，太太？」
「箱里统共两本书。书脊上有没有 I 和 V？」
「V、O、L、I、V。」老米尔德里德一个字母一个字母拼；她耳朵里还烧着，一心等着查尔斯·费尔菲尔德到的动静。
「就是它。你该读的正是这本。这一本我是长期订的，其余的都租，还租一本法文的，从霍克斯顿的租书铺。我让莫莉·金克斯，那个又脏又饿的小女仆，每天念两个钟头给我听。她如今倒有点听出瘾了。你眼睛怎么样？」
「戴上眼镜，一口气对付得了十二四行，再多不行。」
「那就把眼镜戴上。这是《上流社会杂志暨宫廷风雅公报》（*Magazine of the Beau-Monde, and Court and Vashionable Gazette*），插图满满。翻吧。」
「哎呀，太太，真漂亮；可这种东西，同我什么相干？」
「这样，找出那身梅紫*斜纹绸*的散步裙，约莫第二十九页，回头我再指图给你看。快着点。那身我穿了四年，还很过得去，只是人后来富态了些，如今不合身了。你念吧，念了就知道我要怎么打扮你。」
她把头俯前，眉头拧紧，听得入神；老米尔德里德便磕磕绊绊往下念，每念两个字就被那位瞎眼的恩主纠正一次、提携一把，两人竟成了二重诵读。

===Original===

“Walking dress,” said Mildred﻿—
“Go on,” said the lady, who, having this like other descriptions in that cherished work pretty well by heart, led off energetically with her lean old companion, and together they read﻿—
“A pelisse of puce-coloured *gros de Naples*, the corsage made to sit close to the shape, with a large round pelerine which wraps across in front. The sleeve is excessively large at the upper part of the arm. The fullness of the lower is more moderate. It is confined in three places by bands and terminated by a broad wristband. The pelerine and bands of the sleeves are cased with satin to correspond, and three satin rouleaus are arranged *en tablier* on the front of the skirt. The bonnet is of rice straw of the cottage shape, trimmed under the brim on the right side, with a band and *nœud* of gold-coloured ribbon. The crown being also ornamented with gold-coloured ribbon, and a sprig of lilac, placed perpendicularly. Half-boots of black *gros de Naples*, tipped with black kid.”
Here they drew breath, and Mildred Tarnley was silent for a minute, thinking how much more like a lady her mother used to dress than *she* was able, and what fine presents of old clothes old Mrs. Fairfield used to send her now and then from Wyvern. For a moment an air of dignity, a sense of feminine vanity, showed itself in the face and mien of Mrs. Tarnley.
“That rice straw bonnet, with the gold-coloured *nœud*, of course I haven’t got, nor the *gros de Naples’* boots﻿—they’re gone, of course, long ago; but it reads best, altogether, and I hadn’t the heart to stop you, nor you to stop reading till we got to the end. And look at the pictures, you’ll easily find it; and I’ll write and have the pelisse sent here by the day-coach. It will be here on Sunday. Do you like it?”
“It is a bit too fine for me, I’m afraid,” said Mildred, smiling in spite of herself, with a grim elation; “my poor mother used to dress herself grand enough, in her day, and keep me handsome also when I was a young thing.

===Chinese===

「散步裙。」米尔德里德念——
「往下念。」夫人说；这部心爱的书里，这段同别段一样，她早已烂熟，便领起头，劲头十足，带着干瘦的老同伴一齐念——
「一件梅紫色*斜纹绸*长外套，紧贴身裁的上身，配一大幅圆形披肩式领，胸前交叠裹拢。袖子上臂处极宽，下臂稍稍收小，三处束带收拢，末了是一道宽袖口。披肩领同袖带镶缘同色缎子，裙前再以三条缎带滚边*作围裙式*排布。帽子为米色草编村舍式，右侧帽檐下缀金棕色缎带一道、*蝴蝶结*一枚；帽顶亦以金棕缎带装点，另添紫丁香一枝，直立而插。半筒靴为黑色*斜纹绸*，包黑羊皮头。」
念到这里两人换气；米尔德里德·塔恩利静了一分钟，想着她母亲当年穿得多么像个贵妇——远非她本人办得到——又想着老费尔菲尔德太太从前隔三差五从怀弗恩送来的那些体面旧衣。有那么一刻，塔恩利太太的脸色做派里，透出一派尊严，一份女人的虚荣。
「那顶米草帽，带金棕*蝴蝶结*的，我自然早没有了；*斜纹绸*靴子也没有——早没了，可不。可这一段念起来最过瘾，我不忍打断你，你也不忍停，一路念到了底。图你去看，一找就着；我这就写信，叫日班车把那件外套送来。礼拜天就到。你喜欢么？」
「只怕我穿太好了点儿。」米尔德里德说，不由自主笑了，笑得阴沉沉地得意；「我可怜的母亲当年也把自己穿得够阔气，我小时也把我拾掇得漂漂亮亮。

===Original===

But since the ladies come no more to Carwell the Grange has been a dull place, and gives a body enough to do to live, and little thought o’ fine dresses, and few to see them, except o’ Sundays, if ’twas here; not but ’twould be more for the credit o’ the family if old Mildred Tarnley, that’s known down here for housekeeper at the Grange of Carwell, wasn’t turned out quite so poor and dowdy, and seeing them taking the wall o’ me, which their mothers used to courtesy to mine, at church and market, and come up here to the Grange as humble as you please, when money was stirring at Carwell, and I, young as I was, thought more on, a deal more, than the best o’ them.”
“I drink your health, Mildred; as you won’t pledge me, I do it alone.”
“I thank ye, ma’am.”
“Ha, yes, that does me good; I’m tired to death, Mildred.”
“There’s two on us so, ma’am; shall I get you to bed, please?”
“In a minute; give me your hand again, girl; come, come, come﻿—yes, I have it. I think you are more friendly, eh? I *think* so; but the little goodwill I ever show you now is *nothing* to what I mean for you when I come to Wyvern﻿—nothing.”
And she strengthened the present assurance with an oath, and grasped Mildred’s hard brown hand very tight.
“And you’ll be kind to me, Mildred, when I want it; and I *shall* want it, mind, and I’ll never forget it to you; ’twill be the making of you. I’ll show you how much I trust you, for I’ll put myself in your power.”
And, hereupon, she shook her hand harder. Her face and manner were changed, and she looked horribly frightened for some minutes.
“I don’t blame you, Mildred, but this thing must not go on﻿—it must not be.”
Mildred in her own way looked disconcerted and even agitated at this odd speech. She screwed her mouth sharply to one side, and with her brow knit had turned a frightened gaze on her visitor.
“There’s things as can’t be undone, and things as can,” said she, after a pause oracularly; “best not meddle or make﻿—worms that is, and dust that will be, and God over all.”
“God over all, why not?” repeated the old soldier vaguely, and stood up suddenly with a kind of terrified shudder, “take me, hold me, quick.”
“A fit? La bless us,” cried Tarnley, seizing her in her lean arms.
The lady answered nothing, but grasped her fast by the wrist and shoulder, and so she stood for a time shuddering and swaying.

===Chinese===

「可自打太太小姐们不再登卡韦尔的门，格兰奇就成了个闷地方，糊口就够人忙的，讲究穿戴的心思少了，看的人也少——真置办了，也就礼拜天露露；不过话说回来，老米尔德里德·塔恩利，这一带谁不知是卡韦尔格兰奇的管家，若到头来落得又穷又寒碜，这家人的体面上也说不过去；如今瞧那些人在教堂、在集市上抢我的道——她们的娘当年见了我的娘可是要屈膝的——瞧她们如今那副神气；想当年卡韦尔有钱使的时候，她们上格兰奇来，谦卑得你别提；而我呢，年纪虽小，心里的见识，比她们顶好的那位还强，强得远。」
「我敬你健康，米尔德里德；你不肯陪我，我一个人喝。」
「谢谢您，太太。」
「哈，这一口受用；我累死了，米尔德里德。」
「咱们俩都累，太太；伺候您上床好么？」
「这就来；再把手给我，婆娘；来，来，来——嗯，接着了。我觉得你亲善些了，嗯？我*觉得*是；可我如今给你这点好脸色，同我到了怀弗恩以后为你备下的相比——不算什么，什么都不算。」
她又赌了个咒，给这句许诺上牢，把米尔德里德那只粗糙的棕手攝得死紧。
「等我用得着的时候，你也要好好待我，米尔德里德；我*一定*用得着，记住；这份情我永世不忘；那是你发家的日子。我让你瞧瞧我信你信到什么地步——我要把我这条命，放到你手里。」
说着，她把手攝得更狠了。她的脸色和做派都变了，有好几分钟，看上去怕得吓人。
「我不怪你，米尔德里德；可这件事不能再下去——万万不能。」
这句没头没尾的话，说得米尔德里德也自乱了方寸，甚至发起慌来。她把嘴狠狠拧向一边，眉头紧锁，惊惺地瞪着她的客人。
「有的事挽不回，有的事挽得回。」她顿了顿，像神谕似的说；「顶好是不插手、不搅和——如今是虫，往后是尘土，上帝在万物之上。」
「上帝在万物之上——为什么不？」老兵恍恍惚惚地重复，猛地站起，带着一阵吓人的哆嗦，「扶我，抱住我，快。」
「发病了？老天爷保佑。」塔恩利喊着，用两条精瘦胳膊架住她。
那夫人一言不答，只死死抓住她的手腕和肩膀；就这么立了一阵，抖着，晃着。

===Original===

“Better at last,” she said, “a little﻿—put me in the chair.”
And she made a great shuddering sigh or two, and called for water and “hartshorn,” and the hysteria subsided. And now she seemed overpowered with languor, and answered faintly and in monosyllables to old Mrs. Tarnley’s uncomfortable inquiries.
“Now I shall get a sleep,” she said at last, in low drowsy tones, interrupted with heavy sighs, and she looked so ill that old Mildred more than ever wished her back again at Hoxton Old Town.
“Help me to my bed﻿—support me﻿—get off my things,” she moaned and mumbled, and at last lay down with a great groaning sigh.
“What am I to do with her now?” thought Mrs. Tarnley, who was doubtful whether in this state she could be safely left to herself.
But the patient set her at ease upon the point.
“Get your ear down,” she whispered, “near, *near*﻿—you need not stay any longer﻿—only﻿—one thing﻿—the closet with the long row of pegs and the three presses in it, that lies between *her* room and mine, I remember it well﻿—it isn’t open﻿—I shouldn’t like her to find me here.”
“No, ma’am, it ain’t open, the doors were papered over, this room and hers, as I told you, when the rooms was done up.”
The old soldier sighed and whispered﻿—
“My head is very bad, make no noise, dear, don’t move the tray, don’t touch anything﻿—leave me to myself, and I’ll sleep till eleven o’clock tomorrow morning; but go out softly, and then, no noise, for my sleep,” groaned this huge woman, “is a bird’s sleep﻿—a bird’s sleep, and a pin dropping wakes me, a mouse stirring wakes me﻿—oh﻿—oh﻿—oh. That’s all.” Glad to be dismissed on these easy terms, Mildred Tarnley bid her softly good night, having left her basket with her sal volatile, and all other comforts, on the table at her bedside.
And so, softly she stole on tiptoe out of the room, and closed her door, waiting for a moment to clear her head, and be quite sure that the “Dutchwoman,” whom they very much hated and feared, was actually established in her bedroom at Carwell Grange.

===Chinese===

「好些了，」她说，「好一点了——扶我坐椅子上。」
她长长地打着颤叹了两声，要水，要「鹿角精」；那阵癔症退了。这会儿她像被倦怠整个压倒，塔恩利太太忐忑地问这问那，她只单字单字地虚应。
「这会儿能睡了。」她终于说，声音低而昏沉，隔几口一声重叹；那模样病得这样重，老米尔德里德比先前更巴望她回霍克斯顿老城去了。
「扶我上床——撑住我——宽了我的衣裳。」她呻吟着咕哝着，终于带着一声长叹躺下了。
「这会儿拿她怎么办？」塔恩利太太想；这副光景掠下她一个人，稳不稳当，她拿不准。
倒是病人自己把这一层替她安了心。
「把耳朵凑下来，」她悄声道，「近些，*再近些*——你不必再守着了——只有——一件事——那间挂衣房，里头一长排挂钩、三口衣橱，夹在*她*屋同我屋中间，我记得清楚——它没开过——我不愿叫她撞见我在这儿。」
「没开，太太；两间屋翻修那会儿，这间同她那间的门都封上糊了纸，我告诉过您。」
老兵叹一声，悄声道——
「我头疼得凶，别出声，亲爱的，别动托盘，别碰任何东西——让我自己待着，我一觉能睡到明天上午十一点；可你出去要轻，往后也别出声，因为我这一觉，」这大块头女人呻吟着说，「是鸟儿觉——鸟儿觉；一根针落地也惊我，一只耗子动弹也惊我——哦——哦——哦。就这样。」这样轻易得了退令，米尔德里德·塔恩利软声道了晚安，把她那篮碳酸铵挥发药水和一应舒服物件，留在了床头的桌上。
就这样，她踮着脚轻轻溜出房间，掩上门，立了一刻，定一定神，好叫自己确信：那个他们又恨又怕的「荷兰女人」，当真就安顿在了卡韦尔格兰奇她这间卧房里。

## IV The Old Squire and Alice Maybell / 第四章 老乡绅与爱丽丝·梅贝尔
===Original===

Wyvern is a very pretty old house. It is built of a light gray stone, in the later Tudor style. A portion of it is overgrown with thick ivy. It stands not far away from the high road, among grand old trees, and is one of the most interesting features in a richly-wooded landscape, that rises into little hills, and, breaking into rocky and forest-darkened glens, and sometimes into dimpling hollows, where the cattle pasture beside pleasant brooks, presents one of the prettiest countries to be found in England.
The old squire, Henry Fairfield, has seen his summer and his autumn days out. It is winter with him now.
He is not a pleasant picture of an English squire, but such, nevertheless, as the old portraits on the walls of Wyvern here and there testify, the family of Fairfield have occasionally turned out.
He is not cheery nor kindly. Bleak, dark, and austere as a northern winter, is the age of that gaunt old man.
He is too proud to grumble, and never asked anyone for sympathy. But it is plain that he parts with his strength and his pleasures bitterly. Of course, seeing the old churchyard, down in the hollow at the left, as he stands of an evening on the steps, thoughts will strike him. He does not acquiesce in death. He resents the order of things. But he keeps his repinings to himself, and retaliates his mortification on the people about him.
Though his hair is snowy, and his shoulders stooped, there is that in his length of bone and his stature that accords with the tradition of his early prowess and activity.
He has long been a widower﻿—fully thirty years. He has two sons, and no daughter. Two sons whom he does not much trust﻿—neither of them young﻿—Charles and Henry.
By no means young are they. The elder, now forty-three, the younger only a year or two less. Charles has led a wandering life, and tried a good many things. He had been fond of play, and other expensive follies. He had sobered, however, people thought, and it might be his mission, notwithstanding his wild and wasteful young days, to pay off the debts of the estate.
Henry, the younger son, a shrewd dealer in horses, liked being king of his company, condescended to strong ale, made love to the barmaid at the “George,” in the little town of Wyvern, and affected the conversation of dog-fanciers, horse-jockeys, wrestlers, and similar celebrities.

===Chinese===

怀弗恩（Wyvern）是一座非常漂亮的老宅。浅灰色石头砌的，晚期都铎式样。一部分墙体覆满厚厚的常春藤。它离大路不远，立在一片堂皇的老树中间，是这一带林木丰茂的风景里最耐看的一景：地势隆起成一座座小丘，裂出一道道岩石嶙峋、林荫昏暗的峡谷，有时又凹成一窝窝浅浅的洼地，牛群在悦人的溪边吃草——英格兰数得着的秀美乡野，这里便是一份。
老乡绅亨利·费尔菲尔德，夏天和秋天都过尽了。他如今是冬天。
他不是英格兰乡绅的一幅讨喜画像；可怀弗恩墙上四处挂着的老画像可以作证：费尔菲尔德家偶尔就出这一号人。
他不快活，也不慈和。这位枯瘦老人的晚境，正如北国的冬天：荒寒，阴沉，凛冽。
他骄傲得不屑抱怨，从没向人讨过怜悯。可他同自己的力气、自己的享乐分手，分明分得咬牙切齿。傍晚立在台阶上，望见左下方洼地里那片老墓地，念头自然要袭上来。他不肯向死亡低头。他怨这天地的章程。可他把怨气闷在肚里，转头把这一腔挫败，发泄到左右人身上。
他头发已白如霜雪，肩膀也塌了；可那副骨架的修长、那副身量，仍同他早年骁勇矫健的传闻对得上。
他鳏居多年——足足三十年了。两个儿子，没有女儿。两个他都不大信得过的儿子——哪一个也不年轻了——查尔斯和亨利。
年纪确实都不小了。大的如今四十三，小的不过小一两岁。查尔斯一辈子漂泊，各行当试了个遍。他好过赌，也沾过别的费钱的荒唐。不过人们认为他收了心；尽管青年时代荒唐挥霍，替这份产业清偿债务，兴许正是他的天命。
亨利，那位小儿子，是个精明的马贩子；喜欢在自己那伙人里称王，屈尊喝喝烈性麦酒，同怀弗恩小镇上「乔治」酒店的女招待调调情，爱搭的是玩狗的、赛马的、摔跤的之类名流的谈吐。

===Original===

The old Squire was not much considered, and less beloved, by his sons. The gaunt old man was, however, more feared by these matured scions than their pride would have easily allowed. The fears of childhood survive its pleasures. Something of the ghostly terrors of the nursery haunt us through life, and the tyrant of early days maintains a strange and unavowed ascendancy over the imagination, long after his real power to inflict pain or privation has quite come to an end.
As this tall, grim, handsome old man moves about the room, as he stands, or sits down, or turns eastward at the Creed in church﻿—as he marches slowly toppling along the terrace, with his gold-headed cane in his hand, surveying the long familiar scenes which will soon bloom and brown no more for him﻿—with sullen eyes, thinking his solitary thoughts﻿—as in the long summer evenings he dozes in the great chair by the fire, which even in the dog-days smoulders in the drawing-room grate﻿—looking like a gigantic effigy of winter﻿—a pair of large and soft gray eyes follow, or steal towards him﻿—removed when observed﻿—but ever and anon returning. People have remarked this, and talked it over, and laughed and shook their heads, and built odd speculations upon it.
Alice Maybell had grown up from orphan childhood under the roof of Wyvern. The old squire had been, after a fashion, kind to that pretty waif of humanity, which a chance wave of fortune had thrown at his door. She was the child of a distant cousin, who had happened, being a clergyman, to die in occupation of the vicarage of Wyvern. Her young mother lay, under the branches of the two great trees, in the lonely corner of the village churchyard; and not two years later the Vicar died, and was buried beside her.
Melancholy, gentle Vicar! Some good judges, I believe, pronounced his sermons admirable. Seedily clothed, with kindly patience visiting his poor; very frugal﻿—his pretty young wife and he were yet happy in the light and glow of the true love that is eternal. He was to her the nonpareil of vicars﻿—the loveliest, wisest, wittiest, and best of men. She to him﻿—what shall I say? The *same* beautiful first love. Never a day older. Every summer threw new gold on her rich hair, and a softer and brighter bloom on her cheeks, and made her dearer and dearer than he could speak.

===Chinese===

两个儿子对老父谈不上敬重，更谈不上爱。然而这位枯瘦老人叫这俩成年的枝条畏惧之深，远过他们的自尊肯承认的份量。童年的乐趣早谢了，童年的恐惧还活着。育儿室里那些鬼气森森的惊怖，一生都缠着我们；早年那个暴君，在真能叫我们吃苦、受剥夺的权柄早已告终之后很久，仍对想象维持着一种古怪的、不认账的统治。
这高大的、冷面俊朗的老人在屋里踱来踱去，站着，坐下，教堂里念信经时朝东转身——他手执金头手杖，在露台上慢慢挪行，颤颤欲倾，巡望那些看熟了的长景，它们不久便不再为他开花、为他转黄——他眼色阴沉，想他孤独的想头——在长长的夏日黄昏，他在炉边大椅里打盹，连三伏天，客厅炉格里也煨着火——他整个像一尊冬天的巨大造像——这一幕幕里，总有一双又大又软的灰眼睛追随着他，或悄悄溜向他——一被察觉便移开——可不时又回来。人们注意到这个，议论过，笑过，摇过头，还在上面搭起种种古怪的猜测。
爱丽丝·梅贝尔（Alice Maybell）自幼失怙，就在怀弗恩的屋檐下长大成人。命运一阵偶然的浪，把人类这件漂亮的漂泊物抛到他门口，老乡绅对她也算有过他那种样子的好。她是一位远房表亲的孩子；那位表亲恰巧是牧师，身任怀弗恩牧师职时死去。她年轻的母亲躺在村墓地冷清的一角，两棵大树底下；不出两年，牧师也死了，葬在她身旁。
忧郁而温厚的牧师！我相信，几位行家盛赞过他的布道。衣衫寒素，怀着慈爱的耐性探望他的穷人；极其俭省——可他同他美丽的年轻妻子，仍沐浴在那种永恒真爱的光与暖里，幸福得很。于她，他是牧师中的无双——最可爱、最聪明、最风趣、最好的人。她于他——我怎么说呢？*还是*那场美丽的初恋。一天也不曾老去。每个夏天都往她丰厚的头发上镀一层新金，往她脸颊上添一层更柔更亮的红晕，把她变得他言语说不出地、一天比一天亲爱。

===Original===

He could only look and feel his heart swelling with a vain yearning to tell the love that lighted his face with its glory and called a mist to his kind eye.
And then came a time when she had a secret to tell her Willie. Full of a wild fear and delight, in their tiny drawing-room, clasped in each other’s arms, they wept for joy, and a kind of wonder and some dim unspoken tremblings of fear, and loved one another, it seemed, as it were more desperately than ever.
And then, as he read aloud to her in the evenings, her pretty fingers were busy with a new sort of work, full of wonderful and delightful interest. A little guest was coming, a little creature with an immortal soul, that was to be as clever and handsome as Willie.
“And, oh, Willie, darling, don’t you hope I may live to see it? Ah, Willie, would not it be sad?”
And then the Vicar, smiling through tears, would put his arms round her, and comfort her, breaking into a rapturous castle-building and a painting of pictures of this great new happiness and treasure that was coming.
And so in due time the little caps and frocks and all the tiny wardrobe were finished; and the day came when the long-pictured treasure was to come. It was there; but its young mother’s eyes were dim, and the pretty hands that had made its little dress and longed to clasp it were laid beside her, never to stir again.
“The Lord gave and the Lord hath taken away﻿—blessed be the name of the Lord.” Yes, blessed be the name of the Lord for that love that outlives the separation of death﻿—that saddens and glorifies memory with its melancholy light, and illuminates far futurity with a lamp whose trembling ray is the thread that draws us toward heaven. Blessed in giving and in taking﻿—blessed for the yearning remembrances, and for the agony of hope.
The little baby﻿—the relic﻿—the treasure was there. Poor little forlorn baby! And with this little mute companion to look at and sit by, his sorrow was stealing away into a wonderful love; and in this love a consolation and a living fountain of sympathy with his darling who was gone.
A trouble of a new kind had come. Squire Fairfield, who wanted money, raised a claim for rent for the vicarage and its little garden. The Vicar hated law and feared it, and would no doubt have submitted; but this was a battle in which the Bishop took command, and insisted on fighting it out.

===Chinese===

他只能望着，觉出心口鼓胀，胀着一腔说不出的渴慕——那爱点亮他的脸，给他和善的眼睛蒙上一层雾。
后来到了那样一个时候：她有一桩秘密要告诉她的威利。在那间小小的客厅里，两人搂作一团，又惊又喜，欢喜得直哭，还带着几分惊奇，和几缕说不出口的、朦胧的惧怕，彼此相爱着——仿佛比从前爱得更不管不顾了。
再后来，晚间他朗读书给她听时，她那双漂亮手指便忙起一种新针线，满是奇妙而快乐的意趣。一位小客人要来了，一个带着不朽灵魂的小人儿，将来会同威利一样聪明、一样俊。
「还有，哦，威利，亲爱的，你不盼我能活着见到他么？唉，威利，要是我见不着，那多叫人难过？」
牧师便含泪带笑搂住她，宽慰她，兴高采烈地筑起空中楼阁，一幅一幅描画那即将降临的大幸福、大珍宝。
于是到了时候，小帽、小裙和一整套小小衣橱都做齐了；那描画了许久的珍宝要来的日子也到了。它来了；可它年轻的母亲眼睛已暗了，那双缝了它小衣裳、渴望搂住它的漂亮的手，安放在身旁，再也不会动了。
「赏赐的是耶和华，收取的也是耶和华——耶和华的名是应当称颂的。」是的，耶和华的名应当称颂，为那胜过死亡之隔的爱——它用忧郁的光把回忆染愁，也把它照得辉煌；它点起一盏灯，照彻漫漫来日，那颤动的光线，正是牵引我们走向天国的丝线。赐下时称颂，收取时也称颂——为那渴念的追忆称颂，也为希望的煎熬称颂。
那小小的婴儿——遗物——珍宝，在那里。可怜的孤零零的小婴儿！守着这个无言的小东西看着、坐着，他的悲伤悄悄化成一场奇妙的爱；在这爱里，有一份安慰，有一眼活泉，流着对亡人儿的体恤。
一场新的祸事来了。费尔菲尔德乡绅等钱用，提出牧师住宅连同小园要收租。牧师厌恶官司，也怕官司，多半就认了；可这一仗由主教接了帅印，非打到底不可。

===Original===

It was a tedious business.
It had lasted two years nearly, and was still alive and angry, when the Reverend William Maybell took a cold, which no one thought would signify. A brother clergyman from Willowford kindly undertook his duty for one Sunday, and on the next he had died.
The Wyvern doctor said the *vis vitae* was wanting﻿—he had lived quite too low, and had not stamina, and so sank like a child.
But there was more. When on Sundays, as the sweet bell of Wyvern trembled in the air, the Vicar had walked alone up to the old gray porch, and saw the two trees near the ivied nook of the churchyard-wall, a homesickness yearned at his heart, and when the hour came his spirit acquiesced in death.
Old Squire Fairfield knew that it was the Bishop who really, and, as I believe, rightly opposed him, for to this day the vicarage pays no rent; but the proud and violent man chose to make the Vicar feel his resentment. He beheld him with a gloomy and thunderous aspect, never a word more would he exchange with him; he turned his back upon him; he forbid him the footpath across the fields of Wyvern, that made the way to church shorter. He walked out of church grimly when his sermon began. He turned the Vicar’s cow off the common, and made him every way feel the weight of his displeasure.
Well, now the Vicar was dead. He had borne it all very gently and sadly, and it was over, a page in the past, no line erasable, no line addible forever.
“So Parson’s dead and buried; serve him right,” said the Squire of Wyvern. “Thankless rascal. You go down and tell them I must have the house up on the 24th, and if they don’t go, you bundle ’em out, Thomas Rooke.”
“There’ll be the Vicar’s little child there; who’s to take it in, Squire?” asked Tom Rooke, after a hesitation.
“You may, or the Bishop, d﻿—﻿— him.”
“I’m a poor man, and, for the Bishop, he’s not like to﻿—”
“Let ’em try the workhouse,” said the Squire, “where many a better man’s brat is.”
And he gave Tom Rooke a look that might have knocked him down, and turned his back on him and walked away.
A week or so after he went down himself to the vicarage with Tom Rooke. Old Dulcibella Crane went over the lower part of the house with Tom, and the Squire strode up the stairs, and stooping his tall head as he entered the door, walked into the first room he met with, in a surly mood.

===Chinese===

这是一场旷日持久的拉锯。
缠了将近两年，还活着，还冒着火气；就在这时，可敬的威廉·梅贝尔牧师得了个风寒，谁都当不算回事。威洛福德（Willowford）一位同僚牧师好意替他主了一个礼拜天的圣工；到下一个礼拜天，他已经死了。
怀弗恩的大夫说，是缺了*生命力*（*vis vitae*）——他日子过得太亏，底子没有存住，于是像孩子似的沉了下去。
可还有别的。多少个礼拜天，怀弗恩清甜的钟声在空气里颤着，牧师独自走到那灰色的老门廊，望见墓地墙头爬满常春藤的角落近旁那两棵树，一腔思乡的渴念就在心口烧；时辰一到，他的性灵便向死亡低了头——归家去了。
老乡绅费尔菲尔德心里明白，真正顶他的是主教——而且我相信，顶得在理：直到今天，牧师宅也没交过租。可这个高傲暴烈的人，偏要把这口怨气记在牧师头上。他见他时一脸阴沉欲雷，再不肯同他换一个字；他掉头不理他；他禁了他抄近路的教堂便道——那条横穿怀弗恩田地的小径；牧师一上讲坛，他便铁青着脸走出教堂；他把牧师的牛赶下公地，处处叫他掂出这份不痛快有多重。
好了，如今牧师死了。这一切他温温顺顺、凄凄惶惶地受完了；事情了了，成了往昔的一页——再无一字可涂，也再无一字可添，永永远远。
「牧师死了，埋了；活该。」怀弗恩的乡绅说。「忘恩负义的东西。你去底下告诉他们，二十四号我要收宅子；他们不走，你就把他们卷铺盖撵出去，托马斯·鲁克（Thomas Rooke）。」
「牧师的小娃还在那儿呢；谁收留，乡绅？」汤姆·鲁克迟疑了一下问。
「你收去，或者主教收去，叫他见鬼。」
「我是个穷人；至于主教，他不见得——」
「叫他们上济贫院去。」乡绅说，「多少比他强的人的崽子都在那儿。」
他朝汤姆·鲁克看了一眼，那一眼险些把他看倒；随即掉背转身，走了。
约莫一礼拜后，他亲自带着汤姆·鲁克下牧师宅来。老杜尔西贝拉·克兰陪汤姆查点楼下一层；乡绅大步上楼，进门时低下他那颗高脑袋，一脸愠色，径直走进头一间屋。

===Original===

The clatter of his boots prevented his hearing, till he had got well into the room, the low crying of a little child in a cradle. He stayed his step for a moment. He had quite forgotten that unimportant being, and he half turned to go out again, but changed his mind. He stooped over the cradle, and the little child’s crying ceased. It was a very pretty face and large eyes, still wet with tears, that looked up with an earnest wondering gaze at him from out the tiny blankets.
Old Dulcibella Crane had gone down, and the solitude, no doubt, affrighted it, and there was consolation even in the presence of the grim Squire, into whose face those large eyes looked with innocent trust.
Who would have thought it? Below lay the little image of utter human weakness; above stooped a statue of inflexibility and power, a strong statue with a grim contracted eye. There was a heart, steeled against man’s remonstrance, and a pride that would have burst into fury at a hint of reproof. Below lay the mere wonder and vagueness of dumb infancy. Could contest be imagined more hopeless! But “the faithful Creator,” who loved the poor Vicar, had brought those eyes to meet.
The little child’s crying was hushed; big tears hung in its great wondering eyes, and the little face looked up pale and forlorn. It was a gaze that lasted while you might count four or five. But its mysterious work of love was done. “All things were made by Him, and without Him was not anything made that was made.”
Squire Fairfield walked round this room, and went out and examined the others, and went downstairs in silence, and when he was going out at the hall-door he stopped and looked at old Dulcibella Crane, who stood courtesying at it in great fear, and said he﻿—
“The child’ll be better at home wi’ me, up at Wyvern, and I’ll send down for it and you in the afternoon, till﻿—something’s settled.”
And on this invitation little Alice Maybell and her nurse, Dulcibella Crane, came to Wyvern Manor, and had remained there now for twenty years.

===Chinese===

靴声橐橐，遮住了他的耳朵；等他走进屋好一阵，才听见摇篮里小娃娃低低的哭。他收住脚。这个小东西他早忘了个干净；他半转身要出去，又改了主意。他俯向摇篮，小娃娃不哭了。那是一张很好看的脸、一双很大的眼睛，泪还没干，从小小的毯子里仰起来，怔怔地、认真地望着他。
老杜尔西贝拉·克兰下楼去了；这份冷清，不用说，吓着了它；于是连冷面乡绅的在场也成了安慰——那双大眼睛望着他的脸，一脸无辜的信。
谁想得到呢？底下躺着人类全然无助的小小化身；上头俯着一尊冷硬与权势的像，一尊目光紧锁的强横石像。那里有一颗心，炼得听不进人的劝，有一份傲气，一句责备就能点成狂怒。底下躺着的，不过是哑巴婴孩的一片懵懂与新奇。还有比这更没指望的对垒么！可那位疼爱穷牧师的「信实的造物主」，偏偏安排了这双眼睛来相会。
小娃娃的哭声止了；大颗的泪珠悬在充满惊奇的大眼睛里，小小的脸仰着，苍白而孤单。这一望，长够你数四下五下。可它那神秘的爱的工程，已经做完。「万物是藉着他造的；凡被造的，没有一样不是藉着他造的。」
费尔菲尔德乡绅绕屋走了一圈，又出去查看别的房间，默默下了楼；走到正门口，他停下来，望着惶恐万状、连连屈膝的老杜尔西贝拉·克兰，说——
「孩子跟我回家好，上怀弗恩去；下午我打发人来接它和你——等——事情有个安置。」
便凭着这一句相邀，小爱丽丝·梅贝尔同她的乳母杜尔西贝拉·克兰进了怀弗恩庄宅；一住，至今已是二十年。

## LVI The Old Squire Leaves Wyvern / 第五十六章 老乡绅辞别怀弗恩
===Original===

The old folk can’t go on living always. The King’s messenger had called at Wyvern, and the old Squire must needs get up and go.
Sickness was a cross he had never been used to bear, and now that it was laid on his old shoulders he knew that he could not keep his feet very long.
He had the Wyvern lawyer, who did the business of the estate, up to his room, and the parson and his own son, Harry Fairfield. He made the attorney read the will, which he had told him to bring up with him, and the Squire listened as it was read slowly.
After the clergyman had gone﻿—
“Have ye ought to say to that, son Harry?” said the old Squire.
“ ’Tis an old will, father,” said Harry.
“It ain’t,” said the Squire.
“Eight years less two months,” said the lawyer.
“About the age rum’s fit to drink,” said the old Squire. “What say ye to it﻿—now’s your time, son?”
