“Priests, women, and poultry, they say, has never enough. There’s bin changes since, and I don’t see why Wyvern should be charged so heavy.”
“There’s three hundred a year to Alice, that’s what ye mean!” said the old Squire.
His son was silent.
“Well, I don’t owe her nothin’, that’s true, but I’ll let it stand, mind. And Harry, lad, the day ye do a good thing there will be seven new moons.”
“What was parson a-whisperin’ about in the window wi’ ye?” he asked of the attorney after a time.
“Some claim upon the vicarage which he thought you said you meant to remit by will.”
“I ’a thought upon it, and I won’t. Paternoster built churches, and Our Father pulled ’em down. There’s o’er many parsons for the churches, and o’er many churches for the people﻿—tell him I won’t.”
“What the devil made you talk about that to him?” said Harry, with a dark look, when he and the attorney had got out of the room.
“My dear sir,” said the lawyer, “we must be true to our clients, and beside, don’t you remember the clergyman said he’d be here tomorrow at one to administer the Lord’s Supper, and he’ll be certain to speak of it then to our client.”
At nightfall the Squire grew worse, and his head wandered.
“Tell that white-faced Vicar Maybell, there’s never a one but the thankless in hell﻿—I’ll not sit under none o’ his sermons﻿—Ay, he frowns at that.”
“Hey, dear?” whispered the housekeeper, gazing at him from the hearth where they were sitting.
“And who does he mean, ma’am?” asked the nurse.
“God knows﻿—old times, I suppose,” she answered.

===Chinese===

老人不能永远活下去。国王的信使已经叩过怀弗恩的门，老乡绅只好起身随他去了。
病是他素来不曾背过的十字架；如今压上他老迈的肩头，他自己明白，撑不了多久了。
他把替产业办事的怀弗恩律师请到房里，还有牧师和他自己的儿子哈利·费尔菲尔德。他叫律师把遗嘱读一遍——事先吩咐他随身带上来——遗嘱缓缓读着，乡绅听着。
牧师走后——
「对这个你有什么可说，哈利儿？」老乡绅说。
「这是一份旧遗嘱了，父亲。」哈利说。
「不旧。」乡绅说。
「八年差两个月。」律师说。
「差不多正是朗姆酒到喝口的年份。」老乡绅说。「你怎么说——现在正是时候，儿子？」
「俗话说，教士、女人和家禽，永远不知足。这以后世事有变，我不明白怀弗恩凭什么背这么重的担子。」
「给爱丽丝一年三百，你是这个意思吧！」老乡绅说。
他儿子不响。
「嗯，我确实不欠她什么，这不假；可我留着不动，记着。还有哈利小子，你肯做一件好事的那天，天上要出七个月亮。」
「方才牧师在窗口同你嘀咕什么？」过了一阵他问律师。
「牧师宅有笔旧账，他以为您说过，要在遗嘱里豁免。」
「我想过了，我不豁免。念主祷文的人盖教堂，念天父经的人拆教堂。牧师太多，教堂装不下；教堂太多，百姓养不起——告诉他，我不。」
「你见什么鬼偏同他提这个？」出了房门，哈利黑着脸问律师。
「亲爱的先生，」律师说，「对主顾我们必须实心；再者，您不记得牧师说过，明天一点钟他要来主领圣餐；到那时，他保准要同咱们这位主顾提起这桩事。」
日暮时分，乡绅病势转沉，神志飘忽起来。
「告诉那个白脸的梅贝尔牧师，地狱里全是忘恩负义的东西——他的布道我一篇也不听——哎，他冲这个皱眉了。」
「哎，老爷？」管家太太轻声应着，从两人围坐的炉边望着他。
「他说的是谁呀，太太？」看护问。
「天知道——多半是年头久远的事。」她答。

===Original===

“There’s a glass broke, Tom, who’s kicking up the row?” mumbled the Squire﻿—“Play, women, and wine undoes men, laughin’﻿—Ay, light it, I’m very dark﻿—Who’s he, ye fool?﻿—Joan and my lady’s all one in the dark.”
“That’s Tom Ward he’s thinkin’ on?” said the nurse.
“Ay, he liked Tom ever. He wouldn’t think ’twas Wyvern without Tom,” answered the housekeeper.
In a little time he said more distinctly and sternly﻿—
“The dead should do nothing.﻿—So that’s the bishop.﻿—Ay﻿—ay﻿—The devil, mind ye, isn’t always at one door.﻿—If there was a good man here he’d put a clout over that face﻿—Ye’ll never do it.”
Then it would sink into mumbling, and then again grow more distinct.
At last the morning came, and the Squire so many hours nearer death, was, nevertheless, now like himself.
In due course the clergyman arrived, and the housekeeper, and serious Jim Hopper of the mill, close by, attended to make up a little congregation with whom the dying Squire was to receive that most “comfortable” Sacrament, before setting out on his long journey.
“You’re distinctly a Church of England man?” inquired the clergyman gently.
“Ay, what do you take me for?”
“I make it a rule, dear sir, to inquire. I have once or twice found Presbyterians and other Dissenters among the attendants at my church at Nottingham before I came here, and I am happy to hear so clear an answer to my inquiry,” said the clergyman with a gracious solemnity.
“The crow thinks her own bird fairest﻿—go on,” said Squire Harry.
After these rites were over, the Squire needed rest.
Then, after an hour or so, he called for Tom Ward.
“Well, Tom, we a’ lived a long while together﻿—here in Wyvern﻿—you and me, and ‘be the day never so long, at last cometh even’-song,’ as they say, and now the doctor thinks my time be come, and I sent for ye to shake hands, Tom, and bid ye goodbye.”
Tom was drying his eyes hastily, and his old face was more puckered than ever.
“Yer honour was always kind to me﻿—”
“Come, Tom, ye mustn’t be cryin’, man. Penny in pocket’s a merry companion, and I wrote ye down for somethin’ in my will, and ye a’ brewed me many a tankard, Tom﻿—ye’ll never brew me another﻿—and I wouldn’t go without a word and a shake by the hand.”
When this was over, the nurse signed to Tom to go.

===Chinese===

「有杯子碎了，汤姆，谁在闹？」乡绅咕哝道——「赌、女人、酒，笑着就把男人毁了——对，点上灯，我眼前黑得很——他是谁，你这蠢货？——黑地里，琼娘同夫人是一个人。」
「他这是想着汤姆·沃德吧？」看护说。
「是啊，他一向喜欢汤姆。没有汤姆，他就不认得是怀弗恩。」管家太太答。
过了一小会儿，他说得清楚了些，也严厉了——
「死人什么也不该做。——哦，那就是主教。——嗯——嗯——记住，魔鬼并不总守一扇门。——这儿要有个好人，就该拿块破布蒙上那张脸。——你们永远办不成。」
话音渐渐又沉成咕哝，忽而又清楚起来。
终于早晨来了；乡绅离死亡又近了几个钟头，人却清明如常了。
到了时辰，牧师到了；管家太太，还有近旁磨坊那位端方严肃的吉姆·霍珀，凑成一小会众；临终的乡绅要同他们一起领受那「最安慰人」的圣餐，然后启程上他遥远的路。
「您确确实实是国教徒？」牧师温声问。
「是，你当我是哪路人？」
「我有个规矩，亲爱的先生，先问明。来这儿之前，在诺丁汉（Nottingham）我主持的教堂里，我不止一回发现长老会和其他不服国教的人混在会众里；听到这么干脆的回答，我很欣慰。」牧师说，一脸慈和的庄重。
「乌鸦总当自家雏儿白——开始吧。」哈利乡绅说。
仪礼完毕，乡绅需要歇息。
过了约莫一个钟头，他叫人请汤姆·沃德。
「好了，汤姆，咱们做了多少年老伙伴——就在这怀弗恩——你和我；俗话说，『白日再长，晚祷终要响』；如今大夫觉得我的时辰到了，我叫你来，汤姆，握握手，道个别。」
汤姆忙着擦眼睛，那张老脸皱得前所未有。
「老爷一向待我恩厚——」
「好了，汤姆，不许哭，汉子。兜里一个便士，就是个快活的伴；遗嘱里我给你写下了一份；你给我斟过多少缸潘趣酒，汤姆——再也斟不上一缸了——我总不能不辞一句别、不握一回手就走。」
这一节过了，看护朝汤姆摆手，叫他出去。

===Original===

I wonder how the grim old man, with near a week’s white stubble on his chin, felt as he saw Tom Ward glide away softly, with tears on his rugged cheeks. For Tom, it was the breaking up and foundering of old Wyvern in the deep. He was too old to live in the new Wyvern that was coming, mayhap.
“I’ll never get the old days out o’ my head, nor ever like the new, and ’twon’t be long, I’m thinkin’, before I follow him down the ash-tree road to Wyvern churchyard.”
And so for the old Squire it came, the last day of light, and the first of death.
It was a stately funeral in the old-fashioned way. All the good old houses of the county were represented there. The neighbours, great and small, mustered; the shops in the town were all shut, and the tenants attended in masses.
This solemn feast and pageant over, the fuss subsided, and Harry entered upon his reign with a gravity becoming his new prerogative and responsibilities.
Sergeant-Major Archdale was an influential, and prosperous, and reserved minister under the new regime. He had a snug berth at Warhampton, as Harry Fairfield had promised, and from that distant legation he was summoned every now and then to Wyvern, and there conferred with the Squire. I have called him Sergeant-Major, but he was so no longer. He had retired some time before from the militia and was now plain Mr. Archdale.

===Chinese===

这个冷面的老人，下巴上蓄了近一个礼拜的白须茬，望着汤姆·沃德带着满脸沟壑上的泪轻轻退出去时，心里是什么滋味，我不知道。对汤姆来说，那是老怀弗恩在深海里散架、沉没。行将到来的新怀弗恩，他这把年纪怕是住不惯了。
「旧日子我一辈子忘不掉，新日子我一辈子爱不上；我琢磨着，不须多久，我就跟着他，顺那条梣树路，下怀弗恩墓地去了。」
就这样，老乡绅的那一天到了：光明的最后一日，死亡的头一日。
那是一场按老派操办的堂皇葬礼。全郡的世家旧族都派了人。乡邻不论大小，齐聚一堂；镇上店铺一律歇业，佃户成群结队前来。
这场庄严的宴飨与仪典过后，喧腾平息；哈利登基视事，一脸凝重，正配他新的权柄与责任。
军士长阿奇代尔，在新朝里是一位有权有势、兴旺而缄默的重臣。正如哈利·费尔菲尔德许诺的，他在沃勒姆普顿得了个肥缺；从那处遥远的使馆，他不时被召到怀弗恩，同乡绅议事。我一路叫他军士长，其实早不是了。他前些时已从民团退役，如今只是平平常常的阿奇代尔先生。

## VI The Old Squire Unlike Himself / 第六章 老乡绅一反常态
===Original===

He talked very little that night in the old-fashioned drawing-room, where Alice played his favourite old airs for him on the piano, which he still called the “harpsichord.” He sat sometimes dozing, sometimes listening to her music, in the great chair by the fire. He ruminated, perhaps, but he did not open the subject, whatever it might be, which he had hinted at.
But before ten o’clock came, he got up and stood with his back to the fire. Is there any age at which folly has quite done with us, and we cease from building castles in the air?
“My wife was a tartar,” said he rather abruptly, “and she was always telling me I’d marry again before she was cold in her grave, and I made answer, ‘I’ve had enough of that market, I thank you; one wife in a life is one too many.’ But she wasn’t like you﻿—no more than chalk to cheese﻿—a head devil she was. Play me the ‘Week Before Easter’ again, lass.”
And the young lady thrice over played that pretty but vulgar old air; and when she paused the gaunt old Squire chanted the refrain from the hearthrug, somewhat quaveringly and discordantly.
“You should have heard Tom Snedly sing that round a bowl of punch. My sons, a pair o’ dull dogs﻿—we were pleasanter fellows then﻿—I don’t care if they was at the bottom of the Lunnon canal. Gi’e us the ‘Lincolnshire Poacher,’ lass. Pippin-squeezing rascals﻿—and never loved me. I sometimes think I don’t know what the world’s a-comin’ to. I’d be a younger lad by a score o’ years, if neighbours were as I remember ’em.”
At that moment entered old Tom Ward, who, like his master, had seen younger, if not better days, bearing something hot in a silver tankard on a little tray. Tom looked at the Squire. The Squire pointed to the little table by the hearthrug, and pulled out his great gold watch, and found it was time for his “nightcap.”
Tom was skilled in the brew that pleased his master, and stood with his shrewd gray eye on him, till he had swallowed his first glass, then the Squire nodded gruffly, and he knew all was right, and was relieved, for everyone stood in awe of old Fairfield.

===Chinese===

那晚在老式客厅里，他话很少；爱丽丝在钢琴上为他弹他心爱的老调子——那琴他至今还叫「大键琴」。他坐在炉边大椅里，时而打盹，时而听她的琴。他心里也许在盘算，可他暗示过的那件事，不论是什么，他没有挑开。
十点未到，他站起来，背炉而立。人活到哪个岁数，荒唐才算同我们两讫，我们才不再筑空中的楼阁？
「我那老婆子是个母夜叉。」他说得突兀，「她老念叨，说她坟土未干我就会再娶；我回她：『那个市场我领教够了，谢谢；一辈一个老婆，一个也嫌多。』可她不像你——天差地远——她是恶鬼的头儿。再给我弹一遍《复活节前一周》吧，丫头。」
年轻小姐把那支又好听又俚俗的老调子弹了三遍；她一停，枯瘦的老乡绅便在炉前地毯上唱起副歌来，颤颤的，荒腔走板。
「你该听听汤姆·斯内德利围着一缸潘趣酒唱这个。我那两个儿子，一对闷葫芦——我们那会儿的人可有趣多了——他们沉进伦敦运河底我都不可惜。来一曲《林肯郡偷猎者》，丫头。榨苹果汁的恶棍——从没爱过我。我有时想，这世界成什么样子了。乡邻若还像我记得的那样，我能年轻二十岁。」
正这时，老汤姆·沃德进来了——同他主人一样，也见过更年轻、不见得更体面的岁月——小托盘上一只银缸，托着热腾腾的东西。汤姆看乡绅；乡绅朝炉前小桌一指，掣出他那块大金表，一看，到喝「睡前酒」的时辰了。
汤姆调那手讨主人欢喜的酒最拿手，站在一旁，精明的灰眼睛盯着，等他咽下头一杯；乡绅粗声一点头，他知道成了，松了口气——老费尔菲尔德的威严，人人怕。

===Original===

Tom was gone, and the Squire drank a second glass, slowly, and then a third, and stood up again with his back to the fire and filled his glass with the last precious drops of his cordial, and placed it on the chimneypiece, and looked steadfastly on the girl, whose eyes looked sad on the notes, while her slender fingers played those hilarious airs which Squire Fairfield delighted to listen to.
“Down in the mouth, lass﻿—hey?” said the Squire with a suddenness that made the unconscious girl start.
When she looked up he was standing grinning upon her, from the hearthrug, with his glass in his fingers, and his face flushed.
“You girls, when you like a lad, you’re always in the dumps﻿—ain’t ye?﻿—mopin’ and moultin’ like a sick bird, till the fellow comes out wi’ his mind, and then all’s right, flutter and song and new feathers, and﻿—come, what do you think o’ me, lass?”
She looked at him dumbly, with a colourless and frightened face. She saw no object in the room but the tall figure of the old man, flushed with punch, and leering with a horrid jollity, straight before her like a vivid magic-lantern figure in the dark. He was grinning and wagging his head with exulting encouragement.
Had Squire Fairfield, as men have done, all on a sudden grown insane; and was that leering mask, the furrows and contortions of which, and its glittering eyes, were fixing themselves horribly on her brain, a familiar face transformed by madness?
“Come, lass, do ye like me?” demanded the phantom.
“Well, you’re tongue-tied, ye little fool﻿—shamefaced, and all that, I see,” he resumed after a little pause. “But you *shall* answer﻿—ye must; you do﻿—you like old Wyvern, the old Squire. You’d feel strange in another place﻿—ye would, and a younger fellow would not be a tithe so kind as me﻿—and I like ye well, chick-a-biddy, chick-a-biddy﻿—ye’ll be my little queen, and I’ll keep ye brave satins and ribbons, and laces, and lawn; and I’ll gi’e ye the jewellery﻿—d’ye hear?

===Chinese===

汤姆去了；乡绅慢慢喝下第二杯，又第三杯；再起身背炉而立，把那甜酒最后一滴珍贵的残浆斟满杯，掠到壁炉台上，眼睛定定望住姑娘——她纤细的手指弹着费尔菲尔德乡绅爱听的那些欢快调子，眼睛落在琴谱上，落得愁愁的。
「嘴撇下来了，丫头——嗯？」乡绅说得突然，浑然不觉的姑娘吓了一跳。
她抬头，他正站在炉前地毯上朝她赃着牙笑，指间夹着杯，满脸通红。
「你们这些丫头，一相中了小伙子，就蔫头耷脑——是不是？——像只病鸟，脱毛、闷着，等那小子把话说出口，立刻样样都好，扑翅膀、唱歌、换新羽——来，你看我这个人怎么样，丫头？」
她怔怔望着他，脸上没了血色，满眼惊惺。屋里她什么也看不见了，只剩老人的高大身影，潘趣酒烧红的，一脸可怕的涎笑，直挺挺立在她面前，像黑暗里幻灯映出的一幅活人。他赃着牙，一颠一颠晃着脑袋，一副得胜的鼓励。
费尔菲尔德乡绅莫非像世上有过的人那样，忽然疯了？那张涎笑的面具——沟壑、扭绞，还有那双闪光的眼——正可怕地钉进她脑髓；那是不是一张熟脸，叫疯狂改了形？
「说呀，丫头，喜欢不喜欢我？」那影子催问。
「嗐，你这是让舌头打了结，小傻瓜——害臊了，什么的，我看得出。」他顿了顿又接着说。「可你*必须*回答——非答不可；你是喜欢的——你喜欢老怀弗恩，喜欢老乡绅。换个别的地方你要别扭的——肯定的；年轻小子待你的好，抵不上我十分之一——我喜欢你，小乖乖，小乖乖——你要做我的小王后，我给你备下鲜亮的缎子、缎带、花边、细麻纱；首饰我也给你——听见没有？

===Original===

﻿—necklaces, and earrings, and bodkins, and all the rest, for your own, mind; for the Captain nor Jack shall never hang them on wife o’ theirs, mind ye﻿—and ye’ll be the grandest lady has ever bin in Wyvern this hundred years﻿—and ye’ll have nothing to do but sit all day in the window, or ride in the coach, and order your maids about; and I’ll leave you every acre and stick and stone, and silver spoon, that’s in or round about Wyvern﻿—for you’re a good lass, and I’ll make a woman of you; and I’d like to break them young rascals’ necks﻿—they never deserved a shilling o’ mine; so gi’e’s your hand, lass, and the bargain’s made.”
So the Squire strode a step or two nearer, extending his huge bony hand, and Alice, aghast, stared with wide open eyes fixed on him, and exclaiming faintly, “Oh, sir!﻿—oh, Mr. Fairfield!”
“*Oh!* to be sure, and *oh*, Squire Fairfield!” chuckled he, mimicking the young lady, as he drew near; “ye need not be shy, nor scared by me, little Alice; I like you too well to hurt the tip o’ your little finger, look ye﻿—and you’ll sleep on’t, and tell me all tomorrow morning.”
And he laid his mighty hands, that had lifted wrestlers from the earth, and hurled boxers headlong in his day, tremulously on her two little shoulders. “And ye’ll say good night, and gi’e me a buss; good night to ye, lass, and we’ll talk again in the morning, and ye’ll say naught, mind, to the boys, d﻿—﻿—﻿n ’em, till all’s settled﻿—ye smooth-cheeked, bright-eyed, cherry-lipped little”﻿—﻿—﻿
And here the ancient Squire boisterously “bussed” the young lady, as he had threatened, and two or three times again, till scrubbed by the white stubble of his chin, she broke away, with her cheeks flaming, and still more alarmed, reached the door.
“Say good night, won’t ye, hey?” bawled the Squire, still in a chuckle and shoving the chairs out of his way as he stumbled after her.
“Good night, sir,” cried she, and made her escape through the door, and under the arch that opened from the hall, and up the stairs toward her room, calling as unconcernedly as she could, but with tremulous eagerness to her old servant, “Dulcibella, are you there?” and immensely relieved when she heard her kindly old voice, and saw the light of her candle.
“I say﻿—hallo﻿—why wench, what the devil’s come over ye?” halloed the voice of the old man from the foot of the stairs.

===Chinese===

——项链、耳环、簪子，全套的，都归你，记着；上尉也好、杰克也好，谁也别想挂到他媳妇脖子上，记着——你将是这一百年里怀弗恩最阔气的夫人——你什么也不用做，成天坐在窗口，或是坐马车兜风，支使你的女仆；怀弗恩里里外外，一亩地、一根棍、一块石、一把银匙，我全留给你——你是个好丫头，我要把你栽培成个夫人；那两个小恶棍我恨不得拧断他们的脖子——我的一份先令他们从来不配得；来，把手给我，丫头，这买卖就算定了。」
乡绅迈近一两步，那只骨骼粗大的手伸过来；爱丽丝骇住了，眼睛睁得大大的钉在他身上，微弱地喊：「哦，先生！——哦，费尔菲尔德先生！」
「*哦*！对了，*哦*，费尔菲尔德乡绅！」他学舌年轻人的腔调，笑着捱近；「你不用害臊，也不用怕我，小爱丽丝；我喜欢你还来不及，你小指头尖我都伤不着，你瞧——这事你睡一夜，明早一五一十回我。」
他把那双巨掌——当年能把角力士举离地面、把拳击手掼倒的那双手——颤颤地搭上她两只小肩膀。「还有，道个晚安，亲我一口；晚安了，丫头，明早咱们再谈；那两个小子那边，一个字也别露，记着，去他们的——等全定了再说——你这个脸蛋光光、眼睛亮亮、嘴唇樱桃似的小——」
说到这里，老古董乡绅当真大张旗鼓地「亲」了年轻小姐一口，如他所恫吓的那样，还又亲了两三回；直到被他下巴的白胡茬扎得受不了，她挣脱开，两颊烧得通红，愈发惊惺，逃到了门口。
「道个晚安，好不好，嗯？」乡绅还在笑，磕磕绊绊追着她，把挡道的椅子一 一推开。
「晚安，先生。」她喊着，夺门而出，穿过大厅的拱门，上楼直奔自己房间，一面竭力装出若无其事，颤巍巍急切地唤老仆人：「杜尔西贝拉，你在吗？」听见那慈祥的苍老嗓音、望见她的烛光时，她大大松了口气。
「我说——喂——怎么，丫头，你这是中了什么邪？」楼梯脚下传来老人的喊声。

===Original===

“That’s the trick of you rogues all﻿—ye run away to draw us after; well, it won’t do﻿—another time. I say, good night, ye wild bird.”
“Thank you, sir﻿—good night, sir﻿—good night, sir,” repeated the voice of Alice, higher and higher up the stairs, and he heard her door shut.
He stood with a flushed face, and a sardonic grin for a while, looking up the stairs, with his big bony hand on the banister, and wondering how young he was; and he laughed and muttered pleasantly, and resolved it should all be settled between them next evening; and so again he looked at his watch, and found that she had not gone, after all, earlier than usual, and went back to his fire, and rang the bell, and got a second “nightcap,” as he called his flagon of punch.
Tom remarked how straight the Squire stood that night, with his back to the fire, eyeing him as he entered from the corners of his eyes, with a grin, and a wicked wag of his head.
“A dull dog, Tom. Who’s a-goin’ to hang ye? D﻿—﻿—﻿n ye, look brighter, or I’ll stir ye up with the poker. Never shake your head, man; ye may brew yourself a tankard o’ this, and ye’ll find you’re younger than ye think for, and some of the wenches will be throwing a sheep’s eye at you﻿—who knows?”
Tom did not quite know what to make of this fierce lighting up of gaiety and benevolence. An inquisitive glance he fixed stealthily on his master, and thanked him dubiously﻿—for he was habitually afraid of him; and as he walked away through the passages, he sometimes thought the letter that came that afternoon might have told of the death of old Lady Drayton, or some other relief of the estate; and sometimes his suspicions were nearer to the truth, for in drowsy houses like Wyvern, where events are few, all theses of conversation are valuable and speculation is active, and you may be sure that what was talked of in the town was no mystery in the servants’ hall, though more gossiped over than believed.
Men who are kings in very small dominions are whimsical, as well as imperious﻿—eccentricity is the companion of seclusion﻿—and the Squire had a jealous custom, in his house, which was among the oddities of his despotism; it was simply this: the staircase up which Alice Maybell flew, that night, to old Dulcibella and her room, is that which ascends the northern wing of the house.

===Chinese===

「你们这些小狐狸精全是这路数——跑，是想勾我们追；哼，下回可不成了。我说，晚安，你这只野鸟儿。」
「谢谢您，先生——晚安，先生——晚安，先生。」爱丽丝的声音顺着楼梯越飘越高；他听见她的门关上了。
他满脸涨红，一脸讪笑，在楼梯口立了一阵，大手扒着扶栏，仰头望着，诧异自己竟这样年轻；他笑着，美滋滋地咕哝，打定主意明晚把这一切敲定；又看看表，发觉她今晚到底也没比往常走得早；便回到炉边，拉铃，又要了一缸「睡前酒」——他对他那缸潘趣酒就这么叫。
汤姆注意到，那晚乡绅背炉立得笔直，他进来时，斜着眼角瞟他，咧着嘴，脑袋不怀好意地一晃一晃。
「闷葫芦，汤姆。谁来吊死你？该死的，精神点儿，不然我拿通条给你醒醒。别摇头，汉子；你也自个儿调一缸喝喝，就知道你比自想的年轻，那些婆娘里保准有人朝你递媚眼——谁知道呢？」
老爷身上这阵兴头和恩典的猛火，汤姆一时摸不着头脑。他偷偷觑了主人一眼，将信将疑地谢了——他一向怕他；穿廊子走开时，他一时想，下午到的那封信，多半报了老德雷顿夫人的死讯，或产业上别的什么宽裕事；一时又疑到离真相不远的地方——像怀弗恩这样打瞌睡的宅子，大事稀少，什么话柄都金贵，揣测最活跃；镇上议论的，仆役厅里断无不知之理——只是嚼舌的多，肯信的少。
在小小疆土里称王的人，既专横，又古怪——离群索居，必生乖僻——乡绅在宅里有一道戒备森严的老规矩，也算他专制里的一桩奇处：那晚爱丽丝·梅贝尔飞奔上去找老杜尔西贝拉和她房间的楼梯，正是上宅子北翼的那一道。

===Original===

A strong door in the short passage leading to it from the hall shuts it off from the rest of the building on that level.
For this young lady, then, while she was still a child, Squire Fairfield had easily made an Oriental seclusion in his household, by locking, with his own hand, that door every night, and securing more permanently the doors which, on other levels, afforded access to the same wing.
He had a slight opinion of the other sex, and an evil one of his own, and would have no Romeo and Juliet tragedies. As he locked this door after Miss Alice Maybell’s “good night,” he would sometimes wag his head shrewdly and wink to himself in the lonely oak hall, as he dropped the key into his deep coat pocket﻿—“safe bind, safe find,” “better sure than sorry,” and other wise saws seconding the precaution.
So this night he recollected the key, as usual, which in the early morning, when he drank his glass of beer at his room-door, he handed to old Mrs. Durdin, who turned it in the lock, and restored access for the day.
This custom was too ancient﻿—reaching back beyond her earliest memory﻿—to suggest the idea of an affront, and so it was acquiesced in and never troubled Miss Maybell; the lock was not tampered with, the door was never passed, although the Squire, versed in old saws, was simple to rely on that security against a power that laughs at locksmiths.

===Chinese===

从大厅通向它的一条短过道里，有一道厚实的门，把它同这一层的其余屋子隔开。
当年这位小姐还是个孩子，费尔菲尔德乡绅便轻而易举地在他宅里造出一座东方式的深闺：每夜亲手锁上那道门，其余几层通向同一翼的门，也加了更牢靠的锁。
他对女人看得轻，对男人看得坏，不容家里演罗密欧与朱丽叶。爱丽丝·梅贝尔小姐道过「晚安」，他锁上这道门，在空荡荡的橡木大厅里，有时会精明地一晃脑袋，朝自己眨眨眼，把钥匙丢进外衣深兜——「绑得牢，丢不了」「宁可稳妥，莫图侥幸」，诸般格言都给这道防备作保。
这一夜他照例记得那把钥匙；清晨，他站在房门口喝那一杯啤酒时，把钥匙交给老杜尔丁太太；她拧开锁，这一天的通路便又恢复了。
这规矩太老了——老得超出她最早的记忆——根本引不起受辱的想头；于是人人默认，梅贝尔小姐也从没为它烦过心。锁没被人动过手脚，门没有被人越过——虽说谙熟老格言的乡绅，也天真了些：他指望这一道闩锁，抵挡那个连锁匠都要嘲笑的力量。

## XII The Omen of Carwell Grange / 第十二章 卡韦尔格兰奇的凶兆
===Original===

The high wall that surrounded the courtyard, and the towering foliage of the old trees, were gloomy. Still if the quaint stone front of the house had shown through its many windows the glow of life and welcome, I dare say the effect of those sombre accessories would have been lost in pleasanter associations, and the house might have showed cheerily and cozily enough. As it was, with no relief but the cold moonlight that mottled the pavement and tipped the chimney tops, the silence and deep shadow were chilling, and it needed the deep enthusiasm of true love to see in that dismal frontage the delightful picture that Alice Maybell’s eyes beheld.
“Welcome, darling, to our poor retreat, made bright and beautiful by your presence,” said he, with a gush of tenderness; “but how unworthy to receive you none knows better than your poor Ry. Still for a short time﻿—and it will be but short﻿—you will endure it. Delightful your presence will make it to me; and to you, darling, my love will perhaps render it tolerable. Take my hand, and get down; and welcome to Carwell Grange.”
Lightly she touched the ground, with her hand on his strong arm, for love rather than for assistance.
“I know how I shall like this quaint, quiet place,” said she, “love it, and grow perhaps fit for no other, if only my darling is always with me. You’ll show it all to me in daylight tomorrow﻿—won’t you?”
Their little talk was murmured, and unheard by others, under friendly cover of the snorting horses, and the talk of the men about the luggage.
“But I must get our door opened,” said he with a little laugh; and with the heavy old knocker he hammered a long echoing summons at the door.
In a minute more lights flickered in the hall. The door was opened, and the old woman smiling her best, though that was far from being very pleasant. Her eye was dark and lifeless and never smiled, and there were lines of ill-temper, or worse, near them which never relaxed. Still she was doing her best, dropping little courtesies all the time, and holding her flaring tallow candle in its brass candlestick, and thus illuminating the furrows and minuter wrinkles of her forbidding face with a yellow light that suited its boxwood complexion.

===Chinese===

围着院子的那圈高墙，老树高耸的浓叶，都透着阴沉。话虽如此，倘若宅子那古趣的石砌正面，能从一扇扇窗里透出人气和迎客的暖光，我敢说，这些晦暗的陪衬早化进更可亲的联想里去了，这宅子本也可以显得又亮堂又安适。可眼下，除了冷冷的月光给石板路洒下斑驳、给烟囱顶描一道白边，别无一点亮色；寂静与浓影寒气逼人——要在这样愁惨的门脸上看出一幅赏心悦目的画，非得有真爱情那份深深的痴迷不可；而这画，爱丽丝·梅贝尔的眼睛偏偏看出来了。
「欢迎你，亲爱的，来到我们寒素的栖身之所——有你来，它就亮了，美了。」他满腔柔情涌动地说；「可它怎么配接待你，你可怜的莱比谁都清楚。不过只是短短一阵——只会是短短一阵——你且将就。有你在，它于我就是天堂；而我对你的爱，亲爱的，兴许也能叫你将它将就。拉住我的手，下来吧；欢迎你，来到卡韦尔格兰奇（Carwell Grange）。」
她轻轻落地，手搭在他结实的臂膀上——为的是恩爱，不为攙扶。
「我知道我会怎样喜欢这个古雅清静的地方，」她说，「会爱上它，兴许除了它再住不惯别处——只要我的亲人总陪着我。明天白天你领我处处看一看——好不好？」
这一席小小的絮语压得低低的，旁人听不见——喷着响鼻的马和仆人们搬行李的吆喝，好心替他们打了掩护。
「可得叫开咱们的门了。」他轻声一笑说；举起那沉重的老门环，把门敲得回声悠长。
一分钟后，厅里烛光摇曳。门开了，那老妇人堆出她最好看的笑——虽然离好看还差得远。她的眼睛又黑又死气，从不会笑；眼角边一道道坏脾气的纹路，或是更坏的纹路，也从不见舒展。可她已尽了力，一路小幅地屈膝，举着黄铜烛台上那支烛火熊熊的牛脂蜡烛，就这样，用一层黄光照亮她自己那张拒人的脸上的沟壑和细纹——那黄光，倒正配她那黄杨木似的脸色。

===Original===

Behind her, with another mutton-fat, for this was a state occasion, stood a square-shouldered little girl, some twelve years old, with a brown, somewhat flat face, and no good feature but her dark eyes and white teeth. This was Lilly Dogger, who had been called in to help the crone who stood in the foreground. With a grave, observing stare, she was watching the young lady, who, smiling, stepped into the hall.
“Welcome, my lady﻿—very welcome to Carwell,” said the old woman. “Welcome, Squire, very welcome to Carwell.”
“Thank you very much. I’m sure I shall like it,” said the young lady, smiling happily; “it is such a fine old place; and it’s so quiet﻿—I like quiet.”
“Old enough and quiet enough, anyhow,” answered the old woman. “You’ll not see many new faces to trouble you here, Miss﻿—Ma’am, my lady, I mean.”
“But we’ll all try to make her as pleasant and as comfortable as we can!” said Charles Fairfield, clapping the old woman on the shoulder a little impatiently.
“There don’t lay much in *my* way to make her time pass pleasant, Master Charles; but I suppose we’ll all do what we can?”
“And more we can’t,” said Charles Fairfield. “Come, darling. I suppose there’s a bit of fire somewhere; it’s a little cold, isn’t it?”
“A fire burning all day, sir, in the cedar-room; and the kettle’s a-boiling on the hob, if the lady ’d like a cup o’ tea?”
“Yes, of course,” said Charles; “and a fire in the room upstairs?”
“Yes, so there is, sir, a great fire all day long, and everything well aired.”
“Well, darling, shall we look first at the cedar-room?” he asked, and smiling, hand in hand, they walked through the hall, and by a staircase, and through a second and smaller hall, with a back stair off it, and so into a comfortable panelled room, with a great cheery fire of mingled coal and wood, and old-fashioned furniture, which though faded, was scrupulously neat.
Old and homely as was the room, it agreeably surprised Alice, who was prepared to be delighted with everything, and at sight of this, exclaimed quite in a rapture﻿—so honest a rapture that Charles Fairfield could not forbear laughing, though he felt also very grateful.

===Chinese===

她身后，也举着一支羊脂烛——今儿算大典——立着一个肩膀宽宽的小姑娘，约莫十二岁，棕色脸盘，有些扁，除了黑眼睛和白牙齿，别无可取。这便是莉莉·多格，被叫来给前头这位老太婆帮手的。她一脸正经的、打量的凝视，望着微笑着跨进厅来的年轻小姐。
「欢迎您，夫人——万分欢迎来卡韦尔。」老妇人说。「欢迎您，乡绅，万分欢迎来卡韦尔。」
「多谢。我准会喜欢这儿的。」年轻小姐说着，笑得幸福；「多好的一座老宅子；又这样清静——我就爱清静。」
「够老，也够静，怎么都够了。」老妇人答。「您在这儿不会见着多少生面孔搅扰您，小姐——太太，我是说，夫人。」
「可咱们大伙儿总要尽力叫她住得快活、住得舒坦！」查尔斯·费尔菲尔德说着，有点不耐烦地拍拍老妇人的肩。
「要叫她日子过得快活，*我*这条道上是没什么可添的，查尔斯少爷；不过咱们大伙儿各尽其力，是吧？」
「再多也拿不出了。」查尔斯·费尔菲尔德说。「来，亲爱的。哪儿总生着点火吧；有点冷，是不是？」
「雪松厅里火生了一天了，先生；炉台上的水壶正开着，夫人要不要来杯茶？」
「好啊，当然。」查尔斯说；「楼上房里也有火？」
「有，先生，大火生了一天，样样都熏得干爽。」
「那么，亲爱的，先看看雪松厅？」他问；两人手拉手，含着笑穿过大厅，经过一道楼梯，又穿过第二间小些的厅，厅旁岔着一道后楼梯，便进了这间舒适的镶板房间：煤和柴混烧的一炉旺火，暖融融的，老式家具虽已褪色，却拾掇得一尘不染。
这屋子又老又土，却叫爱丽丝喜出望外——她本就打定主意要喜欢一切；一见这屋子，欢天喜地叫出声来——那欢喜那样实心实意，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德忍俊不禁，心里却又说不出的感激。

===Original===

“Well, I admit,” he said, looking round, “it does look wonderfully comfortable, all things considered; but here, I am afraid, is the beginning and the end of our magnificence﻿—for the present, of course, and by-and-by, little by little, we may improve and extend; but I don’t think in the whole house there’s a habitable room﻿—sitting-room I mean﻿—but this,” he laughed.
“It is the pleasantest room I ever was in, Charlie﻿—a delightful room﻿—I’m more than content,” said she.
“You are a good little creature,” said he, “at all events, the best little wife in the world, determined to make the best of everything, and as I said, we certainly shall be better very soon, and in the meantime, good humour and cheerfulness will make our quarters, poor as they are, brighter and better than luxury and ill-temper could find in a palace. Here are tea-things, and a kettle boiling﻿—very primitive, very cosy﻿—we’ll be more like civilised people tomorrow or next day, when we have had time to look about us, and in the meantime, suppose I make tea while you run upstairs and put off your things﻿—what do you say?”
“Yes, certainly,” and she looked at the old woman, who stood with her ominous smile at the door.
“I ought to have told you her name, Mildred Tarnley﻿—the genius loci. Mildred, you’ll show your mistress to her room.”
And he and his young wife smiled a mutual farewell. A little curious she was to see something more of the old house, and she peeped about her as she went up, and asked a few questions as they went along. “And this room,” she asked, peeping into a door that opened from the back stairs which they were ascending, “it has such a large fireplace and little ovens, or what are they?”
“It was the still-room once, my lady, my mother remembered the time, but it was always shut up in my day.”
“Oh, and can you tell me﻿—I forget﻿—where is my servant?”
“Upstairs, please, with your things, ma’am, when the man brought up your boxes.”
Still looking about her and delaying, she went on. There was nothing stately about this house; but there was that about it which, if Alice had been in less cheerful and happy spirits, would have quelled and awed her. Thick walls, windows deep sunk, double doors now and then, wainscoting, and oak floors, warped with age.
On the landing there was an archway admitting to a gallery.

===Chinese===

「好，我认了。」他环顾着说，「通盘算来，它瞧着确实舒服极了；可只怕咱们的排场，就此起，也到此止——眼下当然如此；往后一点一点，兴许能添置扩充；只是我想，全宅上下，像样的屋——我说的是起居间——除这一间，再没有第二间了。」他笑道。
「这是我住过的最可心的屋子，查理——一间叫人欢喜的屋子——我满足得不能再满足了。」她说。
「你真是个好东西，」他说，「无论如何，是天底下最好的小妻子，事事都往好处安顿；我说过了，咱们很快就会好起来的；这当口，好性子和快活劲，能把咱们这寒舍点得又亮又暖——比坏脾气在宫殿里找见的还强。茶具在这儿，壶开着——原始得很，温馨得很——明儿后儿咱们缓过神来，就更像文明人了；眼下这样吧：我来沏茶，你上楼换衣裳去——怎么样？」
「好，当然。」她朝门口望去；老妇人正立在那里，笑容里带着不祥。
「早该告诉你她叫什么：米尔德里德·塔恩利——本宅的土地神。米尔德里德，领太太去她房里。」
他同年轻妻子相视一笑，互道小别。她对这老宅还有几分好奇，一路上一面走一面张望，问这问那。「这间屋，」她朝后楼梯旁一扇门里窥了一眼，问，「好大的壁炉，还有那些小炉膛，是什么呀？」——她们正顺后楼梯往上走。
「从前是蒸馏房，夫人，我母亲还记得那时候；到我这辈，就一直锁着了。」
「哦，还有——我忘了——我的女仆在哪儿？」
「在楼上呢，回夫人，守着您的物件，汉子们把箱子送上去那会儿。」
她仍旧边走边张望，走走停停。这宅子谈不上堂皇；可它自有一股东西，爱丽丝若不是满怀欢喜幸福，早被它慑住、镇住了：厚墙，深凹的窗，不时一道双重的门，护墙板，还有让岁月拱翘了的橡木地板。
楼梯口有一道拱门，通往一道画廊。

===Original===

In this archway was no door, and, on the landing, Alice Fairfield, as I may now call her, stood for a moment and looked round.
Happy as she was, I cannot tell what effect these faintly lighted glimpses of old and desolate rooms, aided by the repulsive companionship of her ancient guide, may have insensibly wrought upon her imagination, or what a trick that faculty may have just then played upon her senses, but turning round to enter the gallery under the open arch, the old woman standing by her, with the candle raised a little, Alice Fairfield stepped back, startled, with a little exclamation of surprise.
The ugly face of old Mildred Tarnley peeped curiously over the young lady’s shoulder. She stepped before her and peered, right and left, into the gallery; and then, with ominous inquiry into the young lady’s eyes, “I thought it might be a bat, my lady; there was one last night got in,” she said; “but there’s no such a thing now﻿—was you afeared of anything, my lady?”
“I﻿—didn’t you see it?” said the young lady, both frightened and disconcerted.
“I saw’d nothing, ma’am.”
“It’s very odd. I *did* see it; I *swear* I saw it, and felt the air all stirred about my face and dress by it.”
“On here, miss﻿—my lady; was it?”
“Yes; *here*, before us. I﻿—weren’t you looking?”
“Not that way, miss﻿—I don’t know,” she said.
“Well, something fell down before us﻿—all the way﻿—from the top to the bottom of this place.”
And with a slight movement of her hand and eyes, she indicated the open archway before which they stood.
“Oh, lawk! Well, I dare to say it may a bin a fancy, just.”
“Yes; but it’s very odd﻿—a great heavy curtain of black fell down in folds from the top to the floor just as I was going to step through. It seemed to make a little cloud of dust about our feet; and I felt a wind from it quite distinctly.”
“Hey, then it was a *black* curtain, I suppose,” said the old woman, looking hard at her.
“Yes﻿—but why do you suppose so?”
“Sich nonsense is always black, ye know. I see’d nothing﻿—nothing﻿—no more there was nothing. Didn’t ye see me walk through?”
And she stepped back and forward, candle in hand, with an uncomfortable laugh.
“Oh, I know perfectly well there is nothing; but I saw it. I﻿—I wish I hadn’t,” said the young lady.
“I wish ye hadn’t, too,” said Mildred Tarnley, pale and lowering.

===Chinese===

拱门上没有门扇；在楼梯口，爱丽丝·费尔菲尔德——如今可以这么称她了——立了片刻，四下环顾。
她固然快活，可我说不清：那几眼昏暗烛光里荒凉老屋的惊鸿一瞥，加上身旁这位老向导那张拒人的脸，会在不知不觉间对她的想象做了什么手脚；也说不清那份官能，恰在此时对她的感官玩了什么把戏——只在她转身要从敞开的拱门下走进画廊、老妇人立在旁边微微举高蜡烛的那一刻，爱丽丝·费尔菲尔德猛地退了一步，一声惊呼。
老米尔德里德·塔恩利那张丑脸，好奇地从年轻小姐肩头探过来。她抢到前头，朝画廊左右张了张；然后满含不祥地探问进年轻小姐的眼睛：「我当是只蝙蝠呢，夫人；昨儿夜里就飞进来过一只。」她说；「可这会儿什么也没有——夫人可是怕什么了？」
「我——你没看见么？」年轻小姐说，又惊又乱。
「俺什么也没瞧见，太太。」
「太怪了。我*真*看见了；我*起誓*我看见了，还觉出它搅起的风，扑了我一脸、一裙。」
「就在这儿，小姐——夫人；是么？」
「是；就*这儿*，咱们跟前。我——你不是正看着么？」
「没朝那边看，小姐——说不好。」她说。
「可真有个东西掉下来，就在咱们跟前——一路下来——从这地方顶上，直到底下。」
她的手和眼睛轻轻一动，指了指两人面前那道敞开的拱门。
「哎呀！喏，我敢说，兴许就是个眼花，一时的事。」
「是；可太怪了——一幅又大又沉的黑帷幕，一层层叠着，从顶上一直落到地，正赶上我要跨进去。它落下来，像在咱们脚边卷起一小团尘雾；那带起的风，我清清楚楚觉着了。」
「嘿，那敢情是幅*黑*帷幕了。」老妇人说，死死盯着她。
「是——可你怎么这样猜？」
「这路胡话，从来都是黑的，您知道。俺什么也没瞧见——什么也没有——压根儿什么也没有。您没瞧见俺走过去么？」
她举着烛，在拱门下走过去又走回来，笑得叫人不自在。
「哦，我完全明白什么也没有；可我看见了。我——但愿我没看见。」年轻小姐说。
「俺也但愿您没有。」米尔德里德·塔恩利说，脸色发白，阴沉下来。

===Original===

“Them as says their prayers, they needn’t be afeard o’ sich things; and, for my part, I never see’d anything in the Grange, and I’m an old woman, and lived here girl, and woman, good sixty years and more.”
“Let us go on, please,” said Alice.
“At your service, my lady,” said the crone, with a courtesy, and conducted her to her room.

===Chinese===

「凡祷告的人，犯不着怕这些东西；再说俺自个儿，在这格兰奇里什么也没见过；俺是老妇人了，从丫头到老婆子，在这宅里住了足足六十年还多。」
「咱们走吧，劳驾。」爱丽丝说。
「听您吩咐，夫人。」老太婆说着，屈膝一礼，领她去了她的房间。

## VII The Squire’s Eldest Son Comes Home / 第七章 乡绅的长子回家
===Original===

Thus was old Squire Fairfield unexpectedly transformed, and much to the horror of pretty Alice Maybell, appeared in the character of a lover, grim, ungainly, and without the least chance of that brighter transformation which ultimately more than reconciles “beauty” to her conjugal relations with the “beast.”
Grotesque and even ghastly it would have seemed at any time. But now it was positively dismaying, and poor troubled little Alice Maybell, on reaching her room, sat down on the side of her bed, and to the horror and bewilderment of old Dulcibella, wept bitterly and long.
The harmless gabble of the old nurse, who placed herself by her side, patting her all the time upon the shoulder, was as the sound of a humming in the woods in summer time, or the crooning of a brook. Though her ear was hardly conscious of it, perhaps it soothed her.
Next day there was a little stir at Wyvern, for Charles﻿—or as he was oftener called, Captain Fairfield﻿—arrived. This “elderly young gentleman,” as Lady Wyndale called him, led a listless life there. He did not much affect rustic amusements; he fished now and then, but cared little for shooting, and less for hunting. His time hung heavy on his hands, and he did not well know what to do with himself. He smoked and strolled about a good deal, and rode into Wyvern and talked with the townspeople. But the country plainly bored him, and not the less that his sojourn had been in London, and the contrast made matters worse. Alice Maybell had a headache that morning, and not caring to meet the Squire earlier than was inevitable, chose to say so.
The Captain, who, travelling by the mail, had arrived at eight o’clock, took his place at the breakfast-table at nine, and received for welcome a gruff nod from the Squire, and the tacit permission to grasp the knuckles which he grudgingly extended to him to shake.
In that little drama in which the old Squire chose now to figure, his son Charles was confoundedly in the way.
“Well, and what were you doin’ in Lunnon all this time?” grumbled Squire Harry when he had finished his rasher and his cup of coffee, after a long, hard look at Charles, who, in happy unconsciousness, crunched his toast, and read the county paper.
“I beg your pardon, sir, I didn’t hear﻿—you were saying?” said Charles, looking up and lowering the paper.

===Chinese===

老乡绅费尔菲尔德就这样出了人意料地变了身，叫美丽的爱丽丝·梅贝尔惊骇不已地扮起了情郎：冷硬，笨拙，而且丝毫没有指望盼来那一场更明亮的变身——正是那变身，最终才让「美人」心甘情愿地嫁给「野兽」。
这副光景无论何时看着都荒诞，甚至可怖；此刻更是叫人胆寒。可怜的、心乱如麻的小爱丽丝·梅贝尔回到房里，在床沿坐下，也不管老杜尔西贝拉又惊又惑，痛痛快快哭了老半天。
老乳母挨着她坐下，一路轻轻拍她的肩，那些无害的絮叨，像夏日林中的嗡鸣，像小溪的低唱。她的耳朵几乎没听进去，可兴许正被这声音熨帖着。
第二天，怀弗恩起了一点小骚动：查尔斯——或者说费尔菲尔德上尉，人们更常这么叫他——到了。这位温代尔夫人口中的「上了年纪的年轻绅士」，在宅里过着百无聊赖的日子。乡下的消遣他不大沾；偶尔钓钓鱼，打猎没什么兴致，猎狐更少。光阴在他手上坠得沉，他自己也不知道把自己怎么办。他抽抽烟，多散散步，骑马进怀弗恩镇上同人聊聊。可乡下分明叫他发闷；先前他客居的是伦敦，这一比就更闷。爱丽丝·梅贝尔那天早晨害了头疼——她也不想比躲不掉的更早见乡绅，便索性照实说了。
上尉坐邮车，八点到的；九点入席早餐，得到的欢迎是乡绅粗声粗气的一点头，和一次默许的握手——那几个指节是勉勉强强递过来的。
在老乡绅如今选定出演的这出小戏里，儿子查尔斯碍事碍得厉害。
「喂，你这一向在伦敦都干了些什么？」哈利乡绅用完咸肉片和咖啡，朝查尔斯狠狠盯了半晌，闷声问；查尔斯浑然不觉，嚼着吐司，看郡报。
「对不起，先生，没听清——您说什么？」查尔斯抬头，放下报纸。

===Original===

“Hoo﻿—yes﻿—I was saying, I don’t think you went all the way to Lunnon to say your prayers in St. Paul’s; you’ve bin losing money in those hells and places; when your pocket’s full away you go and leave it wi’ them town blackguards, and back you come as empty as a broken sack to live on me, and so on. Come, now, how much rent do you take by the year from that place your fool of a mother left ye﻿—the tartar!﻿—hey?”
“I think, sir, about three hundred a year,” answered Charles.
“Three hundred *and eighty*,” said the old man, with a grin and a wag of his head. “I’m not so old that I can’t remember *that*﻿—three hundred and eighty; and ye flung that away in Lunnon taverns and operas, on dancers and dicers, and ye come back here without a shillin’ left to bless yourself, to ride my horses and drink my wine; and ye call that fair play. Come along, here.”
And, followed by his mastiff, he marched stiffly out of the room.
Charles was surprised at this explosion, and sat looking after the grim old man, not knowing well what to make of it, for Squire Harry was openhearted enough, and never counted the cost of his hospitalities, and had never grudged him his home at Wyvern before.
“Much he knows about it,” thought Charles; “time enough, though. If I’m de trop here I can take my portmanteau and umbrella, and make my bow and go cheerfully.”
The tall Captain, however, did not look cheerful, but pale and angry, as he stood up and kicked the newspaper, which fell across his foot, fiercely. He looked out of the window, with one hand in his pocket, in sour rumination. Then he took his rod and flies and cigar-case, and strolled down to the river, where, in that engrossing and monotonous delight, celebrated of old by Venables and Walton, he dreamed away the dull hours.
Blessed resource for those mysterious mortals to whom nature accords it﻿—stealing away, as they wander solitary along the devious riverbank, the memory, the remorse, and the miseries of life, like the flow and music of the shadowy Lethe.
This Captain did not look like the man his father had described him﻿—an anxious man, rather than a man of pleasure﻿—a man who was no sooner alone than he seemed to brood over some intolerable care, and, except during the exercise of his “gentle craft,” his looks were seldom happy or serene.
The hour of dinner came. A party of three, by no means well assorted.

===Chinese===

「嗬——是——我是说，你千里迢迢上伦敦，总不是去圣保罗教堂做祷告的吧；你是在那些赌窟什么的里头输钱；口袋一满你就去，把钱掠给城里那帮恶棍，回来时瘪得像只破口袋，吃我的喝我的，如此等等。说吧，你那个傻瓜老妈子留给你的地方，一年收多少租——那母夜叉！——嗯？」
「我想，先生，一年约莫三百。」查尔斯答。
「三百*八*十。」老人说，咧嘴一赃牙，脑袋一晃。「我还没老到记不住*这个*——三百八十；你把这笔钱扔进了伦敦的酒馆和戏园子，扔给了跳舞的和掷骰子的；回到这儿，一个先令不剩，连声谢天都谢不起，来骑我的马、喝我的酒；你还管这叫公道。跟我来。」
说罢，大獒犬跟着他，他梗着脖子大步出了屋。
这一顿爆发叫查尔斯摸不着头脑，他坐在那儿望着老人的背影发怔：哈利乡绅为人向来爽快，待客从不计较花销，从前也从没嫌弃过他住怀弗恩。
「他哪知道底细，」查尔斯想，「不过来日方长。这儿若容不下我，我提起旅行箱和伞，鞠一躬，走得痛痛快快。」
可是高个子上尉的神情并不痛快；他站起身，狠狠一脚踢开落在脚面的报纸，脸色苍白，满腔怒气。他一手插兜，望着窗外，酸溜溜地出神。随后他拿了钓竿、毛钩和雪茄匣，踱到河边；在那门古老而单调的乐事里——维纳布尔斯和沃尔顿当年笔下的名堂——把沉闷的时辰梦了过去。
于那些得天独厚的神秘凡人，这是一份有福的排遣——他们独自沿曲折的河岸游荡，记忆、悔恨和人生的种种苦楚，便如冥河的暗流和低唱，被一路悄悄偷走。
这位上尉，不像他父亲形容的那种人——与其说是寻欢作乐之徒，不如说是个心事重重的人——一独处，就像有一桩熬不住的牵挂压上眉头；除了操练他那门「高雅技艺」的时候，他的脸色少有快活和安宁。
晚膳的时辰到了。三人一席，怎么也凑不到一处。

===Original===

The old Squire in no genial mood and awfully silent. Charles silent and abstracted too; his body sitting there eating its dinner, and his soul wandering with black care and other phantoms by far-off Styx. The young lady had her own thoughts to herself, uncomfortable thoughts.
At last the Squire spoke to the intruder, with a look that might have laid him in the Red Sea.
“In my time young fellows were more alive, and had something to say for themselves. I don’t want your talk myself over my victuals, but you should ’a spoke to *her*﻿—’tisn’t civil﻿—’tweren’t the way in my day. I don’t think ye asked her ‘How are ye?’ since ye came back. Lunnon manners, maybe.”
“Oh, but I assure you I did. I could not have made such an omission. Alice will tell you I was not quite so stupid,” said Charles, raising his eyes, and looking at her.
“Not that it signifies, mind ye, the crack of a whip, whether ye did or no,” continued the Squire; “but ye may as well remember that ye’re not brother and sister exactly, and ye’ll call her Miss Maybell, and not Alice no longer.”
The Captain stared. The old Squire looked resolutely at the brandy-flask from which he was pouring into his tumbler. Alice Maybell’s eyes were lowered to the edge of her plate, and with the tip of her finger she fiddled with the crumbs on the tablecloth. She did not know what to say, or what might be coming.
So soon as the Squire had quite compounded his brandy-and-water he lifted his surly eyes to his son with a flush on his aged cheek, and wagged his head with oracular grimness, and silence descended again for a time upon the three kinsfolk.
This uncomfortable party, I suppose, were off again, each on their own thoughts, in another minute. But no one said a word for some time.
“By the by, Alice﻿—Miss Maybell, I mean﻿—I saw in London a little picture that would have interested you,” said the Captain, “an enamelled miniature of Marie Antoinette, a pretty little thing, only the size of your watch; you can’t think how spirited and beautiful it was.”
“And why the dickens didn’t ye buy it, and make her a compliment of it? Much good tellin’ her how pretty it was,” said the Squire, sulkily; “ ’twasn’t for want o’ money.

===Chinese===

老乡绅全无好兴致，沉默得吓人。查尔斯也沉默、出神；身子坐在那儿用膳，魂魄却跟着黑色的忧虑和别的幽灵，荡到遥远的冥河边去了。年轻小姐自怀着自家的心事——些不痛快的心事。
终于，乡绅朝那位碍事的人开了口，那一眼，险些把他看沉到红海底。
「我们那时候，小伙子们活泛得多，肚里有话拿得出话。我吃饭不要你陪我说话，可你总该同*她*讲讲——这才叫礼数——我们那时候是这个规矩。我看你回来了连一句『你好』都没问过她。伦敦派头，兴许。」
「哦，我向您保证，问过。这样的疏漏我犯不出。爱丽丝可以作证，我没那么蠢。」查尔斯说着，抬眼看她。
「做没做，也不过是马鞭响一声的事，记着。」乡绅接着说；「可你最好记着，你们俩究竟不是兄妹；往后叫她梅贝尔小姐，不许再叫爱丽丝。」
上尉瞪直了眼。老乡绅一门心思盯着白兰地酒瓶，只顾往杯里斟。爱丽丝·梅贝尔的眼帘垂到盘沿，指尖拨弄着桌布上的面包屑。她不知该说什么，也不知接下来还有什么。
乡绅兑好那杯掺水白兰地，把阴沉的眼睛抬起，望向儿子，老颊上升起一抹红，神谕似的狠狠一点头；沉默又降临到这三位亲人头上，压了一阵。
我料想，这一席坐立难安的人，不出一分钟又各奔各的心事去了。可有好一阵，谁也不吐一个字。
「对了，爱丽丝——我是说梅贝尔小姐——我在伦敦见着一幅小画，您见了准感兴趣，」上尉说，「一幅玛丽·安托瓦内特的珐琅袖珍像，精巧的小东西，才怀表那么大；它多有神、多美，您想都想不到。」
「见鬼，为什么不当场买下，讨她的好？光夸它漂亮顶什么用，」乡绅赌着气说；「又不缺那几个钱。」

===Original===

D﻿—﻿— it, in my day a young fellow ’d be ashamed to talk of such a thing without he had it in his pocket to make an offer of;” and the old Squire muttered sardonically to his brandy-and-water, and neither Miss Alice nor Captain Fairfield knew well what to say. The old man seemed bent on extinguishing every little symptom of a lighting up of the gloom which his presence induced.
They came at last into the drawing-room. The Squire took his accustomed place by the fire. In due time came his “nightcap.” Miss Alice played his airs over and over on the piano. The Captain yawned stealthily into his hand at intervals, and at last stole away.
“Well, Ally, here we are at last, girl. That moping rascal’s gone to his bed; I thought he’d never ’a gone. And now come here, ye little fool, I want to talk to ye. Come, I say, what the devil be ye afeared on? I’d like to see the fellow ’d be uncivil to you. My wife, as soon as the lawyers can write out the parchments, the best settlements has ever bin made on a Fairfield’s wife since my great uncle’s time. Why, ye look as frightened, ye pretty little fool, as if I was a-going to rob ye, instead of making ye lady o’ Wyvern, and giving ye every blessed thing I have on earth. That’s right!”
He had taken her timid little hand in his bony and tremulous grasp.
“I’ll have ye grander than any that ever has been”﻿—he was looking in her face with an exulting glare of admiration﻿—“and I’ll give ye the diamonds for your own, mind, and I’ll have your picture took by a painter. There was never a lady o’ Wyvern fit to hold a candle to ye, and I’m a better man than half the young fellows that’s going; and ye’ll do as ye like﻿—wi’ servants, and house and horses and all﻿—I’ll deny ye in nothing. And why, sweetheart, didn’t you come down this morning? Was you ailing, child﻿—was pretty Ally sick in earnest?”
“A headache, sir. I﻿—I have it still﻿—if﻿—if you would not mind, I’ll be better, sir, in my room. I’ve had a very bad headache. It will be quite well, I daresay, by tomorrow. You are very kind, sir; you have always been very kind, sir; I never can thank you﻿—never, never, sir, as I feel.”
“Tut, folly, nonsense, child; wait till all’s done, and thank me then, if ye will. I’ll make ye as fine as the queen, and finer.”

===Chinese===

「该死，我们那时候，小伙子口袋里不揣着现成的、开得了口的东西，是羞于把这种话挂在嘴上的。」老乡绅对着他那杯掺水白兰地冷嘲热讽地咕哝；爱丽丝小姐和费尔菲尔德上尉都不知说什么好。老人像是打定主意，要把他亲手罩下的这团阴郁里每一星亮光，都掐灭了才罢。
末了他们进了客厅。乡绅在炉边坐了老位子。到了时辰，他的「睡前酒」来了。爱丽丝小姐在钢琴上把他的调子一遍遍弹。上尉时不时用手掩着嘴偷打哈欠，终于悄悄溜了。
「好了，艾莉，总算剩咱们俩了，丫头。那个闷葫芦上他床去了；我还当他一辈子不去睡呢。过来，小傻瓜，我有话同你说。来呀，你到底怕个什么鬼？谁敢对你无礼，我倒要见见。我的妻子——只等律师们把羊皮文书誊出来——自有我叔祖爷那年起，费尔菲尔德家娶妇，再没立过比你更厚的婚产。嗐，你那样子吓的，漂亮的小傻瓜，倒像我要抢你，不是要立你做怀弗恩的夫人，把我世上样样有福的东西都给你。这就对了！」
他那把嶙峋而发颤的手，已把她怯生生的小手攝住了。
「我要把你打扮得比历来的都阔气」——他直望着她的脸，一脸得意的、灼灼的激赏——「钻石给你，归你自己，记着；还要请画师给你画肖像。怀弗恩历来的夫人，没一个配给你提鞋；我也比眼下那班小伙子一半人都强；你爱怎样就怎样——仆人、宅子、马匹，一应——我一样不驳你。说吧，心肝，今早怎么不下来？身子不爽么，孩子——漂亮的艾莉真病了？」
「头疼，先生。我——现在还疼——要是——要是您不见怪，我回房里会好些，先生。头疼得厉害。明天一早，我敢说就全好了。您真好，先生；您一向待我真好，先生；您的恩情我报答不尽——永远报答不尽，先生，我心里有数。」
「去，傻话，胡说，孩子；等一切办妥了，再谢不迟。我要把你装扮得同王后一样，比王后还强。」

===Original===

Every now and then he emphasized his harangue by kissing her cheeks and lips, which added to her perplexity and terror, and made her skin flame with the boisterous rasp of his stubbled chin. “And ye’ll be my little duchess, my beauty; ye will, my queen o’ diamonds, you roguey-poguey-woguey, as cunning as a dog-fox;” and in the midst of these tumultuous endearments she managed to break away from the amorous ogre, and was out of the door, and up the stairs to her room, and old Dulcibella, before his tardy pursuit had reached the cross-door.
An hour has passed, and the young lady stood up, and placing her arms about her neck, kissed old Dulcibella.
“Will you take a candle, darling,” she said, “and go down and see whether the cross-door is shut?”
Down went Dulcibella, the stairs creaking under her, and the young lady, drying her eyes, looked at her watch, drew the curtain at the window, placed the candle on the table near it, and then, shading her eyes with her hand, looked out earnestly.
The window did not command the avenue, it was placed in the side of the house. A moonlighted view she looked out upon; a soft declivity, from whose grassy slopes rose grand old trees, some in isolation, some in groups of twos and threes, all slumbering in the hazy light and still air, and beyond rose, softer in the distance, gentle undulating uplands, studded with trees, and near their summits, more thickly clothed in forest.
She opened the window softly, and looking out, sighed in the fresh air of night, and heard from the hollow the distant rush and moan of running waters, and her eye searched the foreground of this landscape. The trunk of one of the great trees near the house seemed to become animated, and projected a human figure, nothing awful or ghastly﻿—a man in a short cloak, with a wide-awake hat on. Seeing the figure in the window, he lifted his hand, looking towards her, and approaching the side of the house with caution, glanced this way and that till he reached the house.
The old servant at the same time returned and told her that the door *was* locked as usual.
“You remain here, Dulcibella﻿—no﻿—I shan’t take a candle,” and with a heavy sigh she left the room, and treading lightly descended the stairs, and entered a wainscoted room, on the ground floor﻿—with two windows, through which came a faint reflected light.

===Chinese===

他隔几句便亲她的脸颊和嘴，给他这篇演说加着重号；这更添了她的慌乱和恐怖，他胡茬森森的下巴蹭得她皮肤火烧火燎。「你要做我的小公爵夫人，我的美人儿；你要做的，我的钻石王后，你这个小滑头、小精怪、小狐狸精，鬼得像条老公狐」；就在这一片翻江倒海的恩爱中间，她挣脱了那头好色的老妖，出了门，上了楼，扑到自己房里和老杜尔西贝拉身边——他那阵迟缓的追赶，才刚到十字门。
一个钟头过去，年轻小姐站起身，搂住老杜尔西贝拉的脖子，吻了她。
「拿支蜡烛，亲爱的，」她说，「下去看看十字门关了没有。」
杜尔西贝拉下去了，楼梯在她脚下吱呀作响；年轻小姐擦干眼睛，看了看表，拉好窗帘，把烛台搁在近旁的桌上，然后手搭凉棚，凝神朝外望。
这扇窗看不见林荫道，开在宅子侧面。她望出去是一片月光中的景致：一道舒缓的坡，草坡上升起一棵棵堂皇的老树，有的独立，有的两三成群，全都睡在朦胧的光和止住的空气里；再远处，柔和些，隆起一带起伏的高地，点缀着树，近顶处更是密密一层林。
她轻轻推开窗，探身外望，在清新的夜气里叹了口气；听得洼地深处远远的水声奔涌低吟。她的眼睛在这片景致的前景里搜寻。近宅一棵大树的树干像忽然活了，投出一条人影——毫无可怖之处——一个披短斗篷、戴阔檐软帽的男人。他望见窗中人影，举起一只手，朝她望着，小心向宅子侧面挪近，东张西望，一路到了宅前。
老仆人恰好回来，告诉她那扇门*果然*照常锁着。
「你在这儿等着，杜尔西贝拉——不——我不带蜡烛。」她重重一叹，出了房，放轻脚步下了楼，进了底层一间镶板屋子——两扇窗，透进一线淡淡的反光。

===Original===

Standing close to the nearer of these was the man with whom she had exchanged from the upper room the signals I have mentioned.

===Chinese===

靠着近处那扇窗立着的，正是方才她在楼上同他打过暗号的那个人。

## XXIV The Summons / 第二十四章 召唤
===Original===

When Charles Fairfield came into the wainscoted dining-room a few minutes later it looked very cosy. The sun had broken the pile of western clouds, and sent low and level a red light flecked with trembling leaves on the dark panels that faced the windows.
Outside in that farewell glory of the day the cawing crows were heard returning to the sombre woods of Carwell, and the small birds whistled and warbled pleasantly in the clear air, and chatty sparrows in the ivy round gossiped and fluttered merrily before the little community betook themselves to their leafy nooks and couched their busy little heads for the night under their brown wings.
He looked through the window towards the gloriously-stained sky and darkening trees, and he thought﻿—
“A fellow like me, who has seen out his foolish days and got to value better things, who likes a pretty view, and a cigar, and a stroll by a trout-brook, and a song now and then, and a book, and a friendly guest, and a quiet glass of wine, and who has a creature like Alice to love and be loved by, might be devilish happy in this queer lonely corner, if only the load were off his heart.”
He sighed; but something of that load was for the moment removed; and as pretty Alice came in at the open door, he went to meet her, and drew her fondly to his heart.
“We must be very happy this evening, Alice. Somehow I feel that everything will go well with us yet. If just a few little hitches and annoyances were got over, I should be the happiest fellow, I think, that ever bore the name of Fairfield; and you, darling creature, are the light of that happiness. My crown and my life﻿—my beautiful Alice, my joy and my glory﻿—I wish you knew half how I love you, and how proud I am of you.”
“Oh, Charlie, Charlie, this is delightful. Oh, Ry, my darling! I’m too happy.”
And with these words, in the strain of her slender embrace, she clung to him as he held her locked to his heart.
The affection was there; the love was true. In the indolent nature of Charles Fairfield capabilities of good were not wanting. That dreadful interval in the soul’s history, between the weak and comparatively noble state of childhood and that later period when experience saddens and illuminates and begins to turn our looks regretfully backward, was long past with him.

===Chinese===

几分钟后查尔斯·费尔菲尔德走进镶板餐室，屋里瞧着温馨极了。太阳冲开西天那一堆云，把一道低低的、平射的红光送进来，颤动的树叶影子斑斑点点，落在朝窗的深色护壁板上。
外面，在白日告别的辉煌里，聒噪的乌鸦一阵阵叫着，归卡韦尔阴沉的林子去；小鸟在清朗的空气里啼啭得可爱；常春藤间多嘴的麻雀叽叽喳喳、扑扑棱棱，热闹够了，这一小群才各归叶间的窝，把忙碌的小脑袋收进棕色翅膀底下过夜。
他隔着窗，望向那染得辉煌的天空和暗下来的树，心想——
「像我这样的人，荒唐日子熬尽了头，学会看重些更好的东西；爱看景致，抽支雪茄，鳟鱼溪边遛遛，不时听支歌，读本书，有位投缘的客，静静喝上一杯；又有爱丽丝这样一个人儿可疼、也疼我——只消心上那副担子卸了，在这个古怪的冷角落里，也尽可以快活似神仙。」
他叹了口气；可那担子这会儿竟真的卸下了一角。美丽的爱丽丝从敞开的门进来，他迎上去，把她深情地搂到心口。
「今晚咱们要痛痛快快，爱丽丝。不知怎么，我觉得咱们的好运还在后头。只要再迈过几道小坎、几桩烦事，我想我就是姓费尔菲尔德的人里头最快活的一个；而你，亲爱的宝贝，就是那份快活的光。我的冠冕，我的性命——我美丽的爱丽丝，我的欢喜，我的荣耀——但愿你知道我一半怎样爱你，怎样为你骄傲。」
「哦，查理，查理，这太好了。哦，莱，我的亲人！我快活死了。」
说着，她纤纤的双臂紧紧拢住他；他把她锁在心口，她恨着不动。
情是真的；爱也是真的。查尔斯·费尔菲尔德疏懒的天性里，向善的根苗并不缺。灵魂史上那段可怕的间隔——一头是童年柔弱而相对高洁的状态，一头是后来阅历使人悲、使人明、教人开始留恋回望的时期——在他早已远过去了。

===Original===

The period when women “come out” and see the world, and men in the old-fashioned phrase “sow their wild oats”﻿—that glorious summertime of self-love, sin, and folly﻿—that bleak and bitter winter of the soul, through which the mercy of God alone preserves for us alive the dormant germs of good, was past for him, without killing, as it sometimes does, all the tenderness and truth of the nursery. In this man, Charles Fairfield, were the trodden-down but still living affections which now, in this season, unfolded themselves anew﻿—simplicity unkilled, and the purity not of Eden, not of childhood, but of *recoil*. Altogether a man who had not lost himself﻿—capable of being happy﻿—capable of being regenerated.
I know not exactly what had evoked this sudden glow and effervescence. Perhaps it needs some manifold confluence of internal and external conditions, trifling and unnoticed, except for such unexplained results, to evolve these tremblings and lightings up that surprise us like the fiercer analogies of volcanic chemistry.
It is sad to see what appear capabilities and opportunities of a great happiness so nearly secured, and yet by reason of some inflexible caprice of circumstance quite unattainable.
It was not for some hours, and until after his wife had gone to her room, that the darkness and chill that portended the return of his worst care crept over him as he sat and turned over the leaves of his book.
He got up and loitered discontentedly about the room. Stopping now before the little bookshelves between the windows and adjusting unconsciously their contents; now at the little oak table, and fiddling with the flowers which Alice had arranged in a tall old glass, one of the relics of other days of Carwell; and so on, listless, irresolute.
“So here I am once more﻿—back again among my enemies! Happiness for me, a momentary illusion﻿—hope a cheat. My *reality* is the blackness of the abyss. God help me!”
He turned up his eyes, and he groaned this prayer, unconscious that it was a prayer.
“I will,” he thought, “extract the sting from this miserable mystery. Between me and Alice it shall be a secret no longer. I’ll tell her tomorrow. I’ll look out an opportunity; I will by﻿—”
And to nail himself to his promise this irresolute man repeated the same passionate oath, and he struck his hand on the table.

===Chinese===

女子「出来见世面」、男子照老话「撒野燕麦」的那段年纪——自私、罪愆与愚行那场辉煌的夏季——灵魂那场荒寒苦楚的寒冬（唯独上帝的慈悲，才替我们把向善的休眠种子活着保存过去）——他也熬过来了，而且没有像有些人那样，把育儿室里全部的温情和真话一并冻死。查尔斯·费尔菲尔德这个人身上，那些被踩倒却仍活着的情意，如今在这个季节里重新舒展开来——天真未灭；还有一种纯净，不是伊甸的，不是童年的，而是*回头是岸*的。总而言之，这是一个不曾把自己丢掉的人——还快活得起来——还洗得回来。
我说不准是什么唤起了这阵突发的暖意和沸腾。也许，须得内外种种条件千流汇聚——它们琐屑得无人留意，若非结出这类无由解释的果实——才会酝酿出这样的震颤和亮起，像火山化学那些更烈的表亲，叫我们措手不及。
一场大幸福，眼看种种机缘都齐备了、只差一线，却因世局某一点不肯通融的乖张，终究求而不得——看着最叫人难过。
过了几个钟头，妻子回房之后，他坐着翻书，那阵预示头号心事回潮的阴冷，才悄悄爬上他心头。
他起身，惻惻地在屋里踱。一时停在窗间的小书架前，无意识地摆弄架上的书；一时停在小橡木桌旁，拨弄爱丽丝插在一只高脚老玻璃瓶里的花——那是卡韦尔旧日的遗物之一；如此这般，无精打采，主意全无。
「瞧，我又回到这儿了——又落回仇人中间！幸福于我，一场转瞬的幻象——希望是个骗子。我的*实在*是深渊的黑。上帝救我！」
他抬起眼，呻吟出这一句祷告，自己不觉得那是祷告。
「我要，」他想，「把这桩惨事的毒刺拔出来。我同爱丽丝之间，不许它再是秘密。明天我就告诉她。我要寻个机会；我发誓——」
为了把自己钉上这句许诺，这个没主见的人把那句狠誓又念了一遍，一巴掌拍在桌上。

===Original===

Next day, therefore, when Alice was again among the flowers in the garden he entered that antique and solemn shade with a strange sensation at his heart of fear and grief. How would Alice look on him after it was over? How would she bear it?
Pale as the man who walks after the coffin of his darling, between the tall gray piers he entered that wild and umbrageous enclosure.
His heart seemed to stop still as he saw little Alice, all unsuspicious of his dreadful message, working with her tiny trowel at the one sunny spot of the garden.
She stood up﻿—how pretty she was!﻿—looking on her work; and as she stood with one tiny foot advanced, and her arms folded, with her garden-gloves on, and the little diamond-shaped trowel glittering in her hand, she sang low to herself an air which he remembered her singing when she was quite a little thing long ago at Wyvern﻿—when he never dreamed she would be anything to him﻿—just a picture of a little brown-haired girl and nothing dearer.
Then she saw him, and﻿—
“Oh, Ry, darling!” she cried, as making a diagonal from the distant point, she ran towards him through tall trees and old raspberries, and under the boughs of overgrown fruit trees, which nowadays bore more moss and lichen than pears or cherries upon them.
“Ry, how delightful! You so seldom come here, and now I have you, you shall see all I’m doing, and how industrious I have been; and we are going to have such a happy little ramble. Has anything happened, darling?” she said, suddenly stopping and looking in his face.
Here was an opportunity; but if his resolution was still there, presence of mind failed him, and forcing a smile, he instantly answered﻿—
“Nothing, darling﻿—nothing whatever. Come, let us look at your work; you are so industrious, and you have such wonderful taste.”

===Chinese===

于是第二天，爱丽丝又在园中花间忙时，他走进了那片古旧肃穆的浓荫，心口压着一股又怕又痛的异样。事过之后，爱丽丝会怎样看他？她受得住么？
他脸色苍白，像跟在亲人棺后送葬的人，从高高的灰石柱间，走进了那片野趣浓荫的园地。
他望见小爱丽丝正在园中唯一向阳的一角，拿小铲子松土，全然想不到他带来的是怎样可怕的话——他的心像停住了。
她直起身——多俊俏啊！——端详着自己的活计；她一只小脚在前，双臂拢着，戴着园艺手套，那柄菱形小铲在手里闪着光，低低给自己哼着一支调子——他记得，多年前她还是个小不点儿的时候，在怀弗恩就哼过这支——那时他做梦也没想到她同自己会有什么干系——不过是一幅棕发小姑娘的画，再无更深的意思。
随后她看见了他——
「哦，莱，亲爱的！」她喊道，从远处斜插过来，穿过高树和老悬钩子丛，从疯长的果树底下跑向他——那些树如今结的苔藓和地衣，比梨和樱桃都多。
「莱，多好呀！你难得来这儿；这下逮着你了，我要让你看看我忙的这些，我多勤快；咱们还要痛痛快快遛一小圈。出什么事了么，亲爱的？」她说着，猛地站住，望着他的脸。
机会就在眼前；可决心纵然还在，临场的胆气却没了；他硬挤出一个笑，立刻答——
「没事，亲爱的——什么事也没有。来，看看你的活计；你这样勤快，品味又这样好。」

===Original===

And as, reassured, and holding his hand, she prattled and laughed, leading him round by the grass-grown walks to her garden, as she called that favoured bit of ground on which the sun shone, he hardly saw the old currant bushes or gray trunks of the rugged trees; his sight seemed dazzled; his hearing seemed confused; and he thought to himself﻿—
“Where am I﻿—what is this﻿—and can it be true that I am so weak or so mad as to be turned from the purpose over which I have been brooding for a day and a night, and to which I had screwed my courage so resolutely, by a smile and a question﻿—What is this? Black currant; and this is groundsel; and little Alice, your glove wants a stitch or two,” he added aloud; “and oh! here we are. Now you must enlighten me; and what a grove of little sticks, and little inscriptions. These are your annuals, I suppose?”
And so they talked, and she laughed and chatted very merrily, and he had not the heart﻿—perhaps the courage﻿—to deliver his detested message; and again it was postponed.
The next day Charles Fairfield fell into his old gloom and anxieties; the temporary relief was felt no more, and the usual reaction followed.
It is something to have adopted a resolution. The anguish of suspense, at least, is ended, and even if it be to undergo an operation, and to blow one’s own brains out, men will become composed, and sometimes even cheerful, as the coroner’s inquest discovers, when once the way and the end are known.
But this melancholy serenity now failed Charles Fairfield, for without acknowledging it, he began a little to recede from his resolution. Then was the dreadful question, how will she bear it, and even worse, how will she view the position? Is she not just the person to leave forthwith a husband thus ambiguously placed, and to insist that this frightful claim, however shadowy, should be met and determined in the light of day?
“I know very well what an idol she makes of me, poor little thing; but she would not stay here an hour after she heard it; she would go straight to Lady Wyndale. It would break her heart, but she would do it.”
It was this fear that restrained him. Impelling him, however, was the thought that, sooner or later, if Harry’s story were true, his enemy would find him out, and his last state be worse than his first.

===Chinese===

她放了心，牵着他的手，叽叽喳喳笑着，领他沿长草的小径绕到她说的「园子」——那块得太阳青睐的宝地；老茶蔍子丛、虬树的灰干，他几乎视而不见；眼像花了，耳像乱了；他心里想——
「我在哪儿——这是怎么了——难道我当真这样软弱、这样疯，苦熬了一天一夜的主意、咬牙攒足的胆气，竟叫一个微笑、一句问话就拐跑了——这是什么？黑茶蔍子；这是千里光；小爱丽丝，你的手套该补两针了。」他高声添道；「哦！到了。这下你得开导开导我；好一片小枝条的林子，还带着一块块小牌子。这些是你的一年生草花吧？」
两人就这样谈着；她又说又笑，兴致极高；他到底狠不下心——也许是鼓不起胆——把那句可憎的话说出口；于是又推了一天。
第二天，查尔斯·费尔菲尔德又落回旧日的阴郁和焦灼里；那阵一时的松快无影无踪，照例的反扑跟着来了。
主意拿定，总是桩事。至少，悬而不决的煎熬到头了；哪怕定下的是挨一刀、是崩掉自己的脑子，只要路数和结局都明确了，人也会安下来，有时甚至快活起来——验尸官的研讯里常见这样的发现。
可这份愁惨的安宁，如今也不肯眷顾查尔斯·费尔菲尔德了：他嘴上不认，心里却开始一点点从决心上退下来。接着便是那个可怕的问题：她受得住么？更糟的是，她会怎样看待这处境？处在这种不明不白地位上的丈夫，她岂不正是那种立刻就离开的人？岂不正是那种坚持要把这骇人的主张——管它多么影影绰绰——摆到大天亮底下来对质、来断清的人？
「她把我当神像供着，这个我清楚得很，可怜的小东西；可这话一入她的耳，她在这儿一个钟头也待不住；她会径直投温代尔夫人去。那要碎了她的心，可她会那么做。」
拦住他的，正是这份怕。推着他的，却是另一个念头：哈利的话若是不假，那个仇家早晚寻得着他；到那时，他的下场比先前还要糟。

===Original===

Again and again he cursed his own folly for not having consulted his shrewd brother before his marriage. How horribly were his words justified. How easy it would have been comparatively to disclose all to Alice before leading her into such a position. He did not believe that there was actual danger in this claim. He could swear that he meant no villainy. Weak and irresolute, in a trying situation, he had been﻿—that was all. But could he be sure that the world would not stigmatize him as a villain?
Another day passed, and he could not tell what a day might bring﻿—a day of feverish melancholy, of abstraction, of agitation.
She had gone to her room. It was twelve o’clock at night, when, having made up his mind to make his agitating shrift, he mounted the old oak stairs, with his candle in his hand.
“Who’s there?” said his wife’s voice from the room.
“I, darling.”
And at the door she met him in her dressing-gown. Her face was pale and miserable, and her eyes swollen with crying.
“Oh, Ry, darling, I’m so miserable; I think I shall go mad.”
And she hugged him fast in trembling arms, and sobbed convulsively on his breast.
Charles Fairfield froze with a kind of terror. He thought, “she has found out the whole story.” She looked up in his face, and that was the face of a ghost.
“Oh, Ry, darling, for God’s sake tell me﻿—is there anything very bad﻿—is it debt only that makes you so wretched? I am in such dreadful uncertainty. Have mercy on your poor little miserable wife, and tell me whatever it is﻿—tell me all!”
Here you would have said was something more urgent than the opportunity which he coveted; but the sight of that gaze of wildest misery smote and terrified him, it looked in reality so near despair, so near insanity.
“To tell her will be to kill her,” something seemed to whisper, and he drew her closer to him, and kissed her and laughed.
“Nothing on earth but money﻿—the want of money﻿—debt. Upon my soul you frightened me, Alice, you looked so, so piteous. I thought you had something dreadful to tell me; but, thank God, you are quite well, and haven’t even seen a ghost. You must not always be such a foolish little creature. I’m afraid this place will turn our heads. Here we are safe and sound, and nothing wrong but my abominable debts.

===Chinese===

他一遍又一遍咒自己蠢：成婚之前竟没请教那位精明的兄弟。他的话应验得多狠。当初领她进这步田地之前，向爱丽丝和盘托出，本是多么容易。他不信这主张里有什么实际的危险。他可以起誓，他无心行恶。他不过是在磨人的境地中软弱了、没主见了——仅此而已。可他能拿得准，世人不会给他钉上恶棍的标签么？
又一天过去；一天会带来什么，他说不清——热病似的愁闷、恍惚、心潮翻涌的一天。
她回房去了。夜里十二点，他打定主意要做那场揪心的忏悔，便手执蜡烛，登上老橡木楼梯。
「谁呀？」房里传来妻子的声音。
「我，亲爱的。」
她披着晨衣在门口迎住他。脸色苍白愁苦，眼睛哭肿了。
「哦，莱，亲爱的，我难受死了；我想我要疯了。」
她用发抖的胳膊死死抱住他，伏在他胸前一抽一抽地哭。
查尔斯·费尔菲尔德吓呆了，一种恐怖攝住了他。他想：「她全知道了。」她仰起脸望他，而他那张脸，白得像鬼。
「哦，莱，亲爱的，看上帝份上告诉我——是不是出了很坏的事——叫你这样苦的，只是债么？我悬在这儿，怕得要死。可怜可怜你可怜的小妻子吧，不管是什么，告诉我——全告诉我！」
你会说，这比他梦寐以求的机会还要逼到眼前；可那一望的惨绝人寰，把他击中了，吓住了——那神情实在离绝望太近，离疯狂太近。
「说出来就是杀她。」有个声音似乎在耳边低语；他把她搂得更紧，吻她，笑了。
「什么都没有，只是钱——缺钱——债。凭良心说，你吓了我一跳，爱丽丝，你那样子，那样可怜见儿的。我还当是出了什么可怕的事；可感谢上帝，你安然无恙，连鬼都没见着一个。往后可不许再这么傻了。我看这地方要把咱们的头都搅晕。咱们在这儿安安稳稳，除了我那些可恶的债，什么毛病也没有。

===Original===

You would not wonder at my moping if you knew what debt is; but I won’t look, if I can help it, quite so miserable for the future; for, after all, we must have money soon, and you know they can’t hang me for owing them a few hundreds; and I’m quite angry with myself for having annoyed you so, you poor little thing.”
“My noble Ry, it is so good of you, you make me so happy, I did not know what to think, but you have made me quite cheerful again, and I really do think it is being so much alone, I watch your looks so much, and everything preys on me so, and that seems so odious when I have my darling along with me; but Ry will forgive his foolish little wife, I know he will, he’s always so good and kind.”
Then followed more reassuring speeches from Charles, and more raptures from poor Alice. And the end was that for a time Charles was quite turned away from his purpose. I don’t know, however, that he was able to keep his promise about more cheerful looks, certainly not beyond a day or two.
A few days later he heard a tragic bit of news. Tom related to him that the miller’s young wife, down at Raxleigh, hearing on a sudden that her husband was drowned in the mill-stream, though ’twas nothing after all but a ducking, was “took wi’ fits, and died in three days’ time.”
So much for surprising young wives with alarming stories! Charles Fairfield listened, and made the application for himself.
A few days later a letter was brought into the room where rather silently Charles and his wife were at breakfast. It came when he had almost given up the idea of receiving one for some days, perhaps weeks, and he had begun to please himself with the idea that the delay augured well, and Harry’s silence was a sign that the alarm was subsiding.
Here, however, was a letter addressed to him in Harry’s bold hand. His poor little wife sitting next the tea-things, eyed her husband as he opened it, with breathless alarm; she saw him grow pale as he glanced at it; he lowered it to the tablecloth, and bit his lip, his eye still fixed on it.
As he did not turn over the leaf, she saw it could not be a long one, and must all be comprised within one page.
“Ry, darling,” she asked, also very pale, in a timid voice, “it’s nothing very bad. Oh, darling, what is it?”
He got up and walked to the window silently.
“What do you say, darling?” he asked, suddenly, after a little pause.

===Chinese===

「你要知道欠债是什么滋味，就不会怪我闷闷不乐了；可往后我尽量不摆这副苦相；说到底，钱早晚会有；你也知道，欠他们几百镑，还不至于把我绞死；把你搅得这样，我自己也真恼，可怜的小东西。」
「我高贵的莱，你真好，你叫我这样快活；我原先不知该怎么想才好，如今你又叫我心里亮堂了。细想起来，实在是独处的时辰太多，我又太留意你的脸色，样样都压在心上；明明亲人就在身边，还这样，多不该；可莱会饶他傻小媳妇这一回的，我知道他会，他一向那样好、那样亲。」
接着，查尔斯又说了好些宽心话，可怜的爱丽丝又欢喜了好几阵。结局是，查尔斯一时把初衷完全丢开了。只是那份「摆出快活脸色」的许诺守没守住，我难说——肯定超不过一两天。
过了几天，他听到一段惨闻。汤姆讲给他听：拉克斯利那边磨坊主的年轻媳妇，猛听得丈夫淹死在磨坊水渠里——其实不过是落了场水——当下「发了疯病，三天人就没了」。
拿吓人的话惊动年轻媳妇，下场如此！查尔斯·费尔菲尔德听着，把这条道理用到了自己身上。
又过了几天，一封信送进屋来，查尔斯和妻子正相对无言地用早餐。信来得时候，他几乎已断了这几天、甚至这几周收到信的念头，正暗自欢喜，觉得迟迟不来是好兆头，哈利的沉默正说明风声渐息。
可眼前这封信，正是哈利的遒劲笔迹写给他的。可怜的小妻子坐在茶具旁，屏着气看丈夫拆信；见他扫了一眼，脸就白了；他把信按到桌布上，咬住嘴唇，眼睛还钉在上面。
他没有翻页，她看得出信不长，全拢在一页之内。
「莱，亲爱的，」她也脸色煞白，怯怯地问，「不是什么很坏的事吧。哦，亲爱的，是什么？」
他起身，默默走到窗前。
「你说什么，亲爱的？」顿了一小会儿，他忽然问。

===Original===

She repeated her question.
“No, darling, nothing, but﻿—but possibly we may have to leave this. You can read it, darling.”
He laid the letter gently on the tablecloth beside her, and she picked it up, and read﻿—

> “My dear Charlie,
> “The old soldier means business. I think you must go up to London, but be sure to meet me tomorrow at Hatherton, say the Commercial Hotel, at four o’clock, p.m.
> “Your affectionate brother,
> “Harry Fairfield.”

===Chinese===

她把问话又说了一遍。
「不，亲爱的，没事；只是——只是咱们兴许得离开这儿。你可以看，亲爱的。」
他把信轻轻放在她手边的桌布上；她拿起来，念——

> 「亲爱的查理：
> 「『老兵』是动真格的了。我看你必须上伦敦一趟；但明天务必到哈瑟顿见我，就约商务旅馆（Commercial Hotel），下午四点。
> 「爱你的兄弟，
> 哈利·费尔菲尔德。」

===Original===

“Who does he mean by the old soldier?” asked Alice, very much frightened, after a silence.
“One of those d﻿—﻿—﻿d people who are plaguing me,” said Charles, who had returned to the window, and answered, still looking out.
“And what is his real name, darling?”
“I’m ashamed to say that Harry knows ten times as well as I all about my affairs. I pay interest through his hands, and he watches those people’s movements; he’s a rough diamond, but he has been very kind, and you see his note﻿—where is it? Oh, thanks. I must be off in half an hour, to meet the coach at the ‘Pied Horse.’ ”
“Let me go up, darling, and help you to pack, I know where all your things are,” said poor little Alice, who looked as if she was going to faint.
“Thank you, darling, you are such a good little creature, and never think of yourself﻿—never, never﻿—*half enough*.”
His hands were on her shoulders, and he was looking in her face, with sad strange eyes, as he said this, slowly, like a man spelling out an inscription.
“I wish﻿—I wish a thousand things. God knows how heavy my heart is. If you cared for yourself, Alice, like other women, or that I weren’t a fool﻿—but﻿—but you, poor little thing, it was such a venture, such a sea, such a crazy boat to sail in.”
“I would not give up my Ry, my darling, my husband, my handsome, clever, noble Ry﻿—I’d lose a thousand lives if I had them, one by one, for you, Charlie; and oh, if you left me, I should die.”
“Poor little thing,” he said, drawing her to him with a trembling strain, and in his eyes, unseen by her, tears were standing.
“If you leave this, won’t you take me, Charlie? won’t you let me go wherever you go? and oh, if they take my man﻿—I’m to go with you, Charlie, promise that, and oh, my darling, you’re not sorry you married your poor little Alley.”
“Come, darling, come up; you shall hear from me in a day or two, or see me. This will blow over, as so many other troubles have done,” he said, kissing her fondly.
And now began the short fuss and confusion of a packing on brief notice, while Tom harnessed the horse, and put him to the dogcart.

===Chinese===

「他说的『老兵』是谁？」静了一阵，爱丽丝问，怕极了。
「缠着我的那班该死的家伙里的一个。」查尔斯说；他已回到窗前，仍旧望着外面答话。
「他究竟叫什么名字，亲爱的？」
「说来惭愧，我的事，哈利知道得比我细十倍。利息经他的手付，那班人的动静也由他盯着；他是块粗坯子，可待我极好；你看见他的条子了——在哪儿？哦，谢谢。半个钟头后我就得动身，去『花马』赶驿车。」
「让我上楼帮你收拾行李吧，亲爱的，你的东西都在哪儿我全知道。」可怜的小爱丽丝说，人像要晕倒。
「谢谢你，亲爱的；你真是个好东西，从来不想到自己——从来不——*怎么也想不到一半*。」
他双手搭在她肩上，望着她的脸，眼神哀而异样；这句话他说得很慢，像一个人拼读碑上的铭文。
「我但愿——但愿一千样事。上帝知道我的心多重。爱丽丝，你若像别的女人那样顾惜自己，或者我不是个傻瓜——可是——可是你这可怜的小东西，这一程好险，这样的海，这样一条破船。」
「我决不舍我的莱，我的亲人，我的丈夫，我俊朗、聪明、高贵的莱——我若有一千条命，查理，我肯为你一条一条丢尽；哦，你要是丢下我，我就活不成。」
「可怜的小东西。」他说，颤颤地把她揽近；他眼里立着泪，她没有看见。
「你要是离开这儿，带上我好不好，查理？你走到哪儿，让我跟到哪儿，好不好？哦，他们要是把我男人带走——我也要跟你去，查理，你答应我；还有，哦，亲爱的，你娶了你可怜的小艾莉，不后悔吧？」
「来，亲爱的，上楼吧；一两天你就收到我的信，要不就见着我。这一阵会过去的，多少麻烦不都过去了。」他说着，深情地吻她。
仓促动身的忙乱短促地开场；汤姆套马，把它驾上双轮狗车。

===Original===

And the moment having arrived, down came Charles Fairfield, and Tom swung his portmanteau into its place, and poor little Alice was there with, as Old Dulcibella said, “her poor little face all cried,” to have a last look, and a last word, her tiny feet on the big unequal paving stones, and her eyes following Charlie’s face, as he stepped up and arranged his rug and coat on the seat, and then jumped down for the last hug; and the wild, close, hurried whisperings, last words of love and cheer from laden hearts, and pale smiles, and the last, *really* the last look, and the dogcart and Tom, and the portmanteau and Charlie, and the sun’s blessed light, disappear together through the old gateway under the wide stone arch, with tufted ivy and careless sparrows, and little Alice stands alone on the pavement for a moment, and runs out to have one last wild look at the disappearing “trap,” under the old trees, as it rattled swiftly down to the narrow road of Carwell Valley.
It vanished﻿—it was gone﻿—the tinkling of the wheels was heard no more. The parting, for the present, was quite over, and poor little Alice turned at last, and threw her arms about the neck of kind old Dulcibella, who had held her when a baby in her arms in the little room at Wyvern Vicarage, and saw her now a young wife, “wooed and married, and a’,” in the beauty and the sorrows of life; and the light air of autumn rustled in the foliage above her, and a withered leaf or two fell from the sunlit summits to the shadow at her feet; and the old woman’s kind eyes filled with tears, and she whispered homely comfort, and told her she would have him back again in a day or two, and not to take on so; and with her gentle hand, as she embraced her, patted her on the shoulder, as she used in other years﻿—that seemed like yesterday﻿—to comfort her in nursery troubles. But our sorrows outgrow their simple consolations, and turn us in their gigantic maturity to the sympathy and wisdom that is sublime and eternal.
Days passed away, and a precious note from Charlie came. It told her where to write to him in London, and very little more.
The hasty scrawl added, indeed emphatically, that she was to tell his address to no one. So she shut it up in the drawer of the old-fashioned dressing-table, the key of which she always kept with her.
Other days passed. The hour was dull at Carwell Grange for Alice.

===Chinese===

时辰到了。查尔斯·费尔菲尔德下了楼；汤姆把旅行箱甩上车座；可怜的小爱丽丝也在——用老杜尔西贝拉的话说，「可怜的小脸都哭花了」——来道最后一面、说最后一句话；她一双小脚立在高高低低的大石板上，眼睛一路追着查理的脸，望他跨上车、铺好毯子和外套，又跳下来最后抱一抱；乱纷纷、紧贴贴、急匆匆的耳语，两颗沉甸甸的心吐出最后的情话和打气，惨白的微笑，最后一眼——*真正*的最后一眼；随后狗车、汤姆、旅行箱、查理，连同太阳那有福的光，一齐消失在宽石拱下的老门洞里——拱上垂着簇簇常春藤，跳着无心的麻雀；小爱丽丝独自在石板上立了片刻，又奔出去，在老树底下，最后疯望一眼那越走越远的「怪车」，它正辚辚疾驰，下到卡韦尔谷那条窄路上。
车影没了——人去了——轮子的叮当再也听不见了。眼下这一别，就这样过完了。可怜的小爱丽丝终于转过身，扑上去接住仁慈的老杜尔西贝拉的脖子——当年在怀弗恩牧师宅那间小屋里，老妇人还把她抱在怀里；如今眼见她做了少妇，「求了亲，嫁了人，一切齐备」，正立在人生的美与愁里。秋日轻爽的风在她头顶的叶间沙沙响，一两片枯叶从洒满阳光的树顶飘落，落在她脚边的阴影里。老妇人慈祥的眼睛盈满泪，凑着她耳朵说家常的宽慰话，说他一两天就回来，叫她别这样伤心；搂着她，还用那只温柔的手轻轻拍她的肩，一如当年——恍如昨日——在育儿室的小委屈里哄她那样。只是我们的悲伤，早长过了那些朴素的安慰；悲伤长成它巨大的成年，便把我们推向那种崇高而永恒的体恤与智慧。
一天天过去，查理一封珍贵的短信来了。信上告诉她往伦敦哪儿写，此外几乎没有别的。
那笔潦草的字还添了一句，千叮万嘱：他的地址，对谁也不许说。她便把它锁进老式梳妆台的抽屉，钥匙一贯随身带着。

===Original===

But things moved on in their dull routine without event or alarm.
Old Mildred Tarnley was sour and hard as of old, and up to a certain time neither darker nor brighter than customary. Upon a day, however, there came a shadow and a fear upon her.
Two or three times on that day and the next, was Mrs. Tarnley gliding, when old Dulcibella with her mistress was in the garden, about Alice’s bedroom, noiselessly as a shadow. The little girl downstairs did not know where she was. It was known but to herself﻿—and what she was about. Coming down those dark stairs, and going up, she went on tiptoe, and looked black and stern as if she was “laying out” a corpse upstairs.
Accidentally old Dulcibella, coming into the room on a message from the garden, surprised lean, straight Mrs. Tarnley, feloniously trying to turn a key, from a bunch in her hand, in the lock of the dressing-table drawer.
“Oh, la! Mrs. Tarnley,” cried old Dulcibella, very much startled.
The two women stood perfectly still, staring at one another. Each looked scared. Stiff Mildred Tarnley, without, I think, being the least aware of it, dropped a stiff short courtesy, and for some seconds more the silence continued.
“What *be* you a-doing here, Mrs. Tarnley?” at length demanded Dulcibella Crane.
“No occasion to tell *you*,” replied Mildred, intrepidly. “Another one, that owed her as little as I’m like ever to do, would tell your young mistress. But I don’t want to break her heart﻿—what for should I? There’s dark stories enough about the Grange without no one hangin’ theirself in their garters. What I want is where to direct a letter to Master Charles﻿—that’s all.”
“I can’t say, I’m sure,” said old Dulcibella.
“She got a letter from him o’ Thursday last; ’twill be in it no doubt, and that I take it, ma’am, is in this drawer, for she used not to lock it; and I expect you, if ye love your young mistress, to help me to get at it,” said Mrs. Tarnley, firmly.
“Lor, Mrs. Tarnley, ma’am! *me* to pick a lock, ma’am! I’d die first. Ye can’t mean it?”
“I knowd ye was a fool. I shouldn’t ’a said nothing to ye about it,” said Mildred, with sharp disdain.
“Lawk! I never was so frightened in my life!” responded Dulcibella.
“Ye’ll be more so, mayhap. I wash my hands o’ ye,” said Mrs. Tarnley, with a furious look, and a sharp little stamp on the floor.

===Chinese===

好在日子照它乏味的老套挪着，无事，也无惊。
老米尔德里德·塔恩利照旧又酸又硬；直到某一天为止，不比往常更阴，也不更亮。可就在某一天，一片阴影和一份惊惺，罩上了她。
那一天和第二天，趁老杜尔西贝拉陪着主母在园中，塔恩利太太两三回溜到爱丽丝卧房左近，悄没声像道影子。楼下的小丫头不知她在哪儿。知道她在哪儿、在忙什么的，只有她自己。上楼下楼，那段黑楼梯她全踮着脚走，脸色阴沉严厉，像楼上正停着一具等着装殓的尸首。
老杜尔西贝拉从园里捎话进屋，撞了个正着：精瘦笔挺的塔恩利太太，正拿手里一串钥匙中的一把，鬼鬼祟祟地捅梳妆台抽屉的锁。
「哎呀！塔恩利太太。」老杜尔西贝拉喊出声，吓了一大跳。
两个女人钉在原地，直眉瞪眼望着彼此，个个面带惊惺。僵直的米尔德里德·塔恩利——我想她自己全没察觉——硬邦邦屈了个短促的膝；又是几秒钟的沉默。
「您*在这儿*干什么呢，塔恩利太太？」杜尔西贝拉·克兰终于开口质问。
「犯不着告诉*你*。」米尔德里德面无惧色地答。「换一个——她于我的情分，不会比我打算给她的多——早报告你家少奶奶了。可我不想碎她的心——我图什么？格兰奇的晦气故事够多了，不必再添一个拿吊袜带自尽的。我要的，是给查尔斯少爷寄信的地址——就这一件。」
「这我说不上，真的。」老杜尔西贝拉说。
「上礼拜四她接了他一封信；地址八成就在信上；而那封信，太太，我断定就在这抽屉里——她从前是不上锁的；你若疼你家少奶奶，就帮我把它拿出来。」塔恩利太太说得斩钉截铁。
「老天，塔恩利太太！*我去*撬锁，太太！我宁可死。您不会是这个意思吧？」
「我就知道你是个傻子。本来什么也不该对你说。」米尔德里德说，尖刻地不屑。
「哎哟！我一辈子没这样怕过！」杜尔西贝拉应道。
「兴许还有更怕的。我跟你两清了。」塔恩利太太说，一脸怒容，朝地上狠狠一顿脚。

===Original===

“I thought o’ nothing but your mistress’s good, and if ye tell her I was here, I’ll explain all, for I won’t lie under no surmises, and I think ’twill be the death of her.”
“Oh, this place, this hawful place! I never was so frightened in my days,” said Dulcibella, looking very white.
“She’s in the garden now, I do suppose,” said Mildred, “and if ye mean to tell her what I was about, ’taint a pin’s head to me, but I’ll go out and tell her myself, and even if she lives through it, she’ll never hold up her head more, and that’s all *you’ll* hear from Mildred Tarnley.”
“Oh, dear! dear! dear! my heart, how it goes!”
“Come, come, woman, you’re nothin’ so squeamish, I dare say.”
“Well,” said Dulcibella; “it may be all as you say, ma’am, and I’ll say ye this justice, I ha’n’t missed to the value of a pennypiece since we come here, but if ye promise me, only ye won’t come up here no more while we’re out, Mrs. Tarnley, I won’t say nothing about it.”
“That settles it, keep your word, Mrs. Crane, and I’ll keep mine; I’ll burn my fingers no more in other people’s messes;” and she shook the key with a considerable jingle of the whole bunch from the keyhole, and popped it grimly into her pocket.
“Your sarvant, Mrs. Crane.”
“Yours, Mrs. Tarnley, ma’am,” replied Dulcibella.
And the interview which had commenced so brusquely, ended with ceremony, as Mildred Tarnley withdrew.
That old woman was in a sort of fever that afternoon and the next day, and her temper, Lilly Dogger thought, grew more and more savage as night approached. She had in her pocket a friendly fulsome little letter, which had reached her through the post, announcing an arrival for the night that was now approaching. The coach that changed horses at the “Pied Horse,” was due there at half-past eleven, p.m., but might not be there till twelve, and then there was a long drive to Carwell Grange.
“I’m wore out wi’ them, I’m tired to death; I’m wore off my feet wi’ them; I’m worked like a hoss. ’Twould be well for Mildred Tarnley, I’m thinkin’, she was under the mould wi’ a stone at her head, and shut o’ them all.”

===Chinese===

「我一心只为你家太太好；你若告诉她我来过，我就当面把话说明白——我不肯背着一层疑影儿做人；再说，我怕这一下就要她的命。」
「哦，这地方，这吓死人的地方！我这辈子没这样怕过。」杜尔西贝拉说，脸白得很。
「这会儿她在园子里，我料着。」米尔德里德说，「你若打算把我干的事告诉她，于我不值一根针尖；我索性自己出去对她讲；她纵然熬得过，这辈子头也抬不成了——米尔德里德·塔恩利给你的话，就这一句。」
「哎呀！天哪！天哪！我这心，跳成什么样了！」
「好了，好了，婆娘，你也没那么娇气，我量着。」
「好吧。」杜尔西贝拉说；「兴许都像您说的，太太；我也替您说句公道话：打咱们来这儿，我没短过一枚便士价值的东西；可您只要答应我——往后我们外出，您不再上这屋来——塔恩利太太，这事我一个字不提。」
「这就结了。你守你的话，克兰太太，我守我的；旁人的烂摊子，我再也不烫这双手了。」她把那把钥匙从锁眼里抖出来，整串铜响，冷着脸塞进兜里。
「您仆人，克兰太太。」
「您仆人，塔恩利太太。」杜尔西贝拉答。
这场开头那样粗声硬气的会面，在客客气气里收了尾，米尔德里德·塔恩利退了出去。
那天下午和第二天，老妇人一直处在一种低烧似的烦躁里；莉莉·多格觉得，夜越近，她的火气越凶。她兜里掠着一封亲热得发腻的小信，邮局送来的，通报今夜将有一位到达。在「花马」换马的那班驿车，该在夜里十一点半到，兴许拖到十二点；之后还有长长一段路，才到卡韦尔格兰奇。
「我叫他们拖垮了，我累死了；两条腿都叫他们磨没了；我使唤得像匹马。我琢磨着，米尔德里德·塔恩利还是躺进土里、头顶压块石板的好，一了百了，谁也找不着。」

## V The Terrace Garden / 第五章 露台花园
===Original===

Alice Maybell grew up very pretty; not a riant beauty, without much colour, rather pale, indeed, and a little sad. What struck one at first sight was a slender figure, with a prettiness in every motion. A clear-tinted oval face, with very large dark gray eyes, such as Chaucer describes in his beauties as “ey‑es gray as glass,” with very long lashes; her lips of a very brilliant red, with even little teeth, and when she smiled a great many tiny soft dimples.
This pretty creature led a lonely life at Wyvern. Between her and the young squires, Charles and Henry, there intervened the great gulf of twenty years, and she was left very much to herself.
Sometimes she rode into the village with the old Squire; she sat in the Wyvern pew every Sunday; but except on those and like occasions, the townsfolk saw little of her.
“ ’Taint after her father or mother she takes with them airs of hers; there was no pride in the Vicar or poor Mrs. Maybell, and she’ll never be like her mother, a nice little thing she was.”
So said Mrs. Ford of the George Inn at Wyvern﻿—but what she called pride was in reality shyness.
