The whole thing was like a dream. The room seemed all a cloud. She could see nothing but the white figure that was still close, climbing swiftly over the bed, with one hand extended now and the knife in the other.
Not knowing how she got there, she was now standing with her back to the wall, in the further corner of the room, staring at the dreadful figure in a catalepsy of terror.
There was hardly a momentary pause. She was afraid to stir lest the slightest motion should betray her to the search of this woman. Had she, as she stood and listened sharply, heard her breathing?
With sudden decision, long light steps, and her hand laid to the wall, she glided swiftly toward her. With a gasp Alice awoke, as it were, from her nightmare, and, almost wild with terror, fled round the bed to the door. Hastening, jostling by the furniture, gliding, on the whole, very adroitly after her, her face strained with a horrible eagerness and fear, came the blind woman.
Alice tried to pull open the door. She had locked it herself, but in her agitation forgot.
Now she seized the key and tried to turn it, but the strong hand of the stranger, in forcing it round a second time, had twisted it so that it was caught in the lock and would not turn.
Alice felt as people feel in dreams, when pursuit is urgent and some little obstruction entangles flight and threatens to deliver the fugitive into the hands of an implacable pursuer.

===Chinese===

不管这女人进屋来是为了什么，爱丽丝心存希望地想：至少，她此刻站在自己跟前，脸色惨白、神情阴沉，白翳蒙眼，眉心拧着恶毒的算计——她对她们么也不知道，正如她分明什么也看不见。
爱丽丝死人似的僵坐着，惊恐万状地凝望着这张离自己不过两码开外的、没有视力的脸。
忽然，这两码的距离消失了：那女人两大步抢上，一只手直伸过来，站到床边，一把攥住爱丽丝的睡衣，用力朝自己拖去。爱丽丝拼死挣扎，尖叫一声朝后挣，睡衣撕开了。可高大的女人立刻改攥她的肩头，膝行着扑上床来，把她整个人拖倒在床褥上，几乎同时，一柄刀朝她的咽喉刺下。
为了这一击她不得不腾出一只手，爱丽丝拼死一扑，堪堪避过了刀锋。
整个情景恍如梦境。屋子仿佛成了一团云雾。她什么也看不见，只看见那白色的影子还紧紧逼着，飞快地爬过床来，一手前探，一手握刀。
不知怎么到的，她已背贴着墙，站在屋子远端的角落里，吓成一尊石像，瞪着那可怕的身影。
几乎没有停顿。她不敢动，生怕一丝一毫的动静就会把自己暴露给这女人的搜寻。那女人站着尖起耳朵听的时候，可曾听见她的呼吸？
那女人陡然下了决心，迈开轻而长的步子，一只手扶着墙，飞快地朝她滑过来。爱丽丝喘息一声，仿佛从噩梦中惊醒，吓得几乎疯了，绕过床朝房门逃去。瞎眼女人跟着追来，磕磕绊绊擦过家具，总体上却追得相当灵巧，脸上绷着一副狰狞的急切与凶惧。
爱丽丝去拉门。门是她自己锁的，慌乱中竟忘了。
这时她抓起钥匙去拧，可那陌生人方才硬拧过第二圈，钥匙已经拧别在锁里，转不动了。
爱丽丝的心境，正像人在梦中：追兵已急，偏偏一道小小障碍缠住了逃命的路，眼看要把逃的人送进那赶尽杀绝的追凶手里。

===Original===

A frantic pull, and a twist or two of the key in vain, and the hand of the pursuer was all but upon her. Again she sprang and scrambled across the bed, and it seemed enraged by the delay, and with a face sharpening and darkening with insanity, the murderess, guided by the sound, flung herself after her; and now, through the room and lobbies pealed shrieks of murder, as Alice flew before the outstretched hand of the beldame, who, balked of her prey, followed with reckless fury, careless now against what she struck or rushed, and clawing the air, as it seemed, within an inch of Alice’s shoulder.
Unequal as it appeared, in this small pen, the struggle to escape could not have lasted very long. The old closet door, thinly covered with paper, through which the sharp knife had glided almost without noise, was locked, and escape through it as hopeless as through the other door. Through the window she would have thrown herself, but it was fastened, and one moment’s delay would have been death. Had a weapon been in her hand, had she thought of it in this extremity of terror, her softer instincts might have been reversed, and she might have turned on her pursuer and fought, as timid creatures have done, with the ferocity of despair, for her life. But the chance that might have so transformed her did not come. Flight was her one thought, and that ended suddenly, for tripping in the upturned carpet she fell helplessly to the floor. In a moment, with a gasp, her pursuer was kneeling by her side, with her hand in her dishevelled hair, and drawing herself close for those sure strokes of the knife with which she meant to mangle her.
As the eyes of the white owl glare through the leaves on the awaking bird, and its brain swims, and its little heart bounces into a gallop, seeing its most dreadful dream accomplished, escape impossible, its last hour come﻿—then the talons of the spectre clutch its throat, and its short harmless life is out, so might it have been with pretty Alice.
In that dreadful second of time all things that her eyes beheld looked strange, in a new reality﻿—the room contracted, and familiar things were unlike themselves, and the certainty and nearness of that which she now knew﻿—all her life before was but a dream to her﻿—what an infidel, what a fool she had been﻿—*here* it was, and *now*﻿—death.

===Chinese===

她拚命一拽，钥匙拧了几拧，白费劲，追凶的手眼看就要落到她身上。她又纵身翻过床去；那女人像是被这阻延激怒了，脸因癫狂而抽紧、发黑，循着声响猛扑过来。于是，房里和过道里响彻了「杀人啦」的尖叫——爱丽丝在那老妖婆直伸的手掌前奔逃，妖婆眼看到口的猎物飞了，跟着狂追，凶性大发，此刻撞着什么、碰着什么全不顾了，那双爪仿佛已在爱丽丝肩头一寸之内乱抓。
这场舍虽然看上去实力悬殊，在这小小的牢笼里，逃命也撑不了多久。那扇薄薄糊着纸的老衣橱门——锋利的刀几乎无声地从纸上滑过——锁着，从那儿逃出去同走正门一样无望。她想从窗口跳下去，可窗子闩死了，而耽搁一刻便是死。倘若她手里有件武器，倘若在这恐怖的极点上她能想起，她天性里的温软或许会倒转过来，叫她回身扑向追凶，像怯懦的生灵偶尔做过的那样，以绝望的凶狠为自己的性命一搏。可那足以叫她脱胎换骨的机会终究没有来。逃，是她唯一的念头；而这念头也戛然而止——她被翻卷的地毯绊住，无可奈何地摔倒在地板上。转瞬之间，追她的人喘着气已跪在她身旁，一只手攥进她披散的头发，把身子凑拢过来，要抢那稳稳的几刀，把她碎尸。
正如白鸮的目光穿透枝叶照住惊醒的小鸟，小鸟头晕目眩，小心脏狂跳成一串奔马，眼见最可怕的梦成了真、逃生无路、末时已到——幽灵的利爪随即掐住它的咽喉，那短促无辜的生命便告终结——漂亮的爱丽丝，眼看也要落得这般下场。
在那骇人的一瞬，她眼里看到的一切都变了样，带着一种崭新的真实——屋子缩小了，熟悉的东西面目全非，而她如今认清的那件事又如此确凿、如此迫近——此前的一生对她不过是一场梦——自己曾是多么盲目、多么傻——它就在*这儿*，就在*此刻*——死。

===Original===

The helpless yell that burst from her lips, as this dreadful woman shuffled nearer on her knees, was answered by a crash from the door burst in, and a cry from a manly voice﻿—the door flew wide, and Alice saw her husband pale as death; with a single savage blow he stretched her assailant on the floor﻿—in another moment Alice, wild with terror, half-fainting, was in his arms.
And﻿—did he *strike* her? Good God!﻿—had he struck her! How did she lie there bleeding? For a moment a dreadful remorse was bursting at his heart﻿—he would have kneeled﻿—he could have killed himself. Oh, manhood! Gratitude! Charity! Could he, even in a moment of frenzy, have struck down any creature so﻿—that had ever stood to him in the relation of that love? What a rush of remembrances, and hell of compunction was there!﻿—and for a rival! She the reckless, forlorn, guilty old love cast off, blasted with deformity and privation, and now this last fell atrocity! Alice was clinging to him, the words “darling, darling, my Ry, my saviour, my Ry,” were in his ears, and he felt as if he hated Alice﻿—hated her worse even than himself. He froze with horror and agony as he beheld the ineffaceable image of that white, bloodstained twitching face, with sightless eyes, and on the floor those straggling locks of changed, grizzled hair, that once were as black as a raven’s wing to which he used to compare them. Oh maddening picture of degradation and cruelty! To what had they both come at last?
But an iron necessity was upon him, and with an energy of hypocrisy, he said﻿—“Alice, my treasure, my darling, you’re safe, aren’t you?”
“Oh, darling, yes,” she gasped.
“Not here﻿—you mustn’t stay here﻿—run down﻿—she’s mad﻿—she’s a mad woman﻿—not here a moment.”
Half stunned and dreamy with horror, Alice glided down the stairs, passing honest Tom who was stumbling up, half awake, but quite dressed excepting his coat.
“Run, Tom, help your master, for God’s sake﻿—there’s something dreadful,” she said as she passed him with her trembling hands raised.
“Where, ma’am, may’t be?” said Tom, pausing with a coolness that was dreadful, she thought.
“There, there, in his room, my room; go, for heaven’s sake!”
Up ran Tom, making a glorious clatter with his hobnails, and down ran Alice, and just at the foot of the stair she met Mildred Tarnley’s tall slim figure.

===Chinese===

这可怕的女人膝行着又逼近来，爱丽丝唇边迸出一声无助的惨呼——回应她的，是房门被撞开的一声轰响和一个男人的呼喊——门扇大敞，爱丽丝看见丈夫面无人色地站在那里；他一记凶狠的重拳，把行凶的女人打翻在地——转眼间，吓得魂飞魄散、几乎晕厥的爱丽丝已在他怀里。
——他*打了*她么？天哪！——他打了她！她怎么躺在那儿流血？刹那间，一阵可怕的悔恨冲上他的心口——他恨不得跪下去——恨不得杀了自己。哦，男子汉的气性！恩情！仁爱！难道他即便在疯狂的一瞬，竟能这样打倒一个生灵——一个曾以那样的恩爱与他相依的人？多少往事一齐涌来，悔恨如地狱般煎熬！——何况，为了一个情敌！她，那个任性、孤苦、有罪的旧情人，被抛弃了，毁于残缺与困顿，如今又添上这最后一桩狠毒的暴行！爱丽丝紧偎着他，「亲爱的，亲爱的，我的瑞，我的救命恩人，我的瑞」的话语响在他耳边，他却觉得自己恨爱丽丝——恨她甚至胜过恨自己。他望着那张惨白、染血、抽搐的脸那一双瞎眼的模样，永世磨灭不去，望着地板上那几绺斑白了的乱发——从前它们黑得赛过乌鸦的翅膀，他还拿来作过比方——他吓得心都凉了。哦，这沉沦与残酷的疯狂图景！他们两个到底走到了什么地步！
可一股铁一般的紧迫压着他，他拿出虚伪到底的劲头，说道——「爱丽丝，我的宝贝，我的亲亲，你平安了，是吧？」
「哦，亲爱的，平安了。」她喘着气说。
「别待在这儿——你不能待在这儿——快下去——她疯了——她是个疯女人——一刻也别待在这儿。」
爱丽丝吓昏了头，恍恍惚惚地飘下楼去，迎面碰上老实的汤姆正磕磕绊绊地上来，睡眼惺忪，只是外衣还没穿。
「快，汤姆，去帮老爷，看在上帝份上——出了可怕的事了。」她经过他身边时举起颤抖的双手说。
「哪儿呢，太太，出了什么事？」汤姆停住脚问，那份镇定她觉得可怕极了。
「那儿，那儿，在他房里，我的房里；快去，千万快去！」
汤姆钉着铁掌的鞋噔噔噔跑上楼去，爱丽丝跑下楼来，恰在楼梯脚下迎面撞见米尔德里德·塔恩利那瘦高的身影。

===Original===

The old woman drew to the banister, and stood still, looking darkly and shrewdly at her.
“Oh! good Mildred﻿—oh, Mrs. Tarnley, for God’s sake don’t leave me.”
“And what’s the row, ma’am, what is it?” asked Mrs. Tarnley, with her lean arm supporting the poor trembling young lady who clung to her.
“Oh, Mrs. Tarnley, take me with you﻿—take me out﻿—I can’t stay in the house; take me away﻿—into the woods﻿—anywhere out of the house.”
“Well, well, come down, come along,” she said, more tenderly than was her wont, and watching her face hard from the corners of her eyes. She was convinced that the “old soldier” was the cause of these horrors.
“Put your arm over my shouther, ma’am; there﻿—that’s it﻿—an’ I’ll put mine round you, if you don’t think I’m making too bold. There now, you’re more easy, I think.”
And as they got on through the passage she asked﻿—
“ ’Twas you that skritched, hey?”
“I? I dare say﻿—did I?”
“Ay did ye, with a will, whoever skritched. Ye seen summat. What may ye have seen that frightened ye like that?”
“We’ll talk by-and-by. I’m ill﻿—I’m horribly ill. Come away.”
“Come, then, if ye like best, ma’am,” said Mildred Tarnley, leading her through the kitchen, and by the outer door into the open air, but she had hardly got a step into the yard when the young lady, holding her fast, stopped short in renewed terrors.
“Oh, Mildred, if she follows us, if she overtook us out here?”
“Hoot, ma’am, who are ye afeard on? Is it that crazy blind woman, or who?”
“Oh, Mildred, yes, it is she. Oh, Mildred, where shall we go, where can I hide myself? there’s nowhere safe.”
“Now you’re just drivin’ yourself distracted, you be. What for need ye fear her? She’s crazy, I’ll not deny, but she’s blind too, and she can’t follow ye here, if she was so minded. Why she couldn’t cross the stile, nor follow ye through a spinnie. But see, ye’ve nout but yer dressin’ gown over yer night clothes, and yer bare feet. Odd’s I’ll not go wi’ ye﻿—ye’ll come back, and if ye must come abroad, ye’ll get yer cloaks and your shoon.”
“No, no, no, Mildred, I’ll go as I am,” cried the terrified lady, at the same time hurrying onward to the yard door.
“Well,” said the old woman following, “wilful lass will ha’ her way, but ye’ll clap this ower your shouthers.”

===Chinese===

老太婆退到栏杆边站定，阴沉沉、尖刁刁地打量她。
「哦！好心的米尔德里德——哦，塔恩利太太，看在上帝份上，别离开我。」
「出什么事了，太太，什么事？」塔恩利太太问，用她瘦瘦的胳膊扶住这位攥着她、抖个不停的可怜少妇。
「哦，塔恩利太太，带我一块儿走——带我出去——我没法待在宅子里；带我走——到林子里去——只要出了这宅子，哪儿都成。」
「好好，下来，跟我来。」她说，语气比平时温柔，眼角却狠狠盯着她的脸。她已经认定，这些祸事的根子就是那位「老丘八」。
「把胳膊搭在我肩上，太太；对——就是这样——我再把胳膊搂住您，您可别嫌我冒失。这就好多了，我想。」
两人穿过过道时，她问——
「方才尖叫的是您吧，嗯？」
「我？大概是——是我么？」
「就是您，叫得可响，不管是谁叫的。您撞见什么了。到底是什么，把您吓成这样？」
「回头再说。我不舒服——难受得厉害。快走。」
「那就随您，太太。」米尔德里德·塔恩利说着，领她穿过厨房，从外门到了露天里；可她刚踏进院子一步，少妇便紧攥着她，在新的恐怖里猛地站住了。
「哦，米尔德里德，她要是追来呢，要是她在外面追上我们呢？」
「嗐，太太，您怕的是谁？是那个瞎眼的疯婆子，还是谁？」
「哦，米尔德里德，就是她。哦，米尔德里德，我们去哪儿，我往哪儿躲？没有一处安全的地方。」
「您这是自个儿把自个儿逼疯了。您干吗要怕她？她是疯了，我不赖账，可她眼睛也瞎呀；她就是想追，也追不到这儿来。她连那道田埂墙都翻不过，也跟不上您钻树丛。可您瞧，您睡衣外头就披了件晨袍，还光着脚。怪了，我不能就这么陪您出去——您得回来，真要出去，也得披上斗篷、穿上鞋。」
「不，不，不，米尔德里德，我就这样去。」吓坏了的少妇嚷道，一边急急朝院门奔去。
「得，」老太婆跟在后头说，「犟丫头总要由着她——可您把这个披在肩上。」

===Original===

And she placed her own shawl on them, and together they passed into the lonely woodlands that, spreading upward from the glen of Carwell, embower the deep ravine that flanks the side of the Grange, and widening and deepening, enter the kindred shadows of the glen.

===Chinese===

说着她把自己的披巾披在少妇肩上；两人一同走进那片寂寞的林地——林子从卡韦尔峡谷一路漫上坡来，笼罩着格兰奇侧畔的深壑，越展越宽、越展越深，最后没入峡谷同源的幽暗里。

## XXIX Tom Is Ordered Up / 第二十九章 汤姆奉命上楼
===Original===

When she reached the foot of the stairs that leads to the gallery on which the room occupied by Alice opens, instead of pursuing her way to the kitchen she turned into a narrow and dark passage that is hemmed in on the side opposite to the wall by the ascending staircase.
The shadows of the banisters on the panelled oak flew after one another in sudden chase as the old woman glided by, and looking up and back she stopped at the door of a small room, constructed as we see in similar old houses, under the stairs. On the panel of this she struck a muffled summons with her fist and on the third or fourth the startled voice of Tom demanded roughly from within﻿—
“*What’s* that?”
“Hish!” said the old woman, through a bit of the open door.
“ ’Tis Mrs. Tarnley﻿—only me.”
“Lauk, woman, ye did take a rise out o’ me. I thought ye was﻿—I don’t know what﻿—I was a-dreaming, I think.”
“Never mind, you must be awake for an hour or so,” said Mrs. Tarnley, entering the den without more ceremony.
Tom didn’t mind Mrs. Tarnley, nor Mrs. Tarnley Tom, a rush. She set the candle on the tiled floor. Tom was sitting in his shirt on the side of his “settlebed,” with his hands on his knees.
“Ye must get on your things, Tom, and if ever you stirred yourself, be alive now. The master’s a-comin’, and may be here, across Cressley Common in half an hour, or might be in five minutes, and ye must go out a bit and meet him, and﻿—are ye awake?”
“Starin’. Go on.”
“Ye’ll tell him just this, the big woman as lives at Hoxton﻿—”
“Hoxton! *Well?*”
“That Master Harry has all the trouble wi’, has come here, angry, in search of Master Harry, mind, and is in the bedroom over the hall-door. Will ye mind all that now?”
“Ay,” said Tom, and repeated it.
“Well, he’ll know better whether it’s best for him to come on or turn back. But if come on he will, let him come in at the kitchen door, mind, and you go that way, too, and he’ll find neither bolt nor bar, but open doors, and nothing but the latch between him and the kitchen, and me sitting by the fire; but don’t you clap a door, nor tread heavy, but remember there’s a sharp pair of ears that ’d hear a cricket through the three walls of Carwell Grange.”
She took up the candle, and herself listened for a moment at the door, and again turned her earnest and sinister face on Tom.

===Chinese===

她走到通往长廊的楼梯脚下——爱丽丝的房门就开在那条长廊上——却没有继续往厨房去，而是拐进一条窄而黑的过道，过道一侧是墙，另一侧被上行的楼梯挤着。
老太婆轻身滑过时，嵌板橡木墙上栏杆的影子忽然你追我赶地飞掠起来；她抬头回望，在一扇小门的跟前停住——那间小屋就造在楼梯底下，同类似老宅里的情形一样。她用拳头在门板上闷闷地敲了几下，敲到第三下还是第四下，门里传来汤姆惊起的声音，粗声粗气地问——
「干什么？」
「嘹！」老太婆从半开的门缝里说。
「是塔恩利太太——就我一个。」
「嗐，婆子，可把我唬了一跳。我还当你是——不知道是什么——我八成在做梦呢。」
「别管那个，你得醒上一两个钟头。」塔恩利太太说着，不再多礼，径自进了这间小窝。
汤姆不忌惮塔恩利太太，塔恩利太太也丝毫不怕汤姆。她把蜡烛搁在砖地上。汤姆只穿着衬衫坐在他的「折叠床」边上，两手按着膝盖。
「你得穿上衣裳，汤姆，要是你这辈子勤快过，这回可得活络起来。老爷要来了，过克雷斯利荒原，也许半个钟头就到，也许五分钟就到；你得出去一段，迎着他，还有——你醒了没有？」
「醒得雪亮。说吧。」
「你就这么告诉他：住在霍克斯顿的那个高大女人——」
「霍克斯顿！怎么样？」
「就是跟哈利少爷纠扯不清的那个，找上门来了，气冲冲的，是来找哈利少爷的，记住——如今就在正门上头那间卧房里。这些你都记住了？」
「嗯。」汤姆应着，复述了一遍。
「好，是往前走还是回头，他自己掂量着办。可他要是非来不可，就叫他走厨房门，记住；你也走那条道。门闩门杠都不会有，门全敞着，隔在他和厨房之间的只有门搭扣，我坐在炉边等着；可你千万别摔门，脚下别踩重了——记住，卡韦尔格兰奇这儿有双尖耳朵，隔着三堵墙连蛐蛐叫都听得见。」
她端起蜡烛，在门口自己听了片刻，又把那张急切而阴沉的脸转向汤姆。

===Original===

“And again, I say, Tom, if ever ye was quick, be quick now,” and she clapped her lean hand down on his shoulder with a sort of fierce shake; “and if ever ye trod soft, go softly now, *mind*.”
Tom, who was scratching his head, and staring in her face, nodded.
“And mind you, the kitchen way, and afraid o’ slips, say ye the message over again to me?”
This he did, glibly enough.
“Here, light your candle from this, and if ye fail your master now, never call yourself man again.”
Having thus charged him, she went softly from this nook with its slanting roof, and thinking of the thankless world, and all the trouble her old bones and brain were put to, she lost her temper, at the foot of the great staircase, and was near turning back again to the kitchen, or perhaps whisking out of the door herself, and marching off to Cressley Common to meet her master, and shock and scare him all she could, and place her resignation, as more distinguished functionaries sometimes do theirs, in the hands of her employer, to prove his helplessness and her own importance, and so assert herself for time past and to come.
Her interview with Tom had not occupied much time. She knocked at the Vrau’s door, and entering, found that person at the close of a greedy repast.
Emotions of fear, I suppose, disturb the appetite, much more than others. Not caring one farthing about Charles, she did not grieve at his infidelity; taking profligacy for granted as the rule of life, it did not even shock her. But she was stung with a furious pang of jealousy, for that needs no love, being in its essence the sense of property invaded, supremacy insulted, and self despised. In this sort of jealousy there is neither the sublimity of despair nor the pathos of sorrow, but simply the malice, fury, and revenge of outraged egotism.
There she sat, unconscious of the glimmer of the firelight, feeding as a beast will bleeding after a blow. Beast she was, with the bestial faculty of cherishing a long revenge, with bestial treachery and seeming unconcern.
“Ho oh! you’ve come back,” she cried, with playful reproach, “cruel old girl! you leave your poor Vrau alone, alone among the ghosts﻿—now, sit down, are you sitting? and tell me everything, and all the news﻿—did you bring a little brandy or what?”
Her open hand was extended, and gently moving over the tray at about the level of the top of a bottle.

===Chinese===

「还有，汤姆，我要说你这辈子麻利过，这回就得麻利。」她把瘦手往他肩上一拍，狠狠晃了一下，「你这辈子要是放轻过脚步，这回就给我轻着走，记住。」
汤姆挠着头、瞪着她的脸，点了点头。
「记住了，走厨房那条道。怕出错，把口信再给我说一遍。」
他一字不差、挺利索地背了一遍。
「来，拿你的蜡烛到我这儿引个火；这回你要是误了老爷的事，就再别自称是条汉子了。」
交代完，她便轻轻走出这个斜顶的小窝，心里想着这不知好歹的世界，想着她这把老骨头和老脑筋受的这些劳苦；走到大楼梯脚下，她的火气涌上来，差点转身回厨房，要不就自己闪出门去，开步奔克雷斯利荒原迎老爷，把能吓的能惊的都给他来个够，把她的辞呈——正如某些更显赫的达官偶尔做的那样——交到雇主手里，好让他知道他离了她不行，显显她自己的要紧，为她过去和将来的日子张目。
她同汤姆这番话没费多少工夫。她敲了敲「弗劳」的门，走进去时，正见那位吃到了一顿饕餮的尾声。
我想，恐惧这一类情绪最搅胃口，胜过其他一切。她对查尔斯毫不在意，所以他的负心并不叫她伤心；把放荡当作人生通例，她甚至连震惊也谈不上。可她被一阵狂怒的醋意蜇得生疼——醋意原不需要爱情，它的本质不过是财产被侵犯之感、至尊被冒犯之感、自我被轻贱之感。这样的醋意里没有绝望的崇高，也没有哀伤的凄婉，只有被冒犯的私心那份恶毒、狂怒与报复。
她就坐在那儿，对炉火的微光浑然不觉，像一头挨了一击的畜生，就着血腥进食。她是头畜生，有畜生那般把一桩仇记到天长地久的本事，有畜生那般的奸诈和满不在乎的假象。
「嗬哦！你回来啦。」她带着戏谑的埋怨喊道，「狠心的老姐妹！把可怜的弗劳一个人扔下，扔在一群鬼魂中间——来，坐下，坐下了么？把什么都告诉我，把所有新闻都告诉我——你带了点白兰地来没有，啊？」
她摊开手掌，在托盘上方约莫瓶口的高度轻轻来回摸索。

===Original===

“No, ma’am, I haven’t none in my charge, but there’s a smell o’ brandy about,” said Mildred, who liked saying a disagreeable thing.
“So there ought,” said the gaunt woman placidly, and lifted a big black bottle that lay in her lap, like a baby, folded in a grey shawl. “But I’ll want this, don’t you see, when I’m on my rambles again﻿—get a little, there’s a good girl, or if you can’t get that, there’s rum or gin, there never was a country-house without something in it; you know very well where Harry Vairvield is there will be liquor﻿—I know him well.”
“But he baint here now, as is well known to you, ma’am,” said Mildred, dryly.
“I’m not going to waste my drink, while I think there’s drink in the house. Who has a right before me, old girl?” said the stranger, grimly.
“Tut, ma’am, ’tis childish talkin’ so, there’s none in my charge, never a drop. Master Harry, I dare say, has summat under lock and key, but not me, and why should I tell you a lie about the like?”
“You never tell lies, old Mildred, I forgot that﻿—but young as she is, I lay my life the woman, Mrs. Harry Vairvield, upstairs, likes a nip now and then, hey? and she has a boddle, I’ll be bound, in her wardrobe, or if she’s shy, ’twixt her bed and her mattress, ole rogue! you know very well, I think, does she? and if she likes it she sleeps sound, and go you, and while she snores, borrow you the bottle.”
“She’s nothing of the sort, she drinks nothing nowhere, much less in her bedroom, she’s a perfect lady,” said Mrs. Tarnley, in no mood to flatter her companion.
“Oh, ho! that’s so like old Mildred Tarnley! Dear old cat, I’m so amused, I could stroke her thin ribs, and pet her for making me laugh so by her frisks and capers instead of throwing you by the neck out of the window for scratching and spitting﻿—I’m so good-natured. Do you tell lies, Mildred?”
“I ’a told a shameful lot in my day, ma’am, but not more mayhap than many a one that hasn’t grace to say so.”
“You read your Bible, Mildred,” said the lady, who with a knife and fork was securing on her plate the morsels to which old Mildred helped her.
“Ay, ma’am, a bit now, and a bit again, never too late to repent, ma’am.”
“Repentance and grace, you’ll do, Mrs. Tarnley. It’s a pleasure to hear you,” said the lady, with her mouth rather full; “and you never see my husband?”
“Now and again, now and again, once and away he looks in.”

===Chinese===

「没有，太太，我手上一滴也没有；不过这屋里可有一股子白兰地味儿。」米尔德里德说；她就爱说叫人不痛快的话。
「有才对呢。」瘦骨嶙峋的女人心平气和地说，举起怀里躺着的一只黑色大瓶——像抱娃娃似的，裹在灰披巾里。「可我再度出门闲逛时还用得着它呢——去弄一点来，好姑娘；弄不着白兰地，朗姆酒、杜松子酒也成，天底下还没有一座乡下宅子是空空如也的；你也知道得很清楚，哪儿有哈利·费尔菲尔德，哪儿就有酒——我太知道他了。」
「可他眼下不在这儿，这您也一清二楚，太太。」米尔德里德干巴巴地说。
「我料着屋里还有酒，就不会糟蹋自带的。论先来后到，谁排得到我前头，老姐妹？」生客阴沉沉地说。
「啐，太太，这么说可小孩子气了；我手上一滴也没有，半滴也没有。哈利少爷名下，我敢说，倒是有一些上了锁的，可不是我的，我干吗为这种事编瞎话？」
「你从不撒谎，老米尔德里德，我把这茬忘了——不过她年纪再轻，我拿性命打赌，楼上那位哈利·费尔菲尔德太太，也爱时不时咂一口，嗯？她衣柜里准藏着一瓶，要不就是害臊，把它塞在床板和褥子当中，老滑头！你心里清楚得很，我说的是不是？她要是有这个嗜好，睡得就沉；你去，趁她打呼噜，把那瓶借来。」
「她才不是那种人；她在哪儿都不沾酒，更别说在卧房里了；她是位地道的太太。」塔恩利太太说，全无心思奉承她的同伴。
「嗬嗬！这可真像老米尔德里德·塔恩利说的话！亲爱的老猫，我可开心了，真想摸摸她那几根瘦肋巴骨，疼她一疼——她连蹦带跳把我逗乐了，我竟没掐住你的脖子把你从窗户扔出去，你这又叫又啐的——我这人脾气多好。你撒谎么，米尔德里德？」
「我这一辈子撒过不老少，太太，可说不定也不比许多嘴上不肯认账的人多。」
「你读《圣经》，米尔德里德。」那位太太说；她正用刀叉把老米尔德里德布给她的吃食一样样拢进盘中。
「读，太太，这儿读一点，那儿读一点；悔改永远不嫌迟，太太。」
「又悔改又蒙恩，你准能得救，塔恩利太太。听你说话真是受用。」太太嘴里塞得相当满地说，「那么——你从没见过我丈夫？」
「偶尔见见，偶尔见见，隔三差五他来望一眼。」

===Original===

“Never stays a week or a month at a time?”
“Week or a month!” echoed Mrs. Tarnley, looking quickly in the serene face of the lady, and then laughing off the suggestion scornfully. “You’re thinking of old times, ma’am.”
“Thinking, thinking, I don’t think I was thinking at all,” said the lady, answering Mildred’s laugh with one more careless; “old times when he had a wife here, eh? old times! How old are they? Eh﻿—that’s eighteen years ago﻿—you hardly knew me when I called here?”
“There was a change surely. I’d like to know who wouldn’t in eighteen years, there’s a change in me since then.”
“I shouldn’t wonder,” said the lady, quietly. “Did he ever tell you how we quarrelled?”
“Not he,” answered Mildred.
“He’s very close,” said the stranger.
“A deal closer than Mr. Harry,” acquiesced Mildred.
“Not like you and me, Mrs. Tarnley, that can’t keep a secret﻿—*never*. That tell truth, and shame the devil. I, because I don’t care a snap of my fingers for you, or him, or the Archbishop of Canterbury; and you, because you’re all for grace and repentance. How am I looking tonight﻿—tired?”
“Tired, to be sure; you ought to be in your bed, ma’am, an hour ago; you’re as white as that plate, ma’am.”
“White are they?﻿—so they used to be long ago,” said the visitor.
“The same set, ma’am. ’Twas a long set in my mother’s time, though ’tis little better than a short set now; but I don’t think there’s more than three plates, and the cracked butter-boat, that had a stitch in it. You’ll mind, although ye may ’a forgot, for I usen’t to send it up to table﻿—only them three, and the butter-boat broke since; and that butter-boat, ’twouldn’t a brought three ha’pence by auction, and ’twas that little slut downstairs, that doesn’t never do nothing right, that knocked it off the shelf, with her smashing.”
“And I’m not looking well tonight?” said this pallid woman.
“You’d be the better of a little blood to your cheeks; you’re as white as paper, ma’am,” answered Mildred.
“I never *have* any colour now, they tell me﻿—always pale, pale, pale; but it isn’t muddy; ’taint what you call *putty*?”
“Well, no.”
“Ha! no; I knew that﻿—*no*, and I’d rather be a little pale. I don’t like your great, coarse peony-faced women; it’s seven years in May last since I lost my sight. Some people are persecuted; one curse after another﻿—rank injustice!

===Chinese===

「从没一连住上过一个礼拜、一个月的？」
「一个礼拜、一个月！」塔恩利太太应声重复，飞快看了那位太太恬静的脸一眼，随即冷笑着把这话撇开，「您是想当年的事了，太太。」
「想当年，想当年——我不记得我想过什么。」那太太说，用一阵更漫不经心的笑回应米尔德里德的笑，「当年他在这儿有个老婆的时候，嗯？想当年！有多当了？咦——那是十八年前了——我上回收拾上门来，你都快认不出我了吧？」
「变化是当然有的。我倒想知道，十八年谁能不变？打那以后我自己也变了。」
「那不奇怪。」那太太淡淡地说。「他跟你讲过我们是怎么闹翻的么？」
「他才不讲呢。」米尔德里德答。
「他嘴可严。」生客说。
「比哈利先生严得多了。」米尔德里德附和。
「不像你我，塔恩利太太，藏不住话——从来藏不住。咱们说实话，叫魔鬼羞愧。我呢，是因为你也好、他也好、坎特伯雷大主教也好，我全不在乎，打个响指的事；你呢，是因为你一心只讲恩典和悔改。我今晚气色怎么样——累么？」
「累，可不；您一个钟头前就该躺下了；您白得跟那只盘子一样，太太。」
「盘子白么？——从前它们也一直白来着。」客人说。
「同一套，太太。我妈那时候还是一大家什呢，如今也就比一小套多不了多少；我数着满共不过三只盘子，外加那只裂了缝的黄油船，接过一道锔子。您该记得，兴许忘了——我从不上它到桌面——就那三只，黄油船是后来碎的；那只黄油船，上拍卖场连三个半便士也换不来，还是楼下那个小懒货给撞下架子的，那丫头干什么都不像样。」
「我今晚气色不好看？」这惨白的女人问。
「您脸上要有点血色才好；您白得跟纸一样，太太。」米尔德里德答。
「她们说我如今压根儿没血色——老是白呀白呀白；可不算暗沉，也不是你说的那种『灰泥色』，对吧？」
「倒不是。」
「哈！不是；我早知道——不是。我宁愿白着点呢。我不喜欢你们那种粗手大脚、脸盘子跟芍药似的女人；到今年五月，我瞎眼就满七年了。有的人就是遭罪受；一样灾接着一样灾——天大的不公！」

===Original===

Why should *I* lose my sight, that never did anything to signify﻿—not half what others have, who enjoy health, wealth, rank﻿—everything. Things are topsy-turvey a bit just now, but we’ll see them righted yet.”

===Chinese===

凭什么偏偏*我*瞎了眼？我又没干过什么了不得的坏事——连旁人干下的一半都不到，可人家照样享受着健康、钱财、地位——样样齐全。眼下这世道是有点儿颠倒，可咱们走着瞧，总有扳回来的一天。」

## LX Tom Orange / 第六十章 汤姆·奥兰治
===Original===

Little Miss Amy had a slight cold, and the next tea-party was put off for a day. On the evening following Harry’s visit at Stanlake Farm, Marjory Trevellian being at that time absent in the village to make some frugal purchases, who should suddenly appear before the little boy’s eyes, as he lifted them from his fleet upon the pond, but his friend, Tom Orange, as usual in high and delightful spirits.
Need I say how welcome Tom was? He asked in a minute or two for Marjory, and took her temporary absence with great good humour. Tom affected chilliness, and indeed the evening was a little sharp, and proposed that they should retire to the cottage, and sit down there.
“How soon do you suppose, youngster, the old hen will come home?”
“Who?”
“Marjory Daw, down the chimney.”
“Oh, Granny?”
This nickname was the only pleasantry of Mr. Orange which did not quite please the boy.
Tom Orange here interpolated his performance of the jackdaw, with his eyelids turned inside out and the pupils quivering, which, although it may possibly have resembled the jackdaw of heraldry, was not an exact portraiture of the bird familiar to us in natural history; and when this was over he asked again﻿—“How soon will she be home?”
“She walked down to the town, and I think she can’t be more than about halfway back again.”
“That’s a mile, and three miles an hour is the best of her paces if she was runnin’ for a pound o’ sausages and a new cap. Heigh ho! and alas and alack-a-day. No one at home but the maid, and the maid’s gone to church! I wrote her a letter the day before yesterday, and I must read it again before she comes back. Where does she keep her letters?”
“In her work-box on the shelf.”
“This will be it, the wery identical fiddle!” said Tom Orange, playfully, setting it down upon the little deal table, and, opening it, he took out the little sheaf of letters from the end, and took them one by one to the window, where he took the liberty of reading them.
I think he was disappointed, for he pitched them back again into their nook in the little trunk-shaped box contemptuously.
The boy regarded Tom Orange as a friend of the family so confidential, and as a man in all respects so admirable and virtuous, that nothing appeared more desirable and natural than that excellent person’s giving his attention to the domestic correspondence.
He popped the box back again in its berth.

===Chinese===

小埃米小姐有点儿着凉，下一场茶会顺延了一天。哈利到斯坦莱克农庄拜访的次日傍晚，玛乔丽·特雷维里安正巧进村置办些省俭的杂物；小男孩从池塘上的舰队上抬起眼来，忽地站在面前的，不是别人，正是他的朋友汤姆·奥兰治，照例兴致高得叫人欢喜。
还用问汤姆多受欢迎么？他过了一两分钟就问起玛乔丽，听说她一时回不来，也毫不在意。汤姆嫌外头冷——傍晚也确实有点扎人——便提议回屋里坐下。
「小家伙，你猜老母鸡几时到家？」
「谁？」
「烟囱里的玛乔丽·道呀。」
「哦，奶奶？」
这个绰号是奥兰治先生仅有的一个不怎么讨男孩喜欢的玩笑。
汤姆·奥兰治说到这里插演了一段寒鸦：把眼皮翻出来，瞳孔直哆嗦——这副尊容或许像纹章上的寒鸦，同博物学里我们熟识的那种鸟可并不怎么肖似。演完他又问——「她几时回来？」
「她进城去了，我想这会儿多半正走到半路。」
「那就是还有一里地；就算为一磅香肠、一顶新帽子撒腿跑，她一个钟头也顶多三里。嗨嗬！唉，苦也。家里就剩丫头，丫头还上教堂了！我前天给她写了封信，趁她没回来，我得再看一遍。她的信搁哪儿？」
「搁在架上她的针线盒里。」
「准是这个，千真万确的小提琴盒子！」汤姆·奥兰治打趣地说着，把它放到小松木桌上，打开来，从末尾抽出一小扎信，一封一封拿到窗前，竟自顾读了起来。
我看他是失望了，因为他把信轻蔑地丢回那只小箱子式的盒子的老窝里。
男孩把汤姆·奥兰治看作这家贴心贴腹的世交，一个各方面都可敬可佩的正人君子，所以这位大好人留心一下人家的家信，在他看来再自然再合适不过了。
他把盒子原样塞回架上。

===Original===

Then he treated the young gentleman to Lingo’s song with the rag-tag-merry-derry perrywig and hatband, etc., and at the conclusion of the performance admitted that he was “dry,” and with a pleasant wink, and the tip of his finger pushing the end of his nose a good deal to the left, he asked him whether he could tell him where Mrs. Trevellian, who would be deeply grieved if she thought that Tom was detained for a drink till her return, kept her liquor.
“Yes, I can show you,” said the boy.
“Wait a minute, my guide, my comforter, and friend,” said Tom Orange; and he ascertained from the door-stone that no one was inconveniently near.
The boy was getting a teacup off the shelf.
“Never mind sugar, my hero, I’ll sweeten it with a thought of Marjory Daw.”
The boy explained, and led him into the dark nook by the hall door. Tom Orange, well pleased, moved almost on tiptoe, and looked curiously and spoke under his breath, as he groped in this twilight.
“Here it is,” said the boy, frankly.
“Where?”
“Here.”
“This!” said Tom, for his friend had uncovered a crock of water.
Tom Orange glared at him and at the water with grotesque surprise, and the bona fides of the boy and the simplicity of the situation struck Tom comically, and, exploding good-humouredly, he sat down in Marjory’s chair and laughed hilariously.
Having satisfied himself by a confidential dialogue that Marjory Daw had no private bottle of comfort anywhere, this agreeable fellow so far forgot his thirst, that he did not mind drawing water from the crock, and talked on a variety of subjects to the young gentleman. In the course of this conversation he asked him two topographical questions. One was﻿—
“Did you ever hear of a place called Carwell Grange?”
And the other resembled it.
“Did you ever hear of a place called Wyvern?”
“No.”
“Think, lad. Did you never hear Mrs. Trevellian speak of Wyvern? Or of Carwell Grange?”
“No.”
“Because there is the tallest mushroom you ever saw in your life growing there, and it is grown to that degree that it blocks the door so that the Squire can’t get into his own house, and the mushroom is counted one of the wonders of the world upon my little word of honour as a gentleman! And

> ‘Since there’s neither drink nor victuals,

Suppose, my lord, we play at skittles?’

===Chinese===

接着他给小少爷表演了林戈的那支歌——什么破烂假发、帽带云云；唱完了自认「嗓子干了」，一面快活地挤挤眼，用指尖把自己的鼻尖往左边推了老远，问小少爷能不能告诉他，特雷维里安太太的酒藏在哪儿——她要是知道汤姆为了讨口酒喝竟等到她回来，准会过意不去的。
「能，我带您去。」男孩说。
「且慢，我的向导、我的安慰、我的朋友。」汤姆·奥兰治说着，先从门槛上探看了一番，确信近处没有碍事的人。
男孩正从架子上取一只茶杯。
「糖就不必了，我的英雄，我一想到玛乔丽·道，这酒自然是甜的。」
男孩说明位置，领他进了正门旁边那个黑黢黢的旮旯。汤姆·奥兰治心满意足，几乎是踮着脚走，在这片昏暗里一边摸索，一边好奇地张望、压着嗓子说话。
「在这儿呢。」男孩爽快地说。
「哪儿？」
「这儿。」
「就这个！」汤姆说——他的小朋友揭开的是一瓦罐清水。
汤姆·奥兰治瞪着他、又瞪着那罐水，一脸滑稽的惊愕；男孩的实心眼和这局面的简单直把他逗着了，他好脾气地轰然笑倒，一屁股坐进玛乔丽的椅子里，笑了个痛快。
他又旁敲侧击地套了一番话，确信玛乔丽·道在任何角落都没有私藏的舒心酒瓶，这位可人兄竟把口渴忘了个干净，索性从瓦罐里舀水解渴，同小少爷天南海北聊了起来。这一番谈话里，他问了两个地理问题。一个是——
「你听说过一个叫卡韦尔格兰奇的地方么？」
另一个同它差不多。
「你听说过一个叫维文的地方么？」
「没有。」
「想想，小子。你没听特雷维里安太太说起过维文？或者卡韦尔格兰奇？」
「没有。」
「因为那儿长着你这辈子见过顶高的一只蘑菇，长得把门都堵死了，老爷进不了自家的宅门；凭我绅士人格的小小信用担保，那蘑菇已被列为世界奇观之一！还有——

> 「既然没酒又没饭，

> 老爷，咱们打九柱玩玩？」

===Original===

And if she’s not back by the end of the game, tell her I had to go on to the bridge to see lame Bill Withershins, and I’ll be back again this evening, I think, or in the morning at latest.”
The game was played, but Marjory did not appear, and Tom Orange, entertaining his young friend with a ludicrous imitation of Bill Withershins’ knock-knees, took his departure, leaving his delighted companion in the state which Moore describes as being usual﻿—

> “When the lamp that lighted

The traveller at first goes out.”

So, having watched Tom till he was quite out of sight, he returned to his neglected navy on the pond, and delivered his admirable Crichton’s message to Marjory Daw on her return.

===Chinese===

「要是这局打完她还不回来，就告诉她，我得先上桥那边去看瘸腿的比尔·威瑟申斯；我想今晚我还会再过来，至迟明天早上。」
球是打完了，玛乔丽却还没露面。汤姆·奥兰治给他的小朋友又学了一段比尔·威瑟申斯的罗圈腿，逗得他乐不可支，这才告辞而去，把他那位心花怒放的小伙伴留在穆尔笔下常说的那种心境里——

> 「当最初为旅人照亮的

> 那盏灯熄灭之时。」

于是，目送汤姆走得没了影，男孩便回到池塘边他那支被冷落的舰队上去；等玛乔丽·道回来，便把那位可敬的克里顿的口信一字不差地传给了她。

## XXXVIII Unreasonable Bertha / 第三十八章 蛮不讲理的伯莎
===Original===

Her husband was at hand﻿—that is to say, under the same roof, and at that moment in the room in which the blind woman was now sitting, bleeding from head and hand, and smiling as she talked, with the false light of a malignant irony.
“So, husband and wife are met again! And what have you to say after so long a time?”
“I’ve nothing to say. Let my deeds speak. I’ve given you year by year fully half my income.”
She laughed scornfully, and exclaimed merely﻿—
“Magnificent man!”
“Miserable pittance it is, but the more miserable, the harder the sacrifice for me. I don’t say I have been able to do much; but I have done more than my means warrant, and I don’t understand what you propose to yourself by laying yourself out to torment and embarrass me. What the devil do you follow me about for? Do you think I’m fool enough to be bullied?”
“A fine question from Charles Vairfield of Wyvern to his wife!” she observed with a pallid simper.
“Wife and husband are terms very easily pronounced,” said he.
“And relations very easily made,” she rejoined.
He was leaning with his shoulder against the high mantelpiece, and looking upon her with a countenance in which you might have seen disdain and fear mingling with something of compunction.
“Relations very easily made, and still more easily affected,” he replied. “Come, Bertha, there is no use in quarrelling over points of law. Past is past, as Leonora says. If I have wronged you anything I am sorry. I’ve tried to make amends; and though many a fellow would have been tired out long ago, I continue to give you proofs that I am not.”
“That is a sort of benevolence,” she said, in her own language, “which may as well be voluntary, for if it be not, the magistrates will compel it.”
“The magistrates are neither fools nor tyrants. You’ll make nothing of the magistrates. You have no rights, and you know it.”
“An odd country where a wife has no rights.”
“Come, Bertha, there is no use in picking a quarrel. While you take me quietly you have your share, and a good deal more. You used to be reasonable.”
“A reasonable wife, I suppose, gives up her position, her character, her prospects, whenever it answers her husband to sacrifice these trifles for his villainous pleasures. Your English wives must be meek souls indeed if they like it. I don’t hear they are such lambs though.”
“I’m not going to argue law points, as I said before.

===Chinese===

她的丈夫就在近旁——也就是说，同在一个屋顶下，此刻就在瞎眼女人坐着的这间屋里；她头上有伤、手上有伤，一边说话一边笑着，笑里闪着恶毒讥诮的假光。
「好啊，夫妻重逢了！隔了这么多年，你有什么可说的？」
「我没什么可说的。让我的行事说话吧。年复一年，我把收入的一半足数给了你。」
她轻蔑地笑起来，只嚷了一句——
「了不起的大善人！」
「微薄得很，可越微薄，我牺牲得越苦。我不说我做成了多少事；可我做的已超出了我的财力。我不明白，你这样处心积虑折磨我、作践我，究竟图什么？你到底见什么鬼一路盯着我？你以为我蠢得可以任你敲诈？」
「维文的查尔斯·费尔菲尔德对妻子说出这种话，好一个体面的问题！」她惨白地假笑着说。
「『妻子』『丈夫』，这两个词说出口最容易。」他说。
「这层关系结起来也最容易。」她回敬。
他斜倚着肩膀靠在高高的壁炉架上，望着她；那张脸上，你分得出轻蔑与恐惧，还搀着几分悔愧。
「结起来容易的关系，装出来更容易。」他答道。「得啦，伯莎，为法律条款吵架毫无用处。过去的事就过去了，像莱奥诺拉说的那样。我若有什么对不住你的地方，我很抱歉。我一直想补偿；多少人早被拖得没了耐性，我却没有，这一点我可以不断向你证明。」
「这一种善心，」她用她自己的话说，「还是自愿的好；要是不自愿，治安官爷们会强迫你自愿的。」
「治安官既不傻也不暴虐。你休想在他们那儿讨到便宜。你没有任何权利，你自己清楚。」
「好一个怪国度：做妻子的竟没有权利。」
「得啦，伯莎，何必寻衅。只要你安分，你那份照旧，还有富余。你从前是讲道理的。」
「『讲道理的妻子』，想必就是：只要丈夫为了他那些下流的享乐要牺牲这些小玩意儿，妻子就得交出她的名分、她的名声、她的前程。你们英国的妻子们要是甘之如饴，可真是温顺的好性子。不过我没听说她们是那样的羔羊。」
「我不打算同你辩法律条款，方才说过了。

===Original===

Lawyers are the proper persons to do that. You used to be reasonable, Bertha﻿—where’s the good in pushing things to extremes?”
“What a gentle creature you are,” she laughed, “and how persuasive!”
“I’m a quiet fellow enough, I believe, as men go, but I’m not persuasive, and I know it. I wish I were.”
“Those whom you have persuaded once are not likely to be persuaded again. Your persuasions are not always lucky. Are they?”
“You want to quarrel about everything. You want to leave no possible point of agreement.”
“Things are at a bad pass when husband and wife are so.”
Charles looked at her angrily for a moment, and then down to the floor, and he whistled a few bars of a tune.
“What do you whistle for?” she demanded.
“Come, Bertha, don’t be foolish.”
“You were once a gentleman. It is a blackguard who whistles in reply to a lady’s words,” she said, on a sudden stretching out her hand tremulously, as if in search of someone to grasp.
“Well, don’t mind. Stick to one thing at a time. For God’s sake say what you want, and have done with it.”
“You must acknowledge me before the world for your wife,” she answered with resolute serenity, and raising her face, and shutting her mouth she sniffed defiantly through her distended nostrils.
“Come, come, Bertha, what good on earth could come of that?”
“Little to you, perhaps.”
“And none to you.”
She laughed savagely. “That lie won’t do.”
“Bertha, Bertha, we may hate one another if you will. But is it not as well to try whether we can agree upon anything. Let us just for the present talk intelligibly.”
“You tried to murder me, you arch-villain.”
“Nonsense,” said he, turning pale, “how can you talk so﻿—how can you? Could I help interposing? You may well be thankful that I did.”
“You tried to murder me,” she screamed.
“You know that’s false. I took the knife from your hand, and by doing so I saved two lives. It was you﻿—not I﻿—who hurt your hand.”
“You villain, you damned villain, I wish I could kill you dead.”
“All the worse for you, Bertha.”
“I wish you were dead and cold in your bed, and my hand on your face to be sure of it.”
“Now you’re growing angry again. I thought we had done with storm and hysterics for a little, and could talk, and perhaps agree upon something, or at all events not waste our few minutes in violence.”
“Violence!﻿—you wretch, who began it?”
“What can you mean, Bertha?”
“You’ve married that woman.

===Chinese===

那是律师们的事。你从前讲道理，伯莎——把事情逼到绝处，有什么好处？」
「你多温柔啊，」她笑道，「多会劝人！」
「我相信，就男人而论，我算是够安静的；可我不大会劝人，这我知道。但愿我会。」
「上过你一回当的人，不会上第二回。你的劝向来不走运，不是么？」
「你事事都要吵，连一点可能达成一致的地方都不肯留。」
「夫妻到了这一步，局面可就糟透了。」
查尔斯恼怒地看了她一会儿，又低头看着地板，用口哨吹了几小节曲子。
「你吹口哨干什么？」她质问。
「得啦，伯莎，别犯傻。」
「你从前还是个体面人。拿口哨回太太的话的，只有流氓。」她说着，突然颤巍巍伸出手来，仿佛要摸索一个可抓的人。
「好，不吹了。一次只说一桩事。看在上帝份上，直说你到底要什么，说完拉倒。」
「你必须当着世人认我做你的妻子。」她斩钉截铁、不动声色地答道，抬起脸，闭紧嘴，翕张着鼻孔挑衅地吸了口气。
「得了，得了，伯莎，那究竟有什么好处？」
「对你，兴许没多少。」
「对你，一丁点也没有。」
她狂笑起来。「这个谎撒不圆。」
「伯莎，伯莎，你要恨就恨吧。可咱们何不试一试，看还有没有一件事谈得拢？就眼下说，先把话说明白。」
「你想要我的命，你这个头号恶棍。」
「胡说，」他说，脸都白了，「你怎么能这样说话——怎么能？我当时能不出手么？你倒该庆幸我出手了。」
「你想要我的命！」她尖叫。
「你知道那不是真的。刀是我从你手里夺下来的，正因如此，我救了两条命。伤你手的，是你自己——不是我。」
「你这恶棍，你这挨千刀的恶棍，我恨不得一刀捅死你。」
「那你更吃亏，伯莎。」
「我恨不得你死在床上，凉冰冰地挺着，我把手捂上你的脸，好确信你真死了。」
「瞧，你又来火了。我本以为风暴和歇斯底里总算消停一阵，咱们能谈谈，兴许在某件事上达成一致，至少不至于把这几分钟糟蹋在撒泼上。」
「撒泼！——你这畜生，是谁开的头？」
「你这是什么意思，伯莎？」
「你娶了那个女人。

===Original===

O I know it all﻿—I your lawful wife living. I’ll have you transported, double-dyed villain.”
“Where’s the good of screaming all this at the top of your voice?” he said, at last growing angry. “You wish you could kill me? I almost wish you could. I’ve been only too good to you, and allowed you to trouble me too long.”
“Ha, ha!﻿—you’d like to put me out of the way?”
“You’ll do that for yourself. Can’t you wait, can’t you listen, can’t you have common reason, just for one moment? What do you want, what do you wish? Do you want every farthing I possess on earth, and to leave me nothing?”
“I’m your wife, and I’ll have my rights.”
“Now listen to me, that’s a question I need not discuss, because you already know what I believe on the subject.”
“You know what your brother Harry thinks.”
“I know what it is his interest to think.”
“You daren’t say that if he were here, you coward.”
“And I don’t care a farthing what he thinks.”
“Ha, ha, ha!”
“But if it had been fifty times over, what it never was, a marriage, your own conduct, long ago, would have dissolved it.”
“And you allow you have married that woman?”
“I shan’t talk to you about it; how I shall act, or may act, or *have* acted is my own affair, and rely upon it I’ll do nothing on the assumption that I ever was married to you.”
Up stood the tall woman, with hands extended toward him, wide open, with a slightly groping motion as if opening a curtain; not a word did she say, but her sightless eyes, which stared full at him, were quivering with that nervous tremor which is so unpleasant to see.
She drew breath two or three times at intervals, long and deep, almost a sob, and then without speaking or moving more she sat down, looking awfully white and wicked.
For a time the old soldier had lost the thread of her discourse. Charles heard a step not very far off. He thought his unreasonable Bertha was about to have a fit, and opening the door he called lustily to Mildred.
It *was* Mrs. Tarnley.
“Will you get her some water, or whatever she ought to have, I think she is ill, and pray be quick.”
With a dark prying look Mildred glanced from one to the other.
“It’s in a madhouse and not here the like of her should be, wi’ them fits and frenzies,” she muttered as she applied herself to the resuscitation of the Dutchwoman.
On her toilet was a little group of bottles labelled “Sal-volatile,” “Asafœtida,” “Valerian.”

===Chinese===

哦，我全知道——我，你的法定妻子，还活着呢。我要把你流放出去，双重罪孽的恶棍。」
「扯着嗓子喊这些有什么用？」他终于也动了火，「你不是想杀我么？我几乎巴不得你能成。我对你只有太好的了，也由着你纠缠得太久了。」
「哈，哈！——你想把我除掉？」
「那个你自己会办到。你就不能等一等、听一听，哪怕片刻地讲一点常理？你要什么，你想怎样？你要我这世上的每一个铜板，叫我片瓦不剩么？」
「我是你的妻子，我要我的权利。」
「那你听着，这个问题我不必讨论，因为在这个题目上我所持的看法，你早已知道。」
「你知道你兄弟哈利的看法。」
「我知道合他利益的是什么看法。」
「他要是在这儿，你就不敢这么说，你这胆小鬼。」
「他才怎么想，我一个铜板也不在乎。」
「哈，哈，哈！」
「可就算那是一桩婚姻——它从来不是——哪怕五十次，你自己的行径，早就把它毁掉了。」
「这么说，你认了你娶了那个女人？」
「这事我不跟你谈；我怎么做、可以怎么做、*已经*怎么做，是我自己的事；你只管放心，我行事绝不会以『我娶过你』为前提。」
高大的女人霍地站起，双手朝他伸去，五指张开，微微摸索着，像在掀一幅帘子；她一言不发，可那双直直瞪着他的瞎眼，正一阵阵神经质地颤动——那副颤相，看了最叫人心里发毛。
她隔一会儿便长长地、深深地吸一两口气，几乎像呜咽；随后不再说话，也不再动，径自坐下，脸白得骇人，一脸凶相。
有一阵子，「老丘八」的话头断了线。查尔斯听见不远处有脚步声。他以为他那位蛮不讲理的伯莎又要发作，便拉开门，大声唤米尔德里德。
来的正是塔恩利太太。
「给她弄点水来，或者她该喝的什么都行，我想她病了；拜托，快些。」
米尔德里德阴沉沉、窥探似的，把两人来回看了一眼。
「她这号人就合待在疯人院，不该待在这儿，动不动就犯病发疯。」她一面咕哝，一面动手救护那荷兰婆子。
她的梳妆台上摆着一小队药瓶，签子上写着「芳香氨醑」「阿魏」「缬草」。

===Original===

“I don’t know which is the right one, but this can’t be far wrong,” she remarked, selecting the sal-volatile, and dropping some into the water.
“La! so it was a sort o’ fit. See how stiff she was. Lor’ bless us, I do wish she was under a mad doctor. See how her feet’s stuck out, and her thumbs tight shut in her fists, and her teeth set,” and old Mildred applied the sal-volatile phial to the patient’s nostrils, and gradually got her into a drowsy, yawning state, in which she seemed to care and comprehend little or nothing of where she was or what had befallen her.
“Tell her I stayed till I saw her better, if she asks, and that I’m coming back again. She says she is hurt.”
“So much the better,” said Mildred; “that will keep her from prowling about the house like a cat or a ghost, as she did, all night, and no good came of it.”
“And will you look to her wrist: she cut it last night, and it is very clumsily tied up, and I’ll come again, tell her.”
So, with a bewildered brain and a direful load at his heart, he left the room.
Where was Alice, he thought. He went downstairs and up again by the back staircase to their room, and there found the wreck and disorder of the odious scene he had witnessed, still undisturbed, and looking somehow more shocking in the sober light of morning.
From this sickening record of the occurrences of last night he turned for a moment to the window, and looked out on the tranquil and sylvan solitudes, and then back again upon the disorder which had so nearly marked a scene of murder.
“How do I keep my reason?” thought he; “is there in England so miserable a man? Why should not I end it?”
Between the room where he stood and the angle of that bedroom in which at that moment was the wretch who agitated every hour of his existence with dismay, there intervened but eight-and-twenty feet, in that polyhedric and irregular old house. If he had but one tithe of her wickedness he had but to take up that poker, strike through, and brain her as she sat there.
Why was he not a little more or a little less wicked? If the latter, he might never have been in his present fix. If the other, he might find a short way out of the thicket﻿—“hew his way out with a bloody axe”﻿—and none but those whose secrecy he might rely on be the wiser!
Avaunt, horrible shadows! Such beckoning phantoms from the abyss were not tempters, but simply terrors.

===Chinese===

「我不知道哪瓶对症，可这瓶总不至于大错。」她说着挑了芳香氨醑，往水里滴了几滴。
「哎呀！还真是一阵疯病。瞧她僵成那样。老天保佑，我真盼她落到疯病大夫手里。瞧她两脚直挺挺戳着，大拇指死死攥在拳头里，牙咬得紧紧的。」老米尔德里德说着，把芳香氨醑的小瓶凑到病人鼻下，渐渐把她熏得昏昏欲睡、呵欠连天，在这种状态里，她在哪儿、出了什么事，她似乎什么都不知道，也什么都不在乎了。
「她要是问起，就说见她好些我才走的，说我还会回来。她说她受了伤。」
「那倒好，」米尔德里德说，「省得她像猫、像鬼似的整夜在宅子里游荡，她昨夜就那样，也没游荡出什么好事来。」
「再请你照看她手腕上的伤：昨夜割的，包扎得糟透了；告诉她我还会来。」
于是他离开了那间屋子，脑子里一片乱麻，心头压着一块可怕的磐石。
爱丽丝在哪儿呢，他想。他下了楼，又从后楼梯上回到他们夫妻的房里；昨夜他亲眼所见的可憎场面留下的残迹还在，原封未动，在清冷的晨光里，不知怎么更显狰狞。
他从这份昨夜惨事的记录前转过身，到窗前站了一会儿，望着外面宁静的林泉幽境，又回头望望那片几乎成了凶案的狼藉。
「我是怎么保住神志的？」他想，「整个英格兰还有比我更惨的人么？我为什么不干脆了结？」
从他站立的这间屋子，到那个拐角卧房——此刻里面正坐着那个搅得他每一小时都惶惶难安的祸根——中间只隔着二十八英尺，在这座多角而不规则的老宅子里。他只要有她十分之一的狠毒，只需抄起那根拨火棍，一棍捅穿过去，把她坐在那儿的脑袋砸烂。
他怎么不再狠一点，或者不那么狠一点呢？若是不那么狠，他或许根本落不到今天这步田地；若是再狠一点，他便能从荆棘丛里寻一条近路出去——「抡起血斧劈出去」——除了他信得过嘴严的人，谁也不会更明白！
去吧，可怕的阴影！这般自深渊招手的幻影，不是诱惑，只是恐怖。

===Original===

No, he was far more likely to load a pistol, put the muzzle in his mouth, and blow his harassed brains out.

===Chinese===

不，他更可能做的事，是给手枪装上子弹，把枪口塞进嘴里，把他那颗煎熬透了的脑袋轰个干净。
